# Paper 2 – Urology II: All Answers by Section

**Compiled from:** 2015–2025 actual Paper 2 (12 papers) and Practice Sets 1–17 Paper 2 — 232 answers.
**Repeats merged:** questions asked more than once are printed once, with every wording and source listed under "Asked as"; their answers are combined so that no fact from any version is lost. 53 merged topics replace 126 original answers; 159 topics in all.
**Layout:** the 17 revision-book sections; in each, long answer questions (LAQ) first, then short answer questions (SAQ), then a Rapid Revision Sheet of that section's key points.

# Section 1 · Basic Principles — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Energy sources in laparoscopic urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3d

### Principles
- Devices seal by denaturing protein. Electrosurgery uses ~500 kHz current, too fast to stimulate nerve or muscle.
- **Cut**: continuous low-voltage waveform boils cell water. **Coagulation**: interrupted high-voltage waveform desiccates, spreading deeper. Also blend and spray (fulguration).

### Types

| Device | Principle | Lateral spread | Notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Monopolar** | Tip → patient → return pad | **5–7 mm** | Versatile; stray current |
| **Bipolar** | Current only between jaws | 1–6 mm | No pad or capacitive coupling |
| **Vessel sealer** (LigaSure) | Compression plus impedance-controlled energy | 1–3 mm | Seals vessels **≤7 mm**; single application |
| **Ultrasonic** (Harmonic) | Blade vibrating at 25–55 kHz; no current | **≈1 mm** | Safe with pacemakers; blade >200 °C, cools slowly |
| **Argon beam** | Monopolar current through argon, non-contact | Superficial | Raw surfaces; **gas embolism** |

- **Also know:** lasers (CO2, KTP (potassium titanyl phosphate), holmium), now rarely used; laparoscopic RFA (radiofrequency ablation, target 60 °C) and cryoablation (below −40 °C, two freeze–thaw cycles) for small renal masses.

### Electrosurgical injury
- Unseen injuries present late as perforation.
1. **Insulation failure**.
2. **Direct coupling**: active electrode touches another metal instrument.
3. **Capacitive coupling**: current induced through intact insulation; avoid hybrid trocars (metal cannula, plastic anchor).
4. **Thermal spread** to ureter, nerves or bowel.
5. **Return pad burns**; 6. **surgical fire**.

### Safety
- **AEM (active electrode monitoring)** detects insulation failure and capacitive coupling; **REM (return electrode monitoring)** detects poor pad contact.
- Lowest effective power (cut 50–80 W, coagulation 30–50 W), tip in view, inspect insulation; bipolar near vital structures and in pacemaker patients.

**Diagram to draw:** Monopolar vs bipolar circuit.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Spread: monopolar 5–7 mm > bipolar 1–6 > LigaSure 1–3 > ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
> - LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; Harmonic vibrates at 25–55 kHz.
> - Stray current: insulation failure, direct and capacitive coupling; use AEM and REM.

## Renal isotope scans and DMSA renal scan
**Asked as:**
- Renal isotope scans — *Winter 2017 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- DMSA Renal Scan. — *Summer 2018 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Principle
- A radiotracer follows a physiological process; a **gamma camera** (sodium iodide crystal, photomultipliers, collimator) images the photons. SPECT (single-photon emission computed tomography) gives 3D images.
- **⁹⁹ᵐTc (technetium-99m):** 6-hour half-life, single 140 keV photon, so it is the workhorse.

### Renal tracers
| Tracer | Handling | Study | Main uses |
|---|---|---|---|
| **DTPA** (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid) | Glomerular filtration only | Dynamic | GFR (glomerular filtration rate); captopril renography; poor images in renal impairment |
| **MAG3** (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) | Tubular secretion (protein-bound), high extraction | Dynamic | **Split function and obstruction (first choice)**; transplant |
| **DMSA** (dimercaptosuccinic acid) | Filtered, then retained by proximal tubules | Static at 2 h | **Pyelonephritis and scars** |

### Diuretic renography
- **Preparation:** good hydration; catheter if outlet obstruction or neurogenic bladder; cap a nephrostomy.
- **Dose:** 5–10 mCi (185–370 MBq) intravenously.
- **Phases:** perfusion (first minute); function: **split function** from the area under each curve at 1–3 min (normal 45:55 to 55:45); drainage.
- **Furosemide** 0.5 mg/kg (adult maximum 40 mg). Timing: F+20 (classic), F+15, F0 or F−15. Always note when it was given.

| Post-furosemide T½ (half-clearance time) | Meaning |
|---|---|
| <10 min | Not obstructed |
| 10–20 min | Indeterminate |
| >20 min | Obstructed |

- **Curve types (O'Reilly):** I normal; II obstructed (rising); IIIa dilated, not obstructed (falls after furosemide); IIIb equivocal.
- **Pitfalls:** poor function, dehydration, full bladder, capacious pelvis.

### DMSA renal scan
#### Principle
- **⁹⁹ᵐTc-DMSA** (technetium-99m dimercaptosuccinic acid) is filtered and then taken up by **proximal tubular cells** (megalin and cubilin receptors). It is retained in the cortex and excreted very slowly.
- So it gives a **static map of functioning cortex**, not drainage.

#### Technique
- Intravenous dose: **50 µCi/kg in children, 5 mCi (185 MBq) in adults**.
- Images at **2–4 hours** (later in renal failure): posterior and oblique views, **pinhole collimator** or **SPECT** (single-photon emission computed tomography), which is slightly more sensitive.
- Radiation about 1 mSv.

#### What it measures
- **DRF (differential renal function):** from geometric-mean counts; normal 45–55%. Better than MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) for ectopic, horseshoe or duplex kidneys and very poor function.
- **Cortical defects:** photopenic areas.

#### Indications
- **Acute pyelonephritis** (gold standard), especially in infants with febrile UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **Renal scarring** in VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) and recurrent UTI; top-down approach (DMSA first, VCUG [voiding cystourethrogram] only if abnormal).
- Function of a duplex upper moiety, ectopic or horseshoe kidney; MCDK (multicystic dysplastic kidney: no uptake).
- Reconstruct or remove a poorly functioning kidney.

#### Acute pyelonephritis versus scar
| | Acute pyelonephritis | Scar |
|---|---|---|
| Timing | Within 1 week of fever (sensitivity falls by 1 month) | **≥6 months** after infection (scars persist at about 6 months) |
| Defect | Ill-defined, may be multiple | Sharp, with cortical thinning |
| Kidney size | Normal or swollen | Small or contracted |
| Repeat scan | Resolves (about 85%) (acute pyelonephritis defects resolve) | Persists |

**Goldraich scar grading**
| Type | Finding |
|---|---|
| 1 | No more than 2 scarred areas |
| 2 | More than 2 scars, normal tissue between |
| 3 | Generalised damage, small kidney |
| 4 | End-stage, shrunken kidney, little or no uptake |

#### Limitations
- Cannot tell **congenital reflux dysplasia** (boys, grade IV–V VUR, no UTI) from acquired scar.
- Gross hydronephrosis gives false defects; no information on drainage; poor uptake in renal failure and proximal tubular disorders.
- **Also know:** MAG3 is best for obstruction; NICE (National Institute for Health and Care Excellence): late DMSA 4–6 months after atypical or recurrent UTI.

### Other uses
- **Captopril renography (DTPA):** captopril removes angiotensin II support of GFR in the stenotic kidney. Improvement of ≥10% off ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors means renovascular hypertension is likely.
- **Transplant:** acute rejection (perfusion falls, worsens) versus delayed graft function (perfusion kept, improves); urine leak.
- **DMSA:** acute pyelonephritis defects resolve; scars persist at about 6 months.
- **Nuclear cystography** for follow-up of reflux (more sensitive, lower radiation than VCUG [voiding cystourethrogram]).
- **Also know:** bone scan (MDP, methylene diphosphonate); labelled leucocyte scans; PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography). Radiation dose is low.

**Diagram to draw:** renogram curves: normal, obstructed (rising) and dilated non-obstructed (falls after furosemide).

**Diagram to draw:** posterior DMSA image: normal kidney and a scarred kidney with polar defects.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - DTPA = filtration; MAG3 = secretion (best for obstruction); DMSA = cortex.
> - DMSA binds proximal tubules: a static cortical scan at 2–4 h.
> - Gold standard for acute pyelonephritis and scars.
> - T½ <10 min not obstructed; >20 min obstructed.
> - Normal split (DRF) 45–55%; furosemide 0.5 mg/kg.
> - Scar only if the defect persists ≥6 months.

## Microscopic Hematuria
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3e
(also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3d)

### Definition
- **MH (microscopic haematuria): ≥3 RBCs/HPF (red blood cells per high-power field)** on microscopy of one properly collected sample (AUA/SUFU (American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction) 2020).
- **Dipstick alone is insufficient**: myoglobin, semen and bacterial peroxidases give false positives.
- Prevalence about 6.5%; malignancy in about 3% (over 85% bladder cancer).

### Causes
- Malignant: bladder, UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma), RCC (renal cell carcinoma), prostate.
- Benign: UTI (urinary tract infection), stones, BPE (benign prostatic enlargement), stricture, TB (tuberculosis), schistosomiasis.
- **Medical renal:** IgA nephropathy (dysmorphic RBCs, casts, proteinuria).
- Anticoagulants do not explain haematuria: evaluate as usual.

### Initial evaluation
- History (smoking, occupation, cyclophosphamide, pelvic radiation, Lynch syndrome), examination, **blood pressure, creatinine**.
- Suspected UTI → culture, treat, **repeat urinalysis**; persistent MH → full evaluation.
- Renal features → nephrology **and** urological evaluation.

### AUA/SUFU 2020 risk stratification
| | Low (all) | Intermediate (any) | High (any) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Age | Women <50; men <40 | Women 50–59; men 40–59 | ≥60 |
| Smoking (pack-years) | Never or <10 | 10–30 | >30 |
| RBCs/HPF | 3–10 | 11–25 | >25 |
| Gross haematuria history | No | No | **Yes** |
| Cancer risk | 0–0.4% | 1–2.5% | About 6% |
| **Work-up** | Shared decision: repeat urinalysis in 6 months **or** cystoscopy + RUS (renal ultrasound) | Cystoscopy + RUS | Cystoscopy + **CT (computed tomography) urography** |

- Additional risk factors (irritative symptoms, radiation, cyclophosphamide, occupational exposure) move low to intermediate.
- CT contraindicated → MR (magnetic resonance) urography, or retrograde pyelograms.
- **No cytology or urine markers initially**; white-light cystoscopy.
- Persistent MH after a negative ultrasound → CT or retrograde imaging. Hereditary RCC risk (VHL (von Hippel–Lindau)) → upper tract imaging in any stratum.

### Follow-up
- Negative evaluation → consider repeat urinalysis within 12 months; release if negative.
- Re-evaluate for gross haematuria, rising MH or new symptoms.

**Diagram to draw:** AUA/SUFU flowchart from MH to risk-based work-up.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ≥3 RBCs/HPF on microscopy; dipstick not enough.
> - Intermediate: cystoscopy + ultrasound; high: cystoscopy + CT urography.
> - Any gross haematuria = high risk.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 1 · Basic Principles

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Energy sources in laparoscopic urology (SAQ)

- Spread: monopolar 5–7 mm > bipolar 1–6 > LigaSure 1–3 > ultrasonic ≈1 mm.
- LigaSure seals ≤7 mm; Harmonic vibrates at 25–55 kHz.
- Stray current: insulation failure, direct and capacitive coupling; use AEM and REM.

#### Renal isotope scans and DMSA renal scan (SAQ)

- DTPA = filtration; MAG3 = secretion (best for obstruction); DMSA = cortex.
- DMSA binds proximal tubules: a static cortical scan at 2–4 h.
- Gold standard for acute pyelonephritis and scars.
- T½ <10 min not obstructed; >20 min obstructed.
- Normal split (DRF) 45–55%; furosemide 0.5 mg/kg.
- Scar only if the defect persists ≥6 months.

#### Microscopic Hematuria (SAQ)

- ≥3 RBCs/HPF on microscopy; dipstick not enough.
- Intermediate: cystoscopy + ultrasound; high: cystoscopy + CT urography.
- Any gross haematuria = high risk.

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe use and principles of immunology and Immunotherapy in Urological Oncology
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q2
(immunomodulation in RCC (renal cell carcinoma): see 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3a)

### Introduction
- Immunotherapy works by either **supplying a danger signal** that starts a Th1 response (BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin), vaccine adjuvant) or **removing a brake** on an existing response (PD-1 (programmed death-1), CTLA-4 (cytotoxic T-lymphocyte antigen-4)).

### Principles of tumour immunology
| Arm | Cells and role |
|---|---|
| **Innate** (rapid, pattern-based) | PAMPs (pathogen-associated molecular patterns) bind **TLRs (Toll-like receptors)**; macrophages, neutrophils; urothelium as sentinel (IFN (interferon), IL-8 (interleukin-8)) |
| **Bridge** | **Dendritic cell (DC)**: takes up antigen, matures on danger signal, migrates to lymph node |
| **Adaptive** (specific, memory) | **CD8 killer** (sees class I MHC (major histocompatibility complex), ~9 amino acids); **CD4 helper** (class II); B cells |
| **Brake** | **Treg** (regulatory T cell, FoxP3+ CD4); MDSCs (myeloid-derived suppressor cells); M2 macrophages |
- **Two signals**: TCR (T-cell receptor) meets MHC–peptide (signal 1) plus **CD28–B7** costimulation (signal 2).
- **Th1 vs Th2**: IL-12 gives Th1 (IL-2, IFN-γ, TNF-α (tumour necrosis factor-α)), which is antitumour. IL-4 gives Th2 (antibody, chronic inflammation).
- **Checkpoints**: CTLA-4 (on Tregs) competes for B7; PD-1 on CD8 cells binds **PD-L1** (programmed death ligand-1); LAG-3 binds class II.
- **Adaptive immune resistance**: CD8 cells secrete IFN-γ, which induces PD-L1 on the tumour, which switches the same T cells off. So PD-L1 marks a suppressed ongoing response.

### Immunoediting (three Es)
- **Elimination** (small tumours destroyed) → **Equilibrium** (growth held) → **Escape** (clinical cancer).
- Escape: loss of antigen or MHC, Treg expansion, checkpoint ligands.
- **GU (genitourinary) landscape**: bladder: inflammation-driven (schistosomiasis), CD8 infiltration good. Kidney: **CD8 paradox** (more CD8, worse outcome). Prostate: Treg-dominated, PD-L1 low, so checkpoint inhibitors fail.

### Immunotherapy in practice
**1. BCG in NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer)**
- Bacilli attach via **fibronectin**; TLR2/4 activation → IL-8 and TNF-α → neutrophil wave → DC priming → **Th1 CD4** response → memory.
- Th1 predicts success, Th2 failure. About half stay in remission for years. Used for intermediate/high-risk NMIBC and CIS (carcinoma in situ) with induction plus maintenance.

**2. Cytokines and gene therapy**
- **IFN-α** and **high-dose IL-2** (durable complete responses in a few mRCC (metastatic renal cell carcinoma) patients; capillary leak). Intravesical IFN-α: mixed.
- **Nadofaragene firadenovec** (adenovirus with IFN-α2b gene + Syn3): BCG-unresponsive CIS, CR (complete response) 53.4%; FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2022.
- **N-803** (IL-15 superagonist) with BCG: FDA approved 2024 for BCG-unresponsive CIS.

**3. Cancer vaccines**
- Antigen plus adjuvant (often GM-CSF (granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor)) to activate DCs.
- **Sipuleucel-T**: autologous blood cells pulsed with **PAP (prostatic acid phosphatase)–GM-CSF**, three infusions. Asymptomatic mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer): **OS (overall survival) 25.8 vs 21.7 months**, **no delay in progression**. First FDA-approved solid-tumour vaccine.
- Failed: PROSTVAC (prostate), IMA901 and AGS-003 (RCC).

**4. Checkpoint inhibitors**
| Site | Use (trial) |
|---|---|
| **BCG-unresponsive CIS** | Pembrolizumab if cystectomy declined/unfit (KEYNOTE-057: CR 41%) |
| **Adjuvant MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer) and UTUC** (upper tract urothelial carcinoma) | Nivolumab after radical surgery (**CheckMate 274**: DFS (disease-free survival) 20.8 vs 10.8 months; included UTUC, benefit uncertain in that small subgroup). Atezolizumab (IMvigor010) negative |
| **Metastatic UC (urothelial carcinoma)** | Avelumab **maintenance** after platinum (JAVELIN Bladder 100, OS gain); pembrolizumab second line (**KEYNOTE-045**: OS 10.3 vs 7.4 months); **enfortumab vedotin (EV) + pembrolizumab** |
| **RCC** | Nivolumab + ipilimumab (CheckMate 214); IO (immuno-oncology) + TKI (tyrosine kinase inhibitor) combinations; adjuvant pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-564, DFS HR (hazard ratio) 0.68) |
| **Prostate** | Monotherapy poor; pembrolizumab only for **MSI-H/dMMR** (microsatellite instability-high/mismatch repair-deficient) tumours |
- VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) suppresses DCs and T-cell entry, so TKIs synergise with IO.
- **Biomarkers**: PD-L1 CPS (combined positive score) only for first-line single-agent pembrolizumab in cisplatin-ineligible UC; TMB (tumour mutational burden), MSI.

**5. Adoptive cell therapy (brief)**
- **CAR-T (chimeric antigen receptor T cells)**: T cells engineered against a surface antigen (e.g. PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen)). Investigational in GU; limited by poor trafficking, hostile microenvironment and cytokine release syndrome.

### Toxicity: irAEs (immune-related adverse events)
- Colitis, hepatitis, pneumonitis, thyroiditis, hypophysitis, nephritis, myocarditis, rash.

| Grade | Action |
|---|---|
| 1 | Continue, monitor |
| 2 | Hold; prednisolone 0.5–1 mg/kg |
| 3–4 | Stop; methylprednisolone 1–2 mg/kg; infliximab or mycophenolate if refractory |
- Endocrinopathies need hormone replacement. BCG toxicity: cystitis, BCG sepsis.

### Recent advances
- **EV-302**: enfortumab vedotin + pembrolizumab first line in metastatic UC, OS 31.5 vs 16.1 months versus platinum; now the preferred first line.
- **NIAGARA**: perioperative durvalumab with neoadjuvant cisplatin chemotherapy improved event-free and overall survival in MIBC.
- **Also know:** CheckMate 901 (nivolumab + gemcitabine–cisplatin); ADT (androgen deprivation) + IO trials; anti-CD38 and LAG-3 targets.

**Diagram to draw:** T cell–tumour synapse with TCR–MHC, CD28–B7, CTLA-4 and PD-1/PD-L1 blocked by antibodies.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Three Es: elimination, equilibrium, escape; Th1 is antitumour.
> - BCG: TLR2/4 → neutrophils → Th1 memory.
> - Sipuleucel-T: OS +4.1 months, no delay in progression.
> - CheckMate 274 adjuvant nivolumab; EV-302 EV + pembrolizumab first line.

# Section 2 · Oncology, DSD, Gender & Ageing — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Female Circumcision
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3f

*Assumed to mean female genital mutilation/cutting. Not covered in the reference chapters; answer follows WHO (World Health Organization) definitions.*

### Definition
- **FGM (female genital mutilation):** all procedures that partly or totally remove the external female genitalia, or otherwise injure them, for non-medical reasons (WHO).
- Over 200 million living women are affected, mainly in Africa, the Middle East and Asia; usually in childhood. Practised in some Indian communities ("khatna").

### WHO classification
| Type | Description |
|---|---|
| I (clitoridectomy) | Partial or total removal of the clitoral glans and/or prepuce |
| II (excision) | Removal of the clitoral glans and labia minora, with or without the labia majora |
| III (infibulation) | Narrowing of the vaginal opening by cutting and joining the labia into a seal, with or without clitoral excision |
| IV (other) | Pricking, piercing, incising, scraping, cauterisation |

### Complications
- **Immediate:** pain, haemorrhage, shock, infection (tetanus), **acute urinary retention**, urethral injury, death.
- **Urological:** slow stream, dysuria, **recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection)**, meatal obstruction under the scar, urethral stenosis, incontinence, **vesicovaginal fistula** after obstructed labour.
- **Gynaecological and sexual:** inclusion cysts, keloid, dyspareunia, infertility.
- **Obstetric:** obstructed labour, perineal tears, postpartum haemorrhage, neonatal death.
- **Psychological:** anxiety, depression, post-traumatic stress disorder.

### Evaluation
- Sensitive history; examination to classify type; flow rate, residual urine; cystoscopy if fistula suspected.

### Management
- **Deinfibulation (type III):** anterior midline incision of the scar under local or regional anaesthesia to expose the urethral meatus and introitus; raw edges oversewn.
  - **Timing:** ideally before pregnancy, or antenatally, or in labour. Relieves retention, UTI and dyspareunia.
  - **Never reinfibulate** (illegal in the UK).
- Excise cysts; treat stenosis; repair fistula; counselling. Clitoral reconstruction has limited evidence.

### Legal and ethical aspects
- WHO and the UN (United Nations) condemn FGM as a human rights violation; WHO opposes its performance by health workers.
- Illegal in many countries (e.g. UK FGM Act 2003, with mandatory reporting in girls under 18). India has no specific law.

**Diagram to draw:** vulva showing types I–III and the deinfibulation incision line.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO types: I clitoridectomy, II excision, III infibulation, IV others.
> - Urological: retention, recurrent UTI, stenosis, fistula.
> - Deinfibulation for type III; never reinfibulate.

## Hereditary urological malignancies.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3f

### Principles
- **Knudson two-hit hypothesis:** every cell carries one inherited mutant tumour-suppressor allele, so one more hit causes cancer: **early, bilateral, multifocal** tumours.
- **4–6% of RCC (renal cell carcinoma)** is familial; all familial RCC syndromes are **autosomal dominant**; only hereditary papillary RCC (HPRC) involves an activated oncogene.

### Hereditary kidney cancer syndromes

| Syndrome | Gene (chromosome) | Kidney tumour | Other features |
|---|---|---|---|
| **VHL** (von Hippel–Lindau) | VHL (3p25) | Clear cell, bilateral, multifocal (~50%) | Retinal angioma, CNS (central nervous system) haemangioblastoma, phaeochromocytoma, pancreatic cysts |
| **HPRC** | MET (7q31) oncogene | Multiple bilateral papillary (type 1-like) | None |
| **HLRCC** (hereditary leiomyomatosis and RCC) | FH (fumarate hydratase, 1q42–43) | **Aggressive**, often solitary papillary | Skin and uterine leiomyomas |
| **BHD** (Birt–Hogg–Dubé) | FLCN (folliculin, 17p11.2) | Chromophobe, hybrid oncocytic, oncocytoma | Fibrofolliculomas, lung cysts, pneumothorax |
| **TSC** (tuberous sclerosis complex) | TSC1 (9q34), TSC2 (16p13) | Multiple AMLs (angiomyolipomas); RCC 1–3% | Angiofibromas, cortical tubers |

- **VHL type 1** (deletions, truncations): no phaeochromocytoma; **type 2** (missense): phaeochromocytoma.
- **Also know:** SDH (succinate dehydrogenase)-deficient RCC (paraganglioma); BAP1 (uveal melanoma, mesothelioma); Cowden (PTEN; breast, thyroid).

### Management principles for kidney
- **3 cm rule:** watch tumours <3 cm; nephron-sparing surgery at ≥3 cm (VHL, HPRC, BHD) to save function for repeat operations. **Exception: HLRCC**: excise promptly, wide margins.
- TSC AML: everolimus; embolisation or partial nephrectomy if ≥5 cm. **Belzutifan** (HIF-2α [hypoxia-inducible factor 2α] inhibitor, FDA [Food and Drug Administration]-approved 2021) for VHL.

### Other urological cancers
- **Prostate:** **BRCA2**, BRCA1, ATM, CHEK2, PALB2, HOXB13, mismatch repair genes; father plus brother: 9-fold risk. Earlier screening, cautious active surveillance, **PARP (poly ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors**.
- **UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma): Lynch syndrome** (MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2): 14-fold risk, about 75-fold with MSH2. Suspect if age <60 or Lynch cancer history; MMR (mismatch repair) immunohistochemistry, then germline testing.
- **Testis:** affected brother RR (relative risk) 8–12, father 2–4.
- **Also know:** Wilms tumour (WT1 gene: WAGR [Wilms, aniridia, genitourinary anomalies, retardation] and Denys–Drash; Beckwith–Wiedemann).
- **Approach:** genetic counselling, germline testing, screening relatives.

**Diagram to draw:** VHL loss → HIF accumulation → VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) → angiogenesis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - All familial RCC is autosomal dominant; 3 cm rule except HLRCC.
> - VHL 3p25, MET 7q31, FH 1q42, FLCN 17p11.
> - Lynch (MSH2) → UTUC; BRCA2 → aggressive prostate cancer.

## Penile inversion vaginoplasty technique.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3b

### Introduction
- Commonest gender-affirming genital operation for transgender women: a functional, aesthetic, sensate vulva and vagina.

### Preparation
- **WPATH (World Professional Association for Transgender Health) SOC8 (Standards of Care, version 8):** one assessment, **6 months of hormones** before gonadectomy.
- **Permanent hair removal** of the scrotal graft area (prevents intravaginal hair).
- BMI (body mass index) usually ≤35 kg/m²; stop smoking; sperm banking; continue oestrogen.

### Principle: homologous tissues
| Male structure | Becomes |
|---|---|
| Glans on dorsal neurovascular bundle | **Clitoris** |
| Preputial mucosal collar | Clitoral hood, inner labia minora |
| Dorsal bulbar urethral plate | Vestibule |
| Penile shaft skin (inverted) | **Neovaginal lining** |
| Scrotal skin (full-thickness graft) | Extra canal lining |
| Lateral scrotal skin + cord fat | Labia majora |

### Steps
```
Lithotomy; tranexamic acid 1 g; catheter
→ harvest, defat and tubularise scrotal skin graft
→ bilateral orchidectomy (leave cord fat for labial bulk)
→ deglove penis between dartos and Buck's fascia
   (keep dartos with skin = blood supply)
→ separate dorsal neurovascular bundle + glans;
   excise ALL corporal erectile tissue
→ clitoroplasty; fix at level of adductor longus tendons
→ shorten and spatulate urethra; excise bulbospongiosus
→ canal dissection: central tendon, behind prostate,
   preserve Denonvilliers fascia, repeated rectal checks
→ invert penile skin + graft into canal; labiaplasty
→ pack canal; negative-pressure dressing
```
- **Why excise all erectile tissue:** residual tissue engorges and obstructs the canal.
- Depth depends on canal dissection, not penile length. **Robotic peritoneal flaps** add depth with less rectal injury; **intestinal vaginoplasty** if skin is inadequate or for revision.

### Postoperative care
- Pack and catheter out on day 5.
- **Lifelong dilation**, e.g. 4 times daily for 6 weeks, tapering to daily after 1 year; pelvic floor physiotherapy.

### Complications
- **Wound dehiscence** (commonest, 12–33%); granulation tissue.
- **Rectovaginal fistula** (0.8% primary, 6.3% revision); urethrovaginal fistula; meatal stenosis, spraying.
- **Vaginal stenosis** (poor dilation); prolapse 4–10%.
- Most achieve orgasm (median about 6 months).

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal view of the lined canal between prostate and rectum.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Glans → clitoris; penile skin → canal; scrotal skin → graft.
> - Excise all erectile tissue; keep Denonvilliers fascia intact.
> - Lifelong dilation prevents stenosis; rectovaginal fistula is the worst complication.

## 46 XX Male
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3c
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3e)

*Assumed meaning: 46,XX testicular DSD (difference of sex development), "XX male" or de la Chapelle syndrome.*

### Definition
- **Testes and male genitalia in a 46,XX individual.** Old term "XX sex reversal"; renamed **46,XX testicular DSD** (Chicago consensus 2006). About **1 in 20,000** males.
- In the DSD classification it is a 46,XX DSD of gonadal development, with ovotesticular DSD; **CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia)** is the androgen-excess type.

### Genetics
| Type | Mechanism | Phenotype |
|---|---|---|
| **SRY-positive** (about 80–90%) | **SRY** (sex-determining region Y) crosses from Yp to X during paternal meiosis | Usually normal male genitalia |
| SRY-negative | Testis genes switched on without SRY: **SOX9** duplication, SOX3, loss of RSPO1 or WNT4 | More hypospadias, undescended testes, ovotestis |

- SRY switches on SOX9 → Sertoli cells → **AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone)**: no uterus. Leydig testosterone virilises.
- The **AZF (azoospermia factor) region of Yq is absent**, so spermatogenesis always fails.

### Clinical features
- Male identity; found at **infertility work-up**, or prenatally (cell-free DNA 46,XX, male ultrasound).
- Small firm testes, **azoospermia**, hypergonadotropic hypogonadism, gynaecomastia; progressive testicular regression.

| Feature | 46,XX male | Klinefelter (47,XXY) |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency | 1 in 20,000 | 1 in 500–1000 |
| Height | Normal or short | Often tall |
| Genital anomalies | More common | Rare |
| Sperm retrieval | **Never (no AZF)** | Possible by micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction) |

### Evaluation
- Semen analysis; FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) and LH (luteinising hormone) high, testosterone low or low-normal.
- **Karyotype** 46,XX; **FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation) for SRY**; gene panel if SRY-negative; scrotal ultrasound.
- In an infant with atypical genitalia, exclude CAH (17-hydroxyprogesterone) and ovotesticular DSD.

### Management
- **Testosterone replacement** when hypogonadal (libido, bone, muscle).
- **Infertility:** sperm retrieval is futile; counsel **donor sperm or adoption**.
- Repair hypospadias or undescended testis; reduction mammoplasty for persistent gynaecomastia.
- Genetic counselling, psychological support, bone density follow-up.

**Diagram to draw:** X and Y chromosomes showing Xp–Yp exchange carrying SRY onto X.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 46,XX testicular DSD: SRY on X in about 80–90%; 1 in 20,000.
> - Azoospermia always (AZF absent): no sperm retrieval.
> - Shorter than Klinefelter; FISH for SRY confirms.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Describe pathophysiology of genitourinary tuberculosis and management of thimble bladder in 30-year male.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q1

### Introduction
- GU (genitourinary) TB (tuberculosis) is caused by the *Mycobacterium tuberculosis* complex (MTBC). It is the second commonest extrapulmonary site after lymph nodes. Untreated, it destroys the kidney and contracts the bladder.

### Pathophysiology
- **Primary infection:** inhaled bacilli spread by lymph and blood, before immunity develops, and seed the kidney and epididymis.
- **Granuloma:** macrophages, epithelioid and giant cells inside a lymphocyte cuff, kept alive by IFN-γ (interferon-γ). In 90–95% it contains the infection; viable bacilli stay dormant. Reactivation follows old age, diabetes, renal failure, HIV (human immunodeficiency virus) or steroids, sometimes decades later. Then **caseation** releases bacilli into urine.

| Route | Lesion |
|---|---|
| **Haematogenous** (principal) | Parenchymal disease: kidney, epididymis, prostate |
| **Descending urinary** | Luminal disease: ureteric stricture, cystitis, urethra |
| **Ascending** (intravesical BCG [bacille Calmette–Guérin]) | Cystitis, prostatitis, epididymo-orchitis |

| Site | Frequency | Pathology |
|---|---|---|
| Kidney | 80% | Periglomerular granulomas, then caseating cavities, papillary necrosis, calcification; **autonephrectomy** ("putty kidney") |
| Ureter | — | Stricture, commonest at the **VUJ** (vesicoureteric junction); "beaded corkscrew" or "pipestem" ureter |
| Bladder | — | Starts at the ureteric orifices ("golf-hole" orifice); ulcers; after about a year of fibrosis it contracts; **thimble bladder <20 mL** |
| Epididymis | Commonest genital site | Starts in globus minor; beaded vas, posterior scrotal sinus |
| Prostate, seminal vesicles | 22–49% | Calcification, abscess; infertility |

- **Key concept:** drugs cure by fibrosis, and fibrosis obstructs. Strictures and bladder contraction can **worsen during treatment**.

### Diagnosis (brief)
- **Sterile pyuria** with or without haematuria (>90%).
- **Urine AFB (acid-fast bacilli) culture** (gold standard): 3–5 first-void morning samples on consecutive days. LJ (Löwenstein–Jensen) solid medium takes 4–6 weeks; use a liquid system as well. Culture gives drug susceptibility.
- **GeneXpert MTB/RIF:** rapid DNA test that also detects rifampicin resistance (marker of MDR [multidrug-resistant] TB).
- **Imaging:** chest radiograph in all; CT (computed tomography) urography: calcification, phantom calyx, Kerr's kink, ureteric strictures, small bladder. Biopsy if culture is negative.

### Medical treatment (first step in all)
- **2HRZE/4HR:** isoniazid, rifampicin, pyrazinamide, ethambutol for 2 months, then isoniazid and rifampicin for 4 months, with pyridoxine. Some extend to 9–12 months in complicated disease.
- Repeat ultrasound during treatment for worsening obstruction; stent or PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy) early. Steroids: anecdotal only.
- **Surgery after at least 4–6 weeks of ATT (antituberculous therapy)**, when inflammation and bacillary load have settled.

### Assessment of a thimble bladder (30-year-old man)
| Assess | How | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Disease activity | Repeat urine AFB culture; bladder biopsy | Do not reconstruct with active TB |
| Capacity and compliance | Cystogram, cystoscopy under anaesthesia, urodynamics | Size and type of reconstruction |
| Upper tracts | Ultrasound, CT urography, MCU (micturating cystourethrogram) | VUJ stricture or reflux needs reimplantation |
| Renal function | Creatinine, GFR (glomerular filtration rate), split function | Bowel reservoir needs creatinine <2 mg/dL, GFR >35–40 mL/min |
| Urethra, bladder neck, sphincter | Urethrogram, endoscopy | Orthotopic reservoir needs a patent, continent outlet |
| Patient | Dexterity, motivation, fertility wishes | May need CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) |

### Surgical options
| Bladder state | Operation |
|---|---|
| Half the bladder diseased, capacity <100 mL | **Augmentation ileocystoplasty** ("clam" or onlay of detubularised ileum) |
| Severely contracted | Augmentation with **ileocaecum** or sigmoid |
| **Thimble bladder (<20 mL)** | **Substitution cystoplasty:** supratrigonal excision of the diseased bladder, keeping trigone and bladder neck, and an orthotopic detubularised ileal or ileocaecal reservoir |

- **Principles:** detubularise and fold the bowel into a spherical, low-pressure reservoir (Laplace's law; interrupts peristalsis). Anastomose widely to the trigone.
- **Ureters:** if the VUJ is strictured or refluxing, excise to healthy ureter and **reimplant into the reservoir**: an antireflux tunnel under a colonic taenia, or an isoperistaltic afferent ileal limb. A Boari flap or psoas hitch is not possible in a contracted bladder.
- **Avoid orthotopic reconstruction** with: poor renal function; strictured or incompetent urethra; inability to perform CIC; active TB; short bowel or inflammatory bowel disease. Then use a continent catheterisable pouch (Mitrofanoff) or an **ileal conduit**.
- **Also know:** laparoscopic and robotic augmentation; transurethral incision of bladder neck contracture.

### Complications
- **Mucus** and catheter blockage; **stones**; bacteriuria and UTI (urinary tract infection); **hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis**; vitamin B12 deficiency if ileum is lost; **perforation** (needs regular emptying); incomplete emptying needing CIC; nocturnal incontinence; late tumour in the reservoir.

### Follow-up
- Urine AFB culture, creatinine, bicarbonate, B12 and ultrasound. GU TB relapses in 6–22% (mean about 5 years), so follow up for up to 10 years.

**Diagram to draw:** supratrigonal excision of a thimble bladder with an ileocaecal substitute and ureteric reimplantation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Kidney 80%; bladder disease descends from the kidney; thimble bladder <20 mL.
> - Urine AFB culture: 3–5 early-morning samples; GeneXpert detects rifampicin resistance.
> - 2HRZE/4HR; operate after 4–6 weeks of ATT.
> - Augment if capacity <100 mL; substitute if <20 mL; needs creatinine <2 mg/dL and a good urethra.

# Section 3 · Infections & Inflammation — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Interstitial cystitis (Bladder pain syndrome).
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3b

### Definitions
- **AUA (American Urological Association):** an unpleasant sensation (pain, pressure, discomfort) perceived to be related to the bladder, with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) for **more than 6 weeks**, without infection or another identifiable cause.
- **ESSIC (European Society for the Study of Interstitial Cystitis, 2008):** chronic (>6 months) bladder-related pain with at least one other urinary symptom (persistent urge or frequency), after confusable diseases are excluded.
- NIDDK (National Institute of Diabetes and Digestive and Kidney Diseases, 1987) research criteria missed about 60% of patients.

### Phenotypes
- **Hunner lesion (HL) disease, "IC":** about 20%, mainly over 50; true inflammation (lymphoplasmacytic infiltrate, mast cells).
- **Non-Hunner BPS (bladder pain syndrome):** bladder normal or shows glomerulations.
- **ESSIC type:** cystoscopy 1 normal, 2 glomerulations, 3 Hunner lesion; biopsy A normal, B inconclusive, C positive (e.g. 3C).

### Pathogenesis (theories)
- Leaky GAG (glycosaminoglycan) layer; mast-cell activation; neurogenic inflammation and central sensitisation; stress-axis dysfunction; small-fibre neuropathy. No infection proven. 75–80% have pain beyond the pelvis (IBS [irritable bowel syndrome], fibromyalgia).

### Evaluation (diagnosis of exclusion)
- History (**ask about ketamine**), pelvic-floor palpation, PVR (post-void residual), urinalysis, culture, voiding chart, symptom scores; cytology if risk factors.
- **Office cystoscopy if over 50 or HL suspected;** biopsy to exclude CIS (carcinoma in situ).
- **Local anaesthetic challenge** localises pain to the bladder. Potassium sensitivity test is not recommended.
- **Also know:** urodynamics optional; hydrodistension glomerulations are non-specific.

### Treatment (AUA 2022: phenotype-based, no fixed tiers)
| Group | Options |
|---|---|
| Behavioural | Education, dietary triggers, stress; **pelvic-floor myofascial therapy** if tender |
| Oral | **Amitriptyline** 10 mg rising to about 50 mg; hydroxyzine; cimetidine; **PPS** (pentosan polysulfate) 100 mg three times daily (**pigmentary maculopathy**: eye checks) |
| Intravesical | **DMSO** (dimethyl sulfoxide) 50%, 50 mL, 15–20 min dwell; heparin; alkalinised lidocaine |
| Procedures | **HL: fulguration and/or triamcinolone injection** (75–90% improve); short low-pressure hydrodistension; onabotulinumtoxinA; sacral neuromodulation |
| Last resort | Ciclosporin A for refractory HL; diversion ± cystectomy (<10%) |

- **Do not offer:** long-term antibiotics, intravesical BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin), long-term systemic steroids.

**Diagram to draw:** stellate Hunner lesion with radiating vessels.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AUA definition: bladder pain with LUTS >6 weeks, no other cause.
> - Hunner lesion: cystoscopy over 50; fulguration or triamcinolone.
> - Amitriptyline, PPS, DMSO; potassium test obsolete.

## Malakoplakia
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 2, Q3e

### Definition
- "Soft plaque": a rare chronic granulomatous inflammation caused by **defective macrophage killing of bacteria**, mostly *E. coli*. Described by Michaelis and Gutmann (1902) and von Hansemann (1903).
- Commonest in the **bladder**; also ureter, kidney, prostate, testis, GI (gastrointestinal) tract, skin, lung.

### Pathogenesis
- Macrophages ingest bacteria but cannot digest them (lysosomal defect, low intracellular cGMP [cyclic guanosine monophosphate]).
- Bacterial remnants become mineralised with calcium phosphate, forming laminated **Michaelis–Gutmann bodies**.
- Hosts are often immunocompromised, diabetic, chronically ill or **renal transplant recipients**.

### Pathology
- Gross: soft yellow-brown plaques or nodules; renal tumour-like masses.
- Micro: sheets of large foamy histiocytes (**von Hansemann cells**) with **Michaelis–Gutmann bodies** (pathognomonic; "owl's eye" targetoid; stain with von Kossa, PAS [periodic acid–Schiff] and Prussian blue).
- MG bodies may be absent early; **α1-antitrypsin** immunostaining helps.

### Clinical features
- Mostly >50 years; urinary tract **female:male 4:1**.
- Bladder: irritative LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), haematuria, recurrent *E. coli* UTI (urinary tract infection); plaques or masses mimicking cancer; distal ureteric stricture.
- Kidney: masses, fever, flank pain; renal vein or IVC thrombosis. Testis: epididymo-orchitis. Prostate: mimics carcinoma.

### Investigations
- Urine culture (*E. coli*). Cystoscopy and **biopsy is diagnostic**.
- Imaging: enlarged kidneys with filling defects, **no stones, calcification or hydronephrosis** (unlike XGP).

| | XGP (xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis) | Malakoplakia |
|---|---|---|
| Cell | Lipid-laden foamy macrophages | Von Hansemann cells with MG bodies |
| Stone/obstruction | Usually (staghorn) | Absent |
| Laterality | Unilateral | Uni- or multifocal, bilateral |
| Treatment | Nephrectomy | Antibiotics; nephrectomy if unilateral, symptomatic |

### Management
- **Long-term antibiotics active inside macrophages:** fluoroquinolones (concentrate in macrophages), trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole, rifampicin, doxycycline.
- **Bethanechol + ascorbic acid** raise intracellular cGMP and correct the degradative defect.
- Reduce immunosuppression where possible.
- Surgery if progression despite medical therapy: TUR (transurethral resection) of bladder lesions; **nephrectomy** for symptomatic unilateral renal disease.

### Prognosis
- Bilateral or transplant-kidney disease: death often within 6 months; overall mortality can exceed 50%. Unilateral: long survival after nephrectomy.

**Diagram to draw:** histiocyte with Michaelis–Gutmann bodies.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Defective macrophage digestion of *E. coli*.
> - Michaelis–Gutmann bodies are pathognomonic.
> - Fluoroquinolones + bethanechol/vitamin C; nephrectomy for unilateral renal disease.

- Augment if capacity <100 mL; substitute if <20 mL; needs creatinine <2 mg/dL and a good urethra.

#### Interstitial cystitis (Bladder pain syndrome). (SAQ)

- Hunner lesion: cystoscopy over 50; fulguration or triamcinolone.

#### Malakoplakia (SAQ)

- Defective macrophage digestion of *E. coli*.
- Michaelis–Gutmann bodies are pathognomonic.
- Fluoroquinolones + bethanechol/vitamin C; nephrectomy for unilateral renal disease.

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Wilms tumour: classification, staging and management
**Asked as:**
- Wilm's tumor — *Practice Set 10 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*
- Describe classification and management of Wilm's bladder — *Practice Set 15 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 15 Paper II Q1: bilateral Wilms tumour also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3e and 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3c; Wilms tumour in Practice Set 10 Paper 2, Q3b)

*Read as: "Wilms tumour" (nephroblastoma); "bladder" is taken as a typo.*

### Introduction
- WT (Wilms tumour) is an embryonal tumour of metanephric blastema. It forms 95% of kidney cancers under 15 years; median age 3.5 years; over 80% present before 5 years.
- **Genetics:** mostly sporadic; familial 1–2%. Genes: **WT1 (11p13)**, IGF2/11p15 (commonest change, about 70%), WTX, CTNNB1; **TP53 in anaplasia (in most anaplastic tumours)**.

**Syndromes:**

| Syndrome | WT risk |
|---|---|
| **WAGR** (Wilms, aniridia, genitourinary anomalies, intellectual disability; WT1 deletion) | 50% |
| **Denys–Drash** (46,XY DSD [disorder of sex development], mesangial sclerosis; WT1 point mutation) | 50% |
| **BWS (Beckwith–Wiedemann syndrome)**, hemihypertrophy (11p15) | 5–10% |

- **Screening:** screen by ultrasound every 3–4 months (3–4-monthly) until (to age) 7 years when risk is >1%.
- **Nephrogenic rests** (precursors) occur in over one-third of WT kidneys and over 90% of bilateral cases.

### Classification

**A. Histological (pathology)**

| Group | Features | Significance |
|---|---|---|
| **Favourable histology** | **Triphasic:** blastema, epithelium (tubules), stroma; pseudocapsule | Survival >90% |
| **Anaplasia** (unfavourable) | Nuclei ≥3× neighbours (larger), hyperchromasia, abnormal mitoses; **focal** or **diffuse** (diffuse worse than focal) | Chemoresistant; poor even at stage I |

- **SIOP (International Society of Paediatric Oncology) post-chemotherapy risk groups:**

| Risk | Histology |
|---|---|
| Low | Completely necrotic |
| Intermediate | All others (regressive, mixed, epithelial, stromal, focal anaplasia) |
| High | **Blastemal-predominant**; diffuse anaplasia |

- Residual blastema predicts relapse; stromal and epithelial tumours shrink little but are cured by excision.

**B. Staging: COG (Children's Oncology Group), surgical-pathological, before chemotherapy**

| Stage | Description |
|---|---|
| I | Confined to kidney, completely resected, capsule intact, no renal sinus vessel invasion |
| II | Beyond kidney (renal sinus vessels, perirenal fat, vein thrombus) but completely (fully) resected |
| III | Residual abdominal disease: **positive nodes, any spill, prior biopsy**, positive margin, peritoneal implants |
| IV | Haematogenous metastases (lung commonest, liver, bone, brain) |
| V | Bilateral at diagnosis (each side staged separately) |

- **SIOP staging** uses the same five stages but is applied **after preoperative chemotherapy**. Necrotic, chemotherapy-induced change in the sinus or perirenal fat does not upstage.
- **Biology:** combined **1p and 16q LOH (loss of heterozygosity)** predicts relapse; 1q gain is the next stratifier.

### Clinical features and evaluation
- Abdominal mass; haematuria about (~) 20%; hypertension about (~) 25% (renin). Look for aniridia, hemihypertrophy, varicocele (IVC thrombus). Varicocele or hepatomegaly suggests IVC (inferior vena cava) thrombus. Acquired von Willebrand disease in up to 8%.
- Investigations: ultrasound with Doppler (IVC) → contrast CT (computed tomography) or MRI abdomen → **chest CT** (lung is the commonest metastatic site; before anaesthesia). Blood count, renal and liver function, coagulation.

### Management: two philosophies

| | COG (North America) | SIOP (Europe) |
|---|---|---|
| Sequence | **Upfront (transperitoneal) radical nephrectomy**, then chemotherapy | **Preoperative VCR + AMD** (vincristine + dactinomycin; 4 weeks; add DOX for 6 weeks if metastatic), no biopsy, then nephrectomy (surgery) |
| Advantage | Untreated histology and biology for risk assignment | Tumour shrinks; spill almost zero |
| Risk basis | Stage + histology + LOH | Post-chemotherapy histology + stage |

(VCR = vincristine; AMD = actinomycin D; DOX = doxorubicin.)

**Surgical principles (radical nephrectomy)**
- Transperitoneal approach; palpate renal vein and IVC before ligation; spare a tumour-free adrenal.
- **Avoid spill** (it makes the tumour stage III).
- **Lymph node sampling of at least 5 nodes** (**sample ≥5 lymph nodes**: hilar, paracaval, para-aortic, interaortocaval) is essential; formal dissection is not. Omitted sampling is the commonest protocol violation.
- IVC thrombus at or above the hepatic veins → preoperative chemotherapy to avoid bypass.

**Postoperative therapy (COG, favourable histology)**

| Group | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Very low risk (<2 years, <550 g, stage I) | Nephrectomy alone (AREN0532) |
| Stage I–II | VCR + AMD (vincristine + dactinomycin) |
| Stage III | VCR + AMD + DOX (add doxorubicin) + flank RT (radiotherapy) |
| Stage IV | Three drugs + whole-lung RT; lung RT omitted if (lung) nodules clear by week 6 (AREN0533) |
| Anaplasia or 1p/16q LOH (loss of heterozygosity) | Intensified regimens (regimen M adds cyclophosphamide + etoposide) |

### Bilateral WT (stage V) and nephron sparing
- Bilateral WT occurs in 5–7%. **No upfront nephrectomy or biopsy.** The aim is cure while preserving nephrons.
- Three-drug chemotherapy, then **NSS (nephron-sparing surgery) by week 12**:
```
VCR + AMD + DOX × 6 weeks → reimaging
  ├─ resectable → bilateral NSS (nephron-sparing surgery)
  └─ not → 6 more weeks → surgery by week 12
       poor response → bilateral open biopsy
```
- **Why by week 12:** in NWTS-4 (National Wilms Tumor Study-4) prolonged chemotherapy delayed surgery and some tumours became anaplastic.
- **AREN0534:** 4-year EFS (event-free survival) 82.1% and OS (overall survival) 94.9%.
- Bilateral nephrectomy and dialysis are rare; transplant after 1–2 years.
- **Unilateral NSS:** for syndromic children and solitary kidney; SIOP UMBRELLA criteria are volume <300 mL, a substantial remnant and negative nodes.

### Prognosis, relapse and late effects
- Favourable histology: survival >90%.
- Salvage survival: 70–80% after VCR/AMD only; about 10% for anaplastic or blastemal relapse.
- **Late effects:** cardiotoxicity (DOX), second cancers (RT), infertility, renal failure (bilateral).

**Diagram to draw:** COG staging diagram showing capsule, renal sinus, nodes and spill.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WT1 at 11p13 (WAGR, Denys-Drash); BWS at 11p15.
> - Anaplasia (≥3× nuclei, hyperchromasia, abnormal mitoses) is unfavourable; TP53.
> - Any spill, biopsy or positive node = stage III; sample ≥5 nodes.
> - COG operates first; SIOP gives chemotherapy (VCR + AMD) first.
> - Bilateral: three drugs, surgery by week 12, NSS.

# Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Retractile testis?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3b

### Definition
- A **descended testis** that moves up out of the scrotum because of an active **cremasteric reflex**. It can be brought to the bottom of the scrotum by hand and **stays there**, at least for a while, after release.
- It is not undescended, but may be a mild form of the same disease and can later become an acquired undescended testis.

### Epidemiology
- Common: suprascrotal in up to one-third of 4-year-olds; 11–15% of boys up to 11 years. Nearly all are scrotal after 12 years.

### Examination (diagnosis is clinical)
- Warm room and hands, relaxed child, soap or gel; supine frog-leg (thigh abduction inhibits the cremasteric reflex), cross-legged or squatting.
- One hand sweeps from the internal ring down the canal; the other pulls the testis to the scrotal bottom. **Sustained gentle traction tires the cremaster.**
- Note size, mobility, contralateral testis and hernia.

| Testis | Finding | Action |
|---|---|---|
| **Retractile** | Reaches scrotal bottom and stays | Observe yearly |
| Gliding (unstable high scrotal) | Pulled down but springs back | Treat as undescended |
| Undescended (palpable) | Cannot reach mid-scrotum | Orchidopexy by 18 months |
| Ascending (acquired) | Previously scrotal, now not | Orchidopexy |

### Natural history
- Ascent: 2.6% in a prospective birth cohort; 7–32% in specialist series.
- Operated retractile testes show small volume, patent processus vaginalis and epididymal anomalies, suggesting a continuum with cryptorchidism.

### Management
- No ultrasound (adds nothing); no hormones (hCG [human chorionic gonadotrophin] neither separates nor treats).
- Reassure parents; **examine yearly** (AUA [American Urological Association] 2014) until puberty, because some testes ascend.
- **Orchidopexy** if it ascends, cannot be brought down, rests mostly high or becomes smaller. If unsure, examine under anaesthesia.

**Diagram to draw:** inguinoscrotal sketch: testis drawn by traction to the scrotal bottom.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Retractile = comes down and stays down.
> - Gliding testis springs back: treat as undescended.
> - Yearly examination until puberty; operate if it ascends.

## Retroileal Ureter
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3b

*"Retroileal" is taken as **retroiliac ureter**, the nearest real entity; retrocaval ureter is compared below (full answer: 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3d).*

### Definition
- The ureter passes **behind the common iliac artery** and is compressed there.
- Better called "**preureteral iliac artery**": the anomaly is presumed vascular; the ureter is trapped.

### Embryology
- Normally the **ventral root of the umbilical artery** is replaced by a more **dorsal** branch, which lies behind the ureter.
- If the **ventral root persists** and the dorsal root fails, the ureter is trapped behind the artery.

### Features
- **Very rare**; either side; **bilateral** cases reported.
- Obstruction at **L5–S1**.
- **Associated anomalies are common**, especially **vasal and mesonephric duct ectopia**: ureter and vas draining together into the posterior urethra; vasa ending in the ureters; ectopic vaginal ureter.

### Presentation and diagnosis
- Flank pain, UTI (urinary tract infection) or hydroureteronephrosis down to the iliac crossing.
- **CT urography (computed tomography urography) with arterial phase** or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) shows ureter and artery together; **diuretic renogram** for function and drainage.
- Caution: a vessel indenting an already dilated ureter may not be the cause.

### Management
- Non-obstructed: observe.
- Obstructed: **divide the ureter, re-route it in front of the artery** and do a spatulated ureteroureterostomy (or reimplantation) over a double-J stent; open, laparoscopic or robotic.

### Comparison with retrocaval ureter
| | Retroiliac | Retrocaval (circumcaval) |
|---|---|---|
| Vessel | Common iliac artery | IVC (inferior vena cava) |
| Embryology | Persistent ventral umbilical artery root | Persistent subcardinal vein |
| Side | Either, may be bilateral | Right |
| Level | L5–S1 | L3–L4, "fishhook" |
| Associations | Vasal/mesonephric ectopia | Horseshoe kidney, left renal anomalies |
- **Also know:** distal ureter obstruction by uterine, umbilical, obturator or hypogastric vessels.

**Diagram to draw:** ureter behind the common iliac artery at L5–S1, dilated above.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Retroiliac = preureteral iliac artery; persistent ventral umbilical artery root.
> - Obstruction at L5–S1; either side or bilateral.
> - Look for vasal and ureteric ectopia.
> - Retrocaval: right, L3–L4, fishhook, 1 in 1500.

## Medullary sponge kidney
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 9 Paper 2, Q3e

### Definition
- **MSK (medullary sponge kidney; precalyceal canalicular ectasia):** dilated (ectatic) distal **collecting ducts** with small cysts and diverticula **confined to the medullary pyramids**. Nephrons are initially normal.
- Usually **non-heritable** (sporadic); usually bilateral.

### Pathogenesis of stones and nephrocalcinosis
- **Stasis and infection** in the ectatic ducts favour crystal deposition.
- **Tubular defects:**
  - **Hypercalciuria** in one-third to one-half (mostly absorptive; sometimes renal leak).
  - **Incomplete distal RTA (renal tubular acidosis)** in 30–40%, shown on acid loading. This causes **hypocitraturia** and alkaline urine.
  - Impaired urinary concentration.
  - Mutations in H⁺-ATPase genes (ATP6V1B1, ATP6V0A4) support an MSK–distal RTA link.
- Result: calcium stones within dilated ducts and medullary **nephrocalcinosis**.

### Clinical features
- Often silent for life; presents **after 20 years**.
- **Renal colic 50–60%**, UTI (urinary tract infection) 20–33%, gross haematuria 10–18%.
- Found in **2.6–21% of stone formers**, more often women.

### Diagnosis
- **Urographic triad:**
  1. Enlarged kidneys, with or without papillary calcification.
  2. Elongated papillary tubules or cavities filling with contrast: **"bristles on a brush"** (paintbrush); when calcified, a **"bouquet of flowers"**.
  3. Papillary blush and persistent medullary opacification.
- CT urography now replaces IVU (intravenous urography); plain CT shows medullary nephrocalcinosis.
- **Metabolic work-up:** 24-hour urine (calcium, citrate, pH), serum bicarbonate and potassium.
- **Differentials:** papillary necrosis, other medullary nephrocalcinosis (distal RTA, hyperparathyroidism), calyceal diverticulum.

### Management
| Agent | Target | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Potassium citrate** | Hypocitraturia, hypercalciuria | **First choice**; reduces calciuria and recurrence |
| Thiazide | Hypercalciuria | Useful even without hypercalciuria |
| Inorganic phosphate | Hypercalciuria | If thiazide unsuitable; avoid with urease infection |
| Fluids, low-sodium diet | Volume, calciuria | All patients |
- Treat infection; stones by SWL (shock-wave lithotripsy) or ureteroscopy; stones inside ducts may be unreachable.
- **Prognosis:** usually benign; renal failure is rare.

**Diagram to draw:** pyramid with ectatic ducts filled with contrast ("brush") and small calculi.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ectatic collecting ducts; sporadic; "bristles on a brush".
> - Hypercalciuria, incomplete distal RTA, hypocitraturia.
> - Potassium citrate is first-line prevention.

## Megameatus intact prepuce
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 15 Paper 2, Q3f

### Definition
- **MIP (megameatus intact prepuce)** is a hypospadias variant (up to 5%) with a **wide, "fish-mouth" glanular or coronal meatus**, a **completely formed prepuce** and **no significant curvature**. Described by Duckett and Keating (1989).

### Embryology
- Normally the prepuce closes ventrally only after the urethral folds fuse (about 20 weeks), so 95% of hypospadias have a hooded, incomplete foreskin.
- In MIP the foreskin is complete, showing that **preputial and urethral development can dissociate**.

### Clinical features
- Hidden under a normal foreskin; often found on **retraction before circumcision** or during circumcision.
- Wide, deep glans groove with a large, patulous meatus; well-developed spongiosum.
- Symptoms: spraying or deflected stream; cosmetic concern.
- **Advice:** retract the foreskin before every newborn circumcision. If found, the circumcision may be completed; prior circumcision does not harm later repair.

### Evaluation
- Examine meatal site and size, glans and plate, curvature (artificial erection at surgery). GMS (glans–meatus–shaft) score.
- No routine upper-tract imaging for isolated distal hypospadias.

### Management
- **Indication:** cosmesis and a straight stream; repair is optional. Usual timing 6–18 months.
- **Pyramid procedure (Duckett and Keating):**
  1. Glans traction suture; retract prepuce.
  2. Incise around the megameatus in a **pyramid shape**, apex on the ventral shaft.
  3. Dissect the wide distal urethra free of the glans wings.
  4. **Excise a ventral wedge** of redundant urethra to taper it.
  5. Tubularise over a catheter; cover with dartos.
  6. Approximate glans wings in two layers (glansplasty); preserve or reconstruct the prepuce.
- **Alternatives:** GAP (glans approximation procedure; pyramid = GAP with intact prepuce), TIP (tubularised incised plate) urethroplasty, Thiersch–Duplay tubularisation, Mathieu.

### Complications
- Fistula, meatal stenosis or regression, glans dehiscence; generally low after distal repair.

**Diagram to draw:** fish-mouth glanular meatus with intact prepuce, and the pyramid incision.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Wide glanular meatus, intact prepuce, no curvature; up to 5% of hypospadias.
> - Often found at circumcision; retract foreskin first.
> - Pyramid repair: dissect, taper and tubularise; glansplasty.

## Undescended testis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 16 Paper 2, Q3d
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 3, Q3a and 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q2)

### Definition and classification
- Testis not in the normal scrotal position: **palpable** (canalicular, high scrotal, **ectopic**, commonest at the superficial inguinal pouch) or **non-palpable** (intra-abdominal, "peeping", vanished/absent).
- **Retractile:** brought down and stays; review yearly. **Acquired:** previously scrotal, ascends later.

### Epidemiology and aetiology
- 2–4% of term boys at birth, 1–2% at 3 months; commoner in preterm infants.
- Descent needs Leydig hormones: **INSL3** (transabdominal phase) and **androgens** (inguinoscrotal phase), with genitofemoral nerve/CGRP (calcitonin gene-related peptide) guiding the gubernaculum.

### Evaluation
- Warm room, frog-leg position; "milk" from the anterior superior iliac spine. Contralateral hypertrophy suggests vanished testis.
- **No ultrasound or MRI** (magnetic resonance imaging): they do not change management.
- **Bilateral non-palpable, or with hypospadias:** urgent karyotype and hormones to exclude **CAH** (congenital adrenal hyperplasia) or other DSD (differences of sex development).

### Management
- **Observe to 6 months corrected age; operate after 6 months, preferably by 12, and by 18 months at the latest** (AUA [American Urological Association] 2014). Early surgery restores testicular growth.
- **Hormones (hCG [human chorionic gonadotrophin], LHRH [luteinising hormone-releasing hormone]): not recommended**; descent in about 20% only.

| Situation | Operation |
|---|---|
| Palpable | Inguinal orchidopexy (mobilise cord, ligate processus, subdartos pouch) or scrotal (Bianchi) |
| Non-palpable | Examination under anaesthesia, then laparoscopy |
| Testis seen, vessels long | Laparoscopic orchidopexy (primary most successful) |
| Short vessels | **Fowler–Stephens** one- or two-stage (testicular vessels divided; survives on vasal collaterals) or Shehata traction |
| Vessels and vas enter ring | Inguinal or scrotal exploration; excise nubbin |
| Blind-ending vessels | Stop: vanished testis |
| Postpubertal, normal contralateral testis | Orchidectomy |

- **Complications:** atrophy (higher after Fowler–Stephens), re-ascent, vas injury.

### Prognosis
- **Fertility:** paternity about 80% unilateral, one-third bilateral.
- **Cancer:** 2–5-fold risk; orchidopexy after 10–11 years carries relative risk 5.8 (Walsh). Teach self-examination.

**Diagram to draw:** laparoscopic view of the internal ring: testis, vessels entering ring, blind-ending vessels.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Operate from 6 months, preferably by 12, latest 18 months.
> - No imaging, no hormones; laparoscopy for non-palpable testis.
> - Bilateral non-palpable: exclude CAH/DSD.

## Techniques of chordee correction
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 17 Paper 2, Q3b
(related: Practice Set 10 Paper 1, Q3d)

### Definition and causes
- Chordee: **ventral penile curvature**, with or without hypospadias. It, not meatal position, decides one-stage versus staged repair.
- Causes: tethered ventral skin, fibrous dysplastic dartos and Buck's fascia, short urethral plate, **corporal disproportion**.
- Devine–Horton (1973) graded the dysplastic layers: I spongiosum, II Buck's and dartos, III dartos only, IV corporal disproportion.

### Assessment
- Parents' photographs; **artificial erection** in theatre: Gittes–McLaughlin (tourniquet at the base plus saline), corporal compression, or prostaglandin. A tourniquet can hide basal curvature.
- Grade: mild <30°, moderate 30–45°, severe ≥45° (goniometer; eye underestimates).

### Stepwise correction
```
Deglove to penoscrotal junction + excise dysplastic dartos → erection test
  ├─ Straight        → urethroplasty
  ├─ Residual <30°   → dorsal plication
  └─ >30°            → divide urethral plate → still >30° → ventral lengthening (stage)
```

| | **Dorsal shortening (plication)** | **Ventral lengthening (corporotomy ± graft)** |
|---|---|---|
| Methods | **Nesbit:** excise dorsal tunical ellipses, close transversely. **Baskin:** single midline plication in the **12 o'clock nerve-free zone**. Heineke–Mikulicz dorsal incision closed transversely; 16-dot plication | 1–3 transverse **full-thickness corporotomies** (STAG, staged tubularised autograft); single **9-to-3 o'clock corporotomy** with graft: **dermis, tunica vaginalis**, SIS (small intestinal submucosa); dermal graft 20% larger than defect |
| Use | ≤15–30° | Moderate–severe after plate division |
| Recurrence | 28% (one series) | 9% |
| Risk | Shortening; dorsal nerve injury off midline | Longer, staged; theoretical erectile dysfunction |

- **Plate division** (transection) releases a short plate; not dividing it is an independent risk factor for recurrence. Repair is then staged.
- **Also know:** "fairy cuts" (superficial tunical incisions); lateral or dorsal curves need multipoint plication; correct **torsion** by dartos flap rotation.
- **Rule:** correct fully the first time and repeat the erection test before urethroplasty. Curvature may recur at puberty.

**Diagram to draw:** penile cross-section with dorsal nerves at 11 and 1 o'clock and midline plication at 12.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Artificial erection after degloving decides the technique.
> - <30°: dorsal plication; >30°: divide plate ± ventral graft and stage.
> - Plication recurrence 28% vs grafting 9%.

## Perineo-scrotal hypospadias
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 17 Paper 2, Q3e
(related: 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q1)

### Definition
- A severe **proximal hypospadias**: meatus at the penoscrotal junction, scrotum or perineum, with severe ventral curvature, a short penis, bifid scrotum and often **penoscrotal transposition**.
- Cause: failed androgen-dependent urethral fold fusion (8–16 weeks).

### Evaluation
- Examine stretched penile length, glans width, transposition and **both testes**.
- **DSD (disorder of sex development) work-up** if there is an undescended testis (especially non-palpable), a perineal meatus or ambiguity: karyotype, ultrasound for Müllerian remnants, testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone), FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), AMH (anti-Müllerian hormone). DSD risk is 64% in proximal vs 7% in distal cases.
- Prostatic utricle is commoner; cystoscopy in severe cases. No routine VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) or renal ultrasound.

### Preparation
- **Timing:** 6–18 months.
- **Preoperative androgen** if glans width <14–15 mm: testosterone 2 mg/kg IM (intramuscular) at 5 and 2 weeks before surgery; evidence mixed.

### Surgery
**1. Curvature correction:** deglove, artificial erection, plication if <30°, plate division and ventral corporotomy ± graft if >30° (see Practice Set 17 Paper 2, Q3b).

**2. Urethroplasty: staged repair is now preferred**
| Repair | Technique | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **Two-stage Bracka / STAG** (staged tubularised autograft) | Stage 1: correct curvature, divide plate, quilt inner-preputial or **buccal graft**. Stage 2 (≥6 months): tubularise, glansplasty, barrier layer | Preferred for most |
| **Two-stage Byars** | Dorsal preputial flaps moved ventrally, tubularised later | Supple flap bed |
| One-stage TPIF (transverse preputial island flap, Duckett tube) or onlay island flap | Flap urethroplasty | Higher complications |
| Proximal TIP (tubularised incised plate) | Plate kept | Stricture, recurrent curvature: abandoned for severe cases |

- **Why stage?** A urethroplasty over a fresh corporal graft fails more often.
- Principles: magnification, tension-free closure, **vascularised barrier layer** (dartos, tunica vaginalis); **scrotoplasty** for transposition.

### Complications
- **23–68%**; 25–75% need further surgery. **Fistula** commonest (3–45%), glans dehiscence, meatal stenosis, stricture, **diverticulum** (long rigid neourethra raises resistance: Poiseuille's law), recurrent curvature, hair-bearing urethra.

**Diagram to draw:** two-stage graft repair: stage 1 graft bed, stage 2 tubularisation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Perineoscrotal hypospadias with undescended testis → DSD work-up.
> - Curvature >30°: plate division ± graft and staging.
> - STAG/Bracka staged repair preferred; fistula commonest complication.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 4 · Paediatric Urology 1

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Wilms tumour: classification, staging and management (LAQ)

- WT1 at 11p13 (WAGR, Denys-Drash); BWS at 11p15.
- Anaplasia (≥3× nuclei, hyperchromasia, abnormal mitoses) is unfavourable; TP53.
- Any spill, biopsy or positive node = stage III; sample ≥5 nodes.
- COG operates first; SIOP gives chemotherapy (VCR + AMD) first.
- Bilateral: three drugs, surgery by week 12, NSS.

#### Retractile testis? (SAQ)

- Retractile = comes down and stays down.
- Gliding testis springs back: treat as undescended.
- Yearly examination until puberty; operate if it ascends.

#### Retroileal Ureter (SAQ)

- Retroiliac = preureteral iliac artery; persistent ventral umbilical artery root.
- Obstruction at L5–S1; either side or bilateral.
- Look for vasal and ureteric ectopia.
- Retrocaval: right, L3–L4, fishhook, 1 in 1500.

#### Medullary sponge kidney (SAQ)

- Ectatic collecting ducts; sporadic; "bristles on a brush".
- Hypercalciuria, incomplete distal RTA, hypocitraturia.
- Potassium citrate is first-line prevention.

#### Megameatus intact prepuce (SAQ)

- Wide glanular meatus, intact prepuce, no curvature; up to 5% of hypospadias.
- Often found at circumcision; retract foreskin first.
- Pyramid repair: dissect, taper and tubularise; glansplasty.

#### Undescended testis (SAQ)

- Operate from 6 months, preferably by 12, latest 18 months.
- No imaging, no hormones; laparoscopy for non-palpable testis.
- Bilateral non-palpable: exclude CAH/DSD.

#### Techniques of chordee correction (SAQ)

- Artificial erection after degloving decides the technique.
- <30°: dorsal plication; >30°: divide plate ± ventral graft and stage.
- Plication recurrence 28% vs grafting 9%.

#### Perineo-scrotal hypospadias (SAQ)

- Perineoscrotal hypospadias with undescended testis → DSD work-up.
- Curvature >30°: plate division ± graft and staging.
- STAG/Bracka staged repair preferred; fistula commonest complication.

# Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2 — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Valve bladder: management
**Asked as:**
- Management of valve bladder. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- Valve bladder. — *Practice Set 12 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)

### Definition
- **Valve bladder syndrome** (Mitchell, 1982): upper tracts and renal function worsen after successful PUV (posterior urethral valve) ablation, without residual obstruction, because of bladder dysfunction.

### Pathophysiology (vicious cycle)
```
Detrusor hypertrophy, collagen, low compliance → poor sensation, rising residual
+ polyuria (nephrogenic diabetes insipidus) → bladder never empty
→ pressure transmitted to dilated upper tracts → they refill the bladder → renal damage
```
- Three processes: **polyuria, poor sensation, residual urine.**
- Age pattern: hyperreflexia in infancy → better compliance in childhood → large atonic bladder (myogenic failure) after puberty.

### Clinical features
- Late continence (about 5.5 years), incontinence, UTI (urinary tract infection), worsening hydronephrosis, rising creatinine. **New incontinence after being dry is a red flag.**

### Evaluation
- Uroflowmetry and PVR (post-void residual) at each visit; ultrasound; creatinine; voiding diary.
- **Videourodynamics** when the picture changes and before transplantation.

### Management (escalating ladder)
1. **Behavioural:** adequate fluids, timed and double voiding, biofeedback.
2. **Drugs:** anticholinergic (oxybutynin 0.1 mg/kg twice daily) for a small high-pressure bladder; α-blocker for high residual. Stop oxybutynin if residual rises.
3. **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)** if residual rises despite drugs.
4. **Overnight bladder drainage** (catheter for 7–10 hours) for worsening hydronephrosis, UTI or renal function; it gives bladder and kidneys a daily rest.
5. **Mitrofanoff appendicovesicostomy** if urethral CIC is difficult (sensate urethra, high bladder neck).
6. **Augmentation** (rare) for a small, high-pressure bladder despite the above: **ureterocystoplasty** with the dilated ureter is preferred, avoiding bowel (mucus, acidosis, stones).
- Reflux is treated through the bladder; reimplantation is rarely needed. Manage CKD (chronic kidney disease).

### Transplantation
- ESRD (end-stage renal disease) in 20–50%. Optimise the bladder first (videourodynamics, CIC or overnight drainage); graft survival is then similar.

**Diagram to draw:** vicious-cycle diagram of the valve bladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Polyuria, poor sensation, residual urine.
> - Overnight drainage breaks the cycle.
> - Ureteric augmentation rather than bowel; optimise before transplant.

## Embryogenesis of urinary bladder
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 2, Q3c
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### 1. Division of the cloaca (weeks 4–6)
- The **urorectal septum** (Tourneux and Rathke folds) divides the endodermal cloaca into a dorsal **anorectal canal** and a ventral **UGS (urogenital sinus)**.
- **Mesonephric (Wolffian) duct entry** is the landmark:
  - **Cranial vesicourethral canal** (continuous with the allantois) → **bladder and pelvic urethra**.
  - **Caudal (definitive) UGS** → phallic urethra (male); distal vagina and vestibule (female).

### 2. Layers of the wall
- **Urothelium:** UGS endoderm.
- **Lamina propria, detrusor, serosa:** surrounding splanchnic mesenchyme; epithelium and mesenchyme induce each other.

### 3. Trigone (weeks 5–6)
- The **common excretory ducts** (mesonephric duct distal to the ureteric bud) dilate and are absorbed into the UGS.
- **Apoptosis** separates ureters from mesonephric ducts. Ureteric orifices move **cranially and laterally**; mesonephric ducts end caudally as ejaculatory ducts.
- **Classic view:** trigone muscle is ureteric. **Current view** (mouse lineage tracing): mostly bladder smooth muscle.
- **Weigert–Meyer rule:** bud too low → lateral orifice, short tunnel → VUR (vesicoureteral reflux); too high → caudal, medial, ectopic orifice.

### 4. Allantois and urachus
- **Months 4–5:** the bladder descends into the pelvis; the allantoic duct elongates and obliterates as the **urachus** → median umbilical ligament.

### 5. Function
- Urine production starts at **week 10**. Fetal bladder seen in about half at 10 weeks, almost all by 13 weeks; empties every 15–20 minutes. After **16 weeks** amniotic fluid is mainly fetal urine.

### Anomalies explained by embryology
| Anomaly | Basis |
|---|---|
| **Exstrophy** | Cloacal membrane not reinforced by infraumbilical mesoderm |
| Urachal (patent urachus, sinus, cyst, diverticulum) | Failed obliteration at different levels |
| Agenesis, duplication | UGS or trigone failure; septation |
| Congenital (Hutch) diverticulum | Detrusor deficiency at the ureteric hiatus |
| VUR, ectopic ureter | Abnormal bud position |

**Diagram to draw:** cloaca divided by the urorectal septum, labelled vesicourethral canal, UGS, allantois.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Urorectal septum weeks 4–6; mesonephric duct entry is the landmark.
> - Trigone: common excretory duct absorption; mostly bladder muscle (current view).
> - Urachus forms at months 4–5; urine from week 10; amniotic fluid mainly urine after 16 weeks.

## Anterior urethral valve
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 17 Paper 2, Q3c

### Definition and epidemiology
- AUV (anterior urethral valve): a congenital obstructing fold or cusp in the anterior urethra (bulbar, penoscrotal or penile). It is the **commonest congenital obstructive lesion of the anterior urethra** but **25–30 times less common than PUV (posterior urethral valves)**.
- Often linked to an **anterior urethral diverticulum**: the distal lip of the diverticulum fills and acts as a valve, or a semilunar fold hangs from the roof.

### Embryology (unclear)
- Hypoplastic corpus spongiosum over the segment suggests a place on the **hypospadias spectrum**, or faulty union of urethral mucosa and fossa navicularis epithelium; a ruptured dilated Cowper (bulbourethral) gland duct is also proposed.

### Clinical features (vary with severity and age)
- Antenatal hydronephrosis, LUTO (lower urinary tract obstruction) in severe cases.
- **Ventral penile bulge** during voiding, with urine expressed by compressing the shaft; post-void dribbling, weak stream, incontinence.
- Palpable bladder, UTI (urinary tract infection), renal insufficiency.

### Evaluation
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram):** dilated anterior urethra proximal to the valve, diverticulum, trabeculated bladder, bladder diverticula, often VUR (vesicoureteric reflux).
- Ultrasound, creatinine; urethroscopy confirms.
- **Differential:** Cowper syringocele, megalourethra, acquired diverticulum, stricture.

### Management
- **Premature or small infant:** vesicostomy until the urethra takes a cystoscope.
- **Most cases:** **endoscopic valve ablation** (Bugbee electrode or laser), usually definitive.
- **Large diverticulum:** open excision and urethroplasty over a catheter.
- Then manage VUR, infection and bladder dysfunction.

### AUV versus PUV
| Feature | AUV | PUV |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency | 25–30 times rarer | Commonest infant outlet obstruction |
| Site | Bulbar, penoscrotal, penile | From the verumontanum |
| Sign | Penile bulge | Weak stream, palpable bladder |
| Bladder dysfunction | Up to 80% | Near universal |
| Renal outcome | 78% normal function | ESRD (end-stage renal disease) 20–50% |

### Prognosis
- Depends on pre-treatment creatinine and GFR (glomerular filtration rate). Azotaemia, VUR and UTI together raise the risk of poor renal outcome 25-fold.

**Diagram to draw:** VCUG sketch: dilated bulbar urethra with diverticulum ending at a valve lip.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 25–30 times rarer than PUV; diverticulum common.
> - Penile bulge on voiding; diagnosis by VCUG.
> - Endoscopic ablation usually definitive; 78% keep normal renal function.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 5 · Paediatric Urology 2

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Valve bladder: management (SAQ)

- Polyuria, poor sensation, residual urine.
- Overnight drainage breaks the cycle.
- Ureteric augmentation rather than bowel; optimise before transplant.

#### Embryogenesis of urinary bladder (SAQ)

- Urorectal septum weeks 4–6; mesonephric duct entry is the landmark.
- Trigone: common excretory duct absorption; mostly bladder muscle (current view).
- Urachus forms at months 4–5; urine from week 10; amniotic fluid mainly urine after 16 weeks.

#### Anterior urethral valve (SAQ)

- 25–30 times rarer than PUV; diverticulum common.
- Penile bulge on voiding; diagnosis by VCUG.
- Endoscopic ablation usually definitive; 78% keep normal renal function.

# Section 6 · Female Urology — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Urethral caruncle.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q3f

### Definition
- A benign, red, fleshy, **inflammatory polypoid lesion of the posterior lip of the distal female urethra**. Not a true tumour, but a benign mimic.
- Common; seen mainly in **postmenopausal women**.

### Aetiology and pathogenesis
- **Oestrogen deficiency (GSM, genitourinary syndrome of menopause)**: atrophy and laxity of distal urethral mucosa.
- Related to **mucosal prolapse**: the everted posterior mucosa is chronically irritated, causing haemorrhage, necrosis and inflammatory growth.

### Pathology
- Classical types: **papillomatous, angiomatous and granulomatous**; inflamed vascular stroma.

### Clinical features
- Often asymptomatic, found on examination.
