- **Spotting on underwear**, bleeding, pain, dysuria; may thrombose into a dark, tender mass.
- Examination: small, red, soft mass at the **6 o'clock** meatal margin; the rest of the meatus looks normal.

### Differential diagnosis
| Lesion | Distinguishing feature |
|---|---|
| **Urethral mucosal prolapse** | **Circumferential "doughnut"** around whole meatus; prepubertal girls and postmenopausal women |
| **Urethral carcinoma** | Firm, ulcerated, indurated, fixed; bleeding mass; biopsy |
| **Urethral diverticulum** | Mid or proximal anterior vaginal wall mass; dysuria, dyspareunia, dribbling; MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) |
| Skene gland cyst or abscess | Lateral to meatus; distorts it; does not communicate |
| Others | Melanoma, lymphoma, TB (tuberculosis), condyloma, intestinal metaplasia; postmenopausal bleeding from the uterus |
- Caruncle is listed as a possible risk factor for female urethral cancer, so atypical lesions must be biopsied.

### Evaluation
- Clinical diagnosis; exclude a gynaecological bleeding source.
- **Excision biopsy** if atypical, indurated, ulcerated, enlarging or refractory; cystourethroscopy if in doubt.

### Treatment
- **Conservative (first line)**: **topical vaginal oestrogen** (shrinks most), anti-inflammatory creams, sitz baths. Ask about breast or endometrial cancer before oestrogen.
- **Surgical excision** for large, symptomatic, thrombosed, refractory or suspicious lesions:
  1. Grasp the tip and apply traction to expose the base.
  2. Excise at the base.
  3. Reapproximate mucosa with absorbable sutures if the defect is large.
  4. Foley catheter for a few days.
- Complications: bleeding, recurrence, meatal stenosis.

**Diagram to draw:** meatus with a 6 o'clock caruncle compared with circumferential prolapse.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Postmenopausal posterior-lip lesion; oestrogen deficiency.
> - Prolapse is circumferential; caruncle is focal.
> - Topical oestrogen first; excise if atypical or refractory.

## Mid-urethral slings for SUI: TOT, TVT and TOT versus TVT
**Asked as:**
- TOT. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- TOT versus TVT in SUI. — *Summer 2021 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- TVT — *Practice Set 16 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(TOT answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3f.) (TVT versus TOT also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)

*Assumed meaning: TOT = transobturator tape, a mid-urethral sling for female stress urinary incontinence.*

### Introduction (definition and history)
- Both are polypropylene **MUS (mid-urethral slings)** for SUI (stress urinary incontinence), based on the **integral theory** (Petros and Ulmsten, 1990): a loose mid-urethral tape replaces the pubourethral ligaments as a backboard on straining.
- **TVT (tension-free vaginal tape, retropubic):** retropubic polypropylene MUS; Ulmsten, 1995–1998.
- **TOT (transobturator tape):** a synthetic MUS of macroporous monofilament polypropylene, passed through the obturator foramina instead of the retropubic space. Delorme (2001) described it to avoid bladder, bowel and vessel injury.

### Mechanism
- **Integral theory** (Petros and Ulmsten, 1990): continence needs pubourethral ligaments, the suburethral vaginal hammock and pubococcygeus (pubourethral ligaments, suburethral hammock and pubococcygeus maintain continence). Hypermobility is a sign of their damage, not the cause.
- The loose tape at mid-urethra secures (supports) the **posterior urethral wall** during stress, which compresses against it on straining (compression, dynamic kinking); it is not tight at rest.
- TOT anchors in the obturator muscles.

### Material
- **Type 1 (Amid) macroporous monofilament polypropylene**, pores >75 µm: allows macrophage entry and fibrous ingrowth, so it resists infection.

### Indications and evaluation
- SUI or stress-predominant mixed incontinence after failed pelvic floor training; index SUI with urethral hypermobility.
- TVT preferred over TOT in **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)** or recurrent SUI.
- History, examination, **objective stress test**, urinalysis, PVR (post-void residual); urodynamics only if complicated.
- Counsel about permanent mesh, mesh risks and alternatives (colposuspension, autologous sling, bulking) (AUA/SUFU [American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics and Female Urology]).

### Technique differences

| | TVT (retropubic) | TOT (transobturator) |
|---|---|---|
| Path | Vagina → retropubic space → suprapubic skin, hugging the pubis; cystoscopy essential | Labiocrural fold → obturator membrane → vagina (outside-in), or reverse (inside-out) |
| Structures at risk | Bladder, bowel, iliac and epigastric vessels | Obturator nerves and vessels, vaginal fornix |

#### TVT technique
1. Local, spinal or general anaesthesia; lithotomy.
2. Two suprapubic stab incisions 2 cm lateral to midline; 1.5 cm midline vaginal incision 1.5 cm from the meatus; minimal dissection, endopelvic fascia not opened.
3. **Bottom-up** 5 mm trocar hugging the back of the pubis, through the retropubic space to the skin (catheter guide deflects the bladder).
4. **Cystoscopy with a 70° lens, full bladder**, after each pass; if perforated, re-pass more laterally.
5. Tension **loosely** with a clamp between tape and urethra; remove sheath.

#### TOT technique (outside-in)
1. Lithotomy with hips hyperflexed; 1.5 cm midline incision 1.5 cm below the meatus; dissect laterally to the ischiopubic ramus.
2. Stab incision in the labiocrural fold, at the notch between inferior pubic ramus and adductor longus tendon.
3. Trocar perforates the obturator membrane, turns medially onto the finger and exits the vaginal incision.
4. Cystourethroscopy; check the vaginal fornix.
5. Tension-free: a clamp passes easily between tape and urethra.
- **Inside-out** (vagina to thigh): fewer vaginal perforations, more voiding dysfunction.

### Outcomes
- TVT: Ulmsten (1998): 91% cured at 1 year. **Ward–Hilton:** equal to colposuspension at 5 years. Long-term cure varies 48–97% by definition.
- **TOMUS (Richter, 2010):** 597 women; 12-month objective cure **80.8% retropubic vs 77.7% transobturator** (not different); voiding dysfunction needing surgery 2.7% vs 0%.
- TOMUS at 5 years: 51.3% vs 43.4%; equivalence not shown; satisfaction similar.
- **Cochrane review (Ford, 2017):** cure similar up to and beyond 5 years; repeat surgery possibly commoner after TOT.
- TOT poorer with **ISD** or a **fixed urethra**: retropubic sling preferred.

### Complications (Cochrane, 2017)

| Complication | TVT (retropubic) | TOT |
|---|---|---|
| Bladder perforation | **4.5%** | **0.6%** |
| Groin pain | 1.3% | **6.4%** |
| Voiding dysfunction, blood loss (bleeding), vascular injury | More | Less |
| Mesh exposure | About 2% | About 2% |

#### TVT complications
| Complication | Notes |
|---|---|
| Bladder perforation | 4.5% (TOT 0.6%); re-pass and continue |
| Urethral injury | Abandon mesh; staged repair |
| Bleeding/haematoma | 2–3%; rarely bowel or vascular injury |
| Voiding dysfunction | Surgery needed 2.7%; incise tape in midline within 3 months |
| Mesh exposure | About 2% |
| Others | De novo urgency, suprapubic pain (groin pain less than TOT) |

#### TOT complications
- Groin pain (usually temporary; NSAIDs [non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs]), mesh exposure, voiding dysfunction (incise sling within 4 weeks), de novo urgency, UTI (urinary tract infection), rarely thigh abscess.

- Urethral injury during either: **do not place mesh** (AUA [American Urological Association] guideline).

### Choosing
- **TVT preferred:** **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)** (ALPP [abdominal leak point pressure] <60 cm H₂O or MUCP [maximum urethral closure pressure] <20 cm H₂O), fixed urethra, recurrent SUI. Failure is 12 times likelier after TOT with low leak point pressure (O'Connor, 2006).
- **TOT preferred:** previous retropubic surgery, obesity, avoiding bladder injury.
- AUA: either may be offered to the index patient.
- **Also know:** single-incision mini-slings (non-inferior to TOT); mesh warnings (FDA [Food and Drug Administration] 2010; UK pause 2018): counsel about alternatives (colposuspension, autologous fascial sling, bulking agents).

**Diagram to draw:** coronal pelvis: U-shaped retropubic TVT at mid-urethra versus flat TOT, tape under mid-urethra through both obturator foramina.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Integral theory; loose tape at mid-urethra.
> - Type 1 macroporous monofilament polypropylene.
> - Delorme 2001; TOT fewer bladder injuries than retropubic.
> - TOMUS: cure equivalent at 1 year (80.8% vs 77.7%).
> - TOT: less bladder injury (0.6% vs 4.5%), more groin pain (6.4% vs 1.3%); worse in ISD.
> - Cystoscopy after each trocar pass; bladder perforation 4.5%.
> - Prefer retropubic TVT in ISD or fixed urethra.

## VVF repair: early versus delayed timing, and surgery for recurrent VVF
**Asked as:**
- Early versus delayed VVF repair. — *Summer 2021 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*
- Surgery For Recurrant Vesico Vaginal Fistulae — *Winter 2025 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(timing of VVF repair: see 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3b)

### Introduction
- VVF (vesicovaginal fistula): an epithelialised bladder–vagina communication causing continuous leakage.
- Causes: **obstetric** (over 90% in low-resource settings; obstructed labour) and **iatrogenic** (over 75% in high-resource settings, mainly hysterectomy); radiation; malignancy.
- Common mechanism: **ischaemic necrosis**, then a tract that epithelialises with time.
- Presents: cystotomy, days; thermal injury, 7–10 days; radiation, months to years.
- Recurrent VVF: persists after a failed repair. **The first repair has the best chance**; redo tissue is scarred and poorly vascularised.
- Failure causes: tension, ischaemia, overlapping suture lines, infection, poor drainage, **malignancy, radiation**, missed second tract.

### The debate: early versus delayed repair

| | Early repair | Delayed repair |
|---|---|---|
| Definition (varies) | Days, <6 weeks or <3 months | Traditionally ≥3 months |
| Rationale | Clean iatrogenic tissue heals well; ends wetness and distress early | Inflammation settles, necrotic tissue sloughs, margins become clear |
| Evidence | **Comparable closure** in most series; Waaldijk: high first-attempt closure with immediate obstetric repair | One small series: more recurrence when repaired <6 weeks |
| Drawback | Friable tissue if inflamed | Prolonged distress, no better outcome |

- Some authors **avoid 4–12 weeks** (active healing phase).
- **EAU (European Association of Urology) consensus:** no minimum delay if **no active infection and no recent irradiation**. 

### Recommended timing by cause
- **Clean post-hysterectomy:** early repair. **Obstetric with necrosis:** catheter trial, wait about 3 months.
- **Radiation fistula:** wait **≥12 months** after radiotherapy; biopsy for recurrence; interposition flap; diversion if the bladder is poor.
- **Recurrent (redo) fistula:** wait until inflammation settles (commonly about 3 months). Radiation: **≥12 months** after radiotherapy; failed radiation repairs were redone after **≥6 months**.

### Conservative trial (during the wait)
- Catheter drainage cures about 15%: best if small (<1 cm, ideally <3 mm), started **within 3 weeks**, fresh and non-irradiated. Once epithelialised (≥6 weeks), unlikely. EAU: if tried, at least 12 weeks.
- **Also know:** fulguration of tiny tracts; fibrin glue.

### Evaluation before redo
- **Biopsy the tract** if any history of cancer (fistula may signal recurrence).
- **Cystoscopy and vaginoscopy**: size, number, relation to ureters and bladder neck.
- **CT urography (computed tomography urography)** to exclude a **ureterovaginal fistula**; dye test.
- Urodynamics for capacity and compliance; treat UTI (urinary tract infection) / infection, optimise nutrition.

### Choice of route
- Vaginal or abdominal (open, laparoscopic, robotic): about 90% first-attempt closure.

| Vaginal | Abdominal (open, laparoscopic, robotic) |
|---|---|
| Distal or trigonal, accessible | **Recurrent ≥2 cm**, supratrigonal, narrow vagina |
| Failed **Latzko** can be cured by a second Latzko | Ureter involved, needs reimplantation |
| Flap-splitting, offset flaps | Augmentation or **Tanagho bladder flap** needed |
| Less pain, shorter stay | **O'Conor** bivalving finds the scarred plane |
- Robotic repair of recurrences gives high closure.

### Principles at repair (operative principles)
- Treat infection, optimise nutrition; ureteric stents if within 1 cm of a ureter.
- Excise scar; wide mobilisation; tension-free, multilayer, **non-overlapping** closure.
- **Interposition flap** (Martius, omentum, peritoneum) if irradiated or recurrent. **Interposition flap is widely used in recurrent fistula** (accepted though not proven superior; an RCT showed no benefit in simple VVF):

| Flap | Route | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Martius** (labial fat pad, pudendal vessels) | Vaginal | Mid–distal, bladder neck; not apex |
| Peritoneal | Vaginal or abdominal | Apical post-hysterectomy |
| **Omentum (J-flap)** | Abdominal | Radiation, recurrent, large |
| Gracilis, Singapore flaps | Vaginal/perineal | Refractory, large tissue loss |
- Fill test 200–300 mL; catheter often ≥2 weeks (recurrent: **≥10–14 days**, then cystogram).

### When repair is unwise
- Multiple failures, severe radiation damage, massive tissue loss: **conduit or continent diversion**; if unfit, ureteric occlusion with nephrostomies.
- **Also know:** rectus abdominis flap (last resort); more than one repair predicts residual incontinence.

**Diagram to draw:** timeline: early window, 4–12 week zone, 3 months (obstetric), 12 months (radiation); Martius flap between bladder and vaginal closures.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Early repair for clean iatrogenic VVF; closure comparable to delayed.
> - Delay ~3 months obstetric, ≥12 months radiation.
> - Catheter trial works only if small, fresh and started within 3 weeks.
> - First repair has the highest success.
> - Biopsy; exclude ureteric fistula before redo.
> - Interposition flap (Martius, peritoneum, omentum) in recurrent repair.
> - Diversion after multiple failures or severe radiation.

## ALPP
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3a
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 1, Q3e and 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3b)

### Definition
- **ALPP (abdominal leak point pressure):** the **intravesical pressure** at which urine leaks because of raised abdominal pressure, **without a detrusor contraction**.
- It measures sphincter strength: **lower ALPP = weaker sphincter**. Seen only in SUI (stress urinary incontinence); there is **no normal value**.
- Provoked by Valsalva (**VLPP**, Valsalva leak point pressure) or cough (**CLPP**, cough leak point pressure). VLPP is lower than CLPP, so state the method.

### Technique
1. Urethral and rectal catheters; standing; fill to **150 mL**.
2. Graded Valsalva or cough.
3. Record **total Pves (vesical pressure) at visible leak**, including baseline.
4. No leak: repeat every 50 mL to capacity; then remove the urethral catheter and read pressure from the rectal line.
5. Report volume, catheter size and provocation.

### Interpretation (McGuire 1993)
| ALPP (cmH₂O) | Inference |
|---|---|
| **<60** | **ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)** |
| 60–90 | Equivocal |
| **≥90** | **Urethral hypermobility**, little ISD |

### Clinical uses
- Separates ISD from hypermobility; low ALPP traditionally favours a sling or bulking agent over colposuspension.
- Post-prostatectomy incontinence: many men leak only once the urethral catheter is removed.

### Limitations
- Falls with larger catheters and volumes; invalid with DO (detrusor overactivity); prolapse masks leak.
- ISD and hypermobility coexist; ALPP alone predicts surgical success poorly.
- **VALUE trial (2012):** office evaluation was non-inferior to urodynamics before surgery for uncomplicated SUI.

### ALPP versus DLPP
| ALPP | DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) |
|---|---|
| Sphincter against abdominal pressure | Bladder pressure against outlet resistance |
| **Low = bad** | **High = bad**: >40 cmH₂O → hydronephrosis or reflux in 85% (McGuire 1981) |
| SUI | Neurogenic, low-compliance bladder |

- **Also know:** newer term "urodynamic stress test" (ICS, International Continence Society); EAU (European Association of Urology): do not use leak point pressure to grade severity.

**Diagram to draw:** Pves, Pabd and Pdet traces with leak on Valsalva, flat Pdet.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pves at leak with strain, no detrusor contraction; start at 150 mL.
> - <60 ISD; 60–90 equivocal; ≥90 hypermobility.
> - VLPP < CLPP; catheter size and volume alter it.

## Vaginal prolapse repair.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 17 Paper 2, Q3d
(related: Practice Set 12 Paper 1, Q3d)

*Read as: surgical repair of vaginal wall and vault prolapse (pelvic organ prolapse).*

### Principles
- POP (pelvic organ prolapse) is a quality-of-life condition: operate for bother, not anatomy. The first operation gives the best chance.
- Identify each compartment by POP-Q (Pelvic Organ Prolapse Quantification). **The apex is the cornerstone**: failing to support it greatly raises recurrence.
- **Three categories:** **restorative** (native tissue), **compensatory** (graft or mesh, for weak tissue or failed repair), **obliterative**.
- Vaginal route: fewer complications, quicker recovery. Abdominal route: more durable.
- Screen for **occult SUI (stress urinary incontinence)** with prolapse reduced; consider a concomitant sling. Finish with **cystoscopy** to confirm ureteric efflux.

### Operations by compartment
| Compartment | Operations | Key points |
|---|---|---|
| **Anterior** (cystocele) | **Anterior colporrhaphy** (midline plication of pubocervical fascia); **paravaginal repair** (fascia reattached to the ATFP (arcus tendineus fasciae pelvis)) | Central vs lateral defect; does not support the apex |
| **Apical** (uterus, vault) | **Uterosacral suspension** (ureter injury 1–11%); **sacrospinous fixation** (suture 1.5–3 cm medial to the ischial spine; gluteal pain 15%); iliococcygeus suspension; McCall culdoplasty; vaginal hysterectomy; hysteropexy; Manchester | OPTIMAL trial: uterosacral = sacrospinous at 2 years |
| **Apical, abdominal** | **Sacrocolpopexy** (open, laparoscopic, robotic): macroporous polypropylene mesh from vaginal apex to sacral promontory | Most durable (>90%); mesh exposure ~10% long term (CARE) |
| **Posterior** (rectocele) | **Midline fascial plication** without levatorplasty; site-specific repair; perineorrhaphy | Levator plication causes dyspareunia; constipation may persist |
| **Obliterative** | **Le Fort** (partial, uterus in situ, lateral channels) or total colpocleisis | Elderly, not sexually active; success >90%; coital regret ≤13% |

- Cochrane 2016: vaginal apical repair has more recurrence and dyspareunia than sacrocolpopexy, but suits many older women.
- Transvaginal synthetic mesh for POP was withdrawn in the USA (FDA (Food and Drug Administration), 2019).

### Complications
- Bladder, ureter or rectal injury; retention; de novo SUI; dyspareunia; recurrence (anterior after sacrospinous fixation); mesh exposure.

**Diagram to draw:** DeLancey levels of support with sacrospinous and sacrocolpopexy fixation points.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Always restore apical support.
> - Sacrocolpopexy most durable; colpocleisis for frail elderly.
> - Cystoscopy after repair; screen for occult SUI.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 6 · Female Urology

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Urethral caruncle. (SAQ)

- Postmenopausal posterior-lip lesion; oestrogen deficiency.
- Prolapse is circumferential; caruncle is focal.
- Topical oestrogen first; excise if atypical or refractory.

#### Mid-urethral slings for SUI: TOT, TVT and TOT versus TVT (SAQ)

- Integral theory; loose tape at mid-urethra.
- Type 1 macroporous monofilament polypropylene.
- Delorme 2001; TOT fewer bladder injuries than retropubic.
- TOMUS: cure equivalent at 1 year (80.8% vs 77.7%).
- TOT: less bladder injury (0.6% vs 4.5%), more groin pain (6.4% vs 1.3%); worse in ISD.
- Cystoscopy after each trocar pass; bladder perforation 4.5%.
- Prefer retropubic TVT in ISD or fixed urethra.

#### VVF repair: early versus delayed timing, and surgery for recurrent VVF (SAQ)

- Early repair for clean iatrogenic VVF; closure comparable to delayed.
- Delay ~3 months obstetric, ≥12 months radiation.
- Catheter trial works only if small, fresh and started within 3 weeks.
- First repair has the highest success.
- Biopsy; exclude ureteric fistula before redo.
- Interposition flap (Martius, peritoneum, omentum) in recurrent repair.
- Diversion after multiple failures or severe radiation.

#### ALPP (SAQ)

- Pves at leak with strain, no detrusor contraction; start at 150 mL.
- <60 ISD; 60–90 equivocal; ≥90 hypermobility.
- VLPP < CLPP; catheter size and volume alter it.

#### Vaginal prolapse repair. (SAQ)

- Always restore apical support.
- Sacrocolpopexy most durable; colpocleisis for frail elderly.
- Cystoscopy after repair; screen for occult SUI.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Pathophysiology, classification and management of LUTS.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q1
(medical and surgical management of BPH also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q1)

### Definition
- **LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms):** symptoms of the storage, voiding and post-micturition phases; not disease-specific; up to half of older men.
- **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)** is histology; BPE is enlargement; BPO is prostatic obstruction; **BOO (bladder outlet obstruction)** has any cause. Not every man with LUTS has BPO.

### Classification
**1. By phase (ICS, International Continence Society)**

| Storage | Voiding | Post-micturition |
|---|---|---|
| Frequency, urgency, **nocturia** (waking ≥1 time to void), urgency incontinence | Weak or split stream, hesitancy, intermittency, straining, terminal dribble | Feeling of incomplete emptying, post-micturition dribble |

- Storage symptoms form **OAB (overactive bladder)**: urgency ± urgency incontinence, usually with frequency and nocturia; most bothersome.

**2. Functional (Wein)**

| | Because of the bladder | Because of the outlet |
|---|---|---|
| Failure to store | DO (detrusor overactivity), low compliance, hypersensitivity | Sphincter weakness (SUI, stress urinary incontinence) |
| Failure to empty | DUA (detrusor underactivity) | BPO, stricture, bladder neck contracture, sphincter dyssynergia |

**3. Severity:** IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score), 7 questions scored 0–5 plus a quality-of-life question: mild 0–7, moderate 8–19, severe 20–35.

**4. By cause:** prostate, bladder, urethra, neurological, polyuria, infection, stone, tumour.

### Pathophysiology
- **Prostate:** BPH is an increase in cell number of stroma and glands in the **transition zone** and periurethral region. **Type 2 5α-reductase** in stromal fibroblasts makes **DHT (dihydrotestosterone)**; androgens are permissive, not mitogenic. Ageing, inflammation and metabolic syndrome contribute.
- **Static component:** gland bulk; the **capsule** transmits expansion to the urethra (TUIP, transurethral incision of the prostate, relieves obstruction without removing tissue).
- **Dynamic component:** **α1A-adrenergic** smooth-muscle tone; the basis of α-blockers.
- Size does not correlate with symptoms or obstruction.
- **Bladder response:** hypertrophy → compensation → decompensation. Collagen causes trabeculation and low compliance; DO causes storage symptoms; late detrusor failure causes retention. One-third keep storage symptoms after surgery.
- **Also know:** nocturnal polyuria (night output >33% of 24 h over age 65); BPO complications: AUR (acute urinary retention), UTI (urinary tract infection), stones, haematuria, renal failure.

### Evaluation (EAU, European Association of Urology, men ≥40)
- **Basic:** history (comorbidity, drugs, sexual function), IPSS, DRE (digital rectal examination) and neurological check, **urinalysis (mandatory)**, PVR (post-void residual), PSA (prostate-specific antigen) if life expectancy >10 years.
- **Frequency–volume chart, 3 days:** for storage symptoms or nocturia.
- **Before treatment:** uroflowmetry (Qmax, maximum flow rate, <10 mL/s suggests BOO); ultrasound for volume and median lobe.
- **Selected:** creatinine and renal ultrasound if large PVR; cystoscopy for haematuria, stricture or before PUL (prostatic urethral lift).
- **Pressure–flow study** (separates BOO from DUA) before surgery if voided volume <150 mL, PVR >300 mL, age <50 or >80, or failed surgery.

### Management: EAU algorithm
```
Bothersome LUTS?  No  -> watchful waiting, lifestyle advice
Absolute surgical indication? Yes -> surgery
Otherwise:
  Nocturnal polyuria      -> lifestyle +/- desmopressin
  Storage predominant     -> antimuscarinic or beta3-agonist
  Prostate >40 mL         -> 5ARI (5-alpha-reductase inhibitor)
                             +/- alpha1-blocker
  Others                  -> alpha1-blocker or PDE5 inhibitor
  Residual storage        -> add antimuscarinic or beta3-agonist
Failure or complication   -> surgery
```
- **Watchful waiting:** education, fluid 1.5–2 L/day (none 2 h before bed), less caffeine and alcohol, double voiding, bladder training; yearly review.

| Drug | Example | Mechanism and notes |
|---|---|---|
| α1-blocker | Tamsulosin 0.4 mg, silodosin 8 mg, alfuzosin 10 mg | Relax prostatic smooth muscle; acts in days; dizziness, ejaculatory dysfunction, IFIS (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome) |
| 5ARI | Finasteride 5 mg, dutasteride 0.5 mg | Lower DHT; volume falls about 20% over 6–12 months; **PSA halves**; reduces AUR and surgery |
| Antimuscarinic | Solifenacin, tolterodine | Block detrusor M3 receptors; for storage symptoms if PVR <200 mL |
| β3-agonist | Mirabegron 50 mg, vibegron | Relax detrusor during storage |
| PDE5I (phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitor) | Tadalafil 5 mg daily | Smooth-muscle relaxation; useful with ED (erectile dysfunction) |

- **MTOPS:** progression cut by doxazosin 39%, finasteride 34%, combination 66%; **CombAT** confirmed. Combine when volume >30–40 mL and PSA >1.5 ng/mL.

### Surgery
- **Indications:** refractory retention, recurrent UTI, bladder stones, recurrent haematuria, BPO-related renal insufficiency; failed medical therapy.

| Prostate volume | Options |
|---|---|
| <30 mL | TUIP, TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) |
| 30–80 mL | **TURP (reference standard)**, bipolar TURP, PVP (photoselective vaporisation of the prostate), enucleation, PUL, Rezum (water vapour), Aquablation |
| >80 mL | **HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation)** or thulium enucleation, open or robotic simple prostatectomy |

- Ejaculation preserved: PUL, Rezum. Anticoagulated: PVP, HoLEP. PAE (prostatic artery embolisation) in selected men.
- **TURP complications:** bleeding, **TUR syndrome** (hyponatraemia with glycine; avoided by bipolar saline), retrograde ejaculation, stricture, incontinence.
- **Also know:** iTind; female LUTS follow OAB and SUI guidelines.

**Diagram to draw:** BPH → BPE → BPO → BOO → bladder changes → LUTS.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ICS: storage, voiding, post-micturition; IPSS 0–7, 8–19, 20–35.
> - Static (bulk, capsule) plus dynamic (α1A) obstruction; size ≠ symptoms.
> - 5ARI for >40 mL; MTOPS combination cut progression 66%.
> - TURP 30–80 mL; HoLEP or simple prostatectomy for >80 mL.

## Describe neuromuscular dysfunction and management of bladder dysfunction following cervical spine surgery in young adult male
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q2

*Assumed meaning: cervical spinal cord injury (traumatic, operated, or from the surgery itself), giving a suprasacral lesion above T6.*

### Introduction
- A cervical cord lesion lies **between the PMC (pontine micturition centre) and the sacral cord**. It causes tetraplegia and the classic suprasacral pattern.
- Complete transection is rare; incomplete lesions are commonest. Manage by **urodynamics, not neurology**.

### Pathophysiology
- The PMC normally coordinates detrusor contraction with sphincter relaxation. Cutting the descending pathway leaves a **spinal reflex**.
- **Why NDO (neurogenic detrusor overactivity) appears:** unmyelinated C-fibre afferents become sensitised (NGF (nerve growth factor)), sprout and drive a new sacral reflex. Bulbospinal inhibition is lost.

| Phase | Detrusor | Outlet | Clinical |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Spinal shock** (usually 6–12 weeks; up to 1–2 years) | Areflexic, smooth bladder | Bladder neck closed; reduced closure pressure | **Retention**; overflow only |
| **Recovery (suprasacral pattern)** | **NDO**, no sensation below lesion | **Striated DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)**; lesion above T7–T8 adds **smooth sphincter dyssynergia** | Reflex leakage, incomplete emptying, high pressure |

- **Blaivas DSD types:** type 1, sphincter relaxes at peak pressure (incomplete lesions); type 2, sporadic contractions; type 3, crescendo–decrescendo obstruction (types 2–3 with complete lesions).
- **Consequences:** high storage and voiding pressure, VUR (vesicoureteric reflux) in 17–25%, hydronephrosis, stones (about 7% in 10 years), UTI (urinary tract infection). Untreated, about half of men with DSD deteriorate (worse if complete, continuous DSD).
- Upper tract risk also rises with low compliance (<12.5 mL/cm H₂O) and NDO for over a third of filling.
- **VUR:** treat the bladder first (lower the pressure); reflux surgery in a thick bladder is difficult.
- **Also know:** cervical myelopathy may instead give an underactive detrusor.

### Autonomic dysreflexia
- Occurs with lesions **above T6** (cervical 60%), after spinal shock. Bladder or bowel distension triggers unopposed spinal sympathetic outflow, as medullary inhibition is lost.
- **Features:** pounding headache, severe hypertension (systolic rise ≥20 mmHg defines it at urodynamics), bradycardia, sweating and flushing above the lesion. It can cause stroke or seizures.
- **Treatment:** sit up, **drain the bladder** (check catheter), then **nitroglycerin 2% paste** above the lesion; intravenous nitroprusside or labetalol if persistent. Prophylaxis with terazosin or prazosin. Monitor blood pressure during cystoscopy and urodynamics; use spinal or general anaesthesia.

### Evaluation
- History, examination, **hand function** (decides self-catheterisation), transfers, carers.
- Urinalysis, creatinine, **renal ultrasound**. Men with good hand function can self-catheterise; others need carers or a suprapubic catheter.
- **VUDS (videourodynamics) after spinal shock**, with blood pressure monitoring: compliance, NDO, DSD, VUR, **DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) ≥40 cm H₂O** = upper tract risk.
- AUA/SUFU (American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction) 2021: suprasacral lesions are "unknown risk" until tested; DSD or poor compliance = high risk.

### Management
**Goals:** protect kidneys, low-pressure storage, low-pressure emptying, no infection, continence, social acceptability. Start with the simplest reversible option.

- **Spinal shock:** **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)** preferred; indwelling catheter early is acceptable. Defer irreversible procedures.

| Aim | Options |
|---|---|
| **Emptying** | **CIC: gold standard**, volumes <500 mL; carer CIC in tetraplegics. **Suprapubic catheter** better than long-term urethral catheter. Credé/Valsalva not recommended |
| **Storage (lower pressure)** | **Antimuscarinics** ± mirabegron (β3 agonist); **intradetrusor onabotulinumtoxinA 200 U** if refractory (repeat when the effect wears off) |
| **Refractory small bladder** | **Augmentation ileocystoplasty** ± continent catheterisable channel (Mitrofanoff) |
| **Men unable to do CIC** | **External sphincterotomy** or intrasphincteric botulinum toxin → reflex voiding into condom catheter. High failure; DLPP ≥40 cm H₂O afterwards = failure. Urethral stents abandoned |
| **Neuromodulation** | **SARS (sacral anterior root stimulator, Brindley) + posterior sacral rhizotomy**: abolishes NDO and dysreflexia, allows voiding on demand |
| **Last resort** | Ileovesicostomy; bladder neck closure + suprapubic catheter; ileal conduit |

- **Also know:** sacral neuromodulation is used for neurogenic bladder without SCI (spinal cord injury); hydrophilic catheters delay first UTI.

### Complications and follow-up
- **UTI:** treat only symptomatic bacteriuria (5–14 days); no routine prophylaxis.
- Stones, VUR (treat the bladder first), bladder cancer (no screening cystoscopy, AUA/SUFU 2021).
- Lifelong: yearly review and renal function, upper tract imaging every 1–2 years, repeat urodynamics if anything changes.

```
Cervical SCI → spinal shock (retention): CIC
   → VUDS at 3 months: NDO + DSD
   → antimuscarinic + CIC → Botox → augmentation
   → cannot CIC: sphincterotomy + condom / SARS / ileovesicostomy
```

**Diagram to draw:** neural axis: PMC, lesion at cervical cord, sympathetic T10–L2, sacral S2–S4, with the resulting NDO + DSD.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Suprasacral lesion = NDO + striated DSD; above T7–T8 also smooth sphincter dyssynergia.
> - Autonomic dysreflexia above T6: drain bladder first, nitrates next.
> - CIC + bladder relaxation (antimuscarinic, Botox 200 U) is the mainstay.
> - DLPP ≥40 cm H₂O threatens the kidneys.

# Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Botulinum neurotoxin and its role in urology
**Asked as:**
- Botulinim neurotoxin. — *Summer 2016 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Botulinum Toxin and its role in Urology — *Winter 2025 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3b)

*Assumed meaning: botulinum neurotoxin (spelling error in the question).*

### Introduction
- Toxin of *Clostridium botulinum* (neurotoxin); serotypes A–G. Urology uses **onabotulinumtoxinA (BoNT-A)**; type B is short-acting.
- 150 kDa: heavy chain (100 kDa) plus zinc-dependent light chain (50 kDa).

### Mechanism
1. Heavy chain binds the cholinergic nerve terminal; the toxin is internalised.
2. Light chain enters the cytosol.
3. It **cleaves SNAP-25 (synaptosomal-associated protein 25)**, a SNARE (vesicle-docking) protein, so vesicles cannot fuse and **acetylcholine release stops**.
- **Afferent action**: less ATP (adenosine triphosphate), substance P and CGRP (calcitonin gene-related peptide) release; fewer TRPV1 (capsaicin) and P2X3 (purinergic) receptors on C fibres, so urgency falls too.
- Effect wears off (wanes) as nerve terminals sprout (about 6–9 months).

### Urological uses
| Indication | Dose and evidence |
|---|---|
| **Idiopathic OAB (overactive bladder)**, third line | **100 U** (EMBARK); incontinence down about 59%, about 25% dry. **ABC** trial: equal to anticholinergic (antimuscarinic), more complete dryness. **ROSETTA**: 200 U equal (comparable) to SNM (sacral neuromodulation), more UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| **NDO (neurogenic detrusor overactivity)**: SCI (spinal cord injury), MS (multiple sclerosis) | **200 U** (DIGNITY trials): less incontinence, lower detrusor pressure, larger capacity |
| Children with NDO (**paediatric NDO**, spina bifida) | About **10 U/kg (maximum 360 U)** in Campbell series; FDA (Food and Drug Administration) label (age ≥5) 6 U/kg, maximum 200 U |
| **DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)** | Intrasphincteric injection in men unable to do CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation) |
| Others: **IC/BPS (interstitial cystitis/bladder pain syndrome)** | 100 U, often trigonal; AUA (American Urological Association) option, off-label |
| **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)** | Intraprostatic (prostate injection for benign enlargement): failed versus sham; placebo-controlled RCTs (randomised controlled trials) showed **no benefit**; not recommended |
- **Also know:** pelvic floor spasm.

### Technique
- Flexible or rigid cystoscopy under local anaesthesia; diluted toxin injected into the detrusor (cystoscopic detrusor injection) at **about 20 sites (100 U) or 30 sites (200 U)**, sparing the trigone (reflux concern).
- Sterile urine, antibiotic cover; check PVR (post-void residual) at 2 weeks.

### Side effects (complications)
- **Retention needing CISC**: 0–12% in OAB trials, but about 20% of women and up to 35% of men in practice; higher (more) in NDO. Each 10 mL rise in baseline PVR raises risk (about) 27%. **Patients must accept CISC beforehand** (patient must accept CISC beforehand).
- **UTI** about 20%; haematuria; rarely generalised weakness.
- Contraindications: active UTI, myasthenia gravis, pregnancy, unwillingness to self-catheterise.
- Repeat injections when the effect fades; response is maintained.

**Diagram to draw:** cholinergic synapse showing SNAP-25 cleavage by the light chain.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cleaves SNAP-25; acts on efferent and afferent nerves (efferent and afferent action).
> - 100 U idiopathic OAB; 200 U NDO; spare trigone.
> - Children: 10 U/kg (max 360 U) in series; FDA label 6 U/kg (max 200 U).
> - Main risks: retention needing CISC, UTI.

## Uro Dynamic Studies.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q3d

### Definition and principles
- UDS (urodynamic studies) test bladder and urethral function in storage and voiding.
- ICS (International Continence Society): **decide the question first**, **reproduce the symptom**; a normal study does not exclude disease.
- Before invasive UDS: symptom score, 3-day **frequency–volume chart**, free uroflow and PVR (post-void residual); treat UTI (urinary tract infection) first.

### Components
| Test | Method | Main values |
|---|---|---|
| **Uroflowmetry** | Voided volume ≥150 mL | Bell curve normal; Qmax <10 mL/s (men) suggests obstruction or weak detrusor; plateau = stricture |
| **PVR** | Bladder scan or catheter | Emptying |
| **Filling cystometry** | Bladder and rectal lines, fill with saline 30–100 mL/min (adults); **Pdet = Pves − Pabd**; zero at atmosphere, transducer at symphysis | Sensation, capacity, **DO (detrusor overactivity)**, **compliance** = ΔV/ΔPdet (<20 mL/cmH₂O impaired) |
| **Leak point pressures** | ALPP (abdominal LPP): cough or Valsalva at 150 mL | Low ALPP = weak sphincter (ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency)) |
| | DLPP (detrusor LPP) | **>40 cmH₂O** risks upper tracts (McGuire) |
| **Pressure–flow study** | Void with lines in | High pressure–low flow = obstruction; low–low = underactivity |
| **EMG (electromyography)** | Perineal surface electrodes | DESD (detrusor–external sphincter dyssynergia) |
| **UPP (urethral pressure profile)** | Catheter withdrawn | Limited value |
| **Videourodynamics** | Above with contrast and fluoroscopy | VUR (vesicoureteric reflux), bladder neck, site of obstruction |

### Indices (men)
- **BOOI (bladder outlet obstruction index)** = PdetQmax − 2Qmax: **>40 obstructed**, 20–40 equivocal, <20 unobstructed.
- **BCI (bladder contractility index)** = PdetQmax + 5Qmax: **>150 strong**, 100–150 normal, **<100 weak**.
- Women: no validated nomogram (Nitti video criteria).

### Indications
- Male LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) before surgery when unclear (young men, equivocal flow, failed TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate)); complex, mixed or recurrent incontinence.
- **Neurogenic bladder** (spinal injury, MS (multiple sclerosis), spina bifida), radiation cystitis, before augmentation.
- Children: neurogenic bladder, PUV (posterior urethral valves); fill at **5–10% of expected capacity per minute**; expected capacity = (age + 2) × 30 mL.

### Urodynamic risk factors
- Impaired compliance, DESD, DISD (detrusor–internal sphincter dyssynergia), high-pressure DO throughout filling.

**Also know:** ambulatory UDS; bethanechol test; antibiotic prophylaxis for risk groups.

**Diagram to draw:** normal trace: Pves, Pabd, Pdet, flow, EMG.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Pdet = Pves − Pabd.
> - BOOI >40 obstructed; BCI <100 weak.
> - DLPP >40 cmH₂O: upper-tract risk.
> - Ask a question first; reproduce the symptom.

## Uroflowmetry.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3f

### Definition
- A non-invasive urodynamic test that records the **volume of urine voided per unit time** (mL/s). It reflects detrusor contraction and outlet resistance together.

### Method
- Patient comes with a **comfortably full bladder** (normal desire) and voids in private, in the usual position.
- **Flowmeters:** weight transducer (load cell), rotating disc, capacitance dipstick.
- **Voided volume ≥150 mL** (Qmax is unreliable below 125–150 mL). Repeat 2–3 times; ask whether the void was typical.
- Measure **PVR** (postvoid residual) by ultrasound afterwards.

### Parameters
| Parameter | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Qmax** (maximum flow rate) | Most useful value; normal men >15 mL/s, women >20 mL/s |
| Qave (average flow rate) | Volume ÷ flow time; less useful |
| Voided volume (VV) | Must be ≥150 mL |
| Flow time / voiding time | Voiding time includes interruptions |
| Time to Qmax | Normally in the first third of the void |
| Voiding efficiency | VV ÷ (VV + PVR) × 100 |

### Curve patterns
| Pattern | Suggests |
|---|---|
| **Bell-shaped** | Normal |
| **Plateau (flattened, box)** | Fixed obstruction: **urethral stricture** |
| Prolonged, low, skewed | BPO (benign prostatic obstruction) or detrusor underactivity |
| **Intermittent / straining** | Detrusor underactivity, abdominal straining or obstruction |
| Staccato | Dysfunctional voiding (sphincter overactivity) |
| Tower (high, short) | Overactive bladder |

### Interpretation
- **Qmax <10 mL/s:** obstruction likely (high specificity). **Qmax <15 mL/s:** more sensitive, less specific.
- **Nomograms** correct for volume: **Siroky** (men), **Liverpool** (men and women, centiles). Corrected Qmax = Qmax ÷ √VV.
- **Artefacts:** "wag" (moving stream), knocking the flowmeter, straining spikes. Read Qmax from the smoothed curve, not single spikes.

### Limitations
- **Cannot separate BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) from detrusor underactivity**; only a pressure–flow study can.
- Varies with volume, position, anxiety and between days.

### Uses
- BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) evaluation before treatment and follow-up after medical or surgical treatment.
- Follow-up after **urethroplasty** or optical urethrotomy.
- Children: dysfunctional voiding, after posterior urethral valve ablation.
- Women: voiding dysfunction before stress incontinence surgery.
- **EAU** (European Association of Urology): before surgery, pressure–flow study if Qmax >10 mL/s.

**Diagram to draw:** flow curves: normal bell, plateau (stricture), prolonged low (BPO), intermittent.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Voided volume ≥150 mL; Qmax is the key value.
> - Plateau = stricture; intermittent = underactivity or straining.
> - Qmax <10 mL/s suggests BOO but cannot exclude underactivity; Siroky and Liverpool nomograms.

## Management of detrusor underactivity
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3d

### Definition
- **DUA (detrusor underactivity), ICS (International Continence Society) 2002:** "a contraction of reduced strength and/or duration, resulting in prolonged bladder emptying and/or failure to achieve complete bladder emptying within a normal time span." **Acontractile detrusor:** no contraction.
- **UAB (underactive bladder):** symptoms of slow stream, hesitancy and straining, with or without incomplete emptying. Found in 40–48% of men over 70 having urodynamics.

### Causes
- **Neurogenic:** cauda equina, disc prolapse, spinal injury, diabetes, multiple system atrophy, early stroke, radical pelvic surgery.
- **Myogenic:** long-standing BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), diabetes, ischaemia, ageing.
- **Drugs:** anticholinergics, opioids. **Functional:** Fowler syndrome, dysfunctional voiding.

### Evaluation
- Bladder diary, flow rate, PVR (post-void residual), sacral neurological examination, spinal MRI if indicated, drug review.
- **Pressure-flow study** is the only accepted test. **BCI (bladder contractility index) = Pdet@Qmax (detrusor pressure at maximum flow) + 5 × Qmax:** <100 weak, 100–150 normal, >150 strong. Low Pdet with low Qmax.
- Ambulatory urodynamics if no contraction on standard study (the "bashful bladder").

### Management
- **Goals:** protect kidneys, prevent UTI (urinary tract infection) and stones, improve symptoms.

| Option | Details |
|---|---|
| Conservative | Timed and double voiding; treat constipation; stop causative drugs |
| **CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation)** | **Mainstay** for high PVR; fewer infections than indwelling catheter. Suprapubic catheter if CISC impossible |
| α-blockers | Lower outlet resistance; modest benefit |
| Parasympathomimetics (bethanechol, distigmine) | **Weak evidence**, systemic side effects (bronchospasm, rarely cardiac arrest); not routine |
| **SNM (sacral neuromodulation)** | For non-obstructive retention, especially Fowler syndrome; needs some detrusor function |
| Sacral anterior root stimulator (Brindley) | Complete spinal cord injury, with posterior rhizotomy |
| Intrasphincteric botulinum toxin | Experimental |
| Outlet surgery (TURP, transurethral resection of the prostate) | Little help in low-PVR men; may free catheter-dependent fit men |
| Diversion | Mitrofanoff when urethral CISC fails; rarely ileal conduit |

- Avoid Credé or Valsalva voiding (reflux risk).

### Investigational
- Intravesical electrotherapy, intravesical prostaglandins, TAC-302, latissimus dorsi detrusor myoplasty, muscle-derived stem cells.

**Diagram to draw:** pressure-flow plot on the Schäfer nomogram with BCI lines at 100 and 150.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - BCI = Pdet@Qmax + 5Qmax; <100 weak.
> - CISC is the mainstay; no effective drug.
> - SNM helps non-obstructive retention; intact sensation predicts response.

## Neuromodulation
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3b
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

*Taken as neuromodulation for lower urinary tract dysfunction.*

### Definition and mechanism
- Electrical stimulation of a peripheral nerve that changes central reflex pathways controlling the bladder. It acts on **afferents**, not directly on muscle.
- **OAB (overactive bladder):** afferent input damps PMC (pontine micturition centre) firing, so fewer involuntary contractions.
- **Non-obstructive retention:** restores awareness of filling and relaxes the external sphincter. Hence one therapy treats opposite disorders.
- Exclude anatomical causes first (obstruction, stones, cancer).

### Modalities
| Modality | Method | Main use |
|---|---|---|
| **SNM (sacral neuromodulation)** | Tined lead in **S3** foramen + implanted generator | Refractory OAB, NOUR (non-obstructive urinary retention), faecal incontinence |
| **PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation)** | 34-gauge needle above medial malleolus | Refractory OAB |
| Implantable tibial devices | Small device over tibial nerve | OAB without clinic visits |
| Pudendal stimulation | Lead at Alcock's canal | SNM failure (off-label) |
| **SARS (sacral anterior root stimulator)** | Anterior S2–S4 roots + dorsal rhizotomy | Complete spinal cord injury |

### Sacral neuromodulation
- **Third-line** for OAB (AUA, American Urological Association; EAU, European Association of Urology).
- **Test phase:** PNE (percutaneous nerve evaluation, 5–7 days) or staged tined lead (up to 4 weeks). **Implant if ≥50% improvement** in diary symptoms.
- **S3 response:** perineal "bellows" plus great-toe flexion.
- **Evidence:** InSite: success 61% vs 42% with medical therapy. ROSETTA: onabotulinumtoxinA 200 U slightly better for UUI (urgency urinary incontinence) but more UTI (urinary tract infection) and self-catheterisation.
- **Problems:** pain, lead migration, loss of effect; revision >30% at 5 years; cost. Newer rechargeable, MRI-conditional devices.

### Tibial nerve stimulation
- Tibial nerve carries **S2–S3** fibres. Needle 3–4 cm above medial malleolus; toe flexion confirms placement; **30 min weekly × 12**, then maintenance.
- **SUmiT:** response 55% vs 21% with sham.

- **Also know:** IVES (intravesical electrical stimulation) in children; dorsal genital nerve stimulation.

**Diagram to draw:** S3 lead with buttock generator, and PTNS needle above the medial malleolus.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Afferent modulation explains benefit in both OAB and retention.
> - S3: bellows + great-toe flexion; implant if ≥50% improvement.
> - PTNS: 12 weekly sessions; SUmiT 55% vs 21%.

## Bladder dysfunction non genitourinary systemic disease
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q3a

*Taken as lower urinary tract dysfunction caused by systemic (non-genitourinary) diseases.*

### Principle
- The **site of the lesion**, not the disease name, predicts the bladder:
  - Brain above the pons: **DO (detrusor overactivity), no DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia)**.
  - Spinal cord: DO **with DSD**.
  - Peripheral nerves or roots: **areflexia, reduced sensation, high PVR (post-void residual)**.
- **Urodynamics** before treatment, because symptoms do not predict findings.

### Diabetes mellitus (most important)
- **Diabetic cystopathy:** sensory then motor autonomic neuropathy. Impaired sensation leads to large capacity, poor contractility, low flow and later high PVR and UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **DO is in fact the commonest urodynamic finding** (early disease or coexisting cerebrovascular disease); hypocontractility comes late.
- Mechanisms: glycation end products, sorbitol pathway, oxidative stress, polyuria.
- Differentiate from BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) by pressure–flow study. Treatment: **timed voiding**, CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation), cautious antimuscarinics.

### Other systemic diseases
| Group | Disease | Usual finding |
|---|---|---|
| Neurological | Stroke | Acute retention, then DO; no true DSD |
| | Parkinson disease | DO; pseudodyssynergia; TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) possible after excluding MSA (multiple system atrophy) |
| | Multiple sclerosis | DO about 38%, DO with DSD 29%, areflexia 26%; changes over time |
| Infective | HIV/AIDS | DO, DSD; areflexia with CMV (cytomegalovirus) |
| | Herpes zoster/simplex, Guillain–Barré | Retention, areflexia; reversible |
| | Spinal tuberculosis, Lyme, HTLV-1 | DO with DSD or areflexia |
| Autoimmune and connective tissue | SLE (systemic lupus erythematosus) | DO; lupus cystitis |
| | Systemic sclerosis | Areflexia, low compliance |
| Infiltrative | Amyloidosis (transthyretin) | Low sensation, underactivity, SUI (stress urinary incontinence) |
| Nutritional | B12 deficiency, alcohol (thiamine) | Mixed; reversible with replacement |
| Endocrine | Hyperthyroidism | Poor emptying; resolves with treatment |

**Also know:** myasthenia gravis; schizophrenia; gastroparesis.

### Management principles
- Treat the disease; reversible therapy.
- Storage: antimuscarinics (trospium, or mirabegron in elderly brains), botulinum toxin, neuromodulation.
- Emptying: CIC; avoid Credé with DSD.

**Diagram to draw:** neuraxis with lesion levels and the bladder pattern at each.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lesion level predicts pattern: brain DO; cord DO + DSD; peripheral areflexia.
> - Diabetic cystopathy: low sensation, large capacity, high PVR; DO common early.
> - Urodynamics before treating; reversible treatment first.

## Overactive bladder and its drug therapy
**Asked as:**
- Overactive bladder — *Practice Set 15 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*
- Drug therapy in OAB — *Practice Set 17 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(OAB also answered in Practice Set 4 Paper 1, Q2; detrusor relaxants (drug therapy) also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 1, Q3e)

### Definition
- **OAB (overactive bladder), ICS (International Continence Society):** **urgency**, usually with frequency and nocturia, with or without UUI (urgency urinary incontinence), in the absence of UTI (urinary tract infection) or other obvious pathology.
- **OAB dry** vs **wet** (with UUI). OAB is a symptom diagnosis; DO (detrusor overactivity) is urodynamic, found in only 44–69% with urgency.

### Pathophysiology
- **Afferent:** silent C fibres become active and hypersensitive.
- **Urothelial:** urothelium releases ATP (adenosine triphosphate) and acetylcholine onto suburothelial afferents.
- **Myogenic:** patchy denervation → spontaneous micromotions that spread.
- **Central:** loss of forebrain inhibition of the pontine micturition centre.
- **Associations:** ageing, BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), metabolic syndrome.

### Evaluation
- **Minimum:** history, examination, **urinalysis**. Bladder diary (3 days), questionnaires, PVR (post-void residual) when indicated.
- Urodynamics and cystoscopy only for complicated or refractory cases.

### Management (AUA/SUFU [American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction]; not a compulsory sequence)

| Line | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Behavioural | Education; reduce fluids by about 25%; cut caffeine; weight loss; **bladder training** (timed voids, interval increased by 15–30 minutes); **PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training)** with urge suppression: 50–80% fewer leaks |
| Drugs | **Antimuscarinics** (oxybutynin, solifenacin, extended-release trospium, darifenacin, fesoterodine); **β3-agonists** (mirabegron, vibegron); combinations |
| Third line | **PTNS (percutaneous tibial nerve stimulation)** weekly × 12; **onabotulinumtoxinA 100 U** intradetrusor (about 25% dry; UTI about 20%; retention); **SNM (sacral neuromodulation)** at S3 (success about 70–80%; revision >30% at 5 years) |
| Last resort | Augmentation cystoplasty or urinary diversion |

- **Trials:** ROSETTA (Botox beat SNM at 6 months, more UTI); SUmiT (PTNS 55% vs sham 21%).

### Drug therapy: place and mechanisms
- In OAB (overactive bladder), drugs are second line, after behavioural therapy. Two main classes: **antimuscarinics** and **β3-adrenoceptor agonists**.
- **Antimuscarinics:** acetylcholine acts on **M3** receptors (raising intracellular calcium) to contract the detrusor; the commoner M2 enhances it. Blockade reduces involuntary contractions and afferent signalling.
- **β3 agonists:** activation raises **cAMP (cyclic adenosine monophosphate)** and relaxes the detrusor during storage.

#### Agents
| Drug | Feature | Usual dose |
|---|---|---|
| Oxybutynin | Non-selective; most dry mouth; patch, gel | 5 mg two to three times daily; ER (extended-release) |
| Tolterodine | Bladder-selective; QT prolongation at high dose | ER 4 mg daily |
| Fesoterodine | Prodrug of tolterodine's active metabolite | 4–8 mg daily |
| Solifenacin | M3-selective; long half-life | 5–10 mg daily |
| Darifenacin | About 30-fold M3-selective | 7.5–15 mg daily |
| Trospium | Quaternary; little brain entry | 20 mg twice daily |
| Mirabegron (β3) | Inhibits CYP2D6 (a liver enzyme): metoprolol interaction; small blood-pressure rise | 25–50 mg daily |
| Vibegron (β3) | No CYP2D6 interaction | 75 mg daily |

#### Comparison
| Feature | Antimuscarinics | β3 agonists |
|---|---|---|
| Efficacy | Similar | Similar |
| Side effects | Dry mouth, constipation, blurred vision | Near placebo |
| Cognition | **Dementia risk 10–65% higher with use >3 months** (antimuscarinic use >3 months raises dementia risk) | No anticholinergic burden |
| Contraindications | Retention, uncontrolled narrow-angle glaucoma, gastric retention | Uncontrolled severe hypertension |

- **Choice:** elderly → prefer β3 agonist (β3-agonists in the elderly), or ER trospium, darifenacin or fesoterodine. ER forms cause less dry mouth. Poor persistence is the main problem; switch dose or drug before third-line therapy.

#### Combination and special groups
- **Solifenacin + mirabegron** beats monotherapy (SYNERGY; BESIDE in non-responders).
- **Men:** α-blocker first; add an antimuscarinic (if PVR is low) or β3 agonist to an α-blocker; retention up to 3%, so check PVR (post-void residual).
- **Also know:** vaginal oestrogen in atrophy; desmopressin for nocturnal polyuria; imipramine (children, mixed incontinence); intravesical oxybutynin; third line onabotulinumtoxinA 100 U and neuromodulation.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder showing urothelial afferents and C fibres with brainstem and cortical inhibition; detrusor cell: acetylcholine → M3 → contraction; noradrenaline → β3 → cAMP → relaxation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - OAB is defined by urgency; DO is urodynamic.
> - Behavioural therapy first; antimuscarinic or β3-agonist next.
> - M3 mediates contraction; β3 raises cAMP to relax.
> - Equal efficacy; β3 avoids dry mouth and dementia risk.
> - Long-term antimuscarinics → dementia risk.
> - Solifenacin + mirabegron combination works (SYNERGY).
> - Botox 100 U, PTNS, SNM for refractory OAB.

## Vesicourodynamics
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 16 Paper 2, Q3a
(also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3d)

*Assumed meaning: videourodynamics (VUDS), i.e. urodynamics with simultaneous fluoroscopic imaging of the bladder and urethra.*

### Definition
- **VUDS:** multichannel urodynamics (pressure–flow ± EMG [electromyography]) recorded with fluoroscopy, using **contrast as the filling fluid**.

### Set-up
- Vesical and rectal catheters (Pdet [detrusor pressure] = Pves − Pabd), flowmeter, EMG; C-arm or tilting table.
- SUI (stress urinary incontinence) views standing; void in the usual position. **Screen only at key moments** (provocation, pressure rise, voiding).

### What fluoroscopy adds
| Situation | VUDS finding |
|---|---|
| NLUTD (neurogenic lower urinary tract dysfunction) | **Gold standard**; DESD (detrusor–external sphincter dyssynergia) and **DISD** (detrusor–internal sphincter dyssynergia, seen only on VUDS); trabeculation, diverticula |
| **VUR** (vesicoureteric reflux) | Pressure and volume at onset, grade; reflux "pops off" pressure, so compliance looks falsely safe |
| **Bladder neck** | Only way to assess it; **PBNO** (primary bladder neck obstruction) |
| Female obstruction | **Nitti criteria:** radiographic narrowing between bladder neck and meatus with a sustained detrusor contraction |
| Dysfunctional voiding | "Spinning-top" urethra at the external sphincter |
| SUI | Leakage with ALPP (abdominal leak point pressure); open bladder neck at rest suggests ISD (intrinsic sphincter deficiency) |
| Male obstruction | Localises prostatic obstruction, bladder neck contracture or stricture |

**PBNO in young men:** type I high pressure, low flow; type II normal pressure, low flow, narrow bladder neck; type III delayed bladder-neck opening.

### Indications
- Known or suspected NLUTD; unexplained retention in women; young men with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms); complex incontinence; prior radical pelvic surgery or radiation; diversion; renal transplant; children with PUV (posterior urethral valves) or dysraphism.
- Shows risk factors: poor compliance, DLPP (detrusor leak point pressure) >40 cm H₂O, DESD.

### Limitations
- Radiation, cost, space, contrast allergy. EAU (European Association of Urology): optimum test in NLUTD; AUA (American Urological Association): lowest dose that answers the question.

**Diagram to draw:** VUDS trace (Pves, Pabd, Pdet, flow, EMG) beside a voiding cystourethrogram image.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - VUDS = urodynamics + fluoroscopy with contrast.
> - Only test for bladder neck dysfunction, PBNO and DISD.
> - Reflux pop-off makes compliance look falsely good.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 7 · Voiding Dysfunction

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Pathophysiology, classification and management of LUTS. (LAQ)

- ICS: storage, voiding, post-micturition; IPSS 0–7, 8–19, 20–35.
- Static (bulk, capsule) plus dynamic (α1A) obstruction; size ≠ symptoms.
- 5ARI for >40 mL; MTOPS combination cut progression 66%.
- TURP 30–80 mL; HoLEP or simple prostatectomy for >80 mL.

#### Describe neuromuscular dysfunction and management of bladder dysfunction following cervical spine surgery in young adult male (LAQ)

- Suprasacral lesion = NDO + striated DSD; above T7–T8 also smooth sphincter dyssynergia.
- Autonomic dysreflexia above T6: drain bladder first, nitrates next.
- CIC + bladder relaxation (antimuscarinic, Botox 200 U) is the mainstay.
- DLPP ≥40 cm H₂O threatens the kidneys.

#### Botulinum neurotoxin and its role in urology (SAQ)

- Cleaves SNAP-25; acts on efferent and afferent nerves (efferent and afferent action).
- 100 U idiopathic OAB; 200 U NDO; spare trigone.
- Children: 10 U/kg (max 360 U) in series; FDA label 6 U/kg (max 200 U).
- Main risks: retention needing CISC, UTI.

#### Uro Dynamic Studies. (SAQ)

- Pdet = Pves − Pabd.
- BOOI >40 obstructed; BCI <100 weak.
- DLPP >40 cmH₂O: upper-tract risk.
- Ask a question first; reproduce the symptom.

#### Uroflowmetry. (SAQ)

- Voided volume ≥150 mL; Qmax is the key value.
- Plateau = stricture; intermittent = underactivity or straining.
- Qmax <10 mL/s suggests BOO but cannot exclude underactivity; Siroky and Liverpool nomograms.

#### Management of detrusor underactivity (SAQ)

- BCI = Pdet@Qmax + 5Qmax; <100 weak.
- CISC is the mainstay; no effective drug.
- SNM helps non-obstructive retention; intact sensation predicts response.

#### Neuromodulation (SAQ)

- Afferent modulation explains benefit in both OAB and retention.
- S3: bellows + great-toe flexion; implant if ≥50% improvement.
- PTNS: 12 weekly sessions; SUmiT 55% vs 21%.

#### Bladder dysfunction non genitourinary systemic disease (SAQ)

- Lesion level predicts pattern: brain DO; cord DO + DSD; peripheral areflexia.
- Diabetic cystopathy: low sensation, large capacity, high PVR; DO common early.
- Urodynamics before treating; reversible treatment first.

#### Overactive bladder and its drug therapy (SAQ)

- OAB is defined by urgency; DO is urodynamic.
- Behavioural therapy first; antimuscarinic or β3-agonist next.
- M3 mediates contraction; β3 raises cAMP to relax.
- Equal efficacy; β3 avoids dry mouth and dementia risk.
- Long-term antimuscarinics → dementia risk.
- Solifenacin + mirabegron combination works (SYNERGY).
- Botox 100 U, PTNS, SNM for refractory OAB.

#### Vesicourodynamics (SAQ)

- VUDS = urodynamics + fluoroscopy with contrast.
- Only test for bladder neck dysfunction, PBNO and DISD.
- Reflux pop-off makes compliance look falsely good.

# Section 8 · Nocturia & Male Incontinence — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Post-prostatectomy incontinence
**Asked as:**
- Post prostatectomy incontinence. — *Summer 2021 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*
- Prostatectomy incontinence — *Practice Set 11 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 11 Paper II Q3f: also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3a)

### Definition and incidence
- PPI (post-prostatectomy incontinence) is mainly **SUI (stress urinary incontinence)** after RP (radical prostatectomy), the commonest cause of male SUI. Rates vary with definition ("no pad" vs "safety pad") and reporting; expert no-pad continence is in the mid-90s % at 1 year; continence improves for up to 2 years.

### Pathophysiology
- RP removes the internal sphincter (bladder neck, prostatic urethra), so continence depends (entirely) on the external **rhabdosphincter**.
- Causes: apical injury to rhabdosphincter or pudendal branches (sphincter or nerve injury at the apex), short membranous urethra, afferent denervation; also detrusor overactivity, low compliance, **VUAS (vesicourethral anastomotic stenosis)**.
- Nerve sparing and posterior reconstruction (Rocco stitch) give **earlier, not better final** continence.

### Evaluation
- History (type, severity, radiotherapy, cancer status), bladder diary, **24-hour pad weight**: mild <200 g, moderate 200–400 g, severe >400 g (another source: mild <150 g, moderate <400 g, severe more; thresholds vary).
- **Standing cough test**, grade (grades) 0 to 4 (0–4: leak reported but not seen; delayed drops; early drops; delayed stream; early persistent stream).
- Urinalysis, creatinine, PVR (post-void residual), PSA (prostate-specific antigen). **Cystoscopy** excludes VUAS and judges (shows) residual sphincter coaptation; urodynamics when needed (if mixed symptoms).

### Management
1. **Wait 6–12 months** (improves up to 2 years). Supervised **PFMT (pelvic floor muscle training)** with biofeedback, ideally preoperative (started before surgery); pads, condom catheter or (penile) clamp.
2. **Duloxetine** (off-label). **Bulking agents are not advised.**
3. **Surgery** (AUA/SUFU [American Urological Association/Society of Urodynamics, Female Pelvic Medicine and Urogenital Reconstruction] 2019, EAU [European Association of Urology]):

| Situation | Choice |
|---|---|
| Mild–moderate, no radiotherapy | **Male sling**: AdVance / AdVance XP (repositions the bulbar urethra; needs a coapting sphincter) or adjustable systems (ATOMS [adjustable transobturator male system]) |
| Severe, or any radiotherapy | **AUS (artificial urinary sphincter), AMS 800**: gold standard; cuff pressure 61–70 cm H₂O |
| ProACT balloons | Expert centres; avoid after radiotherapy |

- **MASTER trial:** male sling non-inferior to AUS at 12 months.
- AUS: social continence (0–1 pad) 55–77%; revision 10–29% at 3 years; lasts (lifespan) 8–10 years. Erosion risks: radiotherapy (risk higher after radiotherapy), prior erosion, urethroplasty (use a **transcorporal cuff** for fragile urethra).
- **Also know:** climacturia; treat VUAS first.

**Diagram to draw:** AMS 800 with cuff, scrotal pump and pressure-regulating balloon.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Wait 6–12 months with PFMT before surgery.
> - Sling for mild–moderate; AUS for severe or irradiated.
> - MASTER: sling non-inferior to AUS at 12 months.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 8 · Nocturia & Male Incontinence

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Post-prostatectomy incontinence (SAQ)

- Wait 6–12 months with PFMT before surgery.
- Sling for mild–moderate; AUS for severe or irradiated.
- MASTER: sling non-inferior to AUS at 12 months.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Surgical management of erectile dysfunction and penile prosthesis
**Asked as:**
- Discuss surgical management of erectile dysfunction. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Penile Prosthesis. — *Summer 2018 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Describe various penile prosthesis in ED — *Practice Set 15 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3c and 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3d; surgery for ED in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

### Introduction
- Surgery for ED (erectile dysfunction) means **penile prosthesis** (main operation) and, rarely, **penile vascular surgery**.
- Prosthesis: most effective, but last-line, treatment of ED; **satisfaction 80–90%**. First devices: Scott inflatable (1972), Small–Carrion rod (1978).
- Lue's goal-directed approach: surgery is for men who fail, cannot use or decline oral PDE5Is (phosphodiesterase type 5 inhibitors), intracavernosal injection, intraurethral alprostadil or a VED (vacuum erection device).
- **Indications:** failed or unacceptable medical therapy (ED not responding to, contraindicated for (contraindication to), or unacceptable with (refusal of) oral PDE5Is, injections or vacuum devices; common after prostatectomy, pelvic radiotherapy, diabetes); cavernosal (corporal) fibrosis (after ischaemic priapism, infection or implant removal; early implant preserves length); Peyronie disease (Peyronie's disease) with ED; penile or pelvic trauma.
- Detailed vascular testing is needed only before vascular surgery.

### A. Penile vascular surgery
| | Arterial revascularisation | Venous surgery |
|---|---|---|
| Defect | Arteriogenic (poor inflow) | Veno-occlusive (venous leak) |
| Work-up | Duplex; selective pudendal angiography (also checks the inferior epigastric donor) | DICC (dynamic infusion cavernosometry and cavernosography) locates the leak |
| Technique | Inferior epigastric artery to dorsal artery (revascularisation) or to deep dorsal vein (arterialisation) | Ligation or embolisation of deep dorsal, superficial dorsal or crural veins; crural plication |
| Selection | Age <55, non-smoker, non-diabetic, no venous leak, focal internal pudendal stenosis; best in young men after pelvic or perineal trauma | Not defined |
| Status | Strictly selected men | Investigational |
| Complications | Glans hyperaemia (commonest), shunt thrombosis, inguinal hernia | Glans numbness, shortening, curvature, skin necrosis |

### B. Penile prosthesis
**Types**
| Type | Devices / Example | Features | Best for |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Malleable (semirigid) rods** | Tactra (nitinol core), Genesis (braided silver), Rigi 10 (stainless steel) | Two bendable rods; simple, cheap; almost never fail (mechanical failure exceedingly rare); always (permanently) firm; erode more in insensate men | Poor manual dexterity (suited to poor dexterity); cost |
| Mechanical rods | Spectra (discontinued) | Articulating segments | – |
| 2-piece (two-piece) inflatable | Ambicor (discontinued) | Cylinders + scrotal pump; no reservoir; poor flaccidity | – |
| **3-piece (three-piece) inflatable** | AMS 700 CX/LGX/CXR; Coloplast Titan, Titan Touch, Titan Narrow; Rigicon Infla 10 | Two cylinders, scrotal pump, abdominal reservoir; **best rigidity with (and) true flaccidity**; most popular | Most men |

**Three-piece inflatable details**
- **Expansion:** girth only (CX, Titan) or girth and length (LGX, up to 25%; Infla 10 AX). **Narrow** cylinders (CXR, Titan Narrow) for fibrotic corpora.
- **Material:** AMS and Rigicon use silicone–fabric–silicone layers; Titan uses polyurethane (Bioflex).
- **Coatings:** InhibiZone (rifampicin + minocycline) on AMS; hydrophilic coating on Titan, dipped in antibiotic.
- **Reservoir:** space of Retzius, or **ectopic submuscular** after pelvic surgery; lockout valve prevents autoinflation.

**Contraindications:** active skin, urinary (UTI [urinary tract infection]) or systemic infection; uncontrolled diabetes; unrealistic expectations; reversible ED. In insensate men (spinal cord injury) use an inflatable device, because rods erode.

**Consent:** the erection may be shorter (pseudocapsule scar); the aim is a firm penis for intercourse; sensation and ejaculation may reduce; infection or mechanical failure may need reoperation.

**Preoperative preparation**
- Treat phimosis and skin or fungal lesions (skin and urine infection); sterile urine; stop anticoagulants and aspirin.
- Blood glucose **<200 mg/dL on the day of surgery** (more important than HbA1c [glycated haemoglobin]).
- Shave in theatre; chlorhexidine–alcohol skin preparation (about 40% fewer wound infections than povidone-iodine); MRSA (methicillin-resistant *Staphylococcus aureus*) decolonisation if a carrier.
- IV vancomycin or a cephalosporin plus gentamicin, 1 hour before incision, for up to 24 hours.

**Operative steps (3-piece, penoscrotal approach)**
```
Catheter → upper scrotal incision → expose corpora lateral to urethra → stay sutures → corporotomy
→ dilate proximally to ischial tuberosity, distally under glans (instruments dorsolateral; 7→13 mm)
→ goalpost test for crossover → measure → cylinders equal to or 1 cm shorter → test inflation
→ close corporotomies → pump in dependent subdartos (dartos) pouch → reservoir in space of Retzius
→ connect tubing → test inflation → irrigate → close in layers
```
- Dilate with Hegar or Brooks dilators, instruments pointing dorsolaterally; check for crossover or perforation; cylinders sized to corporal length (equal to or 1 cm shorter than measured length).
- **Approaches:** penoscrotal (most used, commonest; secures the pump / pump secured; urethra at risk); infrapubic (reservoir placed under vision (direct reservoir view); dorsal nerve at risk); subcoronal or ventral penile for rods.
- Malleable rods (rods are placed slightly shorter): 0.5–1 cm shorter than the measured length; girth chosen with the two-dilator test.
- **Ectopic submuscular reservoir** after pelvic surgery or cystectomy; a lockout valve prevents autoinflation.
- **Surgical principles:** antibiotics, glucose control, no-touch technique.
- **Intraoperative problems:** crossover (redilate laterally); crural perforation (suture sling); distal urethral perforation / urethral injury (abandon that side for 5–6 months); fibrotic corpora (cavernotomes, narrow cylinders); curvature (modelling; modelling for Peyronie's disease); floppy glans (glans fixation).
- **Postoperative:** mummy wrap with glans visible; cylinders partly inflated; drain out by 24 hours; activation at 4–6 weeks.

**Complications**
- **Mechanical failure** (commonest; inflatables): repair in about 15% at 5 years, 30% at 10 years.
- **Infection (1–3%)**: biofilm on the device. Prevent with intravenous vancomycin or cephalosporin plus gentamicin (up to 24 h), chlorhexidine–alcohol preparation, **antibiotic-coated devices** (halve infection; antibiotic-coated devices halve it) and **no-touch technique** (gives <0.5%). Treatment (treat by removing): remove **all** parts (components). **Mulcahy salvage** (antiseptic washout and immediate new device / replacement) succeeds in 84%; delayed reimplantation loses length.
- Others: erosion or extrusion (catheterisation, rods in insensate men), reservoir erosion into bladder or bowel, autoinflation, penile shortening, **glans necrosis** (diabetes, smoking, radiation, previous implant), cylinder aneurysm.

**Special situations (also know):** ischaemic priapism >36 hours (early implantation preserves length); transplant recipients (no extra infection); after phalloplasty (single cylinder, high erosion).

**Outcome (outcomes):** patient and partner satisfaction **80–90%**, the highest of any ED treatment; the main complaint is shorter length.

### Recent advances
- Minimal-dilation technique; flat ectopic reservoirs; penile transplantation. Regenerative therapies (Li-ESWT [low-intensity shock wave therapy], stem cells) remain experimental.

**Diagram to draw:** 3-piece (three-piece) inflatable prosthesis: two cylinders, scrotal pump, reservoir in the space of Retzius.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prosthesis is the last line but gives 80–90% satisfaction.
> - 3-piece (three-piece) inflatable gives the best rigidity and flaccidity.
> - Malleable rods: nitinol (Tactra), silver (Genesis); almost never fail.
> - Glucose <200 mg/dL, chlorhexidine–alcohol, coated device, no-touch technique.
> - Infection 1–3%; coated devices and no-touch technique; infected implant: remove all parts; Mulcahy salvage 84%.
> - Satisfaction 80–90%; repairs 15% at 5 years.
> - Arterial revascularisation only for young men with post-traumatic focal stenosis.

## Discuss the role of urologist in reproductive endocrinology and male infertility practice.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q1

### Introduction
- **Infertility:** no conception after 12 months of regular unprotected intercourse; about **15% of couples** (WHO, World Health Organization). The male contributes about half, yet many men are never examined.
- The urologist (andrologist) has five roles: **diagnose** the male factor, **treat endocrine causes**, **correct surgical causes**, **retrieve sperm for ART (assisted reproductive technology)**, and protect **general health and future fertility**.
- Commonest diagnoses: idiopathic, **varicocele** (commonest correctable), obstruction.

### 1. Diagnostic role
- **History:** duration, coital timing (semen is best after 1–2 days' abstinence), partner's age (fecundity falls after 35), cryptorchidism, mesh herniorrhaphy, RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection), diabetes, spinal injury, smoking, cannabis, opioids, **exogenous testosterone or anabolic steroids**.
- **Examination:** androgenisation; **testis size** (reflects spermatogenic mass); epididymis; **both vasa present?**; varicocele (standing, Valsalva).
- **Semen analysis**, at least two. WHO 6th edition (2021) 5th centiles: volume 1.4 mL, concentration 16 million/mL, total count 39 million, progressive motility 30%, normal forms 4%. These describe fertile men, not fertility cut-offs.
- **Genetics:** karyotype and **Y-chromosome microdeletion** (AZF, azoospermia factor) in non-obstructive azoospermia or severe oligospermia; **CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator)** testing of both partners in CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens).
- **Imaging:** scrotal US (ultrasound); **TRUS (transrectal US)** if volume <1 mL (ejaculatory duct obstruction, EDO), after post-ejaculate urine excludes retrograde ejaculation; renal US in vasal agenesis.

### 2. Endocrine evaluation (the urologist's reproductive endocrinology)
- Spermatogenesis needs a **high intratesticular testosterone (T)**. Morning total T + SHBG (sex hormone-binding globulin) + albumin gives bioavailable T; low is below about **280–300 ng/dL**; repeat borderline values (T is pulsatile and circadian).

| Test | What it tells |
|---|---|
| LH (luteinising hormone) | Localises low T: high → testicular (Leydig) failure; low/normal → pituitary or hypothalamus |
| FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) / inhibin B | Germ-cell mass. Azoospermia with **FSH >7.6 IU/L and testis long axis ≤4.6 cm → spermatogenic failure**; FSH ≤7.6 and >4.6 cm → obstruction |
| Oestradiol (E2) | **T (ng/dL) : E2 (pg/mL) <10** → aromatase excess (obesity) |
| Prolactin | Only with low T, low libido/ED (erectile dysfunction), headache or field defect |
| TSH (thyroid-stimulating hormone), cortisol, 17-hydroxyprogesterone | Only if signs; CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia) |

### 3. Medical (endocrine) treatment
| Condition | Mechanism | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| **Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism** (Kallmann, pituitary) | Testis normal, signal missing: most treatable cause | **hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) 1500–5000 IU 2–3 times weekly**, add hMG (human menopausal gonadotropin) 75 IU or recombinant FSH; pulsatile GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) pump for hypothalamic failure. Sperm may take 1–2 years |
| **Anabolic steroids / exogenous T** | Negative feedback suppresses LH → intratesticular T falls → azoospermia | Stop; hCG ± clomiphene to speed recovery. **Never give T to a man wanting fertility** (AUA/ASRM, American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine) |
| **Hyperprolactinaemia** | Prolactin inhibits GnRH | Repeat level; pituitary MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) if high; **cabergoline** (fewer side effects than bromocriptine) |
| Partial hypogonadism, obesity | Aromatisation of T to E2 suppresses LH | Weight loss; **clomiphene 25 mg daily** (off-label), **anastrozole 1 mg daily** or letrozole if T:E2 <10 |
| **CAH** | Adrenal androgens suppress gonadotropins; testicular adrenal rest tumours | Glucocorticoids |
| Primary testicular failure (Klinefelter 47,XXY) | Germ cells absent or few | No drug restores sperm; exogenous T worsens it; **micro-TESE** (microdissection testicular sperm extraction) |

### 4. Surgical role
| Problem | Operation |
|---|---|
| Clinical varicocele + abnormal semen | **Microsurgical subinguinal varicocelectomy** or embolisation: improves semen, DNA fragmentation and pregnancy. Not for US-only varicocele |
| Vasal or epididymal obstruction | **Vasovasostomy / vasoepididymostomy** (microsurgical intussusception) |
| EDO | **TURED** (transurethral resection of ejaculatory ducts) |
