| Non-obstructive azoospermia | **Micro-TESE**, best technique; no operation for **AZFa/AZFb** deletions (no sperm found) |
| Unreconstructable obstruction | PESA/MESA (percutaneous/microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration), TESA (testicular sperm aspiration) |
| Retrograde ejaculation; anejaculation | Sympathomimetics, urine sperm retrieval; **penile vibratory stimulation**, electroejaculation |

### 5. Partner of the ART team, counselling and men's health
- Choose the least invasive route: natural conception, IUI (intrauterine insemination), IVF/ICSI (in vitro fertilisation/intracytoplasmic sperm injection). **Cryopreserve sperm** at retrieval.
- **Fertility preservation:** sperm banking before chemotherapy, radiotherapy or RPLND; **onco-TESE** if azoospermic.
- **Genetic counselling:** AZFc deletion passes to sons; CFTR risk to offspring; paternal age ≥40 years raises offspring risk.
- **Infertility is a marker of general health:** higher risk of **testis cancer** (scan every azoospermic man), later comorbidity and mortality.

**Recent advances:** sperm DNA fragmentation testing; microfluidic sperm selection; genomic panels; prepubertal testicular tissue cryopreservation (experimental).

**Also know:** antisperm antibodies; globozoospermia and ciliary dyskinesia (ICSI); androgen receptor defects (high T and LH).

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis with feedback loops (T and E2 on LH; inhibin B on FSH).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - FSH >7.6 IU/L + testis ≤4.6 cm = spermatogenic failure.
> - Exogenous testosterone is a contraceptive: never give it for infertility.
> - Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism: hCG ± FSH; most treatable cause.
> - Varicocelectomy only for palpable varicocele with abnormal semen; micro-TESE for non-obstructive azoospermia.

# Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Management of priapism
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3a

### Definition and classification
- Full or partial erection **≥4 hours** beyond, or unrelated to, sexual stimulation (AUA/SMSNA (American Urological Association/Sexual Medicine Society of North America)).
- **Ischaemic** (95%; veno-occlusive, rigid, painful, **emergency**); **non-ischaemic** (arterial fistula after trauma, partly rigid, painless); **stuttering** (recurrent ischaemic episodes, typically SCD (sickle cell disease)).
- **Causes**: ICI (intracavernosal injection), SCD (29–42% of males), α-blockers, trazodone, cocaine, leukaemia, metastases.

### Diagnosis
- **Corporal blood gas**:

| | pO2 (mmHg) | pCO2 (mmHg) | pH |
|---|---|---|---|
| Ischaemic | **<30** | **>60** | **<7.25** |
| Mixed venous (flaccid) | 40 | 50 | 7.35 |
| Non-ischaemic (arterial) | >90 | <40 | 7.40 |

- Colour Doppler if equivocal (absent flow or fistula); blood count, haemoglobin electrophoresis.

### Ischaemic priapism
```
Penile block → aspiration ± irrigation
→ Phenylephrine 100–500 µg/mL, 1 mL every 3–5 min, up to 1 h
→ Distal shunt ± tunnelling → proximal shunt
→ >36 h or refractory → immediate prosthesis
```
- Aspiration alone succeeds in under a quarter. Monitor blood pressure and pulse.
| Shunt | Method |
|---|---|
| **Winter** (distal) | Biopsy needle through glans |
| **Ebbehoj / T-shunt** (distal) | Scalpel through glans; T-shunt turns blade 90° |
| **Al-Ghorab** (distal, open) | Excise tunica tips via dorsal glans incision |
| **Snake tunnelling** | Dilator passed proximally through distal shunt |
| **Quackels** (proximal) | Cavernosum to spongiosum at the bulb |
| **Grayhack** (proximal) | Cavernosum to saphenous vein |
| Penoscrotal decompression | Proximal corporotomy, clot evacuation |
- **Duration predicts ED (erectile dysfunction)**: 0% ≤12 h, 22% at 12–24 h, 56% at 24–36 h, 100% >36 h.
- **Early prosthesis** gives less shortening than delayed insertion.
- Oral agents and hydration are adjuncts only; in SCD, treat intracavernosally with concurrent haematology care.

### Non-ischaemic priapism
- Observe (about 60% resolve); reassess at 4 weeks. Then **selective embolisation**; repeat it, rather than surgery, for recurrence.

### Stuttering priapism
- Prophylaxis: daily PDE5i (phosphodiesterase-5 inhibitor, which resets PDE5 regulation), pseudoephedrine, self-injected phenylephrine; hormonal agents (GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) agonists, antiandrogens) at the cost of libido.

**Diagram to draw:** Distal and proximal shunt sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ischaemic gas: pO2 <30, pCO2 >60, pH <7.25.
> - Distal shunt ± tunnelling before proximal; prosthesis if >36 h.
> - High-flow: observe (~60% resolve), then embolise.

## Azoospermia and non-obstructive azoospermia
**Asked as:**
- Azospermia — *Summer 2017 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- Non obstructive azoospermia — *Winter 2025 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3d)

*Assumed meaning: azoospermia.*

### Definition
- **No sperm in the ejaculate** after **centrifugation** of the sample, on **at least two** semen analyses; 10–15% of infertile men.
- **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia):** no sperm in the **centrifuged** ejaculate on two samples, due to **failure of spermatogenesis**. About 60% of azoospermic men.

### Classification
| Type | Cause | Clues |
|---|---|---|
| **Pre-testicular** (hypogonadotropic) | Kallmann syndrome, pituitary tumour, hyperprolactinaemia, **exogenous testosterone or anabolic steroids** | Low FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone), LH (luteinising hormone), testosterone |
| **Testicular (NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia), spermatogenic failure)** | Klinefelter, Y microdeletion, cryptorchidism, mumps, chemotherapy or radiation, idiopathic | Small soft testes, **FSH >7.6 IU/L**, long axis ≤4.6 cm |
| **Post-testicular (OA (obstructive azoospermia))** | CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens), epididymal obstruction, vasectomy, hernia repair, EDO (ejaculatory duct obstruction) | Normal testes, **FSH ≤7.6**, dilated epididymis |
| Ejaculatory | Retrograde ejaculation, anejaculation | Low volume; sperm in post-ejaculate urine |

#### Causes of NOA
- **Genetic:** **Klinefelter (47,XXY)**, commonest; **Y microdeletion** (AZF (azoospermia factor) a, b, c).
- **Acquired:** cryptorchidism, mumps orchitis, torsion, **chemotherapy or radiation**, trauma, varicocele.
- **Idiopathic**: commonest overall.
- *Note:* hypogonadotropic hypogonadism is pre-testicular and treated medically (hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) ± FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone)).

### Evaluation
- History (mumps, surgery, drugs, testosterone); examination (testis size, **vasa**, epididymis, varicocele).
- **Hormones**: FSH (reflects germ-cell mass), LH, testosterone, prolactin; oestradiol.
- NOA: small soft testes, palpable vasa, normal ejaculate volume. **FSH >7.6 IU/L and testis long axis ≤4.6 cm** suggest NOA.
```
Azoospermia → vasa absent? → CBAVD → CFTR testing (both partners), renal US
            → volume <1 mL? → sperm in post-ejaculate urine → retrograde
                            → none → TRUS for EDO
            → normal volume: FSH ≤7.6 + testis >4.6 cm → obstructive
                             FSH >7.6 + small testes → spermatogenic failure
```
- **Genetics** (AUA/ASRM (American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine) guideline): **karyotype** and **Y microdeletion** in NOA (**in all**, with counselling); Klinefelter (47,XXY) commonest. **AZF (azoospermia factor) a or b deletion: no sperm, do not operate**; AZFc: sperm often found. **CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator)** in CBAVD.
- **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)**: seminal vesicle >1.5 cm, midline cyst → EDO.
- **Testis biopsy**: combined with sperm extraction and freezing.

| Histology | Meaning | Sperm retrieval |
|---|---|---|
| Hypospermatogenesis | All stages, fewer cells | Best |
| Maturation arrest | Halts at one germ-cell stage | About 40% |
| SCO (Sertoli cell–only) | No germ cells | About 30% |

### Management
- **Hypogonadotropic**: stop testosterone; **hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) ± FSH**; dopamine agonist for prolactinoma.
- **Obstructive**: **vasovasostomy** or **vasoepididymostomy** (microsurgical); **TURED (transurethral resection of ejaculatory ducts)** for EDO; or sperm retrieval (MESA (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration), PESA, TESA) + ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection).
- Retrograde ejaculation: sympathomimetics, or sperm from alkalinised urine.
- **Non-obstructive**: **micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction)** + ICSI; sperm in about half. Repair clinical varicocele; hormonal optimisation (off-label). In detail:

**1. Optimisation**: stop exogenous testosterone; repair clinical varicocele (evidence weak); off-label clomiphene, anastrozole or hCG for low testosterone.

**2. Micro-TESE (microdissection testicular sperm extraction)**
- **Principle:** spermatogenesis in NOA is **focal**. At ×20–25 magnification, **larger, opaque tubules** are taken; little tissue removed, blood supply spared.
- Sperm found in **about 50%**; better than conventional TESE.

| Predictor | Retrieval (%) |
|---|---|
| Post-orchidopexy | 74 |
| AZFc deletion | 70 |
| Klinefelter | 68 |
| Post-chemotherapy | 53 |
| **AZFa or AZFb complete deletion** | **0: do not operate** |
- FSH and testis size do not reliably predict retrieval.

**3. ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)** with fresh or frozen sperm. Sons of AZF deletion carriers inherit the deletion.

**4. No sperm:** donor sperm or adoption; genetic counselling.

**Also know:** in-vitro spermatogenesis and stem-cell transplantation are experimental.

**Diagram to draw:**
- Hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis with the three levels of failure.
- Opened testis under microscope: plump tubules versus thin Sertoli cell–only tubules.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Confirm on two centrifuged samples.
> - FSH ≤7.6 and testis >4.6 cm → obstructive; FSH >7.6 and testis ≤4.6 cm suggest NOA.
> - Karyotype and Y microdeletion in NOA (in all); AZFa/b (complete deletion): do not retrieve; no retrieval.
> - NOA: micro-TESE + ICSI; sperm in about half; SCO 30%.

## Oligospermia.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3a

### Definition
- **Oligozoospermia:** sperm concentration below the lower reference limit. **WHO (World Health Organization) 2021 (6th edition): 16 million/mL** (total count 39 million per ejaculate). Earlier thresholds were 20 million (1999) and 15 million (2010).
- These are 5th centile values of fertile men, not fertility cut-offs. A single value must be read with testis size and FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone).
- **Severity:** mild to moderate 5–16 million/mL; **severe <5 million/mL**; cryptozoospermia (sperm only in a centrifuged pellet).

| Other WHO 2021 lower limits | Value |
|---|---|
| Volume | 1.4 mL |
| Progressive motility | 30% |
| Normal forms (strict) | 4% |
| Vitality | 54% |

### Causes
- **Pre-testicular:** hypogonadotropic hypogonadism, **exogenous testosterone or anabolic steroids**, hyperprolactinaemia, obesity (raised oestradiol).
- **Testicular:** **varicocele** (commonest correctable; heat and oxidative stress), cryptorchidism, mumps orchitis, torsion, chemotherapy or radiotherapy, Klinefelter mosaic, **AZFc (azoospermia factor c) deletion**; **idiopathic** (largest group).
- **Post-testicular:** partial ejaculatory duct obstruction (low volume), infection, antisperm antibodies.
- Lifestyle: smoking, alcohol, heat, drugs.

### Evaluation
- **Two semen analyses** after 2–7 days of abstinence.
- History and examination: testis volume, varicocele, vasa, secondary sex characters.
- Morning **testosterone, LH (luteinising hormone), FSH**; oestradiol and prolactin if testosterone is low.
- **<5 million/mL: karyotype and Y-microdeletion** testing (AUA/ASRM [American Urological Association/American Society for Reproductive Medicine] guideline).
- Selected: scrotal Doppler; TRUS (transrectal ultrasonography) if volume is low; semen culture; DNA fragmentation.

### Management
- **General:** stop testosterone and gonadotoxins; weight loss; stop smoking.
- **Varicocelectomy** (microsurgical subinguinal) for a **palpable** varicocele with abnormal semen. It improves semen and pregnancy rates. Do not treat a subclinical one.
- **Medical:** hypogonadotropic: hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) with or without FSH. Idiopathic with low testosterone: off-label clomiphene 25 mg daily, or anastrozole 1 mg daily if the testosterone to oestradiol ratio is low.
- **ART (assisted reproductive technology):** mild: IUI (intrauterine insemination); severe or failed IUI: **IVF/ICSI (in vitro fertilisation/intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**. Bank sperm in AZFc carriers because counts may fall.
- Spermatogenesis takes about 64 days; judge response after 3–6 months.

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis with sites of treatment.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO 2021: 16 million/mL; severe <5 million/mL.
> - Karyotype and Y-microdeletion if <5 million/mL.
> - Repair palpable varicoceles only; never give testosterone.

## Sperm retrieval techniques (sperm extraction in obstructive azoospermia; testicular aspiration and assisted reproduction)
**Asked as:**
- Sperm extraction in Obstructive azoospermia. — *Winter 2024 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- Testicular aspiration of sperms and assisted reproduction — *Summer 2019 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Sperm Retrival techniques — *Practice Set 16 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3b and 2021-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

*Assumed meaning: sperm retrieval techniques.*

### Introduction
- **OA (obstructive azoospermia):** no sperm in the centrifuged ejaculate despite **normal spermatogenesis**, from blockage of the ducts.
- Causes: **CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens)**, vasectomy, epididymal or ejaculatory duct obstruction, hernia-repair injury.
- **Clues:** normal testes, **FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone) ≤7.6 IU/L and testis long axis >4.6 cm**; CBAVD → CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) testing of both partners.
- TESA (testicular sperm aspiration) retrieves sperm from the testis by needle for ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection), which needs only one sperm per oocyte.

### Indications
- **OA (obstructive azoospermia)** with normal spermatogenesis: CBAVD (congenital bilateral absence of the vas deferens), unreconstructable obstruction, failed or declined vasectomy reversal, back-up at reconstruction (backup at vasoepididymostomy, frozen for IVF [in vitro fertilisation]), or when epididymal aspiration fails.
- **NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia):** only testicular retrieval works; TESA gives poor yield; micro-TESE preferred.
- Anejaculation (spinal cord injury): vibratory stimulation or electroejaculation first; retrieval after failed vibratory stimulation or electroejaculation.
- Retrieved sperm need **ICSI (intracytoplasmic sperm injection)**, because sperm from obstructed systems have poor motility; cryopreserve spares.

### Techniques (comparison of retrieval methods)
| Technique | Method / Site | Best for | Merits and limits; drawback; notes |
|---|---|---|---|
| **PESA** (percutaneous epididymal sperm aspiration) | Fine needle into caput (caput epididymis), local anaesthesia | OA | Simple; few sperm (fewer sperm); may obstruct epididymis |
| **MESA** (microsurgical epididymal sperm aspiration) | Single tubule opened under microscope at 16–25×; aspirated; micropipette by **capillary action**, never suction | **OA (best)** | **Best in OA**: many motile sperm, multiple vials (multiple frozen vials); needs microsurgery (microsurgical skill) |
| **TESA** (testicular sperm aspiration) | Testis, needle: 23 G needle with suction, many passes | OA | Least invasive; immature, immotile sperm, few sperm, small yield; poor in NOA |
| Percutaneous core biopsy | 14 G biopsy gun | OA | Fresh retrieval in OA; blind; vessel injury |
| **TESE** (testicular sperm extraction) | Open biopsy, one or more sites | OA, NOA | When epididymis is absent or empty; gold standard for tissue (gold standard for diagnosis); blind sampling |
| **Micro-TESE** | Testis bivalved; larger opaque tubules picked (chosen) under the microscope | **NOA (best)** | Sperm in about half; long operation |
| Vasal aspiration | Vasotomy, testicular end flushed | At reconstruction | |

- **MESA:** move proximally until motile sperm appear (most motile in the efferent ductules); 4–12 pipettes, frozen in aliquots.
- **Micro-TESE (Schlegel 1999):** spermatogenesis in NOA is **focal**. Plump tubules hold sperm; thin white ones are Sertoli cell–only. Map positive sites; if negative, explore the other testis.

#### MESA technique
1. Testis delivered; epididymis inspected at 16–25×.
2. Dilated tubule opened with a microknife; fluid checked at once.
3. No motile sperm: close and **move proximally**, up to the efferent ductules (proximal sperm are more motile).
4. Collect by **capillary action** into micropipettes, never suction.
5. **Cryopreserve** in several aliquots; close tubule with 10-0 suture.

#### TESA technique
1. Local anaesthesia with spermatic cord block.
2. 23 G needle on a high-suction syringe; many passes draw tubules into the needle.
3. Wet examination at once; repeat until sperm are found. Cryopreserve spare sperm.

### Choice and outcome
- Epididymal sperm first; testicular if that fails.

### Assisted reproduction
- **IUI (intrauterine insemination):** washed sperm into the uterus; mild male factor.
- **IVF (in vitro fertilisation):** oocytes and sperm combined in the laboratory.
- **ICSI:** one sperm injected into each oocyte; essential for surgically retrieved sperm (poor motility).
- Frozen retrieved sperm work as well as fresh (frozen sperm match / equal fresh), except (possibly) in spinal cord injury.

### Results, outcomes and concerns
- OA: retrieval almost always succeeds (succeeds in nearly all OA); good pregnancy rates. NOA micro-TESE: sperm in **about half**.
- NOA: retrieval depends on the cause:

| Condition | Retrieval |
|---|---|
| Post-orchidopexy | 74% |
| AZFc (azoospermia factor c) deletion | 70% |
| Klinefelter | 68% |
| Post-chemotherapy | 53% |
| Maturation arrest | 40% |
| Sertoli cell–only (Sertoli-cell-only) | 30% |
| AZFa/AZFb deletion | **0%: do not operate** |

- NOA pregnancies: more miscarriage (more miscarriages occur) (aneuploidy, DNA damage).
- ICSI bypasses natural selection: risk of transmitting genetic defects. Offer karyotype, Y-microdeletion and CFTR (cystic fibrosis transmembrane conductance regulator) testing.
- Female burden: ovarian stimulation, multiple pregnancy.

### Complications
- Haematoma (commonest), infection, pain, vascular injury (TESA), epididymal obstruction (PESA), testicular atrophy or fall in testosterone (TESE).
- **Also know:** micro-TESE is for NOA (non-obstructive azoospermia); post-mortem retrieval needs prior consent; microfluidic sperm sorting.

**Diagram to draw:** testis and epididymis showing PESA, MESA and TESA sites; needle in testis with syringe suction; flow to ICSI; micro-TESE: bivalved testis with plump tubules beside thin Sertoli cell–only tubules.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - OA: normal FSH and testes; ICSI essential.
> - OA: MESA (capillary action); MESA is best: many motile sperm, multiple vials.
> - Move proximally; capillary action, never suction.
> - TESA: 23 G needle, suction, local anaesthesia; for OA.
> - TESA/TESE when epididymal sperm absent.
> - NOA: micro-TESE best; micro-TESE finds sperm in about half of NOA.
> - AZFa/AZFb deletion: 0% (means no sperm), do not operate.
> - All surgically retrieved sperm need ICSI; cryopreserve extras.

## CRE & BEP
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 2, Q3f

*"CRE" is read as carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales, and BEP as bleomycin, etoposide and cisplatin chemotherapy for germ cell tumours; the two are answered separately.*

### Part A: CRE (carbapenem-resistant Enterobacterales)
- **Definition:** Enterobacterales (Klebsiella pneumoniae, Escherichia coli, Enterobacter) resistant to at least one carbapenem, or producing a carbapenemase.

| Mechanism | Example |
|---|---|
| Class A carbapenemase | KPC (Klebsiella pneumoniae carbapenemase) |
| Class B metallo-β-lactamase | **NDM (New Delhi metallo-β-lactamase)**: common in India |
| Class D | OXA-48 |
| Non-enzymatic | ESBL (extended-spectrum β-lactamase) or AmpC plus porin loss |

- **Urological risk:** catheters, stents, prolonged stay, prior carbapenem or fluoroquinolone use, transrectal biopsy, ICU (intensive care unit) care.
- **Treatment** (by culture and carbapenemase type; ID (infectious disease) input):
  - KPC, OXA-48: ceftazidime–avibactam; meropenem–vaborbactam or imipenem–relebactam for KPC.
  - **NDM:** ceftazidime–avibactam plus aztreonam, or cefiderocol.
  - Cystitis: single-dose aminoglycoside, fosfomycin; polymyxins as last resort. Avoid tigecycline for UTI (urinary tract infection) (low urine levels).
- **Prevention:** antibiotic stewardship, contact precautions, rectal screening of high-risk patients, early catheter removal, transperineal rather than transrectal biopsy.

### Part B: BEP (bleomycin, etoposide, cisplatin)
- Standard first-line chemotherapy for metastatic **GCT (germ cell tumour)**; replaced PVB (less neuromuscular toxicity).
- **Usual 21-day cycle:** cisplatin 20 mg/m² days 1–5, etoposide 100 mg/m² days 1–5, bleomycin 30 units days 1, 8, 15.

| IGCCCG (International Germ Cell Cancer Collaborative Group) risk | Regimen |
|---|---|
| Good | BEP ×3 or EP ×4 |
| Intermediate/poor | BEP ×4 (VIP ×4 if lungs poor) |
| Stage I NSGCT (non-seminomatous GCT), high risk | BEP ×1 adjuvant |

- **Toxicity:** bleomycin lung fibrosis (keep FiO₂ low at later RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)) and Raynaud phenomenon; cisplatin nephrotoxicity, ototoxicity, neuropathy; myelosuppression; infertility (bank sperm); late cardiovascular disease and second cancers.

**Diagram to draw:** BEP 21-day cycle timeline.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - CRE: NDM common in India; ceftazidime–avibactam + aztreonam for metallo-β-lactamase.
> - BEP ×3 good risk, ×4 intermediate/poor; EP ×4 if bleomycin avoided.
> - Bleomycin lung: low FiO₂ and restricted fluids at surgery.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 9 · Andrology & Sexual Medicine

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Surgical management of erectile dysfunction and penile prosthesis (LAQ)

- Prosthesis is the last line but gives 80–90% satisfaction.
- 3-piece (three-piece) inflatable gives the best rigidity and flaccidity.
- Malleable rods: nitinol (Tactra), silver (Genesis); almost never fail.
- Glucose <200 mg/dL, chlorhexidine–alcohol, coated device, no-touch technique.
- Infection 1–3%; coated devices and no-touch technique; infected implant: remove all parts; Mulcahy salvage 84%.
- Satisfaction 80–90%; repairs 15% at 5 years.
- Arterial revascularisation only for young men with post-traumatic focal stenosis.

#### Discuss the role of urologist in reproductive endocrinology and male infertility practice. (LAQ)

- FSH >7.6 IU/L + testis ≤4.6 cm = spermatogenic failure.
- Exogenous testosterone is a contraceptive: never give it for infertility.
- Hypogonadotropic hypogonadism: hCG ± FSH; most treatable cause.
- Varicocelectomy only for palpable varicocele with abnormal semen; micro-TESE for non-obstructive azoospermia.

#### Management of priapism (SAQ)

- Ischaemic gas: pO2 <30, pCO2 >60, pH <7.25.
- Distal shunt ± tunnelling before proximal; prosthesis if >36 h.
- High-flow: observe (~60% resolve), then embolise.

#### Azoospermia and non-obstructive azoospermia (SAQ)

- Confirm on two centrifuged samples.
- FSH ≤7.6 and testis >4.6 cm → obstructive; FSH >7.6 and testis ≤4.6 cm suggest NOA.
- Karyotype and Y microdeletion in NOA (in all); AZFa/b (complete deletion): do not retrieve; no retrieval.
- NOA: micro-TESE + ICSI; sperm in about half; SCO 30%.

#### Oligospermia. (SAQ)

- WHO 2021: 16 million/mL; severe <5 million/mL.
- Karyotype and Y-microdeletion if <5 million/mL.
- Repair palpable varicoceles only; never give testosterone.

#### Sperm retrieval techniques (sperm extraction in obstructive azoospermia; testicular aspiration and assisted reproduction) (SAQ)

- OA: normal FSH and testes; ICSI essential.
- OA: MESA (capillary action); MESA is best: many motile sperm, multiple vials.
- Move proximally; capillary action, never suction.
- TESA: 23 G needle, suction, local anaesthesia; for OA.
- TESA/TESE when epididymal sperm absent.
- NOA: micro-TESE best; micro-TESE finds sperm in about half of NOA.
- AZFa/AZFb deletion: 0% (means no sperm), do not operate.
- All surgically retrieved sperm need ICSI; cryopreserve extras.

#### CRE & BEP (SAQ)

- CRE: NDM common in India; ceftazidime–avibactam + aztreonam for metallo-β-lactamase.
- BEP ×3 good risk, ×4 intermediate/poor; EP ×4 if bleomycin avoided.
- Bleomycin lung: low FiO₂ and restricted fluids at surgery.

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Urethral stricture disease: etiology, presentation and management (including recent advances)
**Asked as:**
- Describe etiology, clinical presentation and management of urethral strictures. — *Summer 2016 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe etiology, clinical presentation and recent advances in management of Urethral Structure Disease — *Winter 2025 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

*"Structure" is taken to mean urethral stricture disease.*

### Definition
- A fixed narrowing of the **anterior** urethra (inside the corpus spongiosum) that will not accept instruments without tearing the mucosa. It is an epithelial scar with (often with) **spongiofibrosis**.
- Posterior narrowing is named differently (terminology: WHO (World Health Organization) 2004, ICUD (International Consultation on Urological Diseases) 2010): after pelvic fracture, **PFUI (pelvic fracture urethral injury)** or distraction defect; after prostate surgery or radiation, **stenosis or contracture**.
- Area falls with the square of diameter: halving calibre (30 to 15 Fr) loses (about) three-quarters of the lumen area, so symptoms appear late.

### Anatomy relevant to surgery
- **Bulbar urethra lies eccentric (dorsal)** in the spongiosum; the penile urethra is central.
- **Dual blood supply** (bulbar arteries and/plus retrograde flow from the glans) allows (lets) the bulb to be divided and mobilised.

### Aetiology
| Category | Examples and mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Idiopathic** (commonest, with iatrogenic; about equal to iatrogenic) | No cause found |
| **Iatrogenic** | Catheters (common / very common in LMICs (low- and middle-income countries)), TURP (transurethral resection of prostate), bladder tumour resection (TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)), cystoscopy, hypospadias repair, radiotherapy. Mechanism: mucosal injury, pressure ischaemia, infection |
| **Traumatic** | **Straddle injury**: bulb crushed against the pubis; pelvic fracture (PFUI) gives a posterior obliterative defect |
| **Inflammatory** | Gonorrhoea (historically commonest, now rare); **lichen sclerosus (LS)**. Chlamydia: no proven link |
| Congenital | Rarest; infants only (short anterior stricture in infants) |
- **LS**: chronic skin disease starting at (LS starts at) the glans and meatus. High-pressure voiding forces urine (urine forced) into Littre glands, causing (causes) inflammation and deep spongiofibrosis. It **recurs in any (genital) skin** used to repair it.
- **Jordan's spectrum**: mucosal fold → iris (iris constriction) → full-thickness → deep spongiofibrosis → periurethral fibrosis (fibrosis beyond spongiosum) → fistula or abscess.

### Clinical presentation
- Poor stream, straining, spraying, incomplete emptying, post-void dribble; UTI (urinary tract infection), prostatitis, epididymitis.
- **Retention** after long-tolerated symptoms; periurethral abscess, fistula ("watering-can perineum"), stones, upper-tract damage.

### Evaluation (define 4 things: location, length, depth, density)
| Test | Role |
|---|---|
| **Uroflowmetry and PVR (post-void residual)** | Low, plateau-shaped flow (flat plateau curve); screen and follow-up |
| **Dynamic RUG (retrograde urethrogram) + VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram)** | Site and length; steep oblique views; done by the surgeon |
| **Flexible urethroscopy** | Calibre, appearance; paediatric scope sees beyond |
| **Sonourethrography** | Length and depth of spongiofibrosis |
| MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) | PFUI defect alignment; not routine (for PFUI only) |
- **Urethral rest** (suprapubic catheter 6–8 weeks) shows the true length. Avoid blind dilation. In retention, place a **suprapubic catheter** rather than force a dilation.

### Management (standard management, brief in the 2025 answer)
First agree the aim: **palliation or cure**. The old "reconstructive ladder" (repeat endoscopy before surgery) is abandoned (archaic), because repeated endoscopy makes later urethroplasty harder.

**1. Dilation**: soft or balloon dilation over a guidewire. It stretches, not tears, the scar. Curative only for (cures only) epithelial strictures without spongiofibrosis.

**2. DVIU (direct vision internal urethrotomy)**
- Heals by **secondary intention**: a race between epithelialisation and wound contraction.
- Best (only) for **bulbar strictures <1.5 cm**, without dense spongiofibrosis, at first attempt. A second urethrotomy (DVIU) adds almost nothing. Laser gives no advantage over cold knife (laser equals cold knife).
- Complications: recurrence (commonest), bleeding, extravasation, ED (erectile dysfunction) from deep cuts.
- Adjuncts: **intermittent self-dilation**; mitomycin C injection. **Optilume** paclitaxel-coated balloon (**ROBUST III**: 74.6% vs 26.8% stricture-free at 1 year after failed endoscopy).

**3. Urethroplasty**
| Technique | Indication and key points |
|---|---|
| **Excision and primary anastomosis (EPA)** | **Gold standard**, about 90–95% success; short bulbar strictures (1–2 cm, up to / sometimes 3–5 cm proximally). Excise all scar, spatulate widely, tension-free. **Non-transecting** and **vessel-sparing** variants preserve bulbar arteries |
| **BMG (buccal mucosal graft) onlay** | Longer bulbar strictures: **dorsal onlay (Barbagli)**, ventral onlay, **dorsal inlay (Asopa)**. Graft equals flap. Kulkarni one-sided repair for panurethral strictures |
| **Augmented anastomosis** | Excise the narrowest part, anastomose the floor, graft the roof |
| **Penile skin flaps** (Orandi, Quartey, circular) | Penile strictures (not LS); skin island carried on dartos |
| **Staged repair** (Johanson) | Stage 1 open and graft; tubularise after 6 months or more. For **LS**, failed hypospadias, infection |
| **Perineal urethrostomy** | Complex or panurethral disease, elderly or after (multiple) failures |
- **Onlay is better than (beats) tube** repairs; **avoid genital skin in LS** (use oral mucosa). Topical clobetasol for early LS meatal disease.
- Meatal stenosis: meatotomy; fossa navicularis: flap or graft.
- **PFUI**: suprapubic catheter, then delayed **perineal anastomotic urethroplasty (repair)** after 3 months or more. Progressive steps: bulbar mobilisation, intracrural (corporal / crural) separation, inferior pubectomy, rerouting of the urethra.
- **Urethral stents** (UroLume) are withdrawn; AUA (American Urological Association): no stent if urethroplasty is possible.

### Recent advances in management
1. **Ladder abandoned**: the "reconstructive ladder" is archaic. Repeated endoscopy lowers later urethroplasty success, so **early urethroplasty** is offered after one failed DVIU (AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology) guidelines).
2. **Optilume paclitaxel-coated balloon** (FDA (Food and Drug Administration) 2023): paclitaxel inhibits fibroblasts. **ROBUST III**: stricture-free 74.6% vs 26.8% at 1 year after failed endoscopy; IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) 9 vs 19.9.
3. **Anti-scarring adjuncts after DVIU**: mitomycin C injection reduces recurrence; intermittent self-dilation; removable stents. Permanent UroLume stents withdrawn.
4. **Vessel-sparing and non-transecting EPA**: preserve bulbar arteries, theoretically less ED (erectile dysfunction).
5. **Augmented anastomosis**: excise the narrowest segment, graft the rest.
6. **Kulkarni one-sided dorsal BMG** through a perineal incision for panurethral strictures.
7. **Staged oral mucosa repair for LS**; avoid skin. **BMG perineal urethrostomy** reduces neomeatal stenosis.
8. **Robotic reconstruction** of bladder neck contracture and posterior stenosis (Y-V plasty, BMG).
9. **Tissue-engineered oral mucosa** (investigational). **Prevention**: silicone and hydrophilic catheters, smaller endoscopes.
10. **PROMs (patient-reported outcome measures)**: USS-PROM (Urethral Stricture Surgery PROM) is increasingly used to judge success.
- **Also know:** dartos-island fossa repair; lingual graft; clobetasol for early LS.

### Complications of urethroplasty
- Recurrence, ED (overall similar to circumcision; higher with long repairs), chordee, post-void dribble, graft-site pain, numbness.

### Follow-up
- Uroflow, PVR and symptom score; RUG or cystoscopy if flow falls. Most EPA recurrences occur in the first 6 months (EPA: mostly in first 6 months); graft results decline with time (grafts decline with time).

**Diagram to draw:** RUG of a bulbar stricture, and cross-section of dorsal onlay versus ventral onlay.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Idiopathic and iatrogenic are commonest; LS recurs in skin.
> - Assess (define) location, length, depth, density (dynamic RUG + VCUG, sonourethrography).
> - DVIU only (once) for bulbar <1.5 cm at first attempt; do not repeat.
> - EPA is gold standard; BMG onlay for longer; staged repair for LS.
> - Optilume (ROBUST III 74.6% vs 26.8%); early urethroplasty, ladder abandoned.

## Importance and management of Inguinal lymph nodes in CA penis.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q1

### Importance
- Presence and extent of inguinal metastasis is the **strongest prognostic factor** in penile SCC (squamous cell carcinoma).
- A long **locoregional phase** precedes distant spread, so the groin is a **curable station**; lymphadenectomy alone can cure.
- **Kroon (2005)**: early ILND (inguinal lymph node dissection) for impalpable nodes gave 3-year disease-specific survival about 84% vs about 35% when delayed until nodes were palpable (ENE (extranodal extension) about 20% vs 95%).
- Manage both groins **within 3 months** of diagnosis; aim for **at least 8 nodes per side**.
- About 80% 5-year survival with 2 or fewer unilateral nodes, no ENE, no pelvic nodes.

### Lymphatic drainage
- **Superficial system** (prepuce, shaft skin) → superficial inguinal nodes. **Deep system** (glans, frenulum) → corona → within Buck's fascia → presymphyseal plexus → femoral triangle nodes.
- Presymphyseal crossover makes drainage **bilateral** and unpredictable in side; with one palpable groin, the other has micrometastasis in about 30%.
- **Stepwise, no skip**: superficial → deep inguinal → external iliac; so superficial status gates deep dissection and inguinal status gates pelvic dissection.
- **Superficial nodes** (4–25, above fascia lata) lie in **Daseler's five zones**:

| Zone | Related vessel |
|---|---|
| Central | Saphenofemoral junction |
| Superolateral | Superficial circumflex iliac vein |
| Inferolateral | Lateral femoral cutaneous and superficial circumflex veins |
| **Superomedial** (sentinel nodes mostly here) | Superficial external pudendal and superficial epigastric veins |
| Inferomedial | Great saphenous vein |

- **Deep nodes** (1–3) medial to the femoral vein; **Cloquet's node** is the highest.

### Staging (TNM (tumour–node–metastasis) 8th edition)
| N | Clinical | Pathological |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | One mobile unilateral node | 2 or fewer unilateral, no ENE |
| 2 | Mobile, 2 or more unilateral or bilateral | 3 or more (unilateral or bilateral) |
| 3 | Fixed mass or pelvic nodes | ENE or pelvic nodes |
- **T1a**: no LVI (lymphovascular invasion), no PNI (perineural invasion), not high grade. **T1b**: LVI or PNI or high grade.

### Evaluation
- **Careful bilateral palpation**: cN0 or cN+ (number, side, mobile or fixed). Imaging (US (ultrasound), CT (computed tomography), MRI) only if examination is unreliable (obesity, previous groin surgery).
- **cN+ or high risk**: FDG-PET/CT (fluorodeoxyglucose positron emission tomography–CT) or CT chest–abdomen–pelvis.
- About half of palpable nodes are reactive, yet an antibiotic trial is **no longer recommended** (delays treatment). US-guided **FNAC (fine-needle aspiration cytology)** is specific; a negative result never ends the pathway.

### Management of the impalpable groin (cN0): risk-based
| Risk (EAU-ASCO (European Association of Urology–American Society of Clinical Oncology) 2023) | Primary | Groin |
|---|---|---|
| Low | pTis, pTa, pT1a G1 | Surveillance |
| Intermediate | pT1a G2 | Surveillance (compliant) or surgical staging |
| High | **pT1b or more**, G3 | **DSNB (dynamic sentinel node biopsy)**; ILND if unavailable |
- NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network): pT1b G1–2 is intermediate risk, staged surgically (ILND first).
- **DSNB**: US ± FNAC first (a tumour-replaced node diverts flow → false negative); then 99mTc (technetium-99m) nanocolloid, blue dye and gamma probe; serial sectioning. False-negative about 7% at expert centres.
- **Modified ILND (Catalona)**: smaller field (nothing lateral to the femoral artery or below the fossa ovalis), **saphenous vein preserved**, no sartorius transposition; positive frozen section → complete ILND.
- **Also know:** superficial ILND; fascia-sparing ILND (FS-ILND); **VEIL (video-endoscopic inguinal lymphadenectomy)**/robotic: same yield, fewer wound complications, non-bulky nodes only; Cabanas anatomical sentinel biopsy abandoned.

### Management of palpable nodes (cN+)
```
Palpable node → US-guided FNAC
   positive → radical ILND same side + stage other side
               (superficial ILND, FS-ILND or DSNB)
   negative → excisional biopsy → negative: observe
Bulky (≥4 cm), fixed, bilateral or pelvic nodes
   → neoadjuvant TIP × 4 → responders: ilioinguinal resection
   → cisplatin-unfit: chemoradiation
```
- **Radical ILND** (cN2 or more; cN1 may have FS-ILND): incision 2 cm below and parallel to the inguinal ligament; flaps deep to **Camper's fascia**; boundaries inguinal ligament, sartorius, adductor longus; fascia lata opened; stay medial to the femoral artery (femoral nerve); **sartorius transposition** over the vessels; suction drains.
- **PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)**: if **more than 2 inguinal nodes or ENE** (pN2–3); ipsilateral; external iliac commonest, then obturator.
- **Neoadjuvant TIP** (paclitaxel, ifosfamide, cisplatin), **Pagliaro (2010)**: objective response 50%; survival mainly in responders.
- **Adjuvant**: chemotherapy for pN2–3 (weak evidence); radiotherapy for multiple nodes or ENE (at least 50 Gy; 54 Gy for ENE).

### Complications
- Wound infection, **flap necrosis**, dehiscence, seroma, **lymphocele**, **lymphoedema**, DVT/PE (deep vein thrombosis/pulmonary embolism).
- Reduced by transverse incision, saphenous sparing, drain removal by volume (<30 mL/day), thromboprophylaxis, minimally invasive access; sartorius transposition increases them.

### Follow-up
- Examination 3-monthly for 2 years, then 6-monthly to 5 years (recurrence mostly within 2 years); then self-examination.

**Diagram to draw:** femoral triangle with Daseler's zones and Cloquet's node.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Nodal status is the strongest prognostic factor; ILND can cure.
> - pT1b or higher: stage groins surgically (DSNB preferred) within 3 months.
> - Kroon 2005: early vs delayed ILND, 84% vs 35% 3-year survival.
> - PLND if more than 2 nodes or ENE; bulky → neoadjuvant TIP (InPACT (International Penile Advanced Cancer Trial) testing the sequence).

## Describe anatomy of lymphatic drainage of testis and scrotum. Describe management of stage one seminoma of testis.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q2

### Lymphatic drainage of the testis
- **Why retroperitoneal:** the testis develops beside the kidney (L1–L2) and its lymph returns to where it began.
- **Route:** lymphatics of the testis and the caput and corpus of the epididymis run up the cord with the testicular vessels, through the internal ring and along the gonadal vessels to the lumbar nodes near the renal hilum.
- From there lymph passes to the **cisterna chyli** (in front of L1–L2), the **thoracic duct** and the left innominate vein. This explains **left supraclavicular** node metastases.

**Retroperitoneal nodal regions**
| Region | Extent |
|---|---|
| Left para-aortic | Aortic midline to the left ureter |
| Interaortocaval | Between IVC (inferior vena cava) and aorta |
| Right paracaval | IVC midline to the right ureter |

**Primary landing zones**
| | Right testis | Left testis |
|---|---|---|
| Primary | **Interaortocaval** | **Left para-aortic** (and left hilar) |
| Secondary | Right paracaval, precaval | Interaortocaval, pre-aortic |
| Crossover | Small but real to the left side | Rare to the right, unless bulky |

- **Crossover is right to left** only, because lymph flows from right to left.
- **Cauda of epididymis** drains with the vas to the **external iliac** nodes.
- **Pelvic and inguinal nodes** are involved only if normal drainage is altered: previous scrotal or inguinal surgery (orchidopexy, hernia repair), scrotal violation, or tumour invading the tunica vaginalis or scrotum.

### Lymphatic drainage of the scrotum
- Scrotal skin drains to the **ipsilateral superficial inguinal nodes**. Lymphatics, like vessels and nerves, do not cross the median raphe.

### Clinical relevance
- **Trans-scrotal orchidectomy or biopsy is contraindicated.** It leaves cord behind and opens scrotal lymphatic routes, causing local and inguinal or pelvic recurrence.
- Explains RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection) templates and the para-aortic or "dog-leg" radiotherapy fields.

### Stage I seminoma: definition and staging
- Disease confined to the testis: any pT, N0, M0 on imaging. Stage IA = pT1; IB = pT2–4; IS = markers still raised after orchidectomy.
- **Work-up:** AFP (alpha-fetoprotein), β-hCG (human chorionic gonadotropin) and LDH (lactate dehydrogenase) before and after orchidectomy. CT abdomen and pelvis; chest CT or radiograph. Baseline testosterone; **offer sperm banking**. A raised AFP means non-seminoma.
- **Radical inguinal orchidectomy:** early cord control at the internal ring. It cures 80–85% of stage I seminoma.
- **Risk factors for relapse:** **tumour >4 cm** and **rete testis invasion**. Five-year relapse is 12%, 16% and 32% with 0, 1 and 2 factors. LVI (lymphovascular invasion) predicts relapse in non-seminoma, not seminoma.

### Options after orchidectomy (cancer-specific survival about 100% with each)
| | Surveillance | Carboplatin | Radiotherapy |
|---|---|---|---|
| Method | Clinical review, markers, CT | **One cycle, AUC (area under the curve) 7** | **20 Gy** in 10 fractions to the para-aortic strip |
| Relapse | 14–20% | About 5% | 3–5% |
| Relapse site | Retroperitoneum (84–100%) | Retroperitoneum | Pelvis, chest, left supraclavicular |
| Drawbacks | Late relapse, many scans, compliance | Underdosing; late effects unknown | Second cancers, GI (gastrointestinal) toxicity, oligospermia |
| Status | **Preferred** | If unwilling or unsuitable for surveillance; risk factors | If unwilling or unsuitable for surveillance |

- **Carboplatin (Calvert):** 7 × (GFR [glomerular filtration rate] + 25) mg, using isotope GFR.
- **Trials:** **MRC TE18** (Medical Research Council): 20 Gy equals 30 Gy, with less toxicity. **MRC TE19/EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30982:** one cycle of carboplatin equals radiotherapy (5-year relapse-free 94.7% vs 96%). MRC TE10: para-aortic field equals dog-leg, with 2% pelvic relapse.
- **Risk-adapted (EAU [European Association of Urology]):** surveillance for low risk; with risk factors discuss surveillance or carboplatin (treating all would overtreat two-thirds).

```
Inguinal orchidectomy + markers + CT
            │
     Stage I seminoma
            │
 Surveillance (preferred)  ── unwilling / unreliable ──► Carboplatin AUC7 ×1(–2)
            │                                       (or radiotherapy 20 Gy)
       Relapse (mostly retroperitoneal)
   ├─ small nodes ► dog-leg radiotherapy with boost, or chemotherapy
   └─ bulky/distant ► BEP ×3 or EP ×4
```
- BEP: bleomycin, etoposide, cisplatin; EP: etoposide, cisplatin.

### Relapse and IGCCCG
- Relapses are almost always cured; median 14 months, but 10–20% occur after 4 years.
- **IGCCCG** (International Germ Cell Cancer Collaborative Group): seminoma is good risk unless there are non-pulmonary visceral metastases (intermediate). **There is no poor-risk seminoma**, and markers do not change the risk group.

### Follow-up (surveillance)
- Examination and markers every 2–4 months (years 1–3), 6-monthly (years 4–7), then yearly; CT every 4–6 months (years 1–3), then 6-monthly to year 7, then yearly. CT finds >85% of relapses.

### Recent advances
- **miR-371a-3p** serum microRNA detects relapse better than standard markers.
- MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) instead of CT for surveillance (TRISST trial) to reduce radiation.

**Diagram to draw:** retroperitoneal nodes with right (interaortocaval) and left (para-aortic) landing zones and the right-to-left crossover arrow.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Right testis to interaortocaval; left to para-aortic; crossover only right to left.
> - Scrotum to ipsilateral superficial inguinal nodes; never operate trans-scrotally.
> - Risk factors: >4 cm and rete testis invasion (not LVI).
> - Surveillance preferred; carboplatin AUC7 ×1 (TE19) or RT 20 Gy (TE18).

# Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Management of Urethral meatal stenosis.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3a

### Definition and causes
- Narrowing of the external urethral meatus below the normal calibre for age.

| Age | Normal calibre |
|---|---|
| Under 4 months | 6–10 Fr |
| 1–12 months | 10–12 Fr |
| 1–10 years | 12–14 Fr |
| Over 10 years | 14–18 Fr |

- **Children:** almost always after **neonatal circumcision**: wet nappies irritate the exposed meatus (ammoniacal meatitis) and the ventral lips fuse into a thin membrane. Rarely congenital with hypospadias.
- **Adults:** LS (lichen sclerosus, formerly BXO [balanitis xerotica obliterans]); catheter or instrument trauma; failed hypospadias repair. Usually the tip of a **fossa navicularis stricture**.

### Clinical features
- Thin, forceful stream deflected **upwards**; straining, frequency, incomplete emptying; pain at the start of voiding.

### Evaluation
- Watch the child void; calibrate with a bougie à boule. Uroflowmetry. Urethrography only for UTI (urinary tract infection) or suspected proximal lesion.
- Adults: **retrograde urethrogram (RUG) before meatal surgery**, as a proximal stricture often coexists, especially in LS.

### Management
| Situation | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Meatitis, early narrowing (child) | Topical 0.05% clobetasol; gentle dilation; nappy hygiene |
| Symptomatic stenosis (child) | **Ventral meatotomy** (EMLA [eutectic mixture of local anaesthetics] cream in clinic, or general anaesthesia); parents spread the edges with a dilator tip 3 times daily for 7–10 days |
| Simple adult stenosis | Ventral meatotomy with 3 sutures (apex and each side), mucosa to glans; dilator afterwards |
| Scarred or recurrent | Dorsal meatotomy (Y-V plasty after excising the scar ridge); effective but poorer cosmesis |
| Fossa navicularis involved | Urethroplasty: penile skin island flap (Jordan, De Sy), not in LS |
| **LS** | Topical steroid; extended meatotomy; **buccal mucosa graft** (never genital skin); perineal urethrostomy for pananterior disease |
| Unfit or elderly | Self-dilation with clobetasol as lubricant |

### Complications
- Bleeding, restenosis (commoner in LS), spraying.

**Also know:** biopsy atypical LS (squamous carcinoma risk up to 8%).

**Diagram to draw:** ventral meatotomy: crush, incise ventrally, suture mucosa to glans at apex and both sides.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Child: post-circumcision ventral membrane; ventral meatotomy and parental dilation.
> - Adult: RUG first; often a fossa navicularis stricture.
> - LS: steroid, buccal mucosa, never genital skin.

## Perineal urethrostomy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3c

### Definition
- PU (perineal urethrostomy): the proximal bulbar urethra is opened onto perineal skin as a permanent or staged new meatus; the patient voids sitting. It **bypasses** rather than reconstructs a diseased anterior urethra.

### Indications
- **Complex anterior strictures:** LS (lichen sclerosus) panurethral disease, multiple failed urethroplasties, failed hypospadias repair.
- **Elderly or high-risk patients** who do not want or cannot withstand staged reconstruction.
- **First stage** of a staged (Johanson-type) urethroplasty.
- After **penectomy** or distal urethrectomy for penile or urethral cancer.
- Temporary: stricture after phalloplasty; resectoscope access in morbid obesity; Fournier gangrene.
- AUA (American Urological Association) guideline (2016): PU is an acceptable long-term alternative to urethroplasty.
- LS spares the proximal urethra, so PU is easy in LS.

### Contraindications and limits
- Disease reaching the proximal bulbar or membranous urethra; prior pelvic radiation and LS increase restricture.

### Techniques
**1. Classical (Blandy) PU:** inverted-U perineal skin flap advanced into a ventral bulbar urethrotomy (a random-pattern advancement flap).

**2. BMG (buccal mucosal graft) PU, steps:**
1. High lithotomy; inverted-U incision; divide bulbospongiosus in the midline.
2. Free the spongiosum; **transect** it; **dorsal urethrotomy** until healthy mucosa.
3. Bring the healthy ventral urethra to perineal skin; calibrate proximally to 30 Fr; close the distal urethra.
4. Quilt a BMG on the corpora to complete the dorsal wall up to the skin.
- The graft prevents the circumferential scar that narrows a deep PU.

### Complications and outcomes
- **Stenosis of the urethrostomy** (main failure): revision or periodic dilation.
- Spraying, dribbling, perineal wetness, wound infection, body-image concerns.
- Most succeed at mid-term with high satisfaction in selected patients.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal perineum showing inverted-U flap sutured into the opened bulbar urethra.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PU bypasses, rather than reconstructs, the diseased anterior urethra.
> - Ideal for LS, failed repairs and elderly patients; patient voids sitting.
> - BMG PU prevents circumferential scarring; calibrate to 30 Fr.

## Surgical management of bilateral varicocele
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 17 Paper 2, Q3f
(related: Practice Set 6 Paper 3, Q3e)

### Introduction
- Dilated pampiniform veins: ~15% of men, 35% of primary and 75–81% of secondary infertility. Mostly left (left vein enters the renal vein 8–10 cm higher). With sensitive tests most are bilateral; clinical significance of subclinical ones is uncertain.
- Harm: heat (lost countercurrent exchange), oxidative stress, DNA fragmentation, Leydig dysfunction; progressive.
- Grades: subclinical (Doppler only), I (palpable on Valsalva), II (palpable), III (visible).

### Indications for repair
- Infertile couple, **palpable** varicocele, abnormal semen, normal female partner.
- Pain (dull ache on standing), hypogonadism, high DNA fragmentation; adolescent testicular asymmetry >20%.
- **Do not repair subclinical** sides. Repair each palpable side.

### Techniques
| Technique | Recurrence | Hydrocele | Bilateral use |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Microsurgical subinguinal / inguinal** | **0.5–1%** | **~0** | **Preferred**: two small incisions, local or regional anaesthesia |
| Laparoscopic | 3–15% | 12% | Both sides through same ports; general anaesthesia, serious complications possible |
| Retroperitoneal (Palomo) | 15–25% | 7% | Children; artery ligation acceptable |
| Embolisation / sclerotherapy | 15–25% | 0 | Femoral (left) or jugular (right) access; technical failure |

### Microsurgical steps (each side)
- Subinguinal incision over the external ring; deliver the cord.
- Microscope and **micro-Doppler** to map the testicular artery; papaverine for spasm.
- Ligate all internal and external spermatic veins; preserve **artery, lymphatics, vas and one set of vasal veins**.
- Deliver the testis to ligate gubernacular veins (lower recurrence).
- **Impulse test** confirms no missed vein.

### Bilateral-specific points
- **Never ligate both testicular arteries**: in adults this has caused azoospermia and atrophy. So artery-sparing microsurgery is safest bilaterally.
- An isolated right varicocele, or one that does not decompress lying down: image for a renal or retroperitoneal mass.
- Repair any coexisting hydrocele.

### Complications
- Hydrocele (lymphatic ligation), testicular artery injury (atrophy), recurrence (upright colour Doppler shows reflux).

**Diagram to draw:** spermatic cord cross-section: artery, lymphatics and vas preserved, veins ligated.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Repair only palpable sides with abnormal semen.
> - Microsurgical subinguinal: recurrence <1%, hydrocele nil.
> - Never ligate both testicular arteries.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 10 · Testis, Penis & Urethra

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Urethral stricture disease: etiology, presentation and management (including recent advances) (LAQ)

- Idiopathic and iatrogenic are commonest; LS recurs in skin.
- Assess (define) location, length, depth, density (dynamic RUG + VCUG, sonourethrography).
- DVIU only (once) for bulbar <1.5 cm at first attempt; do not repeat.
- EPA is gold standard; BMG onlay for longer; staged repair for LS.
- Optilume (ROBUST III 74.6% vs 26.8%); early urethroplasty, ladder abandoned.

#### Importance and management of Inguinal lymph nodes in CA penis. (LAQ)

- Nodal status is the strongest prognostic factor; ILND can cure.
- pT1b or higher: stage groins surgically (DSNB preferred) within 3 months.
- Kroon 2005: early vs delayed ILND, 84% vs 35% 3-year survival.
- PLND if more than 2 nodes or ENE; bulky → neoadjuvant TIP (InPACT (International Penile Advanced Cancer Trial) testing the sequence).

#### Describe anatomy of lymphatic drainage of testis and scrotum. Describe management of stage one seminoma of testis. (LAQ)

- Right testis to interaortocaval; left to para-aortic; crossover only right to left.
- Scrotum to ipsilateral superficial inguinal nodes; never operate trans-scrotally.
- Risk factors: >4 cm and rete testis invasion (not LVI).
- Surveillance preferred; carboplatin AUC7 ×1 (TE19) or RT 20 Gy (TE18).

#### Management of Urethral meatal stenosis. (SAQ)

- Child: post-circumcision ventral membrane; ventral meatotomy and parental dilation.
- Adult: RUG first; often a fossa navicularis stricture.
- LS: steroid, buccal mucosa, never genital skin.

#### Perineal urethrostomy. (SAQ)

- PU bypasses, rather than reconstructs, the diseased anterior urethra.
- Ideal for LS, failed repairs and elderly patients; patient voids sitting.
- BMG PU prevents circumferential scarring; calibrate to 30 Fr.

#### Surgical management of bilateral varicocele (SAQ)

- Repair only palpable sides with abnormal semen.
- Microsurgical subinguinal: recurrence <1%, hydrocele nil.
- Never ligate both testicular arteries.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Metabolic evaluation in stone disease (and management of bilateral staghorn calculi)
**Asked as:**
- Discuss metabolic evaluation in urinary calculus disease. — *Summer 2015 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe metabolic evaluation in stone disease — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss evaluation for urolithiasis and management of bilateral renal staghorn calculi — *Winter 2017 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q2 and 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3b)

### Introduction and rationale
- Stone disease is a chronic metabolic disorder (disease): first-time formers have about a **50% risk of recurrence within 10 years** (another source: up to 50% recur within 5–10 years).
- Evaluation finds the treatable abnormality; therapy matched to 24-hour urine results reduces stone events. The **ROKS (Recurrence of Kidney Stone) nomogram** estimates individual risk.

### Physicochemical basis (why we measure these)
- Stones form when urine is **supersaturated**: nucleation, growth, aggregation and retention, often on **Randall plaque**.
- In the **metastable zone** (between solubility and formation products) there is no spontaneous nucleation, but crystals grow on existing nuclei but do not form spontaneously; above it nucleation is spontaneous.
- **Promoters** (calcium, oxalate, uric acid, low volume) raise supersaturation; **inhibitors** (citrate, magnesium, Tamm-Horsfall protein) oppose crystallisation / raise the supersaturation needed to crystallise. The 24-hour urine measures both.

### Evaluation for urolithiasis (Winter 2017)
- **History:** colic, UTI (urinary tract infection), previous stones, fluid and diet, drugs (calcium, vitamins C and D, acetazolamide, steroids), GI (gastrointestinal) disease, gout, family history.
- **Laboratory:** urinalysis (pH, crystals, pyuria) and culture; serum creatinine, electrolytes, calcium, uric acid; PTH (parathyroid hormone) if calcium is high; bicarbonate if RTA (renal tubular acidosis) is suspected.

| Imaging | Role |
|---|---|
| **NCCT** (non-contrast CT) | Gold standard: sensitivity 97%, specificity 95%. Size, site, density (uric acid 200–400 HU [Hounsfield units]; calcium oxalate 600–1200 HU), skin-to-stone distance |
| Low-dose NCCT (≤3 mSv) | Equal accuracy for stones ≥3 mm |
| Ultrasound | First line in pregnancy and children |
| KUB (plain X-ray) | Follow-up of radio-opaque stones only |
| CT urography | Anatomy before PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) |
| MAG3 (mercaptoacetyltriglycine) renogram or DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) scan | Split function |

- **Stone analysis:** infrared spectroscopy or X-ray diffraction.
- **Metabolic evaluation:** screening (history, serum, urine, stone analysis) for every first-time former. **High-risk formers** (recurrent, children, GI disease, obesity, diabetes, hyperparathyroidism, RTA, gout, family history, solitary kidney, large bilateral stones) need one or two **24-h urines** on a usual diet: volume, pH, calcium, oxalate, uric acid, citrate, sodium, potassium, creatinine (cystine if suspected). Bilateral staghorns are high risk by definition.

### Step 1: Screening evaluation (every first-time former; AUA [American Urological Association] and EAU [European Association of Urology])
| Component | What is assessed |
|---|---|
| History | GI (gastrointestinal) disease or bariatric surgery / bowel disease, gout, sarcoidosis, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection), family history; diet; lithogenic drugs: calcium, vitamins C and D, acetazolamide, topiramate, steroids, triamterene, indinavir |
| Blood | Creatinine, sodium, potassium (electrolytes), calcium, uric acid, bicarbonate; **PTH (parathyroid hormone)** if calcium is high or high-normal |
| Urine | pH, crystals (hexagonal = cystine), culture; cyanide–nitroprusside cystine screen, confirmed by quantitative 24-h cystine |
| **Stone analysis** | Infrared spectroscopy or X-ray diffraction: uric acid points to metabolic syndrome, cystine to a genetic cause, struvite to infection |
| Imaging | NCCT (non-contrast computed tomography): burden predicts future events; uric acid usually <500 HU (Hounsfield units) |

- Screening is a **triage**: low risk gets general advice; high risk goes on to 24-hour urine.

### Who is high risk (who needs evaluation)
- **Screening evaluation** for every first-time former (AUA, EAU); **24-h urine** for high-risk and recurrent formers: recurrent formers, **children**, solitary kidney, bilateral or large burden, GI disease or bypass, obesity, metabolic syndrome, diabetes, primary hyperparathyroidism, RTA (renal tubular acidosis), sarcoidosis, gout, family history; uric acid, cystine or infection stones.

### Step 2: Extended metabolic evaluation (24-h urine)
- **One or two 24-hour urines** (collections) on the usual diet, at least (≥) 4 weeks after the episode, stone-free and infection-free. Creatinine checks completeness / adequacy (men about 20–25, women about 15–20 mg/kg/day).

| Parameter | Desirable per day | Abnormality and meaning / causes |
|---|---|---|
| Volume | **>2.5 L** | Low volume: commonest, easiest to fix |
| Calcium | <250 mg women, <300 mg men (or <4 mg/kg) | **Hypercalciuria**: absorptive (PTH suppressed), renal leak (PTH high, serum calcium normal), resorptive (primary hyperparathyroidism) |
| Oxalate | **<40 mg** | Dietary; **enteric** (fat malabsorption, gastric bypass: fat binds calcium, freeing oxalate; often 60–70 mg); **primary** (hepatic, often >100 mg, childhood; type 1 (AGXT gene) ~70%, type 2 (GRHPR), type 3 (HOGA1)) |
| Citrate | **>320 mg** | Hypocitraturia: distal RTA, diarrhoea, hypokalaemia, high animal protein |
| Uric acid | <600 mg (labs: <750 women, <800 men) | Hyperuricosuria promotes calcium oxalate stones |
| pH | 5.8–6.2 | <5.5 uric acid (metabolic syndrome); >6.5–7 calcium phosphate, RTA, infection |
| Sodium | <100 mmol | High sodium raises calcium excretion |
| Cystine | Negative screen | Cystinuria; keep <250 mg/L |

- **Uric acid stones** depend mainly on pH: below the pKa (5.5) uric acid is undissociated and insoluble.
- **Infection stones**: urease-splitting organisms, pH >7, struvite.
- **Also know:** magnesium, potassium, phosphate; hypomagnesiuria; RSS (relative supersaturation) is calculated from these and guides therapy; medullary sponge kidney and calyceal diverticulum (stasis plus metabolic defects); 2,8-dihydroxyadenine stones (APRT (adenine phosphoribosyltransferase) deficiency).

### Interpreting hypercalciuria (Pak classification)
| Type | Defect | Serum calcium | PTH | Fasting urine calcium |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Absorptive (20–40%) | Increased gut absorption | Normal | Normal or suppressed | Normal |
| Renal leak (5–8%) | Tubular calcium loss | Normal | High (secondary) | High |
| Resorptive (3–5%) | Primary hyperparathyroidism | High | High | High |
- Fast and calcium-load subtyping is no longer advised, because it does not change treatment.

### Special tests
- **Distal RTA:** urine pH >6, low citrate, calcium phosphate stones. Incomplete form (forms): oral **ammonium chloride load** (0.1 g/kg) or furosemide–fludrocortisone test fails to lower urine pH below 5.5.
- **Cystinuria:** autosomal recessive transport defect (cystine, ornithine, lysine, arginine); quantitative 24-hour cystine; solubility about 300 mg/L at pH 5 and 400 mg/L at pH 7, so aim for <250 mg/L.
- **Primary hyperoxaluria:** genetic testing (type 1, AGXT gene, about 70%).

```
Stone episode → screening evaluation
  Low risk → general advice
  High risk → 24-h urine ×1–2 → targeted therapy → repeat ≤6 months (within 6 months) → yearly
```

### Linking results to treatment (treatment linked to abnormality)
- **General**: urine >2.5 L, sodium ≤2.3 g/day, normal calcium, less animal protein, more fruit and vegetables.

| Abnormality | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Low volume | Fluid for urine >2.5 L/day |
| Hypercalciuria | **Thiazide** (hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg twice daily, chlorthalidone, indapamide) plus potassium citrate; plus sodium restriction |
| Primary hyperparathyroidism | **Parathyroidectomy** |
| Hypocitraturia, distal RTA | **Potassium citrate** 30–60 mEq/day |
| Enteric hyperoxaluria | Low fat and oxalate; **calcium with meals**; magnesium |
| Primary hyperoxaluria type 1 | Pyridoxine, **lumasiran** (RNAi (ribonucleic acid interference)); liver ± kidney transplant |
| Hyperuricosuria | **Allopurinol** 300 mg/day |
| Uric acid stones | **Potassium citrate** to (urine) pH 6.0–6.5 |
| Cystinuria | Urine ≥3 L/day; pH **7.0–7.5**; **tiopronin** |
| Struvite | Complete clearance; AHA (acetohydroxamic acid) if surgery impossible |

- **Borghi 1996**: urine >2 L/day cut 5-year recurrence (12% vs 27%). **Borghi 2002**: low-salt, low-protein, normal-calcium diet halved recurrence versus low calcium. **NOSTONE (2023)**: hydrochlorothiazide did not reduce recurrence.

### Special groups and follow-up
- **Children:** all evaluated (spot ratios if not toilet-trained; consider Dent disease, monogenic causes). **Pregnancy:** defer 24-hour urine (physiological hypercalciuria).
- **Follow-up (AUA):** repeat 24-hour urine **within 6 months** of starting therapy, then yearly (as in the flowchart); blood tests for drug effects (potassium on thiazide or citrate); analyse / re-analyse every new stone. Adherence is poor; citrus juices are only adjuncts.

### Recent advances
- **Lumasiran** (RNA interference) for primary hyperoxaluria type 1; **NOSTONE (2023):** hydrochlorothiazide did not reduce recurrence.

### Staghorn calculus
- **Definition:** a branched stone filling the renal pelvis and extending into the calyces. **Partial:** pelvis and some calyces; **complete:** pelvis and all major calyces.
- **Composition:** classically struvite (magnesium ammonium phosphate) with carbonate apatite. Bacterial **urease** (Proteus, Klebsiella, Ureaplasma) splits urea into ammonia, so urine pH rises above 7.2 and struvite crystallises inside a bacterial biofilm. Fewer than half of modern staghorns are infection stones; calcium phosphate, cystine and uric acid also occur.
- **Why treat:** untreated staghorns destroy the kidney and cause sepsis, XGP (xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis) and death.

### Work-up before surgery
- NCCT with coronal and 3D views: burden, calyceal anatomy, colon and spleen.
- **Renal function:** eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) and split function, because both kidneys are at risk.
- **Infection:** culture-specific antibiotics before surgery; drain an obstructed or infected kidney (nephrostomy through a calyx usable later for PCNL). Culture the stone.
- **Guy's stone score (2011)** predicts stone-free rate:

| Grade | Stone |
|---|---|
| I | Single stone in mid or lower pole or pelvis, simple anatomy |
| II | Single upper-pole stone; multiple stones in simple anatomy; or single stone in abnormal anatomy |
| III | Multiple stones in abnormal anatomy; calyceal diverticulum; partial staghorn |
| IV | Staghorn; or any stone in spina bifida or spinal injury |

- **Also know:** STONE score, CROES nomogram, S-ReSC score.

### Treatment options (AUA [American Urological Association], EAU [European Association of Urology])
- **Goal: complete clearance**, because each fragment is an infected nidus; also clear infection and preserve function.

| Option | Role |
|---|---|
| **PCNL** | **First line.** Multiple tracts if needed; upper-pole access reaches pelvis, lower pole and ureteropelvic junction. Flexible nephroscopy raises clearance; second-look for residue |
| **ECIRS** (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery) | PCNL (usually supine) plus retrograde flexible ureteroscopy: fewer tracts, less bleeding, higher clearance |
| Staged RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) | Selected partial staghorns; patients who cannot stop anticoagulants |
| SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) | Not as monotherapy; adjunct for residual fragments |
| **Anatrophic nephrolithotomy** (Boyce–Smith) | Incision along Brödel's avascular line, artery clamped, cold ischaemia, infundibula reconstructed. Now rare; when endoscopy cannot clear the stone in a reasonable number of sessions |
| **Nephrectomy** | Poorly functioning kidney (split below about 10–20%) that is infected or painful; XGP |

### Bilateral staghorns: sequence
```
Sepsis or obstruction on either side?
  Yes -> drain + antibiotics -> treat that side once sterile
  No  -> treat the better-functioning (or symptomatic) kidney first
              |
  NCCT: stone-free? -> second look if residue
              |
  Other kidney after recovery
  (selected: same-session bilateral PCNL, or PCNL + contralateral RIRS)
```
- **Why the better kidney first:** it secures renal function early; some start with the simpler side. No trial defines the order.
- **Same-session bilateral surgery:** feasible at experienced centres if the first side was short and uncomplicated; saves an anaesthetic but risks bilateral bleeding or obstruction.
- Check creatinine after each side. Remove a non-functioning kidney only after the other side is cleared.

### Complications and follow-up (staghorn)
- PCNL risks: bleeding (transfusion about 1%; angioembolisation), sepsis, pleural, colon, spleen or liver injury.
- NCCT for stone-free status; treat residual fragments >4 mm. Surveillance cultures.
- 24-h urine even in struvite formers, then targeted therapy; urine ≥2.5 L/day.
- **AHA (acetohydroxamic acid)** 250 mg three times daily, a urease inhibitor, only when surgery is exhausted. Avoid if creatinine >2.5 mg/dL or in pregnancy; causes DVT (deep vein thrombosis), haemolytic anaemia.

**Diagram to draw:** supersaturation zones (undersaturated, metastable, unstable) and the evaluation algorithm; staghorn with an upper-pole PCNL tract and a retrograde flexible scope (ECIRS).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Screening for every first-time former; 24-hour urine for high-risk and recurrent formers.
> - Targets: volume >2.5 L, oxalate <40 mg, citrate >320 mg, calcium <250/300 mg.
> - Distal RTA: ammonium chloride (NH4Cl) fails to lower pH below (<) 5.5; cystine <250 mg/L at pH 7.0–7.5.
> - Borghi 1996: fluid halves recurrence; repeat 24-hour urine within 6 months.
> - NCCT gold standard (97%/95%); 24-h urine for high-risk formers.
> - Staghorn: PCNL first line; complete clearance is the goal.
> - Bilateral: drain an infected side first, then the better kidney.
> - AHA only when surgery is not possible.

## Renal trauma (renal parenchymal and vascular injuries, including grade IV): diagnosis and management
**Asked as:**
- Traumatic renal vascular parenchymal injuries : diagnosis and management. — *Summer 2017 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Diagnosis and Treatment of renal trauma — *Practice Set 9 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Grade 4 Renal trauma. — *Practice Set 3 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(Practice Set 9 Paper II Q1: also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q2; grade IV in Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q3c)
(Practice Set 3 Paper II Q3c: renal trauma also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

*Assumed meaning: diagnosis and management of traumatic renal parenchymal and renal vascular injuries.*

### Introduction
- Kidney is the **commonest urological organ injured** (up to 5% of all trauma); **blunt injury causes 80–90%**; firearms cause most penetrating injuries.
- **Rapid deceleration** stretches the kidney at its fixed points (hilum and UPJ, ureteropelvic junction): arterial intimal tear and thrombosis, vein disruption, pedicle or UPJ avulsion.
- Main renal artery injury is rare (about 0.05% of blunt trauma).

### Classification: AAST (American Association for the Surgery of Trauma) 2018
| Grade | Injury |
|---|---|
| I | Subcapsular haematoma and/or contusion, no laceration |
| II | Perirenal haematoma within Gerota's fascia; laceration ≤1 cm, no extravasation |
| III | Laceration >1 cm without collecting-system injury; **any vascular injury or active bleeding contained within Gerota's fascia** |
| IV | Laceration into collecting system with extravasation; renal pelvis laceration and/or complete UPJ disruption; **segmental artery or vein injury**; active bleeding beyond Gerota's fascia; segmental or complete infarction (thrombosis) |
| V | **Main renal artery or vein laceration or hilar avulsion**; devascularised kidney with active bleeding; shattered kidney |
- Advance one grade for multiple injuries, up to grade III. Change from 1989: main vessel injury moved from IV to V; pseudoaneurysm and AVF (arteriovenous fistula) now grade IV.

#### Definition: AAST 2018, grade IV in detail
- Laceration into the **collecting system with urinary extravasation**.
- **Renal pelvis laceration and/or complete UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) disruption.**
- **Segmental artery or vein injury** (pseudoaneurysm, AVF [arteriovenous fistula]).
- **Active bleeding beyond Gerota's fascia.**
- **Segmental or complete infarction** from thrombosis without active bleeding.
- Change from 1989: main-vessel injury moved to grade V; segmental vascular and UPJ injuries added to grade IV.

### Clinical diagnosis
**History and examination**
- Mechanism is the most important (key) history; mechanism matters. Signs: flank bruise or tenderness, **fractured ribs 9–12**, flank or low chest wound.

**Haematuria**
- Test the **first urine sample**. Significant: gross haematuria, or microscopic (microhaematuria) (>5 RBC [red blood cells] per high-power field, hpf) with **shock (SBP [systolic blood pressure] <90 mmHg)**.
- Degree does **not** match severity; it may be **absent in pedicle injury** and UPJ avulsion (in about half of UPJ injuries), and in grade IV.

### Indications for imaging (AUA (American Urological Association) and EAU (European Association of Urology))
1. Blunt trauma with gross haematuria.
2. Blunt trauma with microhaematuria and **SBP (systolic blood pressure) <90 mmHg** at any time.
3. Rapid deceleration (high-speed collision, fall), even without haematuria.
4. Penetrating trauma with likely renal injury, any haematuria.
5. Children: image liberally (hypotension appears late).
- Microhaematuria without shock or deceleration (microhaematuria alone without shock): observe without imaging.

### Imaging
- **Contrast-enhanced CT (computed tomography)** is the gold standard. Arterial/nephrographic phase (~80 s) shows bleeding; **delayed excretory phase** (5–10 min / minutes) shows urinary extravasation (urine leak).
- **CT red flags:**

| Finding | Suggests |
|---|---|
| Medial haematoma | Pedicle (vascular) injury |
| Medial extravasation, no contrast in distal ureter (without distal ureteric contrast) | Renal pelvis or UPJ avulsion |
| Absent enhancement, cortical rim sign | Main renal artery occlusion |
| Active contrast blush (vascular contrast blush) | Arterial bleeding needing AE (angioembolisation) |
| Haematoma rim >4 cm (another source: >3.5–4 cm), medial laceration | High risk of intervention; bleeding likely to need intervention (≥2 of rim, blush, medial laceration predict intervention) |
- **One-shot IVP (intravenous pyelography)** on table (2 mL/kg, one film at 10 min) in the unstable patient: confirms a **functioning contralateral kidney**. FAST (focused assessment with sonography for trauma) is non-specific. Renal vein injury is poorly seen on CT.

### Management
```
Unstable despite resuscitation → laparotomy (± damage control)
                                  (or angioembolisation / AE if team ready, else laparotomy)
Stable → stage with CT
   Grade I–III → non-operative
   Grade IV–V  → non-operative in selected; AE for active bleeding
   Active blush / pseudoaneurysm / AVF → superselective AE
   Extravasation → observe (90% heal); stent ± nephrostomy if urinoma enlarges,
                   fever, obstruction, or UPJ/pelvis injury
```
- **Non-operative**: bed rest until gross haematuria clears, serial haemoglobin (**every 4–6 h**); success >90% in blunt injury and selected penetrating ones. **Non-operative success** about 85% for grade IV–V even in penetrating series; most failures need only AE, stent or nephrostomy.
- Failure: instability, or falling haemoglobin despite 2–3 units of blood.
- Urinary extravasation heals spontaneously in **76–90%** (another source: up to 90%).
- **Angioembolisation**: superselective coils for active bleeding, pseudoaneurysm, AVF or delayed bleeding; repeat AE often succeeds.
- **Reimaging** (reimage / repeat CT at ~48 hours) for grade IV–V with deep laceration (deep lacerations), or new fever, pain or falling haematocrit (routine reimaging debated).

### Indications for exploration
| Absolute | Relative |
|---|---|
| Haemodynamic instability, no or transient response | Extravasation with devitalised parenchyma (>25%; lowers the threshold) |
| Expanding or pulsatile retroperitoneal haematoma | Arterial thrombosis |
| Suspected pedicle avulsion | Associated colon or pancreatic injury |
| UPJ avulsion | Incomplete staging |

### Operative technique (operative steps)
- Midline transabdominal (laparotomy); life-threatening injuries first.
- **Vascular control before opening Gerota's fascia**: lift small bowel; incise retroperitoneum over the aorta above the inferior mesenteric artery → find the left renal vein crossing the aorta → loop vein and both arteries; then reflect colon, open Gerota's fascia.
- **Renorrhaphy**: debride → suture-ligate vessels → watertight collecting-system closure → close defect over bolster → omental cover → drain. Polar injury: partial nephrectomy.
- **Nephrectomy** if unreconstructable or unstable. **Damage control**: pack, resuscitate in ICU (intensive care unit), relook (return) at 24–48 h.

### Renovascular injury
- **Main artery thrombosis** (intimal tear): unilateral with normal other kidney → observe (revascularisation rarely succeeds). **Solitary or bilateral → revascularise** (warm ischaemia ideally <120 min); endovascular stent for (or stent an) intimal flap.
- **Vein**: left renal vein can be ligated near the IVC (inferior vena cava) (gonadal, adrenal collaterals); right cannot. Repair with 5-0 vascular suture; segmental veins may be ligated.

### Complications
- **Delayed bleeding** (within 3 weeks; usually within 21 days): AE (embolise).
- Urinoma or abscess: stent, nephrostomy, drain.
- **Hypertension**: Goldblatt (arterial stenosis), **Page kidney** (haematoma compression), segmental ischaemia, AVF.
- Hydronephrosis, loss of function; **also know:** AKI (acute kidney injury).

### Follow-up
- Blood pressure, urinalysis, creatinine; EAU: DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid) scan for salvaged grade III–V kidneys; imaging if new symptoms.

**Diagram to draw:** left renal vein crossing the aorta, with vessel loops on the vein and artery. For grade IV: kidney with laceration into a calyx, urinoma and segmental pseudoaneurysm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AAST 2018: segmental vessel injury grade IV; main vessel grade V.
> - Grade IV = collecting system, UPJ, segmental vessel, bleeding beyond Gerota's, infarction.
> - Image if gross haematuria, microhaematuria with SBP <90, deceleration or penetrating injury.
> - Delayed CT phase is essential; blush means AE.
> - Stable patients, even grade IV–V: non-operative ± AE (angioembolisation).
> - Most stable grade IV heal non-operatively; extravasation resolves in up to 90%.
> - Revascularise only a solitary kidney or bilateral injuries.

## ESWL: mechanism, indications, factors affecting outcome, complications and current status
**Asked as:**
- Describe indication, mechanism of stone fragmentation and current status of ESWL? — *Summer 2019 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
