- Describe factors affecting outcome of ESWL. Discuss complications of ESWL and strategies to avoid them. — *Summer 2021 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)* (Mechanism, indications and current status are answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q2.)
- Desc. factors influencing outcome of ESWL. Discuss complic. & strategies to avoid them. — *Practice Set 13 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)* (also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q1; mechanism and indications in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q2)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

### Introduction
- ESWL (extracorporeal shock wave lithotripsy), now SWL, breaks stones with focused acoustic shock waves generated outside the body (generated at F1 and focused on the stone at F2). First human use 1980 (Dornier HM1).
- A shock wave is a short pulse: peak positive pressure 30–100 MPa, then a negative tail of about −10 MPa.
- Stones break by spallation, shear, squeezing and **cavitation**. Cavitation also causes most tissue injury, so steps that improve outcome usually reduce complications too.
- Outcome = SFR (stone-free rate) and need for retreatment.

### Components of a lithotripter
- Energy source at F1 (first focus); focusing system; coupling (water or gel); fluoroscopy and/or ultrasound to place the stone at F2. A focal zone wider than the stone improves breakage.

| Energy source | Principle | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|---|
| Electrohydraulic (HM3) | Underwater spark; ellipsoid reflector | Most effective | Electrode wear; variable shocks |
| Piezoelectric | Ceramic elements on a spherical dish | Long life; little pain | Low energy; tiny focal zone |
| Electromagnetic (modern machines) | Coil moves a metal membrane; lens focuses | Consistent; high energy at F2 | — |

### Mechanisms of stone fragmentation
- Each shock creates stress inside the stone. Above a critical value, cracks form; repeated shocks make them grow and join (**fatigue**).

| Mechanism | How it works |
|---|---|
| **Spallation** | Wave reflects and inverts at the back surface of the stone; tension breaks off the distal face |
| **Shear and tear (superfocusing)** | Waves inside the stone reflect and focus; cracks form where shear peaks |
| **Quasistatic squeezing** | Wave travels faster in stone than fluid; a ring of pressure compresses the stone |
| **Dynamic squeezing** | Shear waves at the stone edges combine; strongest force in initial breakage |
| **Cavitation** | Negative phase forms bubbles; collapse erodes the surface. Excess bubbles shield the stone and injure tissue |

### Indications
- **Renal (AUA/EAU):** <2 cm outside the lower pole and lower pole <1 cm (SWL or URS [ureteroscopy]); EAU: 10–20 mm equal to endourology; >20 mm only after PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy).
- **Ureteric:** any level, best <10 mm; emergency SWL within 48 hours works well. Patients avoiding anaesthesia and stents; children.
- **Poor choices:** >2 cm or staghorn; lower pole >1 cm (**Lower Pole I**: stone-free 21% SWL vs 91% PCNL); cystine, brushite, calcium oxalate monohydrate; infection stones.

### Contraindications
- **Absolute:** pregnancy; uncorrected coagulopathy or (ongoing) antithrombotics; untreated UTI (urinary tract infection); obstruction distal to the stone; nearby arterial aneurysm (aneurysm near the stone; aortic >4 cm); severe uncontrolled (untreated) hypertension.
- **Relative:** obesity or skeletal deformity preventing targeting; pacemaker.

### Factors affecting (influencing) outcome / predictors of success
**1. Stone factors**

| Factor | Favourable | Unfavourable |
|---|---|---|
| Size (most important) | <10 mm | Renal >20 mm (PCNL [percutaneous nephrolithotomy] first); ureteric ≥10 mm favours URS (ureteroscopy) |
| Density on NCCT (non-contrast computed tomography / non-contrast CT [computed tomography]) | <900 HU (Hounsfield units) clears best | >1000 HU predicts failure |
| Composition | Calcium oxalate dihydrate, uric acid | Cystine, brushite, calcium oxalate monohydrate (dense, homogeneous) |
| Impaction (ureter) | Recent, mobile | Chronic impaction (soft-tissue ring of thick ureteral wall around the stone on CT, unchanged position): may be the strongest predictor |
| Burden and site | Single, renal pelvis or upper calyx | Multiple sites; lower pole >1 cm |

- Infection stones and stones in several sites: prefer a more efficient modality.

**2. Renal anatomical factors**
- **Lower pole (lower pole anatomy):** fragments drain against gravity. **Lower Pole I (Albala, 2001):** stones >1 cm, SFR **21% SWL vs 91% PCNL**. A steep infundibulopelvic angle and a long (>10 mm), narrow (<5 mm) infundibulum reduce clearance.
- Obstruction distal to the stone (UPJO [ureteropelvic junction obstruction], infundibular stenosis, calyceal diverticulum) traps fragments. Horseshoe and ectopic kidneys drain poorly.

**3. Patient factors**
- **SSD (skin-to-stone distance) >10 cm** (obesity) predicts failure. Skeletal deformity can prevent targeting.
- Respiratory movement of 10–15 mm moves the stone out of F2.
- **Triple D score:** stone density, stone volume and skin-to-stone distance together predict success.

**4. Machine and technique factors (technique to optimise results)**
1. **Energy source:** electrohydraulic (HM3) breaks best; piezoelectric has low energy and a tiny focal zone; modern machines are electromagnetic. A focal zone wider than the stone breaks better.
2. Treat infection; stop antithrombotics; control blood pressure. No routine stent: it does not improve clearance.
3. **Analgesia:** good analgesia, GA (general anaesthesia) or sedation keeps the stone in focus, because stone movement reduces breakage.
4. **Coupling:** gel as a mound, free of air; air pockets in the gel cut fragmentation by up to two-fifths.
5. **Rate 60–90 shocks/min** beats 120/min, because the bubble cloud clears between shocks (bubbles clear between shocks), so less shielding and renal injury.
6. **Ramping:** start at low energy and step up (ramping energy up also helps); this causes protective vasoconstriction.
7. Positioning (prone for mid-ureteric stones over the sacrum), continuous imaging (monitor with imaging), experienced team. Little gain beyond two sessions (more than two sessions add little).
8. **Adjuncts:** α-blockers (faster fragment clearance); potassium citrate; percussion, diuresis and inversion for lower pole fragments.

### Complications

| Group | Complication | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| Fragment-related | Renal colic; **steinstrasse** ("stone street" of fragments in the ureter); residual fragments | Steinstrasse rises several-fold with burden >2 cm² |
| Infective | UTI (urinary tract infection), pyelonephritis, urosepsis (sepsis) | Infection stones, stents |
| Renal tissue injury | Haematuria (transient, common); **subcapsular or perirenal haematoma** (minority; rarely embolisation) | Risk: age, hypertension, obesity, diabetes, coronary disease, thrombocytopenia, anticoagulation, high energy, many shocks, narrow-focus machines |
| Other organs | Liver, spleen, pancreas, colon / bowel, lung (children), skeletal muscle | Very rare |
| Cardiac | Arrhythmia during treatment | Pause or ECG (electrocardiogram) gating |
| Skin | Petechiae, bruising | Minor |

- **Refuted long-term effects** (**not** proven): new hypertension, diabetes, decline in (loss of) renal function and infertility.

### Strategies to avoid complications
1. **Select.** Absolute contraindications as above. Send large, dense, lower pole or impacted stones to URS or PCNL.
2. **Prepare.** Urine culture; treat bacteriuria. Prophylaxis only with risk factors (stent, nephrostomy, struvite, recent UTI, bowel reconstruction). Stop antithrombotics before and for the day and (about) 48 hours after. Control blood pressure; reprogramme / check pacemakers.
3. **Protect the kidney.** Slow rate; **slow ramping** (ramp over at least 4 minutes) (induces protective renal vasoconstriction and lowers haematoma risk); fewest shocks and lowest effective energy.
4. **Prevent steinstrasse.** PCNL or URS for large burdens. Routine stenting does not improve SFR, though it may reduce steinstrasse with large stones. α-blockers after SWL.
5. **Treat early.** Steinstrasse: MET (medical expulsive therapy), SWL to the lead fragment or URS; **nephrostomy if fever or obstruction**. Haematoma: bed rest, transfusion, embolisation if bleeding continues.
6. **Follow up** with KUB (kidney-ureter-bladder radiograph) and ultrasound.

### Current status
- **Declining use worldwide.** URS has grown with small digital scopes and lasers, giving higher single-session stone-free rates. Newer lithotripters are also less effective than the HM3.
- **SWL vs URS:** SWL has fewer complications, no stent or general anaesthesia, lower cost, but more retreatment. Guidelines still list it first-line for small renal and ureteric stones.

### Recent advances
- **Burst wave lithotripsy:** bursts of 5–100 ultrasound cycles at 4–8 MPa; less cavitation; done awake (no anaesthesia); uniform fragments.
- **Ultrasonic propulsion** moves fragments to help them pass.

**Diagram to draw:** ellipsoid reflector with F1 (spark) and F2 (stone), stone and gel coupling, with spallation and squeezing arrows on the stone; box of failure predictors.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Spallation, squeezing, shear, cavitation; dynamic squeezing strongest.
> - Size is the main predictor; HU >1000 and SSD >10 cm predict failure.
> - Lower Pole I: SFR 21% SWL vs 91% PCNL for lower pole stones >1 cm.
> - Slow rate (60–90 shocks/min), ramping and good coupling improve breakage and reduce injury.
> - Steinstrasse risk rises with burden >2 cm²; no routine stent.
> - Use is declining as URS grows; burst wave lithotripsy is coming.

## Management of upper ureteric stones
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q2
(SWL also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q2 and 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q1; flexible URS in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3b)

### Introduction
- **Upper (proximal) ureter:** UPJ (ureteropelvic junction) to the upper border of the sacroiliac joint.
- Proximal stones pass least often, so many need intervention. Dangers: renal damage and sepsis.

### Evaluation
- **NCCT (non-contrast CT)**: gold standard; gives size, density (HU, Hounsfield units), SSD (skin-to-stone distance) and impaction. KUB (kidney–ureter–bladder radiograph) for SWL follow-up; creatinine, urine culture, coagulation.

### 1. Emergency: drain first
- **AUA (American Urological Association) indications for urgent intervention:** infection with obstruction, bilateral obstruction, solitary kidney, immunosuppression, renal deterioration, refractory pain or vomiting.
- **Obstructed infected kidney: drain only** (double-J stent or PCN, percutaneous nephrostomy; equal for sepsis); definitive treatment later. PCN suits large stones (stent fails if transverse diameter ≥9.5 mm).

### 2. Observation and MET (medical expulsive therapy): limits
| Spontaneous passage | Rate |
|---|---|
| By size | 1–4 mm 78%; 5–7 mm 60%; 8–10 mm 39% |
| By site | UVJ (ureterovesical junction) 92%; distal 75%; mid 60%; **proximal 48%** |

- **Observation** suits small (<5–6 mm) uncomplicated stones: NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) first.
- **MET (alpha-blockers, tamsulosin or silodosin):** reduce spasm so a larger urine bolus pushes the stone. Benefit is clear only for **distal stones 5–10 mm**; meta-analysis shows **no benefit for proximal stones**. SUSPEND (2015) showed no overall benefit.
- Trial of passage is limited to **4–6 weeks**, with imaging.

### 3. Choice of active treatment
- Factors: stone (size, density, impaction), patient (habitus, anticoagulation, preference), surgeon and equipment.

**EAU (European Association of Urology) recommendation for proximal ureteric stones**
| Size | First choice | Alternative |
|---|---|---|
| <10 mm | SWL or URS (ureteroscopy) | – |
| >10 mm | URS (retrograde or antegrade) | SWL |
| Large (>15–20 mm), impacted | PCNL with antegrade URS | Laparoscopic ureterolithotomy |

**SWL versus URS**
| | SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) | URS |
|---|---|---|
| Stone-free rate (AUA/EAU meta-analysis, approximate) | <10 mm about 90%; >10 mm about 70% | <10 mm about 80%; >10 mm about 80% |
| Sessions | More retreatment | Single session; higher SFR at 2 weeks, equal at 3 months |
| Anaesthesia | Sedation or none | Usually general |
| Complications | Fewer | Perforation, stent pain |
| Anticoagulation | Must stop | Laser URS can continue |

- **SWL fails** with size >10–15 mm, impaction (CT "ring" sign), HU >1000, SSD >10 cm, cystine or COM (calcium oxalate monohydrate) stones.
- **SWL technique:** supine; 60–90 shocks/min; ramp energy; no routine pre-stent. Contraindicated in pregnancy, bleeding disorder, untreated UTI (urinary tract infection), distal obstruction, uncontrolled hypertension.

### 4. Ureteroscopy for proximal stones
- **Flexible URS** preferred: it follows a stone that migrates into the kidney.
- Safety wire; access sheath (12/14 Fr); tight ureter: pre-stent and return after 2–4 weeks.
- **Laser:** holmium:YAG (2120 nm) or thulium fibre laser; dusting or fragmenting; keep **≤10 W in the ureter** to avoid thermal injury.
- **Retropulsion** into the kidney: reduced by low-energy settings and antiretropulsion devices (Stone Cone, NTrap); pneumatic lithotripsy works poorly above the brim.
- **Push-back:** stone deliberately pushed into the renal pelvis with a catheter, then treated by SWL, RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) or PCNL; now largely replaced by in-situ flexible URS.
- Stent only for perforation, impaction, infection or a planned second stage.

### 5. Antegrade approach (PCNL with antegrade URS)
- Indications: large (>15–20 mm), hard or **impacted proximal stones**, failed retrograde access, urinary diversion, transplant, concomitant renal stones.
- **Upper or mid-pole posterior calyx** gives a straight line to the UPJ; flexible nephroscope or antegrade URS down the dilated ureter; high SFR.

### 6. Laparoscopic (or robotic) ureterolithotomy
- Indications: large impacted stones, failed SWL/URS, where endourology is unavailable, or with concomitant UPJ obstruction (pyeloplasty).
- Steps: retroperitoneal or transperitoneal access; identify the bulge; **clamp the ureter above the stone** to prevent migration; longitudinal ureterotomy; remove; double-J stent; close with 4-0 absorbable sutures; drain.
- High single-stage SFR; risk of urine leak. **Open ureterolithotomy** is now rare.

### Complications of treatment
| Complication | Points |
|---|---|
| Perforation | 0–4%; stop, stent about 4 weeks |
| **Avulsion** | 0.06–0.5%; **proximal third most at risk**; nephrostomy and delayed reconstruction |
| Stricture | 3–6%; image all after URS |
| Sepsis | About 5% after URS |
| SWL | Steinstrasse, haematoma, colic |

**Also know:** pregnancy: conservative, then stent or URS (no SWL).

### Recent advances
- Single-use digital scopes; pressure sensors; thulium fibre laser; robotic URS; **BWL (burst wave lithotripsy)**.

**Diagram to draw:** algorithm for proximal stone: drain if infected; <10 mm SWL or URS; >10 mm URS; large impacted antegrade or laparoscopic.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Proximal stones pass in only 48%; MET does not help proximal stones.
> - <10 mm: SWL equals URS; >10 mm: URS first (EAU).
> - Flexible URS chases migrated fragments; laser ≤10 W in the ureter.
> - Large impacted: antegrade PCNL or laparoscopic ureterolithotomy; avulsion risk highest in proximal third.

## Lower urinary tract trauma in pelvic fracture: classification, assessment and (early) management of bladder and urethral injuries
**Asked as:**
- Classification and treatment of lower track trauma in # pelvis — *Practice Set 6 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Pelvic # assessment and management — *Practice Set 8 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Early surgical intervention in urethral injuries — *Practice Set 16 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(lower tract trauma in pelvic fracture also answered in Practice Set 6 Paper 2, Q2) (posterior urethral injury also answered in Practice Set 5 Paper 3, Q1)

*Assumed meaning: lower urinary tract trauma (bladder and urethra) in pelvic fracture; urological assessment and management of lower urinary tract injury in pelvic fracture.*

### Introduction
- Pelvic fracture accompanies **83–95% of bladder injuries (ruptures)**, yet only **5–10% of pelvic fractures** injure the bladder. Urethral injury occurs in about **10% of male** and up to 6% of female pelvic fractures.
- 80–94% have other major injuries (associated injuries), which cause the deaths; long-term disability comes from wrong management.

### Mechanism
- **EPR (extraperitoneal rupture):** pelvic disruption (shearing) tears the bladder at its fascial attachments, or bone spicules lacerate it.
- **IPR (intraperitoneal rupture):** a blow on a **full bladder** bursts the dome, the weakest part covered by peritoneum.
- **PFUI (pelvic fracture urethral injury):** the posterior urethra is fixed by the puboprostatic ligaments, so displacement tears it at the **bulbomembranous junction**. The sphincter is usually avulsed but stays functional. Highest risk: straddle fracture (all four rami), vertically unstable fractures.

### Classification
**Bladder: AAST (American Association for the Surgery of Trauma) grades I–V**
| Grade | Injury |
|---|---|
| I | Contusion, intramural haematoma, partial-thickness laceration |
| II | Extraperitoneal wall laceration <2 cm |
| III | Extraperitoneal ≥2 cm, or intraperitoneal <2 cm |
| IV | Intraperitoneal ≥2 cm |
| V | Laceration extending into bladder neck or ureteric orifice (trigone) |
- Practical: contusion, EPR (simple or complex), IPR, combined.

**Urethra: Goldman I–V**
| Type | Injury |
|---|---|
| I | Posterior urethra stretched, intact |
| II | Disruption above the urogenital diaphragm |
| III | Disruption of membranous urethra through the urogenital diaphragm (commonest) |
| IV | Bladder neck injury extending into urethra (IVa: bladder-base injury mimicking IV) |
| V | Anterior urethral injury |
- Chapter's practical grading: **partial injury, minor distraction, major distraction** (a cystoscope cannot pass into the bladder); practically partial vs complete disruption. Stricture risk rises with each.
- Grades: posterior partial, minor or major distraction; anterior contusion, incomplete or complete.

### Assessment
- **ATLS (Advanced Trauma Life Support)** resuscitation first; pelvic binder and stabilisation.

**Clinical features**
- Bladder: **gross haematuria (nearly all)**, suprapubic pain, inability to void, free fluid.
- Urethra: **triad: blood at the meatus, inability to void, palpable bladder**. A "high-riding" prostate and butterfly perineal haematoma are unreliable. **Vaginal examination** in every female; **vaginal and rectal examination.**

### Investigations
- **RUG (retrograde urethrogram) first (before any catheter / before catheterisation) if blood at the meatus** (AUA [American Urological Association]); blood at the meatus mandates RUG. Method: 16 Fr catheter (catheter tip) in fossa navicularis, 3 mL balloon, penile traction, oblique position, 20 mL contrast, film during injection. Females: urethroscopy.
- **Cystography:** absolute indication: **gross haematuria + pelvic fracture** (rupture in ~29%). Relative: gross haematuria alone; microscopic haematuria with fracture.
  - **CT cystography:** retrograde fill with **350 mL** of 2–4% contrast; no drainage film needed.
  - Plain: scout, full-bladder and **drainage films** (posterior leaks otherwise missed); plain cystogram needs a drainage film.
  - Clamping the catheter after IV contrast is inadequate.
- **EPR:** flame-shaped pelvic contrast. **IPR:** contrast outlines (outlining) bowel loops.

### Treatment
**Resuscitation first (ATLS [Advanced Trauma Life Support]); pelvic stabilisation.**

**Principles (urethra)**
- Key decision: **immediate repair versus deliberate delay**. Clean, localised injuries are repaired at once; PFUI (pelvic fracture urethral injury) is drained and reconstructed later.

**Bladder**
- **Uncomplicated EPR:** 22 Fr urethral catheter 2–3 weeks; cystogram before removal; repair if leak persists beyond 4 weeks.
- **Immediate repair:** IPR; penetrating injury; bladder neck injury; bone fragments (bone fragment) in the bladder; rectal or vaginal injury; poor drainage or clots; open pelvic fixation or laparotomy for other reasons.
- IPR: open or laparoscopic two-layer closure; inspect ureteric orifices; cystogram at 7–10 days. At laparotomy, repair EPR from inside the bladder, leaving the pelvic haematoma undisturbed.
- Suprapubic tube adds nothing over urethral catheter (AUA). Antibiotics only around IPR repair.
- Rectal or vaginal injury: separate the walls, no overlapping suture lines, interpose tissue.

**Urethra: acute (posterior urethra, PFUI)**
```
Blood at meatus → RUG
  Partial → one gentle catheter attempt (confirm position)
  Complete → SPC (suprapubic catheter): percutaneous 16 Fr
             or open (if bladder repair needed)
             ± brief primary realignment if stable
             (flexible cystoscopy, antegrade + retrograde)
  Bladder neck/rectal injury → immediate repair
Delayed (2–3 months) → up-and-down-o-gram → perineal excision
  and primary anastomosis (corporal split → pubectomy → rerouting)
```
- **SPC (suprapubic cystostomy) is the standard first step:** percutaneous 16 Fr Foley via peel-away sheath, or open. Avoid small "punch" tubes. SPC is safe with pelvic plating (AUA); place it high.
- **Primary realignment** (AUA [American Urological Association] Urotrauma option): brief attempt in a stable patient, acutely or within days, by combined antegrade and retrograde flexible cystoscopy (avoid prolonged attempts).
  - Catheter out at 4–6 weeks; keep the SPC; remove it 7–14 days later if voiding well.
  - It does **not** lower urethroplasty rates (**no difference** in urethroplasty rates vs SPC); most still form a **1–2 cm stenosis**. Realignment does not add ED (erectile dysfunction) or incontinence.
- Partial tear: one gentle catheter attempt.
- **Immediate open repair** only for **bladder neck or rectal injury**, or when laparotomy is needed anyway.
- **Also know:** female urethral injuries are usually repaired early; in boys tears may extend into the bladder neck.

**Urethra: delayed (2–3 months)**
- **Up-and-down-o-gram** (cystogram + RUG) for gap length; MRI for complex cases; flexible cystoscopy if bladder neck does not open.
- **Perineal excision and primary anastomosis (EPA)** (delayed anastomotic urethroplasty) is the treatment of choice: lithotomy → mobilise bulbar urethra → excise all scar → identify prostatic apex → tension-free spatulated anastomosis.
- Escalating steps for long gaps: **corporal splitting → inferior pubectomy → supracrural rerouting**. Abdominoperineal approach for fistula, bladder neck injury, failed repair, children.
- **Not** "cut-to-the-light" or repeated DVIU (direct visual internal urethrotomy) for gaps ≥1 cm.

**Anterior urethra: early management**
| Injury | Early management |
|---|---|
| Contusion, incomplete straddle tear | Urethral catheter alone |
| Major straddle injury | SPC (standard); primary realignment for lesser injuries; delayed anastomotic urethroplasty |
| **Low-velocity gunshot or stab** | **Immediate primary repair:** debride minimally, spatulate, tension-free anastomosis over a catheter |
| High-velocity or close-range shotgun | SPC, staged repair |
| **Penile fracture with urethral tear** | **Immediate repair:** partial tear oversewn; complete tear debrided and anastomosed; catheter 2–3 weeks |

- Penile fracture: urethral injury in about 20% of Western and 3% of Asian series; **intraoperative flexible cystoscopy** before catheterisation. Limit spongiosal debridement.

### Complications
- ED (erectile dysfunction) up to 82%, mainly from the injury (nerve, arterial, venous).
- Stricture: restenosis 5–15% (DVIU usually works); incontinence <4% (uncommon).
- Missed IPR: ascites, acidosis, azotaemia (peritoneal reabsorption).
- Fistula, abscess, neurogenic voiding dysfunction; follow up ≥1 year.

**Diagram to draw:** pelvis with EPR (flame-shaped) vs IPR on cystogram, and bulbomembranous distraction defect; RUG showing partial vs complete posterior urethral disruption.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Blood at meatus → RUG before catheter.
> - Gross haematuria + pelvic fracture = retrograde CT cystogram (350 mL).
> - EPR: catheter 2–3 weeks; IPR: immediate repair.
> - PFUI: SPC (standard), then delayed perineal anastomotic urethroplasty at 2–3 months.
> - Primary realignment: brief, endoscopic; equal urethroplasty rates.
> - Immediate repair: bladder neck or rectal injury (repair at once), penile fracture urethra, low-velocity penetrating injury.

## Stone formation (etiopathogenesis) and medical management of urolithiasis / renal calculi
**Asked as:**
- Describe etiopathogenesis of stone formation and medical management of urolithiasis — *Practice Set 7 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe medical management of renal calculi — *Practice Set 12 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

### Introduction
- Urolithiasis is a **chronic metabolic disease** punctuated by stone episodes; recurrence is up to 50% within 5 years. Removal is surgical; recurrence is metabolic.
- **Composition**: calcium oxalate about 60%, mixed with apatite about 20%, uric acid and struvite about 7% each, cystine 1–3%.

### Aetiology: risk factors
- **Epidemiological**: male sex (gap narrowing), hot climate and occupation (low urine volume), diet (animal protein, salt), family history.
- **Systemic**: **metabolic syndrome**, obesity, diabetes (insulin resistance lowers urine pH), gout, primary hyperparathyroidism, RTA (renal tubular acidosis), bowel disease or bypass, sarcoidosis.
- **Anatomical**: PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction, horseshoe kidney, calyceal diverticulum, medullary sponge kidney (stasis plus metabolic defects).
- **Drugs**: direct (indinavir, triamterene); indirect (acetazolamide, topiramate, vitamin C and D excess).

### Physicochemistry: state of saturation
| State | Concentration product (CP) | What happens |
|---|---|---|
| Undersaturated | < Ksp (solubility product) | No crystals; stones may dissolve |
| **Metastable** | Between Ksp and Kf (formation product) | No spontaneous nucleation; growth on existing crystals; inhibitors work. **Zone of therapy** |
| Unstable | > Kf | Spontaneous nucleation, growth, aggregation |

- Crystals form in metastable urine by local high CP, stasis, or **heterogeneous nucleation** on cells, debris or other crystals (needs less energy).

### Steps of stone formation
1. **Supersaturation** (low volume, high calcium, oxalate, uric acid; low citrate).
2. **Nucleation**, then **growth** and **aggregation** (aggregation can occlude a tubule within the 5–7 minute nephron transit).
3. **Retention**: fixed-particle theory. **Randall plaque** (calcium apatite) starts in the basement membrane of the thin loop of Henle, spreads through the interstitium, erodes the papillary urothelium and anchors calcium oxalate growth (idiopathic formers). **Ductal plugs** (crystals in collecting ducts) suggest a secondary cause (enteric hyperoxaluria, distal RTA, hyperparathyroidism, cystinuria).

### Inhibitors, promoters, matrix
- **Inhibitors**: **citrate** (complexes calcium), **magnesium** (complexes oxalate), pyrophosphate, glycosaminoglycans, nephrocalcin, **Tamm-Horsfall protein**, osteopontin, bikunin. There is **no inhibitor of uric acid**.
- **Matrix** (about 2.5% of stone weight; matrix substance A): role uncertain.

### Pathogenesis by abnormality
| Abnormality | Mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Hypercalciuria** (commonest) | Absorptive (gut), renal leak (high PTH (parathyroid hormone)), resorptive (primary hyperparathyroidism) |
| Hyperoxaluria | Primary (hepatic enzyme defect, PH1 AGXT), **enteric** (fat binds calcium, freeing oxalate), dietary |
| Hyperuricosuria | Urate promotes calcium oxalate nucleation |
| **Hypocitraturia** | Distal RTA, diarrhoea, hypokalaemia, animal protein (intracellular acidosis) |
| **Uric acid** | **Low urine pH** (<5.5; pKa 5.5) more than excess uric acid |
| **Cystine** | Autosomal recessive defect in renal cystine reabsorption |
| **Struvite** | **Urease** (Proteus) splits urea → ammonia, pH >7.2 → magnesium ammonium phosphate + carbonate apatite; biofilm |

### Medical management
**Aims:** Relieve pain, dissolve suitable stones and **prevent recurrence** (up to 50% at 5 years).

**1. Acute colic (acute care) and MET (medical expulsive therapy)**
- **NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs)** first line; opioids only if refractory. Urgent drainage if infection with obstruction.
- **α-blocker** (tamsulosin; silodosin faster) for **distal ureteric stones 5–10 mm** after migration from the kidney: relaxes ureteric muscle. **SUSPEND (2015)** was negative because it included all sizes and sites. Trial of passage 4–6 weeks.

**2. Dissolution (chemolysis)**
- **Uric acid** stones (radiolucent) dissolve with oral potassium citrate to alkalinise (EAU (European Association of Urology): pH 7.0–7.2 for dissolution); allopurinol if hyperuricosuric.
- **Cystine:** alkalinise to pH 7.0–7.5 with thiol drugs. **Struvite:** acetohydroxamic acid occasionally dissolves.

**3. Prevention: general measures (every former / all formers)**
- **Fluid** for urine **≥2.5 L/day** (Borghi 1996: 12.1% vs 27% recurrence at 5 years; recurrence halved).
- **Normal calcium**, low sodium (low salt), less animal protein, more fruit and vegetables (Borghi 2002: halved recurrence versus low-calcium diet).

**4. Metabolic evaluation** (high-risk and recurrent formers): stone analysis, serum chemistry (serum calcium, uric acid, creatinine, PTH (parathyroid hormone) if needed), one or two **24-h (24-hour) urines**; repeat 24-hour urine within 6 months of therapy, then yearly.

**5. Selective drug therapy**
| Abnormality | Drug and dose | Rationale |
|---|---|---|
| **Hypercalciuria** | **Thiazide**: hydrochlorothiazide 25 mg twice daily, chlorthalidone 25–50 mg, indapamide 2.5 mg | Distal calcium reabsorption; add potassium citrate (hypokalaemia → hypocitraturia; thiazide lowers potassium and citrate) |
| **Hypocitraturia**, distal RTA (renal tubular acidosis) | **Potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day** | Alkali load reduces tubular citrate reabsorption |
| Hyperuricosuria | Allopurinol 300 mg/day | Watch hypersensitivity |
| Uric acid stones | Potassium citrate to **pH 6.0–6.5**; avoid >7.0 (calcium phosphate) | pH disease |
| Enteric hyperoxaluria | **Calcium with meals**, low oxalate and fat, magnesium | Binds gut oxalate |
| Primary hyperoxaluria type 1 | Pyridoxine; **lumasiran** (RNA (ribonucleic acid) interference) | ILLUMINATE-A |
| **Cystinuria** | Urine ≥3 L (another source: >3 L), pH **7.0–7.5**; **tiopronin** or D-penicillamine | Cystine <250 mg/L; mixed disulfide |
| **Struvite** | Complete clearance; AHA (acetohydroxamic acid) 250 mg three times daily (another source: two to three times daily) | Urease inhibitor; thromboembolism |

- **Also know:** recurrent calcium stones with normal work-up: thiazide or citrate (AUA (American Urological Association): thiazide or citrate for recurrent calcium formers even without abnormality); NOSTONE (2023): hydrochlorothiazide did not reduce recurrence; citrus juices are adjuncts.

**Diagram to draw:** saturation zones (Ksp, Kf) and Randall plaque on a papilla; flowchart from stone analysis to selective therapy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Metastable zone between Ksp and Kf is where inhibitors and therapy act.
> - Randall plaque = interstitial apatite anchor; ductal plugs suggest secondary cause.
> - Urine ≥2.5 L/day; normal calcium, low salt; MET for distal 5–10 mm stones.
> - Thiazide for hypercalciuria; potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day for hypocitraturia.
> - Uric acid pH 6.0–6.5; cystine pH 7.0–7.5 and <250 mg/L.

## Uric acid calculi: etiopathogenesis, medical management and allopurinol
**Asked as:**
- Discuss etiopathogenesis of uric acid calculi. Describe rationale of medical management, — *Practice Set 9 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Allopurinol — *Practice Set 10 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- Uric acid stones form **8–10%** of stones in the USA and are increasing with obesity and diabetes. They are **radiolucent** and can be **dissolved medically**.

### Etiopathogenesis
**1. Why humans form uric acid stones**
- Humans and apes lack hepatic **uricase**, which converts uric acid to allantoin (10–100 times more soluble). Blood and urine uric acid are therefore high.

**2. Physical chemistry: pH is the key**
- Uric acid is a weak acid, **pKa about 5.35–5.5**. At pH 5.5 half is free uric acid and half is urate.
- Free uric acid is poorly soluble (about 96–100 mg/L); sodium urate is about 20 times more soluble. At **pH 6.5, over 90% is ionised**.
- **No natural inhibitor** of uric acid crystallisation is known, so stone formation depends almost entirely on supersaturation.

**3. The three determinants**
| Determinant | Causes | Importance |
|---|---|---|
| **Low urine pH (<5.5)** | Insulin resistance, obesity, type 2 diabetes, gout, high animal-protein diet, chronic diarrhoea, ileostomy | **Most important**; most formers have normal uric acid excretion |
| **Low urine volume** | Hot climate or occupation, diarrhoea, dehydration | Concentrates uric acid |
| **Hyperuricosuria (>600 mg/day)** | Purine-rich diet, gout, myeloproliferative disorders, tumour lysis, uricosuric drugs, inherited defects | Least often the driver in idiopathic cases |

**4. Why urine is too acid: insulin resistance**
- Insulin normally stimulates **ammonia production** from glutamine in the proximal tubule and the Na⁺/H⁺ exchanger NHE3.
- Insulin resistance reduces ammonium excretion. Acid is then buffered as titratable acid, so urine pH falls.
- **Renal lipotoxicity:** fat accumulates in the proximal tubule, which then burns fatty acids instead of glutamine, so ammoniagenesis falls further.
- Endogenous acid production is also higher. Uric acid stones are thus a **renal manifestation of the metabolic syndrome**; about a third of stones in diabetics are uric acid.

**5. Aetiological categories**
- **Congenital:** HGPRT (hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase) deficiency (Lesch–Nyhan), PRPP (phosphoribosylpyrophosphate) synthetase overactivity, hereditary renal hypouricaemia (URAT1, the urate transporter).
- **Idiopathic:** low urine pH without gout ("gouty diathesis").

**6. Uric acid and calcium stones**
- In **hyperuricosuric calcium oxalate stone disease**, urate promotes calcium oxalate by removing inhibitors, by "salting out" and by **epitaxy** (urate crystals act as a nidus).
- **Also know:** ammonium acid urate stones (laxative abuse, diarrhoea, ileostomy); theobromine (cocoa) inhibits uric acid crystallisation in vitro.

### Diagnosis in brief
- Radiolucent on X-ray; CT **200–400 HU (Hounsfield units)**; **dual-energy CT** identifies uric acid.
- Urine pH persistently <5.5; serum uric acid; 24-hour urine; screen for diabetes.

### Rationale of medical management
**Principle:** uric acid stones are a **"pH disease" more than a uric acid disease**. Raising pH turns insoluble uric acid into soluble urate. Diluting urine lowers supersaturation. Lowering uric acid production helps only when excretion is high.

**1. Fluids**
- Urine output **at least 2.5 L/day** lowers uric acid concentration.

**2. Diet and lifestyle**
- Limit non-dairy **animal protein** (it gives purines and an acid load; dietary purines provide about 30% of urinary uric acid).
- More fruit and vegetables (alkali load). Weight loss, exercise and glucose control correct the underlying insulin resistance.

**3. Urinary alkalinisation (mainstay)**
- **Potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day** in two or three doses. Citrate is metabolised to bicarbonate, raising urine pH.
- **Target pH 6.0–6.5** for prevention (EAU [European Association of Urology]: 6.2–6.8); **avoid pH >7.0**, which risks calcium phosphate stones.
- **Why potassium, not sodium:** sodium alkali raises urinary calcium and can cause calcium stones. Sodium bicarbonate or sodium citrate is used if potassium is unsafe (renal impairment, hyperkalaemia).
- Patients **monitor urine pH with dipsticks**.

**4. Dissolution (oral chemolysis)**
- Pure uric acid stones dissolve over weeks to months. **EAU: pH 7.0–7.2**; stent or nephrostomy if obstructed or infected; monitor by imaging.

**5. Xanthine oxidase inhibitors**
- **Allopurinol 300 mg/day** lowers uric acid production. It is **not first-line**, because low pH, not hyperuricosuria, is the usual cause.
- Indications: hyperuricosuria with hyperuricaemia (e.g. gout), recurrence despite alkalinisation (AUA, American Urological Association), hyperuricosuric calcium oxalate stones.
- Reduce the dose in renal impairment. Risks: rash; **allopurinol hypersensitivity syndrome** (2–8% have reactions; may be fatal); Stevens–Johnson syndrome.
- **Febuxostat** is an alternative. **Rasburicase** (recombinant uricase) prevents uric acid nephropathy in tumour lysis.

**Landmark trial:** **Ettinger (1986)**, randomised placebo-controlled trial: allopurinol more than halved recurrence in hyperuricosuric, normocalciuric calcium oxalate stone formers.

### Allopurinol (in detail)
#### Introduction and mechanism
- A **purine (hypoxanthine) analogue** that inhibits **xanthine oxidase (xanthine dehydrogenase)**. This blocks hypoxanthine → xanthine → uric acid, lowering serum and urinary uric acid.
- Metabolised in the liver to **oxypurinol**, the active metabolite. It has a long half-life and is cleared by the kidney, so the dose is cut in renal impairment.

#### Uses in urology

| Indication | Rationale |
|---|---|
| **Recurrent calcium oxalate stones with hyperuricosuria** (>600 mg/day) and normal urinary calcium | Urate "seeds" calcium oxalate (heterogeneous nucleation, salting out, inhibitor adsorption). **Ettinger (1986) RCT (randomised controlled trial): recurrence more than halved**; AUA (American Urological Association) recommends it |
| Uric acid stones | **Not first line:** low urine pH is the main cause, so alkalinise with potassium citrate to pH 6.0–6.5. Allopurinol is added for hyperuricosuria, gout or failure of alkalinisation |
| 2,8-DHA (dihydroxyadenine) stones (APRT (adenine phosphoribosyltransferase) deficiency) | Blocks adenine → DHA; febuxostat more effective |
| Tumour lysis prophylaxis; gout; HPRT (hypoxanthine-guanine phosphoribosyltransferase) deficiency, PRPS (phosphoribosylpyrophosphate synthetase) overactivity | Prevents urate nephropathy |

#### Dose
- Adult **300 mg/day** (start 100 mg, titrate); child 4–10 mg/kg/day (maximum 300 mg). Reduce for low creatinine clearance.

#### Adverse effects
- Rash (commonest), myalgia, gastrointestinal upset; gout flare on starting; interstitial nephritis.
- **Hypersensitivity in 2–8%**, from skin reactions to **allopurinol hypersensitivity syndrome** (fatal in up to 27%) and Stevens-Johnson syndrome. Risk is higher with high dose, renal failure, diuretics and statins. **HLA-B\*58:01** carriers (some Asian populations) have higher risk.
- **Xanthine stones** (radiolucent): rare, mainly with high doses in Lesch-Nyhan syndrome.

#### Drug interactions
- **Azathioprine and 6-mercaptopurine:** xanthine oxidase metabolises them, so toxicity (marrow suppression) rises. Reduce their dose sharply (important after transplant).

#### Alternatives
- **Febuxostat:** non-purine xanthine oxidase inhibitor, no dose change in mild to moderate renal impairment. Carries a cardiovascular-death warning.
- **Also know:** rasburicase (urate oxidase) for tumour lysis.

**Diagram to draw:**
- Sigmoid curve of uric acid solubility against urine pH, marking pKa 5.5 and target 6.0–6.5.
- Purine pathway (hypoxanthine → xanthine → uric acid) showing the xanthine oxidase block.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Low urine pH is the main cause; pKa 5.5; no inhibitor exists.
> - Insulin resistance reduces ammoniagenesis, so urine is acid.
> - Potassium citrate to pH 6.0–6.5 (dissolution 7.0–7.2); avoid >7.0.
> - Allopurinol only for hyperuricosuria or failed alkalinisation.
> - Xanthine oxidase inhibitor; active metabolite oxypurinol; 300 mg/day.
> - Use: hyperuricosuric calcium oxalate stones (Ettinger 1986); uric acid stones need alkali first.
> - Hypersensitivity 2–8%; HLA-B*58:01; cut azathioprine dose.

## Urinary crystals
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 2, Q2

### Introduction
- Crystals are precipitated urinary solutes seen on microscopy of the sediment. They occur in both normal and diseased states.
- Their value lies in suggesting stone type, metabolic disease, infection or drug toxicity. **Cystine is the one crystal that is never normal.**

### How crystals form (physicochemistry)
- Urine is normally **supersaturated** with calcium oxalate and phosphate. Crystals form only when the concentration product passes a critical level.

| Saturation zone | What happens |
|---|---|
| Undersaturated (below Ksp, solubility product) | No crystals; stones may dissolve |
| Metastable (Ksp to Kf, formation product) | No spontaneous nucleation; growth on existing crystals; inhibitors effective |
| Unstable (above Kf) | Spontaneous nucleation, growth and aggregation |

- **Nucleation** in vivo is mostly heterogeneous (on cell debris or other crystals). Crystals then grow, aggregate and are retained, for example on **Randall plaque**.
- **Inhibitors:** citrate, magnesium, pyrophosphate, Tamm–Horsfall protein, nephrocalcin, osteopontin. No inhibitor exists for uric acid or cystine, so these depend on pH and concentration.
- **Determinants of crystalluria:** urine volume, solute excretion, pH, temperature and inhibitors.

### Examination of urine for crystals
- Fresh midstream specimen, examined within 2 hours at body or room temperature. **Refrigeration precipitates crystals** that may not redissolve.
- Dipstick pH (physiological range 4.5–8.0); centrifuged sediment; polarised light helps identify crystals.
- Phosphaturia (cloudy alkaline urine) **clears with acetic acid**; pyuria does not.
- Candida mimics calcium oxalate and red cells; budding and hyphae separate it.

### Classification by urine pH and shape

| Urine pH | Crystal | Shape | Clinical significance |
|---|---|---|---|
| Acidic | **Uric acid** | Rhomboid or lemon-shaped plates, rosettes; yellow-brown | Uric acid stones (pH <5.5; pKa 5.35), gout, tumour lysis |
| Acidic | Amorphous urates | Pink "brick-dust" granules | Usually normal (concentrated, cooled urine) |
| Acidic | **Cystine** | **Hexagonal** colourless plates | Always abnormal: cystinuria |
| Acidic or alkaline | **Calcium oxalate dihydrate** | **Envelope** (octahedral) | Common, often normal; hypercalciuria |
| Acidic or alkaline | Calcium oxalate monohydrate | Dumbbell or oval | Hyperoxaluria; ethylene glycol poisoning |
| Alkaline | **Triple phosphate (struvite)** | **Coffin-lid** prisms | Urease-producing infection (Proteus); staghorn stones |
| Alkaline | Amorphous phosphate | White granules | Benign phosphaturia |
| Alkaline | Calcium phosphate (brushite, apatite) | Needles or rosettes; amorphous | RTA (renal tubular acidosis), hyperparathyroidism |
| Alkaline | Ammonium biurate | "Thorny apple" spheres | Old specimens; ammonium urate stones (diarrhoea, laxative abuse, ileostomy) |
| Any | Cholesterol | Notched flat plates | Nephrotic syndrome, lipiduria |

- **Rare metabolic crystals:** 2,8-dihydroxyadenine, brown spiculated spheres (APRT [adenine phosphoribosyltransferase] deficiency, radiolucent stones); xanthine (hereditary xanthinuria, high-dose allopurinol); tyrosine needles and leucine spheres (severe liver disease).

### Drug crystals
- **Two routes:** the drug itself precipitates (direct), or it changes urine chemistry (indirect).

| Drug | Crystal and features | Prevention |
|---|---|---|
| **Indinavir** (protease inhibitor) | Needles or fan-shaped plates; crystalluria in about two-thirds of new users; stones 4–13%; radiolucent, often invisible on CT | Hydration; more soluble below pH 5.5 |
| Atazanavir | Highest stone risk of newer antiretrovirals | Hydration |
| Sulfonamides (sulfadiazine) | "Sheaves of wheat" | Hydration, alkalinise |
| Acyclovir (high-dose intravenous) | Birefringent needles; acute kidney injury | Slow infusion, hydration |
| Amoxicillin, ciprofloxacin (high dose) | Needles; worse in alkaline urine | Hydration |
| Methotrexate (high dose) | Crystal nephropathy | Alkalinise, hydration |
| Triamterene, ephedrine, guaifenesin, silicate antacids | Stones of drug or metabolite | Stop the drug |

- **Indirect:** loop diuretics and steroids (hypercalciuria, calcium stones); acetazolamide and topiramate (alkaline urine, hypocitraturia, calcium phosphate); cytotoxics (uric acid); laxative abuse (ammonium urate).

### Clinical significance
1. **Diagnosis of stone type** before stone analysis is available; crystals in a stone former help direct metabolic work-up.
2. **Recurrence risk:** persistent crystalluria in repeated early-morning samples, and large or aggregated crystals, suggest active stone disease.
3. **Infection:** struvite crystals with urine pH >7.2 signal a urease-producing organism.
4. **Acute kidney injury:** uric acid (tumour lysis), calcium oxalate monohydrate (ethylene glycol) and drug crystals cause intratubular obstruction.
5. **Monitoring therapy:** absence of cystine or uric acid crystals confirms adequate alkalinisation.
- Most crystalluria in healthy people is transient and harmless.

### Management principles
- Repeat on a fresh, warm specimen; confirm pH; 24-hour urine metabolic evaluation and stone analysis.
- **Fluids** for urine output above 2–2.5 L/day.
- **pH manipulation:** alkalinise (potassium citrate) for uric acid, cystine, methotrexate and sulfonamides; avoid alkali in calcium phosphate and struvite.
- Treat the cause: antibiotics and stone clearance for struvite; stop or change the drug; specific therapy (thiols for cystine, allopurinol for uric acid).

**Diagram to draw:** sketches of hexagon (cystine), envelope (calcium oxalate), coffin lid (struvite), rhomboid (uric acid), sheaf of wheat (sulfonamide).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Cystine hexagons are always pathological.
> - Acidic: uric acid, cystine; alkaline: struvite, phosphates; calcium oxalate at any pH.
> - Indinavir: crystalluria in two-thirds, stones 4–13%, radiolucent.
> - Refrigerated urine precipitates crystals: examine fresh within 2 hours.

# Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Renal tubular acidosis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3b

### Definition
- **RTA (renal tubular acidosis)**: hyperchloraemic, normal AG (anion gap) metabolic acidosis from defective tubular H+ secretion or HCO3 reabsorption.

### Comparison of types

| Feature | **Type 1 (distal)** | **Type 2 (proximal)** | **Type 4 (hyperkalaemic)** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Defect | H+ secretion (α-intercalated cell) | Proximal HCO3 reabsorption | Aldosterone deficiency or resistance |
| Urine pH | **Always >6** | <5.5 at steady state | Low |
| Potassium | Low | Low | **High** |
| Serum HCO3 | Low, may be very low | 15–18 mEq/L | Mildly low |
| Urine calcium | High | Normal or mildly high | Low |
| Urine citrate | **Profoundly low** | Near normal | – |
| Stones | **Up to 70%; calcium phosphate, nephrocalcinosis** | Uncommon | Uncommon |
| Causes | Sjögren, lupus, inherited | Fanconi | Diabetes, obstruction |
| Treatment | **Potassium citrate** 1–2 mEq/kg/day | Alkali 10–15 mEq/kg/day plus potassium | Fludrocortisone, low-potassium diet, stop offending drugs |

- **Why distal RTA forms stones**: fixed alkaline urine, profound hypocitraturia (acidosis increases citrate reabsorption) and hypercalciuria from bone buffering.
- **Incomplete distal RTA**: normal bicarbonate, but urine cannot be acidified below 5.5.

### Diagnosis
- **UAG (urine anion gap) = (Na + K) – Cl** reflects urinary ammonium: negative in GI (gastrointestinal) loss, positive in renal causes.
- **Ammonium chloride load** (0.1 g/kg orally, hourly urine pH for about 6 h): pH fails to fall below 5.5 in distal RTA. **Furosemide–fludrocortisone test** is a better-tolerated alternative.
- **Fractional bicarbonate excretion** >15% during bicarbonate loading indicates proximal RTA.

### Urological relevance
- Obstruction causes hyperkalaemic distal RTA, reversible by relief.
- Topiramate and acetazolamide mimic RTA (alkaline urine, low citrate, calcium phosphate stones).
- Potassium citrate beats sodium alkali, which raises calcium excretion.
- **Also know:** CKD (chronic kidney disease) acidosis; amiloride and trimethoprim cause hyperkalaemic distal RTA; mixed type (carbonic anhydrase II deficiency); medullary sponge kidney; laxative abuse (ammonium urate stones); inherited forms (recessive with deafness); growth failure in children.

**Diagram to draw:** Nephron with defect sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Type 1: urine pH >6, hypokalaemia, hypocitraturia, calcium phosphate stones (up to 70%).
> - Type 4: hyperkalaemia, low urine pH; obstructive uropathy.
> - Positive UAG = renal cause; NH4Cl load fails to lower pH <5.5 in distal RTA.

## Trans Uretero Ureteric anastomoses
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q3a

### Definition
- **TUU (transureteroureterostomy)**: the injured (donor) ureter is brought across the midline and joined **end-to-side** to the opposite (recipient) ureter. First clinical use in 1934.

### Indications
- Lower ureteric loss that reimplantation, psoas hitch or Boari flap cannot bridge: small, scarred or irradiated bladder; long ureteric loss; pelvic phlegmon; associated rectal, vascular or extensive bladder injury.
- One ureter short while the other is reimplanted with a psoas hitch (hitch only one side).
- Undiversion and reconstruction in children (Hendren).
- Usually a **delayed** repair, not acute.

### Contraindications
| Absolute | Relative (disease that may involve both ureters) |
|---|---|
| Donor ureter too short to reach without tension | Stone disease, **urothelial carcinoma** |
| Diseased recipient ureter | Retroperitoneal fibrosis, pelvic radiation |
| | Chronic pyelonephritis, genitourinary tuberculosis |
- Recipient reflux must be corrected at the same time, so do a preoperative VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram). Less favoured in children (long life, both kidneys at risk).

### Technique (steps)
1. Midline transperitoneal incision; reflect the colon.
2. Mobilise the **donor ureter with its adventitia** (blood supply); divide it just above the diseased segment.
3. Expose about 5 cm of recipient ureter with minimal mobilisation.
4. Tunnel the donor ureter **behind the sigmoid mesentery, above the IMA (inferior mesenteric artery)** to avoid kinking.
5. **Anteromedial ureterotomy** of the recipient (at least 2 cm); **spatulated, tension-free end-to-side** anastomosis with fine absorbable interrupted sutures.
6. **Double-J stent** from donor kidney to bladder; drain. (Laparoscopic and robotic routes are feasible.)

### Complications
- Urine leak (commonest), urinoma, stricture or obstruction, kinking under the IMA.
- **Main risk: one unilateral problem becomes bilateral obstruction.**
- Later endoscopic access is difficult; use the antegrade route.

### Results
- Success 90–97% in adults; complications (mostly leak) in about one-quarter of complex cases.

**Diagram to draw:** donor ureter crossing above the IMA to an end-to-side anastomosis, with stent.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - End-to-side, spatulated, tension-free; cross above the IMA.
> - Avoid with stones, urothelial cancer, retroperitoneal fibrosis, radiation, tuberculosis.
> - Risk: unilateral injury becomes bilateral.

## RIRS / flexible ureteroscopy
**Asked as:**
- RIRS (Retrograde intrarenal surgery) — *Winter 2017 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- RIRS — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- Flexible Ureteroscopy. — *Summer 2019 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*
- Flexible URS — *Practice Set 1 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3e)
*Covered in part in 2017-Summer Paper 1, Q3b; written in full here.*
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3b; digital scopes in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

### Introduction
- RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) is flexible ureterorenoscopy (fURS; flexible ureteroscopy, flexible URS (ureteroscopy)) inside the kidney through the natural route; it examines and treats the whole upper tract by the natural route.
- Marshall first reported it; a working channel and active deflection made it therapeutic. Active deflection, miniaturised digital scopes and the holmium laser made it practical.

### Instrument
| Feature | Details |
|---|---|
| Tip | 4.5–7.5 Fr, tapered (shaft larger) (digital tips up to about 8.5 Fr) |
| Working channel | **3.6 Fr** (laser fibre, basket) |
| Deflection | **≥270° both ways** (reaches lower pole); lower pole needs about 140° (lower pole angle averages 140°; up to / maximum 175°) |
| **Types** | Fibreoptic; digital; single-use (rows below) |
| **Fibreoptic** | Smallest tip; image degrades with fibre breakage (fibres break → black spots); repair about every 15 cases |
| **Digital** ("chip-on-tip", CCD [charge-coupled device] or CMOS (complementary metal-oxide semiconductor) chip on tip) | Better image; larger tip (about 8.5 Fr); not more durable |
| **Single-use** (LithoVue, 2016) | 7.7 Fr; no repair, reprocessing or sterilisation cost; cheaper below about 99 cases a year |

### Indications
- **Stones:** renal stones **<2 cm**; lower pole <1 cm (or >1 cm / 1–2 cm / larger as an alternative to PCNL [percutaneous nephrolithotomy]); patients who cannot stop (**must continue / must stay on**) anticoagulants; failed SWL (shock wave lithotripsy); obesity; bleeding disorders; staged treatment of larger stones.
- **Others:** diagnosis and (laser) ablation of low-grade UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma); filling defects, positive cytology; strictures and endopyelotomy; calyceal diverticulum; infundibular stenosis; essential haematuria; foreign bodies.

### Technique
1. Sterile urine; single-dose antibiotic; α-blocker or **pre-stenting** if the ureter is tight.
2. Cystoscopy, **safety guidewire**, retrograde pyelogram.
3. **Ureteral access sheath (UAS)** (usually / often 12/14 Fr) over a stiff wire: lowers intrarenal pressure and temperature and allows (eases) repeated passes, but causes ureteric injury in nearly half (severe ureteric injury occurs in about 1 in 8).
4. Low-pressure irrigation. **Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet, 2120 nm) or thulium fibre laser**: dusting (low energy, high frequency) or fragment-and-basket (fragmenting and basketing). Keep power ≤10 W in the ureter and ≤25 W in the kidney (tissue damage above 43 °C; heat above 43 °C damages tissue).
5. Lower pole stones: relocate (move them) the stone to an upper calyx with a basket.
6. Inspect the ureter on withdrawal; stent unless uncomplicated. **Omit stent** (stent may be omitted if anatomy and function are normal) if the case was uncomplicated with normal anatomy, normal renal function (and function), no (ureteric) injury and no second stage (AUA [American Urological Association]).
- **Difficult (unfavourable) lower pole:** steep infundibulopelvic angle, long (>10 mm) or narrow (<5 mm) infundibulum.

### Outcomes
- Stone-free rate is higher than SWL for renal stones <2 cm but falls with size and lower pole location. Larger stones may need staged procedures.
- Reported rates depend on the imaging used (CT strictest).

### Complications
- Stent symptoms (commonest); **sepsis about 5%**; stricture about 3%; perforation (a few per cent); bleeding or subcapsular haematoma <1%; **avulsion <1%** (nephrostomy, delayed reconstruction / repair); scope damage.

### Recent advances
- Single-use digital scopes; **thulium fibre laser** and pulse-modulated (Moses) holmium (less retropulsion, finer dust); **suction and flexible navigable sheaths** (FANS; suction access sheaths); intrarenal pressure sensing (sensors; pressure-sensing scopes); robotic flexible ureteroscopy (robotic fURS / URS); AI (artificial intelligence) navigation.

**Diagram to draw:** flexible scope (through an) access sheath, deflected into the lower pole calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 3.6 Fr channel; ≥270° deflection.
> - Access sheath lowers pressure but injures (may injure) the ureter in about 1 in 8 (ureteric injury).
> - Laser power ≤10 W ureter, ≤25 W kidney.
> - RIRS: stones <2 cm and patients on anticoagulants; first choice (modality of choice) when anticoagulants cannot be stopped (cannot stop).
> - Sepsis about 5%, stricture about 3%.

## Retro peritoneal fibrosis medical management
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q3e

### Introduction
- RPF (retroperitoneal fibrosis): inflammatory fibrous tissue around the distal aorta (L4–L5) that encases the ureters. Age 40–60; male:female 2–3:1.
- **Idiopathic (about 70%):** chronic periaortitis, often autoimmune. Many cases are **IgG4-related disease** (raised serum IgG4, IgG4-positive plasma cells, storiform fibrosis).
- **Secondary (about 30%):** drugs (methysergide, ergots, β-blockers), malignancy (lymphoma commonest), radiotherapy, asbestos, infection (TB, tuberculosis).

### Evaluation before treatment
- Raised ESR (erythrocyte sedimentation rate) and CRP (C-reactive protein), anaemia, renal failure; serum IgG4.
- CT/MRI: mass around aorta and IVC (inferior vena cava), medially deviated ureters; high T2 signal means activity. **PET-CT** is the most sensitive test of activity.
- Biopsy if malignancy is suspected.

### Medical management
1. **Relieve obstruction first:** stents or nephrostomy (nephrostomy if uraemic and anuric); manage post-obstructive diuresis.
2. **Stop the culprit drug;** treat any secondary cause.
3. **Steroids are first line:**
   - Prednisone about 60 mg daily (about 1 mg/kg), tapered to 5 mg, total course up to 2 years.
   - About 80% respond: pain and symptoms settle in days, ESR falls, the mass shrinks.
   - Relapse on tapering is 25–50%. Active inflammation predicts response; burnt-out fibrosis responds poorly.
4. **Tamoxifen:** thought to inhibit fibroblasts. **Vaglio RCT (randomised controlled trial; Lancet 2011):** relapse 17% with prednisone vs 50% with tamoxifen, so steroids remain first line.
5. **Steroid-sparing or second line:** mycophenolate mofetil (with steroids), azathioprine, cyclophosphamide, ciclosporin, colchicine; medroxyprogesterone.
6. **Rituximab:** for refractory or IgG4-related disease.

### Monitoring
- Symptoms, ESR/CRP, creatinine, ultrasound, periodic CT/MRI or PET-CT. Remove stents when ureters drain.
- Watch steroid side effects (diabetes, osteoporosis).

### When medical therapy fails
- About two-thirds clear obstruction with drainage plus drugs; about 15% need **ureterolysis**: bilateral, with biopsies, and the ureters protected by intraperitonealisation or omental wrap (open, laparoscopic or robotic). Autotransplantation is rarely needed.

**Diagram to draw:** mass around aorta at L4–L5 pulling both ureters medially.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Drain first, then steroids (about 80% respond).
> - Vaglio 2011: prednisone better than tamoxifen (relapse 17% vs 50%).
> - Rituximab for IgG4-related or refractory disease; ureterolysis for about 15%.

## Medical expulsive therapy (MET) in urinary calculi
**Asked as:**
- Medical expulsive therapy in urinary calculi. — *Summer 2021 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Medical expulsive Rx for urinary calculi. — *Practice Set 11 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

### Definition
- MET (medical expulsive therapy): drugs that help a ureteric stone pass into the bladder. **α-blockers** are the main agents.

### Rationale: natural history
- Spontaneous passage depends on **width**, size and site:

| Width | Spontaneous passage | Location | Spontaneous passage |
|---|---|---|---|
| 0–3 mm | 98% | Vesicoureteric junction (small stones) | 92% |
| 5 mm | 65% | Distal ureter | 75% |
| >6.5 mm | 9% | Proximal ureter | 48% |

### Mechanism
- **α1 receptors** (mainly **α1A and α1D**) are abundant in (dominate in) the distal ureter and VUJ (vesicoureteric junction). Blockade reduces spasm and the force and frequency of peristalsis (peristaltic force and frequency), so a larger urine bolus pushes the stone / builds higher pressure above the stone.
- Others: calcium-channel blockers (nifedipine); PDE5 (phosphodiesterase-5) inhibitors under study. **NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) relieve pain but do not aid passage.**

### Drugs
- **Tamsulosin 0.4 mg daily** (most studied); **silodosin 8 mg daily** (better expulsion in meta-analyses but twice the retrograde ejaculation); alfuzosin, doxazosin.

### Evidence

| Study | Result |
|---|---|
| **SUSPEND (Pickard, 2015):** 1136 patients; tamsulosin, nifedipine or placebo | No difference in need for intervention at 4 weeks (19% vs 20%) |
| **STONE (Meltzer, 2018):** stones <9 mm (up to 9 mm) | Tamsulosin no better than placebo |
| Meta-analyses and a large distal-stone RCT (randomised controlled trial) | Benefit for **distal stones >5 mm** |

- Stratified, the trials agree: benefit lies in **distal stones of 5–10 mm** (smaller ones pass anyway; larger rarely pass).

### Indications and guideline position (AUA [American Urological Association] and EAU [European Association of Urology])
- AUA and EAU: offer MET for **ureteric stones <10 mm**, especially distal, when immediate intervention is not needed (EAU: distal >5 mm).
- **After SWL (shock wave lithotripsy)** for faster fragment clearance (to clear fragments). Children: off-label but effective.

### Contraindications (need urgent drainage instead; drain urgently)
- Infection with obstruction or sepsis, solitary or bilateral obstruction, renal deterioration (impairment), refractory pain or vomiting.

### Protocol
- NSAID analgesia; counsel on off-label use, dizziness, hypotension, retrograde ejaculation.
- Periodic KUB (kidney–ureter–bladder radiograph) or ultrasound follow-up; **intervene after 4–6 weeks** if not passed.

**Diagram to draw:** ureter with α1 receptors concentrated distally (at the distal ureter and VUJ [vesicoureteric junction]) and a stone above it (above the vesicoureteric junction).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Tamsulosin 0.4 mg; best for distal stones 5–10 mm.
> - SUSPEND 2015: no benefit in an unselected population.
> - Trial of passage 4–6 weeks, then intervene.

## Stones (urolithiasis) in pregnancy
**Asked as:**
- Management of Stones in pregnancy. — *Summer 2022 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Urolithiasis in pregnancy — *Practice Set 4 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(Summer 2022 Paper II Q3d: lower ureteric stone in pregnancy also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3a)
(Practice Set 4 Paper II Q3b: also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

### Background
- 1 in 200 to 1 in 1500 pregnancies; **commonest non-obstetric cause of admission**; mostly second or third trimester.
- Linked with preterm delivery, pre-eclampsia and premature rupture of membranes.
- **Why calcium phosphate stones:** raised GFR (glomerular filtration rate) and placental calcitriol cause hypercalciuria; urine is alkaline. Rising (raised) citrate, magnesium and urine flow protect, so stone rates barely change.
- **Physiological hydronephrosis** (up to 90% by third trimester, right-sided) mimics obstruction.

### Evaluation
- Urinalysis, **urine culture always**, blood count, creatinine; obstetric review.
- **US (ultrasound) first line**; transvaginal US for distal ureter; a rise in Doppler **RI (resistive index)** suggests acute obstruction.
- Second line: **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)** (stone as a filling defect) or **low-dose CT (computed tomography)**. Keep total fetal dose **≤50 mGy**.

### Management
```
Symptomatic stone in pregnancy
 ├─ sepsis, solitary kidney, renal failure, uncontrolled pain
 │     → urgent stent or nephrostomy (+ antibiotics)
 ├─ pain controlled, no infection → observe (50–80% pass)
 └─ failed observation → ureteroscopy + holmium laser
        or temporary stent/nephrostomy until delivery
```
- **Conservative:** hydration, paracetamol, small doses of morphine. **NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) are contraindicated** (third-trimester ductus closure). **MET (medical expulsive therapy)** is off-label.
- **URS (ureteroscopy):** first-line intervention for a non-complex stone (non-complex stones) in any trimester; second trimester is the safest window. Neuraxial anaesthesia, minimal fluoroscopy (lead shield beneath the patient, US guidance), fetal monitoring. Third-trimester URS may raise preterm labour.
- **Stent or PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy):** temporising. Encrustation is rapid (rapid encrustation): change stents about **every 4 weeks** (4–6 weeks in series), nephrostomy about every 3 weeks; definitive treatment after delivery.
- **Asymptomatic renal stones:** observe until after delivery.
- **SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) contraindicated. PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) deferred** until after delivery except in expert centres.

### After delivery
- Clear residual stones; **metabolic evaluation** postpartum.

**Diagram to draw:** the management flowchart above.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - US first; MRI or low-dose CT second (≤50 mGy).
> - Observation first; no NSAIDs.
> - URS with laser is definitive; SWL contraindicated.
> - Stents encrust: change about every 4 weeks.

## Uretero-calycostomy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3f
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 3, Q3d)

### Definition
- End-to-side anastomosis of the spatulated proximal ureter to a dilated **lower-pole calyx**, after **resecting lower-pole parenchyma**, bypassing the pelvis and PUJ (pelviureteric junction).

### Indications
- **Failed pyeloplasty** with a scarred pelvis and short ureter (commonest).
- **Small intrarenal pelvis** with PUJ obstruction.
- **Rotation or fusion anomalies** (horseshoe kidney).
- Proximal ureteric or pelvic loss: trauma, iatrogenic, **TB (tuberculosis) PUJ stricture**.
- **Prerequisites:** dilated lower calyx, ureter reaching it without tension, worthwhile function. Cortex <5 mm with poor function predicts failure.

### Procedure (open, laparoscopic or robotic)
```
Flank position (45–60°); pre-placed double-J stent
→ mobilise ureter with generous periureteral tissue
→ mobilise kidney; partial lower-pole nephrectomy
   to expose the calyx widely (not simple nephrotomy)
→ ligate pelvic outlet; spatulate ureter laterally
→ apex stitch to lateral calyx wall; second at 180°
→ interrupted mucosa-to-mucosa sutures, placed then tied
→ capsule closed over cut surface, away from anastomosis
→ cover with perinephric fat, peritoneum or omentum; drain
```
- **Why resect:** parenchyma or capsule left round the anastomosis contracts and causes stenosis.
- Stent out at 4–6 weeks; urogram about 1 month later, then renogram. Later endoscopy needs **antegrade upper-pole access**.

### Complications
- **Anastomotic stricture** (main failure; mostly within the first year).
- Urine leak, urinoma, bleeding from the lower pole, infection, loss of the renal unit.
- Success about **70%** in the largest (mostly redo) series; better in small paediatric robotic series. The access route does not affect outcome.

**Also know:** alternatives are endopyelotomy, redo pyeloplasty, flap repair, ileal ureter, buccal graft, autotransplantation, nephrectomy (poor kidney); calicovesicostomy for a low-lying kidney.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with resected lower pole and ureter sewn to the calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Salvage for failed pyeloplasty, intrarenal pelvis, horseshoe kidney.
> - Requires a dilated lower calyx and lower-pole resection, not simple nephrotomy.
> - Stricture is the main failure; success about 70% in redo series.

## Physiology of UPJ in relation to urine transport
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3f

*UPJ = ureteropelvic (pelviureteric) junction.*

### Anatomy relevant to function
- The UPJ is the apex of a funnel-shaped pelvis; its spiral smooth muscle is continuous with calyces and ureter.

### Pacemaker and electrical activity
- Peristalsis is **myogenic**: the excised or denervated ureter keeps contracting. Nerves carry pain and only modulate.
- **Primary pacemaker: atypical smooth muscle cells** at the pelvicalyceal border; they fire spontaneously (HCN, hyperpolarisation-activated cyclic nucleotide-gated, channels).
- **ICC-like (interstitial cells of Cajal-like) cells** (c-Kit positive) in the pelvis and UPJ relay the signal to typical smooth muscle and act as back-up pacemakers.
- Signal spreads through **gap junctions**. Calcium entry gives a plateau action potential; calcium–calmodulin activates myosin light-chain kinase, causing contraction.

### Bolus formation and transport
1. Urine fills the pelvis at low pressure; the UPJ is closed.
2. Pelvic contraction pushes urine through the opening UPJ into the upper ureter.
3. The UPJ closes behind it, forming a **bolus**.
4. Ureteric walls **coapt** behind the bolus; the wave (2–6 per minute, 20–80 cm H₂O) carries it to the bladder.
- At **low flow**, the UPJ acts as a filter: several pelvic contractions occur for each ureteric contraction.
- As **flow rises**, ureteric frequency reaches 1:1, then bolus volume rises.
- At **very high flow**, boluses merge into a continuous column and the ureter dilates **without obstruction**.

### Effect of obstruction
- **Acute:** stronger, more frequent contractions.
- **Chronic:** dilatation, walls fail to coapt, peristalsis becomes ineffective; raised pelvic pressure lowers GFR (glomerular filtration rate).
- **Intrinsic UPJO (UPJ obstruction):** abnormal muscle, excess collagen, fewer ICC-like cells, so an **aperistaltic segment** cannot transmit the wave.
- **Also know:** endotoxin and progesterone reduce peristalsis. Diuretic renogram and Whitaker test (chapter: pressure difference >12 cm H₂O suggests obstruction; classic teaching: <15 unobstructed, >22 obstructed) assess transport.

**Diagram to draw:** pelvis, UPJ and ureter showing bolus formation and coaptation.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Myogenic peristalsis from atypical smooth muscle pacemakers; ICC-like cells back up.
> - Low flow: several pelvic beats per ureteric beat; very high flow: dilatation without obstruction.
> - Intrinsic UPJO: aperistaltic segment with fewer ICC-like cells.

## Tubeless PCNL
**Asked as:**
- Tubeless PCNL — *Winter 2025 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- Tubeless PCNL — *Practice Set 9 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3e and 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3e)

### Definition
- PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) that ends **without a nephrostomy tube**.
- **Tubeless**: drainage by a ureteral stent (double-J, or externalised stent or string). **Totally tubeless**: no stent and no tube.
- **Wickham (1984)** did the first tubeless PCNL; **Bellman (1997)** popularised it.

### Rationale
- A large nephrostomy tube causes pain, more analgesia, longer stay and higher cost. Most tracts stop bleeding without tamponade.

### Selection
| Suitable | Keep a nephrostomy |
|---|---|
| Complete clearance, single tract | Pyonephrosis or infected stone |
| Minimal bleeding | Significant bleeding |
| Intact collecting system | Significant collecting-system perforation |
| Supracostal access, bilateral cases (supracostal and bilateral cases shown safe) | Residual stone needing a second look |

### Technique (exit strategy)
1. Confirm clearance by rigid and **flexible nephroscopy** and fluoroscopy.
2. Antegrade double-J stent, or externalised stent or string.
3. Withdraw the sheath under vision; **safety wire is removed last**.
4. Watch the tract; apply pressure (rib as backstop in thin patients). Fibrin glue did not reduce bleeding in randomised trials; some use haemostatic sealants or tract cautery.
5. Urethral catheter overnight.

### Evidence
- Meta-analyses of randomised trials: shorter stay and operative time, and less pain.
- Less pain than large tubes (**>16 Fr**) (pain benefit is over large tubes), but **no benefit over small 8–9 Fr tubes**; small tubes (≤10 Fr) give similar comfort without more bleeding.
- No difference in fever, infection, transfusion or stone-free rate.
- Stent discomfort: worse WiSQoL (Wisconsin Stone Quality of Life) scores than nephrostomy in two trials. Externalised stents come out before discharge without cystoscopy.
- Children: insufficient evidence.

### Disadvantages and complications
- Loss of tract for a second look.
- Stent symptoms and a further procedure for removal.
- Urine leak or urinoma.
- Bleeding without tamponade: re-insert (reinsert) a balloon or Kaye catheter, then **angioembolisation**.

### Current position
- Choose tubeless, small tube or stent case by case (AUA (American Urological Association)). Pairs well with mini-PCNL.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with sealed tract and double-J stent.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Tubeless = stent only; totally tubeless = neither.
> - Benefit is mainly over large (>16 Fr) tubes.
> - Keep a tube for bleeding, perforation, infection or second look.

## Complications of PCNL, including bleeding and persistent haematuria after PCNL
**Asked as:**
- Complications of PCNL — *Practice Set 2 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- What are the causes of bleeding in PCNL? How will you manage persistent hematuria 6 days after PCNL!? — *Practice Set 6 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(mini-PCNL complications in 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q1)
(PCNL complications also in Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q3e)

### Introduction
- PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) is the standard for renal stones >2 cm. Most complications arise from **access and tract dilatation**.
- Graded by the **Clavien–Dindo** system (I: no intervention; II: drugs or transfusion; III: intervention; IV: ICU [intensive care unit]; V: death).
- Bleeding is the commonest serious complication of PCNL. Transfusion was historically ~7%, now nearer 1% with smaller tracts. Angioembolisation is needed in <1%.

### Incidence (systematic review, Seitz 2012, quoted by EAU [European Association of Urology])
| Complication | Rate |
|---|---|
| Fever | 10.8% |
| Transfusion | 7% (nearer 1% with modern and mini tracts) |
| Thoracic | 1.5% |
| Sepsis | 0.5% |
| Organ injury | 0.4% |
| Angioembolisation | 0.4% |
| Death | 0.05% |

### Main complications
| Complication | Cause | Prevention and management |
|---|---|---|
| **Acute bleeding** | Infundibular or segmental vessel injury | Fornix puncture; place sheath, tamponade with balloon or **Kaye catheter**, clamp nephrostomy; transfuse |
| **Delayed bleeding** | Pseudoaneurysm, AV (arteriovenous) fistula | **Selective angioembolisation** |
| **Infection, SIRS (systemic inflammatory response syndrome), sepsis** | Infected stone, high intrarenal pressure | Sterile urine, culture-specific antibiotics, low-pressure irrigation; stop and drain if pus |
| **Pleural** (pneumothorax, hydrothorax) | Supracostal access, highest above 11th rib | Subcostal where possible, puncture in expiration; chest drain |
| **Colon** | Retrorenal colon, left lower pole | CT review; extraperitoneal: double-J stent and withdraw nephrostomy into colon (colostomy tube) |
| **Spleen, liver** | Upper-pole access, organomegaly | Ultrasound guidance; conservative or surgery |
| **Collecting system** | Perforation, extravasation | Stop, drain; later infundibular stenosis |
| **Fluid absorption** | Long operation, perforation | **Normal saline**; hyponatraemia, overload |

**Also know:** venous air embolism, DVT (deep vein thrombosis) and pulmonary embolism, neuropraxia and eye injury (prone), residual fragments, loss of renal function (multiple tracts), urinoma, nephrocutaneous fistula, retained hardware.

### Risk factors
- Staghorn, multiple tracts, supracostal access, large tract, long operation, CKD (chronic kidney disease), diabetes, positive urine culture, inexperience.
- **Bleeding risk factors:** infundibular or upper-pole puncture, multiple or large tracts, staghorn stone, long operation, antithrombotic drugs, coagulopathy, diabetes.

### Reducing risk
- **Tranexamic acid** reduces transfusion (Cochrane).
- Ultrasound-guided access, mini-PCNL, ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery).
- **Bleeding prevention:** puncture through the **calyceal fornix** along a posterior calyx (Sampaio: no arterial injury); correct clotting; mini-tracts.

### Bleeding in PCNL

#### Causes
| Timing | Cause |
|---|---|
| **Intraoperative** | Parenchymal or venous tract bleeding; infundibular artery injury; through-and-through puncture; posterior segmental artery injury (upper-pole access); torn infundibulum or pelvis |
| **Early postoperative** | Tract bleeding after sheath removal; clot retention |
| **Delayed (about 1 week)** | **Pseudoaneurysm**, **AV (arteriovenous) fistula** |

#### Mechanism of delayed bleeding
- **Pseudoaneurysm:** clot seals an arterial tear, then ruptures intermittently.
- **AV fistula:** artery and vein heal together; the thin vein cannot withstand arterial pressure.

#### Management at day 6 (persistent haematuria)
- At day 6, assume an **arterial lesion** until proved otherwise.
```
Resuscitate: IV access, crossmatch, transfuse; Hb, clotting
→ Stop anticoagulants; catheter, irrigate clots
→ Stable, mild: bed rest, clamp nephrostomy, serial Hb
→ Persistent or recurrent bleeding or Hb fall
   → CT angiography (arterial blush) or direct angiography
→ SUPERSELECTIVE ANGIOEMBOLISATION (coils)
→ Fails or unstable after embolisation
   → open exploration, partial or total nephrectomy
```
- **Angiography** diagnoses >90% and treats in the same sitting. It may miss intermittent bleeds; non-selective embolisation loses renal tissue.
- Clot colic or obstruction: double-J stent or nephrostomy.
- Follow-up: Hb and creatinine; check for infection and abscess.

#### Intraoperative ladder (for completeness)
- Pressure on tract → nephrostomy clamped (tamponade) → balloon reinflation or **Kaye tamponade catheter** → angioembolisation.

**Diagram to draw:** kidney cross-section with posterior calyx, Brödel line, pleura and retrorenal colon; angiogram sketch: pseudoaneurysm before and after coiling.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Fever 10.8%, transfusion 7%, thoracic 1.5%, death 0.05%.
> - Fornix puncture avoids arterial injury; delayed bleeding = pseudoaneurysm, embolise.
> - Early bleeding venous: tamponade.
> - Bleeding at about 1 week arterial: pseudoaneurysm or AV fistula.
> - Treatment: superselective angioembolisation.
> - Supracostal access increases pleural injury; colon injury at left lower pole.

## Describe various pt positions in PCNL
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q3b
(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3f)

### Introduction
- PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) position decides calyceal access, working space, airway safety and simultaneous retrograde access.

### Positions

| Position | Set-up | Advantages | Disadvantages |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Prone** (Goodwin, 1955) | Lithotomy for ureteric catheter, then turned prone; horizontal bolsters at nipple line and ASIS (anterior superior iliac spine) bring the kidney down more than vertical ones | **Posterior calyx and upper pole** access; large working area; familiar | Turning; airway hard to reach; pressure injuries; retrorenal colon commoner prone |
