| **Prone split-leg / prone-flexed** | Legs apart for flexible cystoscopy; hip flexion opens the costovertebral space | ECIRS (endoscopic combined intrarenal surgery) prone | Needs split-leg table; prone cystoscopy learning curve |
| **Supine** (Valdivia-Uría, 1987) | Ipsilateral flank at table edge on a lumbar bolster; arm across chest | Airway retained; no turning; **gravity drains fragments**, low pelvic pressure | Less working area; anterior calyces; mobile kidney; harder upper pole |
| **Galdakao-modified supine Valdivia** (Scoffone) | Supine flank tilt with ipsilateral leg extended and other leg in lithotomy | **Simultaneous retrograde access (ECIRS)** in one position | Longer, more horizontal tract |
| **Bart's flank-free modified supine** (Bach) | Bolster under the thorax, flank free | More flank space | Fewer data |
| **Lateral decubitus (flank)** | Kidney-up flank position | Obese, kyphoscoliosis, poor respiratory reserve | Unfamiliar fluoroscopic orientation |

### Positioning safety (prone)
- Bolster shins so toes float; pad knees; arms ≤90° to the torso (brachial plexus); axillary and pectoral rolls; padded face and eyes.
- **Prone-specific complications:** neuropraxia, ocular injury, endotracheal tube displacement.

### Choosing a position
- **Prone:** standard anatomy, upper pole or multiple posterior tracts.
- **Supine variants:** obesity, cardiopulmonary disease, ECIRS, ileal conduit or continent channel, combined ureteric stone.
- **Spinal deformity:** individualise; pad every pressure point.
- **Evidence:** similar stone-free and complication rates; choice is mainly surgeon preference.

**Diagram to draw:** axial CT sketch showing prone (vertical) and supine (horizontal) tracts.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prone: upper pole and posterior calyx access, large working area.
> - Galdakao-modified supine: one position for ECIRS, airway safe, gravity drainage.
> - Outcomes equal; horizontal bolsters drop the kidney more.

## Steinstresse.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 2, Q3e

*Assumed meaning: steinstrasse.*

### Definition
- **Steinstrasse** (German, "stone street"): a column of stone fragments in the ureter that fails to pass and may obstruct. Typically after **SWL (shock wave lithotripsy)**; occasionally after PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) or URS (ureteroscopy).
- Incidence is low overall (about 4–7% after SWL, EAU [European Association of Urology]) but rises several-fold with stone burden **>2 cm²**.

### Risk factors
- Large burden (>2 cm), renal pelvic stones, hard stones that break into large fragments, high energy, impaired ureteric drainage.

### Classification (Coptcoat)
| Type | Composition |
|---|---|
| I | Small fragments only |
| II | Larger lead fragment (4–5 mm) followed by small fragments |
| III | Large fragments throughout |

### Clinical features
- Asymptomatic (silent obstruction), colic, nausea; fever and sepsis if infected; anuria and AKI (acute kidney injury) in a solitary kidney. Most lie in the distal ureter.

### Evaluation
- KUB (kidney, ureter, bladder) X-ray, ultrasound, or non-contrast CT (computed tomography); creatinine, urine culture, blood counts.

### Management
```
Steinstrasse
 ├─ infection / sepsis  → urgent PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy) + antibiotics
 ├─ asymptomatic, no obstruction → MET + analgesia, imaging every 1–2 weeks
 └─ failure, pain, obstruction:
      ├─ lead fragment (type II/III) → SWL to lead fragment
      └─ URS with laser lithotripsy (most definitive)
```
- **MET (medical expulsive therapy):** α-blockers (tamsulosin) improve fragment clearance and reduce pain.
- **PCN** is preferred in infected obstruction: drains without manipulating the ureter, and fragments often pass once the kidney is decompressed. A stent is an alternative.
- Retained fragments with poor progress: URS clears the column.

### Prevention
- Choose PCNL or RIRS (retrograde intrarenal surgery) for large burdens.
- Energy ramping, slower shock rate; MET after SWL.
- **Stent before SWL:** may reduce steinstrasse with large stones but does not improve stone-free rate and adds stent symptoms. The chapters disagree: Chapter 97 says it may reduce steinstrasse; Chapter 98 advises against stenting at SWL.

**Diagram to draw:** ureter with lead fragment and trailing column (Coptcoat types I–III).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Risk rises several-fold with burden >2 cm².
> - Coptcoat I–III by fragment size.
> - Infected: PCN; uncomplicated: MET; failed: SWL to lead fragment or URS.

## Treatment of inferior calyceal calculus
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 2, Q3b

### Introduction
- Lower pole is the **commonest site** of renal stones. Gravity-dependent position makes fragments hard to clear, so **SWL (shock wave lithotripsy) works poorly** here.

### Factors in decision
- **Stone:** size, density (<900 HU [Hounsfield units] favours SWL), composition.
- **Anatomy unfavourable for SWL clearance:** acute **infundibulopelvic angle**, long and narrow infundibulum, skin-to-stone distance >10 cm.
- **Patient:** obesity, anticoagulants (favour URS [ureteroscopy]), pregnancy, preference.
- Evaluation: NCCT (non-contrast CT), urine culture, renal function.

### Treatment by size
| Size | Options |
|---|---|
| **Asymptomatic, small** | Observation acceptable; about half have an event in 5 years. Treat if growing, symptomatic, infected or occupational |
| **<10 mm** | SWL or **RIRS** (retrograde intrarenal surgery). AUA (American Urological Association): either |
| **10–20 mm** | EAU (European Association of Urology): SWL only if favourable anatomy; otherwise **RIRS or PCNL** (percutaneous nephrolithotomy) |
| **>10 mm (AUA)** | **PCNL** (highest SFR [stone-free rate]) or URS; SWL not first-line |
| **>20 mm** | PCNL first; staged RIRS an alternative |

### Evidence
- **Lower Pole I (Albala 2001):** stones >1 cm: SFR 91% PCNL vs 21% SWL.
- Lower Pole II: stones ≤1 cm, URS vs SWL: no significant SFR difference.

### Technique points
- **RIRS:** flexible ureteroscope (deflection up to 270°); relocate the stone to an upper calyx with a basket before laser (holmium or thulium fibre) lithotripsy; access sheath.
- **PCNL:** direct lower pole posterior calyx puncture; mini-PCNL reduces bleeding (transfusion near 1%).
- **SWL adjuncts:** percussion, diuresis, inversion therapy and alpha-blockers improve fragment clearance.
- **Also know:** lower pole calyceal diverticulum or infundibular stenosis favours PCNL; residual fragments >4 mm after URS need treatment.

### Follow-up
- Imaging for residual fragments; metabolic evaluation if recurrent.

**Diagram to draw:** infundibulopelvic angle and infundibular length/width on an IVU (intravenous urogram) tracing.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Lower Pole I: PCNL 91% vs SWL 21% for >1 cm.
> - <1 cm: SWL or RIRS; >1 cm: PCNL or RIRS.
> - Unfavourable: acute angle, long narrow infundibulum, >900 HU, SSD >10 cm.
> - Reposition stone during RIRS.

## Primary Hyperoxaluria
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 10 Paper 2, Q3f

### Definition
- Rare **autosomal recessive** errors of **hepatic glyoxylate metabolism**: glyoxylate is not converted to glycine and is oxidised to oxalate, an end product cleared only by the kidney.
- Hyperoxaluria = urinary oxalate >40 mg/day; in PH (primary hyperoxaluria) often **>100 mg/day**.

### Types

| | PH1 (~70%) | PH2 (~10%) | PH3 (~10%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Enzyme (gene) | **AGT** (alanine-glyoxylate aminotransferase; **AGXT**), liver peroxisome, **pyridoxine (B6) cofactor** | GRHPR (glyoxylate reductase/hydroxypyruvate reductase) | HOGA1 (4-hydroxy-2-oxoglutarate aldolase), mitochondria |
| Urine marker | **Glycolate** | **L-glycerate** | 4-hydroxy-2-oxoglutarate |
| Course | **Most severe**; ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) in 2nd–3rd decade | Milder, can reach ESKD | Childhood stones; rarely ESKD |

### Pathophysiology
- High urinary oxalate → calcium oxalate crystals in tubules → **nephrocalcinosis** and recurrent stones.
- **Vicious circle:** falling GFR (glomerular filtration rate) reduces oxalate clearance → plasma oxalate rises → **systemic oxalosis** (bone, retina, heart, vessels, skin).

### Clinical features
- Spectrum from **infantile oxalosis with renal failure** to occasional adult stones; some diagnosed only after a transplant fails.
- Clues: childhood stones, nephrocalcinosis, family history, **calcium oxalate monohydrate** stones.

### Diagnosis

```
24-h urinary oxalate ↑ (child: spot oxalate:creatinine ratio)
   → urinary glycolate / L-glycerate / 4-hydroxy-2-oxoglutarate
   → genetic testing (AGXT, GRHPR, HOGA1)
   all normal → secondary (enteric, dietary) hyperoxaluria
```

- Plasma oxalate when GFR is low; screen siblings and related living donors.

### Management
- **Hyperhydration** (about 3 L/m²/day), **potassium citrate**, normal calcium intake.
- **Pyridoxine** trial in all PH1: responsive mainly with the Gly170Arg mutation; it restores AGT activity and peroxisomal targeting.
- **Lumasiran** (RNA interference against glycolate oxidase, which cuts glyoxylate supply): **ILLUMINATE-A**: 84% vs 0% reached urinary oxalate ≤1.5 × upper limit of normal. **Also know:** nedosiran.
- Stones: ureteroscopy or PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy); limit SWL (shock wave lithotripsy).
- **ESKD:** intensive dialysis, then **combined liver–kidney transplant for PH1** (only the liver replaces the enzyme; a lone kidney would be re-damaged). Kidney alone usually suffices for PH2.

**Diagram to draw:** hepatic glyoxylate pathway showing the AGT block (PH1) and the lumasiran target (glycolate oxidase).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PH1: AGT (AGXT), pyridoxine cofactor, urinary glycolate; most severe.
> - Urinary oxalate often >100 mg/day; nephrocalcinosis; oxalosis when GFR falls.
> - Lumasiran (ILLUMINATE-A); combined liver–kidney transplant in PH1.

## Fracture penis
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 15 Paper 2, Q3c

### Definition and mechanism
- **Penile fracture** is a tear of the **tunica albuginea** with rupture of the corpus cavernosum, in the **erect** penis.
- Abnormal bending of the erect penis causes an abrupt rise in intracavernosal pressure beyond tunical strength → **transverse tear**.
- The tunica (inner circular, outer longitudinal layers) is **thinnest ventrolaterally**, so most tears are ventral or lateral, proximal to mid shaft.
- **Causes:** intercourse (commonest; rear-entry), masturbation, rolling over; self-inflicted "taqaandan" (Middle East); collagenase injection for Peyronie disease.
- **Bilateral tears in about 10%**; urethral injury about 20% in the West vs about 3% in Asia and the Middle East.

### Clinical features
- **Cracking or popping sound → pain → rapid detumescence → swelling and discoloration.**
- **"Eggplant deformity"** when Buck's fascia is intact; if torn, haematoma spreads to scrotum, perineum and suprapubic area.
- Penis deviates **away from the tear**; tunical defect may be palpable.
- **Urethral injury:** blood at meatus, haematuria, retention.

### Investigations
- **Diagnosis is clinical** (AUA [American Urological Association] Urotrauma Guideline).
- **Ultrasound** for equivocal cases (localises the tear); **MRI** (magnetic resonance imaging) if still equivocal (100% negative predictive value). Cavernosography is discouraged.
- Urethra: **intraoperative flexible cystoscopy** (AUA and EAU [European Association of Urology] preference) or RUG (retrograde urethrogram).
- **"False fracture":** dorsal vein or artery rupture.

### Management
- **Immediate surgical repair** (less curvature, ED [erectile dysfunction] and nodules than conservative care).
1. Incision: ventral penoscrotal or lateral over the defect; subcoronal degloving if site unclear (preputial ischaemia risk).
2. Evacuate haematoma; find the tear (artificial erection helps).
3. Close the tunica with **interrupted 2-0/3-0 absorbable sutures**; avoid debriding erectile tissue.
4. Urethra: partial → close over catheter; complete → tension-free repair; catheter 2–3 weeks.
- Abstinence about 1 month. Delay up to 7 days does not worsen outcome. After collagenase injection without urethral injury, conservative care is acceptable.

### Complications
- About 20%: nodule, curvature, ED, painful erection, infection, stricture or fistula.

**Diagram to draw:** cross-section of penis showing tunical tear, Buck's fascia and haematoma spread.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Snap, detumescence, eggplant deformity.
> - Urethral injury about 20% (West); suspect if bilateral.
> - Immediate exploration and interrupted absorbable tunical closure.

## Ovarian vein syndromes
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 15 Paper 2, Q3e

### Definition
- **Ovarian vein syndrome** (Clark, 1964) is extrinsic compression of the **right ureter** by a dilated or thrombosed **right ovarian vein**, causing intermittent hydroureteronephrosis. It is a **controversial** entity; many cases are now attributed to other causes.
- The plural also covers ovarian vein thrombosis and pelvic congestion.

### Anatomy (basis)
- Ovarian veins run in the infundibulopelvic ligaments. The **right** enters the IVC (inferior vena cava) obliquely; the **left** enters the left renal vein at a right angle.
- Gonadal vessels **cross anterior to the ureter**; ovarian veins dilate greatly in pregnancy.

### Types

| Syndrome | Mechanism | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Ovarian vein (ureteric) syndrome** | Dilated right ovarian vein compresses the ureter at the crossing | Multiparous women; right flank pain (premenstrual or in pregnancy); recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection) |
| **Ovarian vein thrombosis** | Postpartum hypercoagulability and venous stasis; mostly right-sided | Fever and right lower-quadrant or flank pain after delivery; may obstruct ureter; risk of pulmonary embolism |
| **Pelvic congestion syndrome** | Incompetent, refluxing ovarian veins with pelvic varices; sometimes secondary to nutcracker (left renal vein compression) | Chronic pelvic pain worse on standing, dyspareunia, post-coital ache |

### Controversy
- Right-sided dilatation in pregnancy is mostly **physiological hydronephrosis** (progesterone plus uterine compression; stops at the pelvic brim). Venous compression is rarely proven, so diagnosis is by **exclusion**.

### Investigations
- Ultrasound; **CT urography** or IVU (intravenous urography): right hydroureter to a smooth oblique extrinsic indentation, normal ureter below.
- **Diuretic renogram** to prove obstruction; retrograde pyelography.
- CT or MR (magnetic resonance) venography, Doppler; **gonadal venography** (gold standard for reflux).
- **Exclude:** stone, retrocaval ureter, iliac vessel crossing, retroperitoneal fibrosis, endometriosis.

### Management
- **Asymptomatic:** observe. **Obstruction:** stent or nephrostomy if infected or pregnant; definitive **ureterolysis with ligation and division of the ovarian vein** (open or laparoscopic).
- **Thrombosis:** anticoagulation and antibiotics.
- **Pelvic congestion:** progestogens or GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) analogues; **coil or sclerotherapy embolisation**; ligation.

**Diagram to draw:** right ovarian vein crossing anterior to the right ureter, with proximal hydroureteronephrosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Right ovarian vein compresses right ureter; controversial, diagnosis of exclusion.
> - Postpartum ovarian vein thrombosis: right side, anticoagulate.
> - Treatment: ureterolysis with vein division, or embolisation.

## Salvage procedures for Complicated PUJO.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 16 Paper 2, Q3f

*Assumed meaning: salvage options for recurrent PUJO (pelviureteric junction obstruction) after a failed pyeloplasty or endopyelotomy.*

### Definition and causes
- **Failure:** persistent pain or infection, unchanged or worse hydronephrosis, obstructed renogram or falling function. Most failures occur within 2 years, but up to 30% appear later.
- **Causes:** missed crossing vessel, ischaemic or tense anastomosis, urine leak with fibrosis, kinking, non-dependent anastomosis, infection.

### Evaluation
- Diuretic renogram (MAG3, mercaptoacetyltriglycine): split function and drainage.
- CT (computed tomography) angiography for a crossing vessel; retrograde or antegrade pyelogram for stricture length.
- Drain first (stent or nephrostomy) if infected or function is uncertain, then reassess.

### Salvage options
| Option | Indication | Results/notes |
|---|---|---|
| **Endopyelotomy** (antegrade or retrograde ureteroscopic, laser) | Short stricture, no significant crossing vessel, moderate hydronephrosis | About 70% after failed pyeloplasty; lateral incision; stent 4–6 weeks |
| **Redo pyeloplasty** (open, laparoscopic, robotic) | Most failures; failed endopyelotomy | About 83–89%; wide mobilisation of kidney and ureter; transperitoneal (scarred retroperitoneum) |
| **Ureterocalicostomy** | Scarred or small intrarenal pelvis, dilated lower-pole calyx | About 70% in redo series; **resect** lower-pole parenchyma (not nephrotomy) |
| **Buccal mucosa onlay graft** | Long scarred PUJ or proximal ureter, dense scar | Robotic with omental wrap; failure 15–25% (CORRUS) |
| **Appendiceal onlay flap** | Right-sided proximal stricture | Pedicled on appendiceal artery |
| Flaps (spiral, vertical), downward nephropexy | Gain length | Combine with redo repair |
| **Ileal ureter** | Long defect | Contraindicated if creatinine >2 mg/dL, bladder dysfunction, IBD (inflammatory bowel disease), radiation |
| **Autotransplantation** | Solitary or poor contralateral kidney; other repairs impossible | About 90% long term |
| **Nephrectomy** | Function <15% with normal contralateral kidney | Laparoscopic |

- Rule of thumb: failed pyeloplasty → try endourology first if anatomy suits; failed endopyelotomy → redo pyeloplasty.
- **Also know:** robotic near-infrared fluorescence (indocyanine green) to identify ureter and perfusion; Davis intubated ureterotomy (historical).

**Diagram to draw:** ureterocalicostomy: lower-pole parenchyma excised, spatulated ureter sewn to calyx.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Redo pyeloplasty is most successful (about 83–89%); endopyelotomy about 70%.
> - Ureterocalicostomy: resect lower-pole parenchyma; about 70% in redo series.
> - Function <15% with normal other kidney: nephrectomy.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 11 · Stones, Obstruction & Trauma

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Metabolic evaluation in stone disease (and management of bilateral staghorn calculi) (LAQ)

- Screening for every first-time former; 24-hour urine for high-risk and recurrent formers.
- Targets: volume >2.5 L, oxalate <40 mg, citrate >320 mg, calcium <250/300 mg.
- Distal RTA: ammonium chloride (NH4Cl) fails to lower pH below (<) 5.5; cystine <250 mg/L at pH 7.0–7.5.
- Borghi 1996: fluid halves recurrence; repeat 24-hour urine within 6 months.
- NCCT gold standard (97%/95%); 24-h urine for high-risk formers.
- Staghorn: PCNL first line; complete clearance is the goal.
- Bilateral: drain an infected side first, then the better kidney.
- AHA only when surgery is not possible.

#### Renal trauma (renal parenchymal and vascular injuries, including grade IV): diagnosis and management (LAQ)

- AAST 2018: segmental vessel injury grade IV; main vessel grade V.
- Grade IV = collecting system, UPJ, segmental vessel, bleeding beyond Gerota's, infarction.
- Image if gross haematuria, microhaematuria with SBP <90, deceleration or penetrating injury.
- Delayed CT phase is essential; blush means AE.
- Stable patients, even grade IV–V: non-operative ± AE (angioembolisation).
- Most stable grade IV heal non-operatively; extravasation resolves in up to 90%.
- Revascularise only a solitary kidney or bilateral injuries.

#### ESWL: mechanism, indications, factors affecting outcome, complications and current status (LAQ)

- Spallation, squeezing, shear, cavitation; dynamic squeezing strongest.
- Size is the main predictor; HU >1000 and SSD >10 cm predict failure.
- Lower Pole I: SFR 21% SWL vs 91% PCNL for lower pole stones >1 cm.
- Slow rate (60–90 shocks/min), ramping and good coupling improve breakage and reduce injury.
- Steinstrasse risk rises with burden >2 cm²; no routine stent.
- Use is declining as URS grows; burst wave lithotripsy is coming.

#### Management of upper ureteric stones (LAQ)

- Proximal stones pass in only 48%; MET does not help proximal stones.
- <10 mm: SWL equals URS; >10 mm: URS first (EAU).
- Flexible URS chases migrated fragments; laser ≤10 W in the ureter.
- Large impacted: antegrade PCNL or laparoscopic ureterolithotomy; avulsion risk highest in proximal third.

#### Lower urinary tract trauma in pelvic fracture: classification, assessment and (early) management of bladder and urethral injuries (LAQ)

- Blood at meatus → RUG before catheter.
- Gross haematuria + pelvic fracture = retrograde CT cystogram (350 mL).
- EPR: catheter 2–3 weeks; IPR: immediate repair.
- PFUI: SPC (standard), then delayed perineal anastomotic urethroplasty at 2–3 months.
- Primary realignment: brief, endoscopic; equal urethroplasty rates.
- Immediate repair: bladder neck or rectal injury (repair at once), penile fracture urethra, low-velocity penetrating injury.

#### Stone formation (etiopathogenesis) and medical management of urolithiasis / renal calculi (LAQ)

- Metastable zone between Ksp and Kf is where inhibitors and therapy act.
- Randall plaque = interstitial apatite anchor; ductal plugs suggest secondary cause.
- Urine ≥2.5 L/day; normal calcium, low salt; MET for distal 5–10 mm stones.
- Thiazide for hypercalciuria; potassium citrate 30–60 mEq/day for hypocitraturia.
- Uric acid pH 6.0–6.5; cystine pH 7.0–7.5 and <250 mg/L.

#### Uric acid calculi: etiopathogenesis, medical management and allopurinol (LAQ)

- Low urine pH is the main cause; pKa 5.5; no inhibitor exists.
- Insulin resistance reduces ammoniagenesis, so urine is acid.
- Potassium citrate to pH 6.0–6.5 (dissolution 7.0–7.2); avoid >7.0.
- Allopurinol only for hyperuricosuria or failed alkalinisation.
- Xanthine oxidase inhibitor; active metabolite oxypurinol; 300 mg/day.
- Use: hyperuricosuric calcium oxalate stones (Ettinger 1986); uric acid stones need alkali first.
- Hypersensitivity 2–8%; HLA-B*58:01; cut azathioprine dose.

#### Urinary crystals (LAQ)

- Cystine hexagons are always pathological.
- Acidic: uric acid, cystine; alkaline: struvite, phosphates; calcium oxalate at any pH.
- Indinavir: crystalluria in two-thirds, stones 4–13%, radiolucent.
- Refrigerated urine precipitates crystals: examine fresh within 2 hours.

#### Renal tubular acidosis (SAQ)

- Type 1: urine pH >6, hypokalaemia, hypocitraturia, calcium phosphate stones (up to 70%).
- Type 4: hyperkalaemia, low urine pH; obstructive uropathy.
- Positive UAG = renal cause; NH4Cl load fails to lower pH <5.5 in distal RTA.

#### Trans Uretero Ureteric anastomoses (SAQ)

- End-to-side, spatulated, tension-free; cross above the IMA.
- Avoid with stones, urothelial cancer, retroperitoneal fibrosis, radiation, tuberculosis.
- Risk: unilateral injury becomes bilateral.

#### RIRS / flexible ureteroscopy (SAQ)

- 3.6 Fr channel; ≥270° deflection.
- Access sheath lowers pressure but injures (may injure) the ureter in about 1 in 8 (ureteric injury).
- Laser power ≤10 W ureter, ≤25 W kidney.
- RIRS: stones <2 cm and patients on anticoagulants; first choice (modality of choice) when anticoagulants cannot be stopped (cannot stop).
- Sepsis about 5%, stricture about 3%.

#### Retro peritoneal fibrosis medical management (SAQ)

- Drain first, then steroids (about 80% respond).
- Vaglio 2011: prednisone better than tamoxifen (relapse 17% vs 50%).
- Rituximab for IgG4-related or refractory disease; ureterolysis for about 15%.

#### Medical expulsive therapy (MET) in urinary calculi (SAQ)

- Tamsulosin 0.4 mg; best for distal stones 5–10 mm.
- SUSPEND 2015: no benefit in an unselected population.
- Trial of passage 4–6 weeks, then intervene.

#### Stones (urolithiasis) in pregnancy (SAQ)

- US first; MRI or low-dose CT second (≤50 mGy).
- Observation first; no NSAIDs.
- URS with laser is definitive; SWL contraindicated.
- Stents encrust: change about every 4 weeks.

#### Uretero-calycostomy. (SAQ)

- Salvage for failed pyeloplasty, intrarenal pelvis, horseshoe kidney.
- Requires a dilated lower calyx and lower-pole resection, not simple nephrotomy.
- Stricture is the main failure; success about 70% in redo series.

#### Physiology of UPJ in relation to urine transport (SAQ)

- Myogenic peristalsis from atypical smooth muscle pacemakers; ICC-like cells back up.
- Low flow: several pelvic beats per ureteric beat; very high flow: dilatation without obstruction.
- Intrinsic UPJO: aperistaltic segment with fewer ICC-like cells.

#### Tubeless PCNL (SAQ)

- Tubeless = stent only; totally tubeless = neither.
- Benefit is mainly over large (>16 Fr) tubes.
- Keep a tube for bleeding, perforation, infection or second look.

#### Complications of PCNL, including bleeding and persistent haematuria after PCNL (SAQ)

- Fever 10.8%, transfusion 7%, thoracic 1.5%, death 0.05%.
- Fornix puncture avoids arterial injury; delayed bleeding = pseudoaneurysm, embolise.
- Early bleeding venous: tamponade.
- Bleeding at about 1 week arterial: pseudoaneurysm or AV fistula.
- Treatment: superselective angioembolisation.
- Supracostal access increases pleural injury; colon injury at left lower pole.

#### Describe various pt positions in PCNL (SAQ)

- Prone: upper pole and posterior calyx access, large working area.
- Galdakao-modified supine: one position for ECIRS, airway safe, gravity drainage.
- Outcomes equal; horizontal bolsters drop the kidney more.

#### Steinstresse. (SAQ)

- Risk rises several-fold with burden >2 cm².
- Coptcoat I–III by fragment size.
- Infected: PCN; uncomplicated: MET; failed: SWL to lead fragment or URS.

#### Treatment of inferior calyceal calculus (SAQ)

- Lower Pole I: PCNL 91% vs SWL 21% for >1 cm.
- <1 cm: SWL or RIRS; >1 cm: PCNL or RIRS.
- Unfavourable: acute angle, long narrow infundibulum, >900 HU, SSD >10 cm.
- Reposition stone during RIRS.

#### Primary Hyperoxaluria (SAQ)

- PH1: AGT (AGXT), pyridoxine cofactor, urinary glycolate; most severe.
- Urinary oxalate often >100 mg/day; nephrocalcinosis; oxalosis when GFR falls.
- Lumasiran (ILLUMINATE-A); combined liver–kidney transplant in PH1.

#### Fracture penis (SAQ)

- Snap, detumescence, eggplant deformity.
- Urethral injury about 20% (West); suspect if bilateral.
- Immediate exploration and interrupted absorbable tunical closure.

#### Ovarian vein syndromes (SAQ)

- Right ovarian vein compresses right ureter; controversial, diagnosis of exclusion.
- Postpartum ovarian vein thrombosis: right side, anticoagulate.
- Treatment: ureterolysis with vein division, or embolisation.

#### Salvage procedures for Complicated PUJO. (SAQ)

- Redo pyeloplasty is most successful (about 83–89%); endopyelotomy about 70%.
- Ureterocalicostomy: resect lower-pole parenchyma; about 70% in redo series.
- Function <15% with normal other kidney: nephrectomy.

# Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## CA bladder, etiology, newer investigations and treatment.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q1

### Introduction
- Commonest urinary tract cancer; over 90% **UC (urothelial carcinoma)**. Men 3 times commoner; women present later. About 70% are **NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer)**; it recurs often.

### Aetiology (risk factors)
| Factor | Key facts |
|---|---|
| **Smoking** | 30–40% of UC; risk 2–3 times. Aromatic amines form DNA adducts; **NAT2 (N-acetyltransferase 2) slow acetylators** at higher risk. Risk nears never-smokers >15 years after quitting |
| **Occupation** (5–10%) | Aromatic amines (dye, rubber, leather, painters, hairdressers); polycyclic hydrocarbons (aluminium, chimney sweeps) |
| Environment | **Arsenic** in water (West Bengal, Bangladesh, Taiwan) |
| Radiation | Pelvic radiotherapy 1.5–4 times; latency 15–30 years |
| Drugs | **Cyclophosphamide** (dose-dependent, above 20 g); pioglitazone (conflicting) |
| Chronic inflammation | *Schistosoma haematobium*, long-term catheter, stones → **SCC (squamous cell carcinoma)**; exstrophy → adenocarcinoma |
| Genetic | First-degree relatives 2 times; **Lynch syndrome** (MSH2); GSTM1 deletion |
- **Also know:** obesity raises risk; diet has no proven role.

### Pathology and staging
- **WHO (World Health Organization) 2004 grading**: PUNLMP (papillary urothelial neoplasm of low malignant potential), low grade, high grade. Grade predicts progression.
- **Papillary pathway** (FGFR3; recurs, rarely progresses) versus **flat CIS (carcinoma in situ)** (TP53, RB; progresses).

| TNM (tumour, node, metastasis), 2017 | Extent |
|---|---|
| Ta / Tis | Papillary non-invasive / flat CIS |
| T1 | Lamina propria |
| T2a / T2b | Inner / outer half of muscle |
| T3a / T3b | Perivesical fat, microscopic / macroscopic |
| T4a / T4b | Prostatic stroma, uterus, vagina / pelvic or abdominal wall |
| N1 / N2 / N3 | One pelvic node / several / common iliac |

### Clinical features and standard work-up
- **Painless gross haematuria (85%)**; irritative symptoms suggest CIS.
- **Cystoscopy and biopsy (gold standard)**; **CT (computed tomography) urography**; **urine cytology** (sensitivity 31–62%, specificity 94–100%, detects high grade; **Paris System** reporting).

### Newer investigations
| Test | Principle | Value |
|---|---|---|
| **Blue-light cystoscopy (PDD, photodynamic diagnosis)** with HAL (hexaminolevulinate) | Porphyrins in tumour fluoresce red under blue light | About one-third more CIS; recurrence benefit inconsistent |
| **NBI (narrow-band imaging)** | 415 and 540 nm light absorbed by haemoglobin highlights vessels | More tumours; many false positives |
| **Urine biomarkers** | NMP22 (nuclear matrix protein 22), BTA (bladder tumour antigen), **UroVysion FISH (fluorescence in situ hybridisation)** (chromosomes 3, 7, 17, 9p21), ImmunoCyt, Cxbladder | More sensitive, less specific than cytology; **none replaces cystoscopy** |
| **mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) with VI-RADS (Vesical Imaging-Reporting and Data System)** | Muscle-line integrity scored 1–5 | **≥3 suspicious for muscle invasion**; about 80–90% accurate; before TURBT |
| **En bloc TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)** | Tumour removed in one piece with detrusor | Better staging and muscle sampling, fewer perforations; recurrence similar |
- **Also know:** optical coherence tomography; urinary tumour DNA; PET-CT (positron emission tomography) for doubtful metastases.

### Treatment
**1. TURBT**: complete, with muscle in the specimen. **Re-TURBT within 6 weeks** for T1, incomplete resection or no muscle. **Single chemotherapy instillation within 6 hours** (mitomycin C, epirubicin or gemcitabine) for low-risk tumours; never BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) at this time.

**2. NMIBC by risk (AUA (American Urological Association))**
| Risk | Definition | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Low | Solitary low-grade Ta ≤3 cm; PUNLMP | Single instillation; surveillance |
| Intermediate | Low-grade Ta recurrent, >3 cm or multifocal; low-grade T1; high-grade Ta ≤3 cm | Chemotherapy or BCG; maintenance 1 year |
| High | High-grade T1; large, multifocal or recurrent high-grade Ta; any CIS; variant histology; lymphovascular invasion | **BCG induction + 3-year maintenance**; early cystectomy if very high risk |
- **BCG** induces a Th1 immune response; six weekly doses from 2–4 weeks after TURBT, then **SWOG (Southwest Oncology Group) maintenance** (recurrence-free survival 76.8 vs 35.7 months). Only agent that reduces progression.
- **BCG-unresponsive**: radical cystectomy; if unfit, **pembrolizumab** (KEYNOTE-057), **nadofaragene firadenovec**, gemcitabine–docetaxel, TAR-200.

**3. MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer), cT2–T4a N0M0**
- **Cisplatin-based NAC (neoadjuvant chemotherapy)** (dose-dense MVAC (methotrexate, vinblastine, doxorubicin, cisplatin) or gemcitabine–cisplatin) → **radical cystectomy + pelvic lymphadenectomy**. NAC adds 5–8% absolute survival (SWOG 8710: pT0 38% vs 15%). Cisplatin-ineligible: GFR (glomerular filtration rate) <60 mL/min, poor performance status, hearing loss, neuropathy, heart failure.
- **Diversion**: ileal conduit or orthotopic neobladder. Robotic equals open (**RAZOR**).
- **Trimodality therapy**: complete TURBT + chemoradiation (5-FU (fluorouracil) + mitomycin C, **BC2001**). For solitary T2, no CIS, no hydronephrosis; salvage cystectomy 10–30%.
- **Adjuvant nivolumab** for ≥pT3 or N+ (**CheckMate 274**: disease-free survival 20.8 vs 10.8 months). Partial cystectomy for selected solitary dome tumours.

**4. Metastatic**: **enfortumab vedotin + pembrolizumab** (**EV-302**: nearly doubled survival versus platinum). Otherwise platinum then **avelumab maintenance**; **erdafitinib** for FGFR3 alterations.

### Follow-up and recent advances
- Cystoscopy at 3 months, then by risk; upper-tract imaging in high risk.
- Molecular subtypes (basal tumours respond to cisplatin); **NIAGARA** (2024): perioperative durvalumab with NAC improved survival.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder wall layers with Ta, Tis, T1–T4 marked.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Smoking causes 30–40%; aromatic amines; NAT2 slow acetylators.
> - Blue light finds more CIS; VI-RADS ≥3 suggests invasion; no marker replaces cystoscopy.
> - High-risk NMIBC: BCG with 3-year maintenance.
> - MIBC: cisplatin NAC + cystectomy; trimodality in selected.

## Management of T1G3 (high-grade T1) TCC of urinary bladder, including T1 with CIS in a young woman
**Asked as:**
- Describe in detail the management of T1G3 TCC of urinary bladder? — *Summer 2019 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Discuss management of T1G3 bladder tumor — *Practice Set 7 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*
- Discuss treatment of 25 yr female with bladder TCC T1NoMo and C13 — *Practice Set 4 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(Practice Set 7 Paper II Q3a: also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q1)
(Practice Set 4 Paper II Q1: T1G3 management also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 2, Q1)

*Practice Set 4 Paper II Q1: "C13" is read as CIS (carcinoma in situ): T1 N0 M0 urothelial carcinoma with concurrent CIS in a 25-year-old woman.*

### Introduction and risk category
- T1G3 is high-grade (HG) urothelial carcinoma (the current name for TCC, transitional cell carcinoma) that invades (invading) the lamina propria but not the muscularis propria (not muscle). TCC is now urothelial carcinoma. CIS is flat and always high grade.
- T1 is 20–30% of NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer) and its most dangerous form: recurrence >80%, progression to muscle invasion 30–50%.
- Grade, not stage, drives progression. So every HG T1 is **high risk** (AUA [American Urological Association]/SUO [Society of Urologic Oncology] and EAU [European Association of Urology]).
- **T1 with CIS is high-risk NMIBC** (AUA and EAU). CIS is the second strongest progression factor after grade. Untreated CIS progresses to muscle invasion in 40–83%.
- **EAU 2021 very high risk:** T1 high grade (HG) with CIS plus at least one of age >70, multiple papillary tumours, or diameter ≥3 cm. In this woman (25-year-old), age does not count, so number and size decide. LVI (lymphovascular invasion) and variant histology also make it very high risk.
- **Young woman:** long life expectancy, fertility, pregnancy and sexual function shape every choice.

### Risk factors for progression
- **Adverse factors:** concurrent CIS (carcinoma in situ), LVI (lymphovascular invasion), variant histology, size >3 cm, multifocality, prostatic urethral involvement, residual T1 at re-resection.

| Factor | Significance |
|---|---|
| Concurrent CIS (carcinoma in situ) | Second most important factor after grade |
| Depth of invasion (substaging) | Invasion deep to the muscularis mucosae is worse; T1a/T1b proposed, no agreed method |
| LVI (lymphovascular invasion) | Suggests occult spread |
| Variant histology | Micropapillary (any amount), plasmacytoid, sarcomatoid, nested |
| Size >3 cm, multifocal, recurrent | More residual tumour and progression |
| Prostatic urethra, diverticulum | Poor BCG contact; diverticulum lacks muscle |
| Residual T1 at re-TURBT | Progression up to 80% |
| HG T1 at 3 months after BCG | Progression about 82% |
- **Also know:** EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) and CUETO risk tables; hydronephrosis suggests muscle invasion.

### Evaluation
- Cystoscopy, urine cytology (usually positive in CIS), CT (computed tomography) urography (upper tract tumour <5%, more with CIS), bimanual examination under anaesthesia (including vagina).
- Smoking and occupational history.
- **Pregnancy test** before CT and BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin); expert pathology review.

### Step 1: Complete, good-quality TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)
- Resect all visible tumour **with detrusor muscle** in the specimen; send the base separately.
- Blue-light cystoscopy or NBI (narrow-band imaging) finds more CIS (**blue-light cystoscopy** finds about one-third more CIS). **En bloc resection** (where possible) keeps orientation for substaging.
- Biopsy the prostatic urethra if tumour is at the bladder neck or trigone, or with CIS.
- Biopsy red velvety areas and the **bladder neck** (relevant to a later neobladder).

### Step 2: Re-TURBT (restaging) in all T1
- Within **2–6 weeks** (within 6 weeks), before BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin).
- **Why:** residual tumour in ≥40%; upstaging to muscle invasion in 25–30% (about half if no muscle in the first specimen). **Residual T1: progression up to 80%.** Re-TURBT improves recurrence- and progression-free survival: Divrik's RCT (randomised controlled trial) showed better recurrence-free and progression-free survival.

```
HG T1 on TURBT → re-TURBT at 2–6 weeks
   ├─ ≥T2                          → neoadjuvant chemotherapy + cystectomy
   ├─ residual T1 / very high risk → offer early radical cystectomy
   └─ T0, Ta or CIS                → BCG induction + 3-year maintenance
```
- Practice Set 7 version: HG T1 → re-TURBT; T0, Ta, CIS, T1 → BCG (bacille Calmette-Guérin); very high risk → offer early radical cystectomy.
- Practice Set 4 version: T1 + CIS → re-TURBT within 6 weeks; T0/Ta/CIS → BCG induction + 3-year maintenance.

### Step 3a: BCG – standard for most patients (bladder preservation)
- **Mechanism:** BCG attaches to (binds) **fibronectin**, triggers cytokines (IFN-γ [interferon-gamma], IL [interleukin]-2) and a **Th1 (T-helper 1)** immune (cytokine) response that kills tumour. It is the **only intravesical agent that reduces progression**.
- **Schedule:** start 2–4 weeks after TURBT, never immediately (intravasation and sepsis); induction weekly ×6.
- **Maintenance (SWOG [Southwest Oncology Group], Lamm 2000):** 3 weekly doses at 3 and 6 months, then every 6 months (6-monthly) to **3 years**. Median recurrence-free survival 76.8 vs 35.7 months. **EORTC 30962:** full dose for 3 years in high risk.
- **CIS:** tumour-free up to 84%, about half durable; persistent CIS at 3 months: second course gives a further 30–50% complete response.
- BCG is live: avoid pregnancy and breastfeeding during treatment.
- **Contraindications (avoid with):** gross haematuria, traumatic catheterisation, symptomatic UTI (urinary tract infection), previous BCG sepsis.
- **Toxicity:** cystitis common; BCG sepsis needs isoniazid, rifampicin, ethambutol and steroids (antituberculous drugs and steroids).

### Step 3b: Early (immediate) radical cystectomy
- **Rationale:** clinical T1 is understaged in one-third to one-half. Patients who progress under surveillance do worse than those presenting with muscle invasion (progressors do worse than primary muscle-invasive disease). Immediate cystectomy gives about **92% 10-year disease-free survival**.
- **AUA:** offer to fit patients with persistent HG T1 on re-resection, or T1 with CIS, LVI or variant histology.
- **EAU very-high-risk group (2021 model; risk factors: age >70, multiple papillary tumours, diameter ≥3 cm):** T1 HG with CIS plus ≥1 risk factor; T1 HG without CIS but with all three; also LVI, variant histology, CIS of the prostatic urethra (prostatic urethral CIS), persistent T1 at re-TURBT. Offer immediate cystectomy; BCG if declined or unfit.
- **Offer for (Practice Set 4):** very high risk, residual HG T1, LVI, variants, diffuse CIS, or patient preference.
- Also: diverticular tumour, tumour not controllable endoscopically.

### Step 3c: Cystectomy tailored to a young woman
- Standard female operation is anterior exenteration (bladder, urethra, anterior vagina, uterus, cervix, ovaries).
- **Organ-sparing** (≤cT2, bladder neck uninvolved): keep the **uterus and ovaries** (fertility, hormones, avoids surgical menopause; ovaries also make testosterone). Keep the **vagina and neurovascular tissue** (vaginal dissection no more dorsal than 2 and 10 o'clock) for sexual function and continence. Oncological safety of sparing is not fully known.
- **Orthotopic neobladder** suits her: needs a negative bladder-neck biopsy and **urethral frozen section** (negative bladder neck means negative urethra; CIS does not predict urethral involvement). Day continence 80–90%; **retention 20–30% in women**, so she must accept CIC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation). See Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3e.
- **Fertility:** if uterus or ovaries must go, offer oocyte or embryo cryopreservation first. Pregnancy after a neobladder is possible; caesarean uses a high midline incision to avoid the pouch.

### Step 4: BCG failure (BCG-unresponsive disease)
- **Adequate BCG for T1:** ≥5 of 6 induction doses.
- **BCG-unresponsive:** HG T1 at the 3-month check after induction; persistent or recurrent CIS or HG Ta/T1 despite adequate BCG; or HG relapse within 6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS) of the last dose.
- **Standard:** timely radical cystectomy (EAU, AUA).
- **Unfit or refusing (declined):** pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-057: CIS complete response 41%); nadofaragene firadenovec (53%); N-803 with BCG; gemcitabine–docetaxel; TAR-200; trials.
- **Also know:** BCG-intolerant; delayed relapse may respond to BCG rechallenge.

### Follow-up (AUA/SUO high risk)
- Bladder preserved: cystoscopy and cytology every 3–4 months (3-monthly) for 2 years, 6-monthly for 2 years, then yearly for life.
- Upper-tract imaging yearly for 2 years (regular upper-tract imaging).
- Positive cytology with normal cystoscopy: check prostatic urethra and upper tracts.
- After neobladder: B12, metabolic panel, upper tracts.

### Recent advances
- Checkpoint inhibitors with BCG in BCG-naive disease (trials); urine markers predicting BCG response; TAR-200.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder wall layers showing CIS, Ta, T1a/T1b (above and below the muscularis mucosae) and T2.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Re-TURBT in all T1 at 2–6 weeks (within 6 weeks); residual T1 means progression up to 80%.
> - BCG induction + 3-year maintenance (SWOG); only agent that reduces progression.
> - T1 + CIS = high risk; very high risk if multiple or ≥3 cm (EAU 2021).
> - Very high risk (CIS, LVI, variants, prostatic urethra): offer early cystectomy (92% 10-year disease-free survival).
> - BCG-unresponsive: cystectomy; pembrolizumab or nadofaragene if unfit.
> - Young woman: organ-sparing cystectomy, neobladder, fertility preservation.

## Use of intestinal segments in urology, and orthotopic neobladder
**Asked as:**
- Use of intestinal segments in urology — *Winter 2023 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Various uses of intestines in Uro — *Practice Set 17 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Principles of uses of bowel segments in urology. Describe and compare techniques of orthotopic neobladder. — *Practice Set 14 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Neobladder — *Practice Set 4 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered as an SAQ in 2022-Summer Paper 1, Q3a)
(also answered in 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q2)
(related: intestinal segments in 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q2; continent diversion and neobladder in Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q2)
(neobladder also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3a)

*Read as: the various uses of intestinal segments in urology.*

### Introduction
- Bowel is used to divert urine (conduit, continent pouch, neobladder), store urine, carry it to the skin, enlarge the bladder (augmentation), replace the ureter, make catheterisable channels and line a neovagina.
- Uses of bowel: conduit (ileal, colon), continent cutaneous pouch (Indiana, Mainz), **orthotopic neobladder**, augmentation cystoplasty, ileal ureter, catheterisable channels (Mitrofanoff, Yang–Monti).
- Choice depends on renal function, previous surgery or radiation, bowel disease, mesenteric length and the reconstruction needed.

### Uses at a glance
| Use | Segment and operation |
|---|---|
| **Incontinent diversion** | **Ileal conduit** (10–15 cm ileum, 10–15 cm from the ileocaecal valve); colon conduit (transverse) after pelvic radiation; jejunal conduit only if nothing else (avoid) |
| **Continent cutaneous pouch** | Indiana (right colon, plicated ileum as channel), Mainz I, Kock (ileal nipple valves); Mitrofanoff channel; with appendix or tapered ileum channel |
| **Orthotopic neobladder** | Studer (40–44 cm ileum plus afferent limb), Hautmann (~70 cm ileum, W shape), joined to the urethra; about 50–70 cm detubularised ileum |
| **Rectal reservoir** | Ureterosigmoidostomy, Mainz II: now rare (acidosis, cancer) |
| **Augmentation cystoplasty** | "Clam" ileocystoplasty (20–40 cm ileum; ~25 cm ileum), sigmoid, caecum; gastrocystoplasty in renal failure. For refractory neurogenic or small contracted bladder; needs CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) |
| **Ureteric replacement** | **Ileal ureter**, isoperistaltic ileum, for long defects; Yang–Monti tubes |
| **Catheterisable channels** | **Mitrofanoff** appendicovesicostomy; **Yang–Monti** ileal tube (1–2 cm ileum retubularised transversely); MACE (Malone antegrade continence enema) for bowel |
| **Neovagina** | **Sigmoid** preferred (wide lumen, mobile mesentery, less stenosis); ileum if pelvis irradiated; MRKH (Mayer–Rokitansky–Küster–Hauser), cloaca, gender-affirming vaginoplasty |

- Ileal ureter contraindications: creatinine >2 mg/dL, bladder outlet obstruction, inflammatory bowel disease or radiation enteritis.

### Choice of segment
| Segment | Electrolyte disturbance (metabolic effect) | Advantages / use | Drawbacks |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Stomach** | Hypochloraemic, hypokalaemic metabolic alkalosis | Secretes H⁺ and Cl⁻; less mucus; usable for renal failure, short gut, dense adhesions | **Haematuria–dysuria syndrome**, ulcers |
| **Jejunum** | Hyponatraemic, hypochloraemic, **hyperkalaemic acidosis** | Outside radiation field | Severe salt loss; avoid |
| **Ileum** | **Hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis** | Default: mobile, compliant, constant (reliable) blood supply; default conduit, ureteric replacement | B12 and bile-salt loss; obstruction ~10% |
| **Colon** | Hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis, hypokalaemia | Easy antireflux tunnel; transverse colon outside pelvic radiation field | Diarrhoea if ileocaecal valve used; obstruction ~5% |
| Appendix | — | Mitrofanoff catheterisable channel | — |

### Technical (surgical) principles of using bowel
- **Preparation:** ERAS (enhanced recovery after surgery); routine mechanical bowel preparation not needed. Assess renal function. Mark the stoma site before surgery when a stoma is possible, through the rectus.
- **Mesentery:** transilluminate, preserve (keep) a major mesenteric arcade, wide base; avoid watershed areas (Griffiths point at splenic flexure, Sudeck point at rectosigmoid).
- **Bowel anastomosis:** good blood supply (well-vascularised), no tension, no spillage, close the mesenteric window.
- **Detubularisation** (Kock): opening the bowel on its antimesenteric border and refolding it into a sphere gives a spherical reservoir. This **abolishes coordinated (peristaltic) contractions** and increases volume (gives more volume) for the same surface area; a **sphere** gives the largest volume for its surface area; by Laplace's law (pressure ∝ wall tension/radius) a larger radius means lower pressure, so pressure stays low.

### Ureterointestinal anastomoses
| Technique | Type | Stricture |
|---|---|---|
| **Bricker** | Refluxing, end to side | 4–22% (mean 6%) |
| **Wallace** | Refluxing, ureters joined end to end | ~3% (lowest) |
| Leadbetter–Clarke, Goodwin | Nonrefluxing (antireflux) tunnel in colon | 8–14% |

- Principles: spatulate (spatulated), preserve periureteric tissue, tension-free, mucosa to mucosa, soft stents (stented; leak 3–5% without). Bring the bowel to the ureter, not the ureter to the bowel.
- Refluxing (Bricker, Wallace; stricture about 3–6%) versus antireflux tunnels (more strictures).
- Fix and retroperitonealise the anastomosis. The left ureter passes behind the sigmoid mesentery; avoid angulation under the IMA (inferior mesenteric artery), a common stricture site.
- Reflux with normal unobstructed ureters has not been shown to harm the adult kidney; antireflux tunnels stricture more. Obstruction, not low-pressure reflux, damages kidneys.

### Applications
- **Incontinent diversion:** ileal conduit (10–15 cm ileum, 10–15 cm from ileocaecal valve); colon conduit after pelvic radiation.
- **Continent cutaneous pouch** (Indiana, Mainz, Kock) with appendix or tapered ileum channel.
- **Orthotopic neobladder** (Studer, Hautmann): about 50–70 cm detubularised ileum joined to the urethra.
- **Ureterosigmoidostomy:** now rare (acidosis, cancer).
- **Augmentation cystoplasty** ("clam" ileocystoplasty, ~25 cm ileum) for refractory neurogenic or small contracted bladder; needs CIC.
- **Ileal ureter:** isoperistaltic ileum for long defects. Contraindicated if creatinine >2 mg/dL, bladder outlet obstruction, inflammatory bowel disease or radiation enteritis.
- **Catheterisable channels:** Mitrofanoff appendicovesicostomy, Yang–Monti ileal tube.

### Stomas
- **Protruding (nipple, "rosebud") stoma** for appliance wearers: less stenosis, better appliance fit. **Flush stoma** for continent pouches that are catheterised (protruding "rosebud" for appliances; flush for catheterised pouches).
- **Loop-end (Turnbull) stoma** for obese patients with a short mesentery. Site is marked before surgery, through the rectus muscle.

### Selecting conduit versus continent diversion (patient selection)
- **Renal function needed for continent diversion:** creatinine <2 mg/dL or GFR (glomerular filtration rate) >35 mL/min; urine pH ≤5.8 after ammonium chloride load; urine osmolality ≥600 mOsm/kg after water deprivation; minimal proteinuria; normal liver function; ability to self-catheterise.
- **Contraindications to continent diversion:** poor renal or hepatic function, inability or unwillingness to self-catheterise, inflammatory bowel disease or short gut. A neobladder also needs a tumour-free urethra and a competent sphincter.
- A conduit is simplest and fastest; continent options improve body image but carry more reoperations and metabolic problems.

### Complications (to plan for)
**Metabolic** (worse with longer segment, longer dwell (contact) time, poorer renal function)
- **Hyperchloraemic acidosis (ileum, colon):** NH₄⁺ (ammonium) replaces Na⁺ (sodium) on the Na⁺/H⁺ exchanger, coupled with Cl⁻/HCO₃⁻ exchange, so ammonium chloride is absorbed. Treat with sodium bicarbonate or citrate; chlorpromazine or nicotinic acid limit chloride transport.
- **Hypokalaemia:** worse with colon; replace potassium before correcting acidosis (paralysis risk).
- **Jejunal syndrome:** treat with sodium chloride and thiazide. **Gastric alkalosis:** H2 blockers or PPI (proton-pump inhibitor).
- **Altered sensorium:** hyperammonaemia (drain the pouch, lactulose), hypomagnesaemia.
- **Drug reabsorption:** phenytoin, methotrexate; drain pouches during chemotherapy.
- **Osteomalacia:** acidosis, vitamin D resistance, calcium loss; growth retardation (delay) in children.
- **Nutrition:** B12 deficiency (give parenteral B12 after 5–7 years of ileal diversion), bile-salt diarrhoea.

**Other**
- Infection (bacteriuria in about three-quarters of conduits; common; treat pure Proteus or Pseudomonas; treat only symptoms or urea-splitting organisms).
- Mucus.
- **Stones:** colon conduit 3–4%, ileal conduit 10–12%, continent reservoir (reservoirs) ~20% (mucus, staples).
- **Augment perforation (pouch rupture):** surgical emergency; regular CIC prevents it.
- Ureterointestinal stricture (lifelong risk), renal deterioration, bowel obstruction, stomal stenosis and parastomal hernia.
- **Cancer (secondary malignancy):** ureterosigmoidostomy 6–29% after 10–20 years (endoscopy from 5 years); augments (endoscopy from 10 years). Neovagina: stenosis, mucus discharge, adenocarcinoma reported.

### Orthotopic neobladder
**Definition / indication:** an **orthotopic** detubularised bowel (usually ileal) reservoir joined to the native urethra after radical cystectomy, when the urethra is tumour-free, to allow **volitional voiding** per urethra without a stoma. 80–90% of men and about 75% of women are medically eligible.

**Principles:**
1. **Reliable outlet:** intact rhabdosphincter (pudendal nerve), healthy, tumour-free urethra.
2. **Low pressure:** complete **detubularisation** abolishes coordinated contractions; folding into a **sphere** gives most volume for surface area.
3. **Adequate volume (capacity):** ≥500 mL when mature (44 cm double-folded ileum: <200 mL initially (early), **500–600 mL** by 1 year).
4. Unobstructed upper tracts.

**Selection / contraindications:**
- **Absolute contraindications:** tumour at the **urethral margin** (positive urethral margin, frozen section), urethral stricture, poor renal function (eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) <35–40 mL/min), poor or severe liver disease, inability to self-catheterise (catheterise).
- **Relative:** prostatic stromal invasion (men); bladder-neck or anterior vaginal tumour (women); poor sphincter; prior radiation (irradiated pelvis); frailty. Age, obesity and CIS (carcinoma in situ) alone are not bars.

### Neobladder techniques compared (types)
| Design | Segment and shape | Ureters | Advantage | Drawback |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Studer** | 40–44 cm ileum, double-folded U + afferent limb | Refluxing, isoperistaltic limb | Simple, no staples, short ureters; stricture <3% | Reflux on cystogram (benign) |
| **Hautmann W** | About 70 cm ileum, W | Refluxing (since 1997); largest capacity | Largest early capacity, early continence | More late retention, more absorption |
| T pouch | 44 cm + 15 cm T limb | Flap-valve antireflux (antireflux flap valve) | Accepts dilated or short ureters | More reoperations than Studer |
| Abol-Enein | 40 cm ileum, W | Serous-lined tunnel | Durable antireflux | Needs long, non-dilated ureters |
| Camey II | 65 cm ileum, U | Le Duc tunnel (Le Duc antireflux) | Early detubularised design | Le Duc strictures 7–29% |
| Hemi-Kock | 61 cm ileum | Intussuscepted nipple | Good early results | Nipple stenosis, staple stones |
| Padua (VIP) | About 42 cm ileum, funnel | Le Duc (Le Duc antireflux) | Anatomical funnel; robotic | — |
| Mainz III | 10–15 cm caecum + 20–30 cm ileum (ileocaecal) | Submucosal tunnel (tunnelled) | Good compliance | Loses ileocaecal valve |
| Sigmoid (Reddy) | 35 cm sigmoid (colon), U | Tunnelled | Spares ileum (short bowel) | Poor compliance, urgency |

- **Hautmann vs Studer:** larger initial capacity gives earlier continence but more late retention and absorption.
- **Studer steps (technique, brief):** isolate ileum (ileum taken) 15–20 cm proximal to the ileocaecal valve; open distal 40–44 cm antimesenterically; sew into a U and fold into a sphere; stented ureters end-to-side (ureteric anastomoses) to the afferent limb; dependent point to urethra.
- **USC (University of Southern California) RCT (randomised controlled trial):** Studer vs T pouch, no difference in renal function at 3 years; T pouch needed more secondary procedures.

### Neobladder outcomes
| Outcome | Result |
|---|---|
| Day continence | 80–90% (3–6 months) |
| Night incontinence | 20–50% (no sphincter reflex, mucosal water secretion) |
| Retention needing CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) | Men 4–10%; women 20–30%: men leak, women fail to empty |
| Urethral recurrence (men) | About 7% at 5 years |

### Neobladder complications
- **Early:** urine leak, ileus, infection, mucus plugging.
- **Late:** **hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis**, **vitamin B12** deficiency, ureteroileal stricture (<3%), stones, pouch rupture, incisional hernia, urethral recurrence.

### Care and follow-up
- Neobladder: mucus irrigation, bicarbonate; voiding training (sit, relax, strain), timed voiding, night alarm. Annual metabolic panel, B12, upper tracts; urethral cytology if CIS.
- Lifelong: electrolytes, bicarbonate, renal function, B12, upper-tract imaging (ultrasound or CT) for stricture and stones; endoscopy of colonic or augmented segments as above.

### Recent advances
- Robotic intracorporeal diversion (complications similar to open in experienced hands); robotic intracorporeal neobladder comparable to open; tissue-engineered bladder (experimental) and alternatives to bowel (ureterocystoplasty, autoaugmentation) remain limited.
- **Also know:** neobladder uptake is low despite 80–90% of men being eligible; lifelong follow-up of electrolytes, B12, upper tracts.

**Diagram to draw:** ileal conduit with mesenteric arcade and Wallace anastomosis.

**Diagram to draw:** Studer (U-)pouch with afferent limb, ureters and urethral anastomosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Ileum and colon: hyperchloraemic acidosis (NH₄Cl absorption).
> - Jejunum: hyperkalaemia; stomach: hypochloraemic alkalosis.
> - Continent diversion: GFR >35, pH ≤5.8, osmolality ≥600.
> - Wallace has the lowest stricture rate; detubularisation gives low pressure.
> - Sigmoid is the default neovagina; Yang–Monti when the appendix is absent.
> - Detubularise and make a sphere; ≥500 mL; 500–600 mL by 1 year.
> - Neobladder absolute bar: positive urethral margin, stricture, poor renal function.
> - Studer and Hautmann (refluxing) are commonest; USC RCT: Studer = T pouch.
> - Day continence 80–90%, night 20–50%; women retain (20–30%).

## Classification of bladder cancer and bladder-preserving treatment of TCC
**Asked as:**
- Classify CA bladder. Discuss in details organ preserving surgeries for TCC of bladder. — *Practice Set 10 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Bladder preserving approach in invasive TCC of bladder — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

*Read as: classification of bladder cancer, and bladder-preserving treatment of TCC (transitional cell carcinoma, urothelial carcinoma).*

### A. Classification
**1. Histological**

| Type | Share | Note |
|---|---|---|
| **Urothelial carcinoma** | ~90% | Variants: micropapillary, plasmacytoid, sarcomatoid, nested; squamous or glandular differentiation |
| Squamous cell carcinoma | ~5% | Schistosomiasis, catheter, stones |
| Adenocarcinoma | ~1–2% | Urachal (dome), exstrophy |
| Small cell / others | <1% | Neuroendocrine; sarcoma |

**2. Grade (WHO 2004/2016, World Health Organization)**
- PUNLMP (papillary urothelial neoplasm of low malignant potential), low-grade and high-grade carcinoma. WHO 1973 (G1–G3) is still reported by some.
- Low-grade (FGFR3 pathway) recurs but rarely progresses. High-grade and CIS (carcinoma in situ; TP53/RB pathway) progress.

**3. Stage: TNM 2017**

| T | Definition |
|---|---|
| Ta / Tis | Non-invasive papillary / flat CIS |
| T1 | Lamina propria |
| T2a / T2b | Inner / outer half of muscle |
| T3a / T3b | Perivesical fat, microscopic / macroscopic |
| T4a / T4b | Prostatic stroma, uterus, vagina / pelvic or abdominal wall |

- N1 one true-pelvic node; N2 ≥2 pelvic nodes; N3 common iliac; M1a distant nodes; M1b organs.

**4. Clinical groups**
- **NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive, ~70%)**: Ta, Tis, T1. Risk groups: low (solitary primary low-grade Ta <3 cm), intermediate, high (high-grade, T1 or CIS).
- **MIBC (muscle-invasive)**: T2–T4a. Metastatic.
- **Molecular:** luminal papillary versus basal/squamous subtypes.

### B. Organ-preserving surgery
**Why (introduction):** Standard for MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer) is cisplatin NAC (neoadjuvant chemotherapy) plus RC (radical cystectomy). RC is morbid and patients are elderly. Bladder preservation suits **unfit** patients and **fit, motivated patients with limited disease**. Single modalities fail because they leave either the remaining urothelium or occult nodes untreated (cT2 understaged to pT3 in up to about 40%, occult nodes about 25%).

**Options:**

| Option | Use |
|---|---|
| Radical TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour) alone | Not a primary treatment |
| **Partial cystectomy + PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)** | Solitary tumour **<3 cm** in mobile bladder (dome), **no CIS (carcinoma in situ)**, 1–2 cm margin, frozen sections; **urachal adenocarcinoma** |
| RT (radiotherapy) or chemotherapy alone | Inferior |
| **TMT (trimodality therapy)** | Best studied, guideline-accepted |

**1. TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour) for NMIBC: the standard**
- Complete resection with **detrusor muscle in the specimen**; separate base biopsies; cold-cup biopsy of suspicious areas.
- **En bloc resection** (loop or laser) keeps architecture, gives more detrusor and fewer perforations.
- **Repeat TURBT within 6 weeks** for all T1, incomplete resection or no muscle. Residual tumour in ≥40%.
- Then intravesical chemotherapy or BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) by risk group.

**2. Radical TURBT for MIBC**
- Only for tumour in superficial muscle with a negative re-resection; about 10% are pT0 at cystectomy.
- About a third later develop MIBC. Not a primary treatment.

**3. Partial cystectomy**

| Ideal candidate | Contraindication |
|---|---|
| Solitary tumour **<3 cm** in a mobile part (dome) | **CIS**, multifocal tumour |
| No hydronephrosis | Trigone or bladder neck tumour |
| 1–2 cm margin possible; adequate capacity left | Small bladder; prostatic urethral involvement |
| **Urachal adenocarcinoma** (treatment of choice) | |

**Steps:** NAC (neoadjuvant cisplatin chemotherapy) if eligible → PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection) → cystotomy away from tumour → full-thickness excision with perivesical fat → **frozen-section margins** → ureteric reimplantation if needed → watertight closure → drain. For urachal cancer, en bloc dome, urachus and umbilicus.

**Outcomes:** 5-year OS (overall survival) 67–70%; intact bladder 65–74%; salvage RC up to a quarter. Late recurrence, so lifelong follow-up.

**4. TMT (trimodality therapy): the best-studied option**
- **Components:** (1) maximal **visibly complete TURBT**; (2) RT (radiotherapy) to bladder ± nodes; (3) concurrent **radiosensitising chemotherapy**.
- **Selection:** cT2, solitary (small solitary tumour), **no hydronephrosis, no extensive CIS (no CIS)**, complete TURBT, good bladder function, compliant patient. Only 6–19% of MIBC qualify.

| Radiosensitisers | Evidence |
|---|---|
| **Mitomycin C + 5-FU (fluorouracil)** | **BC2001:** 2-year locoregional DFS (disease-free survival) 67% vs 48% for RT alone |
| Cisplatin-based | MGH (Massachusetts General Hospital)/RTOG (Radiation Therapy Oncology Group) protocols |
| Gemcitabine; carbogen + nicotinamide | Cisplatin-ineligible; hypoxia modification |

- **Dose:** 64–66 Gy in 32 fractions or **55 Gy in 20** (non-inferior).

```
TURBT → chemoRT 64 Gy/32 fr or 55 Gy/20 fr → cystoscopy + biopsy at 3–4 months
     ├─ Complete response → surveillance (NMIBC recurrence: TURBT + BCG)
     └─ Persistent / recurrent MIBC (invasive) → salvage cystectomy (10–30%)
```

- **Salvage cystectomy** in **10–30%**: the designed safety net. Non-muscle-invasive (NMIBC) recurrence: TURBT and intravesical BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin).
- **Outcomes:** 5-year CSS (cancer-specific survival) 50–84%. MGH/RTOG: complete response 69%; 80% of 5-year survivors keep the bladder. Propensity-matched RC vs TMT: equal 5-year metastasis-free survival (about 75%) and similar CSS (81% vs 84%). No completed randomised trial (SPARE closed).

### Comparison

| | Radical cystectomy | Bladder preservation |
|---|---|---|
| Staging | Pathological | Clinical only |
| Function | Diversion, sexual dysfunction | Native bladder; RT toxicity |
| Follow-up | Upper tract, urethra | **Cystoscopy 3-monthly for 2 years, to 10 years**, plus CT |

- **Follow-up (bladder preservation):** Cystoscopy every 3–4 months for 2 years, then less often, for 10 years; CT (computed tomography) urogram and chest imaging; cytology.

### Recent advances
- Checkpoint inhibitors with chemoradiation (trials ongoing; under trial: S1806, KEYNOTE-992); bladder sparing after complete response to NAC (RETAIN: about half recurred); robotic partial cystectomy.
- **Also know:** RT alone is quasi-palliative; chemotherapy alone not recommended.

**Diagram to draw:** TNM T-stage diagram of the bladder wall with a TMT flowchart (as above).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - WHO 2004/2016: PUNLMP, low grade, high grade; T3b = macroscopic perivesical fat.
> - Re-TURBT within 6 weeks for T1, no muscle or incomplete resection.
> - Partial cystectomy: solitary <3 cm (dome tumour), no CIS, frozen-section margins, always PLND.
> - TMT = complete TURBT + RT + radiosensitiser; T2, no hydronephrosis, no CIS.
> - TMT: BC2001 MMC/5-FU (mitomycin C/5-FU) + RT, DFS 67% vs 48%; salvage cystectomy 10–30%.

## Discuss principles of continence in continent urinary diversion. Application of Mitrofanoff principle in continent urinary and GI reconstruction.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 2, Q2
(principles also answered in Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q2)

*Assumed meaning: GI = gastrointestinal (bowel) reconstruction, i.e. antegrade continence enema channels.*

### Introduction
- A continent diversion empties at will: by voiding through the urethra (**orthotopic neobladder**) or by CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) of a stoma (**continent cutaneous**).
- **Continence is a pressure balance:** reservoir pressure must stay below outlet resistance. Failure of either side causes leakage.

### Principles of continence

**A. Low-pressure, adequate-capacity reservoir**
- **Detubularisation** (opening bowel on its antimesenteric border) and cross-folding break up coordinated peristaltic contractions.
- **Spherical shape:** greatest volume for a given surface area. By **Laplace's law** (P = 2T/R), a larger radius gives lower pressure for the same wall tension.
- **Capacity ≥500 mL** when mature. Bowel stretches with time (ileum more than colon); 44 cm ileum holds <200 mL at first and 500–600 mL within a year.
- Ileum is most compliant; colon has pressure waves.

**B. Reliable outlet**
- **Orthotopic:** intact **rhabdosphincter** (pudendal nerve), healthy urethra, careful apical dissection, nerve sparing. Voiding is by Valsalva and pelvic floor relaxation. Night leakage (20–50%) reflects loss of the guarding reflex.
- **Continent cutaneous:** an efferent channel that is easy to catheterise and has a durable continence mechanism.

| Mechanism | Example | How it works | Weakness |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Nipple valve** (intussuscepted) | Kock, Mainz I | Reservoir pressure compresses the intussuscepted segment | Same pressure effaces it; failure ≥15%, reoperation about one-third |
| **Flap valve** | **Mitrofanoff**, Yang–Monti, Kropp | Narrow tube in a submucosal tunnel is coapted as pressure rises | No pop-off; stomal stenosis |
| **Imbricated ileocaecal valve + tapered ileum** | Indiana pouch | Continence zone lies at the plicated valve | Bowel frequency, B12 loss |
- **Also know** (native outlet): Kropp, Pippi Salle, slings, AUS (artificial urinary sphincter), bulking, bladder neck closure.

**C. Protect the upper tracts**
- Unobstructed ureterointestinal anastomosis; eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) about ≥35–40 mL/min for the acid load; ability to self-catheterise.

### The Mitrofanoff principle
- **Mitrofanoff (1980):** any supple, narrow tube implanted in a **submucosal tunnel** of a low-pressure reservoir, with its other end at skin, gives a **continent catheterisable channel**.
- As the reservoir fills, pressure transmitted through the mucosa flattens the tunnelled segment against the muscle backing: a **flap valve**. Continence lies in the tunnel, not in the conduit.
- Appendix: only a **2 cm tunnel** suffices because of its small calibre.

**Conduits**
| Conduit | Points |
|---|---|
| **Appendix** (first choice) | Natural tube, minimal harvest morbidity; needs length ≥5–6 cm and ≥12 Fr; caecal cuff or **Mitchell caecal extension** adds length |
| **Yang–Monti** | 2–3 cm ileum opened on the antimesenteric border and retubularised transversely over 12 Fr; double or spiral Monti for obese; twice the revisions |
| Tapered ileum | Long enough; kinks if redundant |
| Ureter, fallopian tube | More stenosis |
| **Casale** bladder flap | No bowel; consumes bladder capacity |

**Technique (appendicovesicostomy)**
1. Mobilise caecum; detach appendix with a caecal cuff, preserving mesoappendix.
2. Spatulate tip; tunnel submucosally (posterolateral or anterior bladder wall), intra- or extravesically.
3. Bring the base to the umbilicus or right iliac fossa with skin flaps, without tension or twist; keep it short and straight; fix the bladder to the abdominal wall.
4. Catheterise after each step. Channel catheter 2–4 weeks; then CIC every 4 hours by day.

### Applications in urinary reconstruction
- **Neurogenic bladder** (spina bifida, spinal injury), often with augmentation, when urethral CIC is difficult (wheelchair-bound, sensate urethra).
- After bladder neck reconstruction or closure; exstrophy; valve bladder.
- **Continent cutaneous diversion** after cystectomy: Penn and right colon pouch (appendix in taenia; up to 100% continence); Yang–Monti if no appendix.
- Unusable urethra: complex stricture, urethrectomy.

### Applications in GI reconstruction: MACE
- **MACE (Malone antegrade continence enema; Malone, 1990):** appendix brought to skin as an appendicocaecostomy with an antireflux tunnel (flap valve) so stool does not leak.
- Antegrade washouts empty the colon, giving **faecal continence** in neurogenic bowel (spina bifida), anorectal malformations, cloaca and refractory constipation.
- Options: Monti-MACE; **split appendix** for both MACE and urinary channel; caecostomy button.

### Results and complications
- **Continence >95%** for flap valves; nipple valves about 85%.
- **Stomal stenosis is the commonest problem**; also false passage, channel polyps or granulation, pouch stones, revision (suprafascial or subfascial).
- Missed CIC: no pop-off, so **reservoir rupture** or upper tract damage.

**Diagram to draw:** flap valve (tunnelled appendix collapsing as pressure rises) beside a nipple valve.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Continence = low reservoir pressure (detubularise, sphere, ≥500 mL) against a reliable outlet.
> - Mitrofanoff 1980: submucosal flap valve; appendix needs only a 2 cm tunnel.
> - Yang–Monti when appendix absent or used for MACE (Malone 1990).
> - Continence >95%; stomal stenosis is the commonest complication.

## Muscle-invasive bladder cancer (TCC): management, neoadjuvant and adjuvant chemotherapy, neobladder types
**Asked as:**
- Outline management of muscle invasive TCC. give critical evaluation of various types of neobladder. — *Practice Set 12 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Outline the management of muscle invasive TCC. Discuss the role of adjuvant & neoadjuvant chemotherapy. — *Practice Set 16 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)

(Practice Set 12 Paper II Q2: neobladder also answered in Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3e and Practice Set 3 Paper 4, Q2)
(Practice Set 16 Paper II Q1: MIBC management also answered in Practice Set 12 Paper 2, Q2; neoadjuvant therapy in 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- MIBC (muscle-invasive bladder cancer; TCC, transitional cell carcinoma, now called urothelial carcinoma) is cT2–T4a disease.
- Nearly half of patients treated by RC (radical cystectomy) alone develop metastases from occult micrometastases, so cure needs local plus systemic therapy, planned by an MDT (multidisciplinary team).

### Staging
- Complete TURBT / Re-TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour) with (detrusor) muscle; **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) with VI-RADS** (Vesical Imaging-Reporting and Data System; ≥3 suggests invasion); CT (computed tomography) urogram and chest CT; bone scan if symptoms (symptomatic).
- Assess fitness and cisplatin eligibility.

### Management algorithm
```
cT2–T4a N0 M0
 ├─ Cisplatin-eligible → cisplatin NAC (neoadjuvant chemotherapy)
 │      → RC + bilateral PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection)
 ├─ Cisplatin-ineligible → RC + PLND directly
 │      (pT3–4 or N+ after either → adjuvant nivolumab or chemotherapy)
 ├─ Selected, motivated → trimodality therapy (TMT)
 └─ Unfit → chemoradiation / palliative radiotherapy
cN+ → induction chemotherapy → consolidation RC in responders
M1  → systemic therapy
```

**1. Cystectomy pathway: radical cystectomy (standard of care for cT2–T4a N0 M0)**
- **Cisplatin-based NAC (neoadjuvant chemotherapy):** ddMVAC (dose-dense methotrexate, vinblastine, doxorubicin, cisplatin) or GC (gemcitabine–cisplatin) ×4 (details below).
- **Cisplatin-ineligible** → direct cystectomy.
- **RC (radical cystectomy) + standard bilateral PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection):** obturator, external and internal iliac nodes. **SWOG S1011:** extended PLND gave no survival gain (benefit).
- Men: cystoprostatectomy. Women: anterior exenteration; organ-sparing in selected cases.
- Within 12 weeks of diagnosis: do not delay beyond **12 weeks** from diagnosis (worse survival). Diversion: ileal conduit, orthotopic neobladder or continent pouch.
- **Adjuvant:** cisplatin chemotherapy if no NAC; **nivolumab** for pT3–4 or N+ (**CheckMate 274:** disease-free survival 20.8 vs 10.8 months) (details below).

**2. Bladder preservation: TMT (trimodality therapy)**
- Maximal (visibly complete) TURBT + RT (radiotherapy) (64 Gy, or 55 Gy in 20 fractions) + radiosensitiser. **BC2001:** mitomycin C + 5-FU (fluorouracil) improved 2-year locoregional disease-free survival (67% vs 48%).
- Best candidates (select): cT2, solitary tumour, **no CIS (carcinoma in situ), no hydronephrosis**, good bladder function.
- 5-year cancer-specific survival 50–84%; salvage cystectomy 10–30%.
- **Partial cystectomy:** solitary dome or urachal tumour, no CIS.
- **Also know:** chemotherapy alone or radical TURBT alone is not recommended.

**3. Metastatic disease**
- First line: **EV (enfortumab vedotin) + pembrolizumab** (EV-302: about doubled overall survival (OS) vs platinum). Alternatives: platinum chemotherapy then **avelumab maintenance** (JAVELIN Bladder 100); erdafitinib for FGFR / FGFR3 (fibroblast growth factor receptor 3) alterations.

### Neoadjuvant chemotherapy (NAC)
**Rationale**
- Treats micrometastases early, while tumour burden is low, and is better tolerated before surgery.
- Downstages; **pT0 at cystectomy** gives about 90% 5-year recurrence-free survival.
- Drawback: delays surgery in non-responders.

**Eligibility:** cT2–T4a N0 M0 and cisplatin-fit. **Cisplatin-ineligible** if any one: GFR (glomerular filtration rate) <60 mL/min, ECOG (Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group) performance status ≥2, grade ≥2 hearing loss or neuropathy, NYHA (New York Heart Association) class III–IV failure → direct cystectomy.

| Trial | Regimen | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **SWOG 8710** | MVAC (methotrexate, vinblastine, doxorubicin, cisplatin) ×3 then RC | pT0 38% vs 15%; median survival 77 vs 46 months; P = 0.06 |
| **BA06 30894** (EORTC/MRC) | CMV (cisplatin, methotrexate, vinblastine) ×3 | 16% relative reduction in mortality; 10-year OS (overall survival) 36% vs 30% |
| **ABC meta-analysis** (Advanced Bladder Cancer) | Cisplatin combinations | OS HR (hazard ratio) 0.86: **5% absolute 5-year survival gain** |
| **VESPER** | ddMVAC (dose-dense MVAC) ×6 vs GC (gemcitabine–cisplatin) ×4 | ddMVAC better PFS than GC: organ-confined response 77% vs 63%; 3-year PFS (progression-free survival) 66% vs 56%; more toxicity |
| **NIAGARA** | Perioperative durvalumab + GC, then adjuvant durvalumab, vs GC | Improved (better) event-free survival (HR 0.68) and OS (HR 0.75) |

- **Regimens:** ddMVAC 3–6 cycles or GC 4 cycles (EAU, AUA, NCCN: European Association of Urology, American Urological Association, National Comprehensive Cancer Network).
- **No benefit:** single-agent cisplatin, carboplatin regimens, neoadjuvant radiotherapy. Cisplatin-ineligible patients go straight to RC.
- The chapter calls neoadjuvant immunotherapy unproven; NIAGARA has since made perioperative durvalumab + GC an option.

### Adjuvant therapy
| | Neoadjuvant | Adjuvant |
|---|---|---|
| Selection | Clinical stage (understaging risk) | True pathological stage, but still overtreats |
| Delivery | Better tolerated | Post-RC morbidity; a quarter never receive it |
| Evidence | Level 1 | Weaker: small, early-closed trials |

**Adjuvant chemotherapy**
- Trials were small or closed early. **ABC 2022 update:** cisplatin-based adjuvant chemotherapy improves 5-year OS by about 6%. Benefit greatest in node-positive and extravesical disease.
- Use: cisplatin-naive patients (if no NAC) with **pT3–4 or N+**: ddMVAC or GC.

**Adjuvant immunotherapy**
- **CheckMate 274:** nivolumab for 1 year after RC (pT3–4a or N+, with or without prior NAC): median disease-free survival **20.8 vs 10.8 months**.
- **IMvigor010** (atezolizumab): negative overall (included pT2); benefit in ctDNA (circulating tumour DNA)-positive patients.
- Use nivolumab if cisplatin-ineligible or declining chemotherapy, or with ypT3 or worse after NAC.
- **Also know:** adjuvant pelvic radiotherapy (Cairo trials) improves pelvic control in pT3–4; ctDNA-guided therapy is emerging.

### B. Urinary diversion options
- Ileal conduit (simplest, commonest), continent cutaneous pouch, or **orthotopic neobladder**. No diversion is proven superior for QoL (quality of life).

### C. Neobladder: principles
1. **Reliable outlet:** intact rhabdosphincter, tumour-free urethra (frozen section).
2. **Low pressure:** complete **detubularisation** and cross-folding abolish peristalsis; a **sphere** gives most volume (Laplace).
3. **Capacity:** 44 cm of folded ileum holds <200 mL at first, **500–600 mL by 1 year**.
- **Contraindications:** positive urethral margin, stricture, eGFR (estimated GFR) <35–40 mL/min, liver failure, inability to self-catheterise. Age, obesity and nodes are not bars.

### D. Critical evaluation of neobladder types
| Type | Bowel; ureteric technique | Strengths | Weaknesses |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Studer** | 40–44 cm ileum + isoperistaltic afferent limb; refluxing | Simple, no staples, takes short ureters; stricture <3%; good long-term function | Night incontinence; reflux on cystogram (harmless) |
| **Hautmann W** | About 70 cm ileum; refluxing since 1997 | Largest capacity, early continence; stricture fell 9.5% → 1% | More retention, acidosis, B12 loss |
| **T pouch** (Stein–Skinner) | 44 cm + tunnelled T-limb flap valve | Antireflux; accepts dilated ureters | Complex; more secondary procedures (USC [University of Southern California] RCT) |
