| **Abol-Enein serous tunnel** | 40 cm W, serosal troughs | Antireflux; stricture about 3.8% | Long ureters needed; no dilated ureters |
| **Hemi-Kock** | Intussuscepted nipple | Good early results | Late nipple stenosis, stones; abandoned |
| **Camey II; Padua (VIP)** | 65 cm or 42 cm ileum; Le Duc tunnel | Early designs | Le Duc stricture 7–29% |
| **Mainz III; right colon; sigmoid (Reddy)** | Colon ± ileum | When ileum short or diseased | High-pressure contractions, poor compliance, more leakage |

**Critical points**
- **Reflux vs obstruction:** Valsalva voiding pressurises the whole system equally, so reflux is benign; every antireflux valve can obstruct. **USC RCT** (484 patients): Studer = T pouch for renal function and infection; T pouch needed more secondary procedures. Hence **Studer and Hautmann** are the most popular.
- **Segment:** ileum is most compliant; colon and stomach give high pressures.
- **Capacity trade-off:** a larger pouch gives earlier continence but more retention and metabolic load.
- **Robotic intracorporeal neobladder:** comparable outcomes; long-term data awaited.

### E. Neobladder outcomes and complications
- **Day continence 80–90%; night 20–50%.** Retention: men 4–10%, **women 20–30%**.
- **Late:** hyperchloraemic metabolic acidosis, B12 deficiency, mucus, stones, ureteroileal stricture, pouch rupture, urethral recurrence (men about 7% at 5 years).
- **Follow-up:** blood gas, B12, upper-tract imaging, urethral cytology if CIS.

**Diagram to draw:** bladder wall T-staging (T2a, T2b, T3, T4) beside the standard PLND template; Studer pouch with afferent limb and urethral anastomosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - NAC: cisplatin combinations only; ABC 5% absolute survival gain; SWOG 8710 pT0 38% vs 15%.
> - VESPER: ddMVAC beat GC; NIAGARA: perioperative durvalumab (adds durvalumab) improved event-free and overall survival.
> - CheckMate 274: adjuvant nivolumab disease-free survival 20.8 vs 10.8 months.
> - TMT: T2, no CIS, no hydronephrosis; salvage cystectomy 10–30%.
> - Studer and Hautmann (refluxing) dominate: obstruction, not reflux, destroys kidneys.
> - USC RCT: Studer = T pouch; T pouch more reoperations.
> - Day continence 80–90%, night 20–50%; women retain 20–30%.

# Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Bladder diverticulum.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q3c

### Definition
- Herniation of bladder mucosa through the **muscularis propria**, forming a sac with a neck. With no functioning muscle it **cannot empty** (stasis).
- Male:female about 9:1.

### Classification
| Feature | Congenital (primary) | Acquired (secondary) |
|---|---|---|
| Age | Children (<10 years) | Men >60 years |
| Cause | Weak detrusor at the ureteric hiatus | **BOO (bladder outlet obstruction)**: BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) in about 70%, stricture; neurogenic bladder |
| Number, site | Solitary; **paraureteral (Hutch)** | Often multiple, anywhere |
| Bladder wall | Smooth | Trabeculated |
| Associations | VUR (vesicoureteral reflux); Ehlers–Danlos, Williams, Menkes syndromes | Stones, cancer |
- **Also know:** iatrogenic (after cystotomy or reimplantation); cellule, saccule and diverticulum form a continuum.

### Clinical features
- Usually incidental while investigating LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), haematuria or UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **Double voiding**, recurrent UTI, stones, retention, ureteric obstruction.
- **Malignancy** in 0.8–10% (urothelial 70–80%, squamous 20–25%). With no muscle layer, tumours invade the fat early (beyond lamina propria = **T3a**).

### Investigations
- **VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram)**: size, VUR, emptying, **paradoxical filling** during voiding.
- **Cystoscopy**: enter and inspect the sac (flexible scope); cytology; biopsy carefully (perforation risk).
- **CT (computed tomography) urography**: tumour, stones, ureteric deviation, hydronephrosis.
- **Urodynamics**: BOO or neurogenic dysfunction (urine "sinks" into the sac, so contractility looks falsely low).

### Management
- **Treat the cause first** (BOO); a small diverticulum may then empty and need only surveillance.
- **Observation** (cystoscopy, cytology) if asymptomatic; **CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation)** if unfit with poor emptying.
- **Endoscopic**: neck incision or resection ± fulguration in frail patients.
- **Diverticulectomy indications**: refractory symptoms, recurrent UTI, stones, upper-tract damage or VUR, premalignant change.
- **Tumour inside**: partial or radical cystectomy.

### Diverticulectomy technique
- Open, laparoscopic or robotic; endoscopic prostate surgery first, same session.
- Steps: ureteric catheter if near the ureter → cystotomy → evert or dissect the sac from its pseudocapsule → close in two layers → catheter about 1 week, cystogram before removal.
- Complications: ureteric injury, leak.

**Diagram to draw:** paraureteral (Hutch) diverticulum with narrow neck and ureter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - No muscle → stasis, UTI, stones, cancer.
> - Acquired type: treat the BOO first.
> - Tumour invading beyond lamina propria is T3a.

## Intravesical gene therapy.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3a

### Concept
- A gene is instilled into the bladder so that urothelial and tumour cells make an anticancer product, or the vector itself kills them.
- **Why the bladder suits it:** direct contact, little systemic exposure, response checked by cystoscopy and cytology.
- **Barrier:** the glycosaminoglycan layer resists vectors → **transduction enhancers** (Syn3; DDM, n-dodecyl-β-D-maltoside).
- **Indication:** high-risk **BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin)-unresponsive NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer)** in patients unfit for or declining radical cystectomy. BCG-unresponsive: persistent or recurrent CIS (carcinoma in situ) or high-grade Ta/T1 after adequate BCG, or relapse within 6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS) of the last dose.

### Agents
| Agent | Vector, mechanism | Key data |
|---|---|---|
| **Nadofaragene firadenovec** | **Replication-incompetent adenovirus type 5** carrying the **interferon-α2b gene**, with **Syn3** surfactant. Urothelium keeps secreting interferon → apoptosis (TRAIL, TNF-related apoptosis-inducing ligand) and immunity, overcoming the short dwell of interferon protein | 75 mL every **3 months**, 1-hour dwell. CIS: **complete response 53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months**; papillary high-grade RFS (recurrence-free survival) 44% at 12 months; serious events 2%. **FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved December 2022**, first bladder gene therapy |
| **Cretostimogene grenadenorepvec (CG0070)** | **Replication-competent oncolytic adenovirus**: E2F promoter limits replication to RB (retinoblastoma) pathway-defective tumour cells; **GM-CSF (granulocyte-macrophage colony-stimulating factor)** transgene adds immunity | Two 6-week inductions + maintenance; single-arm phase III BOND-003 in BCG-unresponsive CIS: high CR (complete response) rate; combination with pembrolizumab under study |
| **Inodiftagene vixteplasmid (BC-819)** | Plasmid: **diphtheria toxin A** gene under tumour-active **H19** promoter | Investigational |

### Administration and toxicity
- Catheter instillation, patient turned during dwell; biosafety precautions.
- Mostly grade 1–2: **bladder spasm, urgency, discharge around catheter, fatigue**.

### Place in practice
- Alternatives: radical cystectomy (standard), **pembrolizumab** (KEYNOTE-057), gemcitabine–docetaxel, **N-803** (interleukin-15 superagonist) + BCG.
- Limits: responses wane (median CR duration about 10 months); single-arm trials; cost.

**Also know:** non-viral IL-12 (interleukin-12) plasmid (EG-70); early adenoviral attempts failed through poor transduction.

**Diagram to draw:** adenovirus transducing a urothelial cell → interferon secretion → tumour apoptosis.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Nadofaragene: non-replicating adenovirus + interferon-α2b + Syn3, every 3 months.
> - CIS CR 53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months.
> - Cretostimogene: replication-competent oncolytic adenovirus + GM-CSF.

## BCG failure
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q3a
(BCG therapy also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q3f)

### Introduction
- BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin) is standard for high-risk NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer). "Failure" was poorly defined; consensus categories now guide treatment.

### Definitions

| Category | Definition | Implication |
|---|---|---|
| **Adequate BCG** | ≥5 of 6 induction + ≥2 of 3 maintenance, or ≥2 doses of a second induction; for T1, ≥5 of 6 induction alone | Threshold for "unresponsive" |
| **BCG-unresponsive** | Persistent or recurrent CIS (carcinoma in situ) or HG (high-grade) Ta/T1 despite adequate BCG; or HG relapse within **6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS)** of last dose; **HG T1 at first 3-month check** | More BCG futile; cystectomy |
| **BCG-resistant** | CIS or HG Ta at 3 months after induction | Reinduction: 30–50% respond |
| Delayed relapse | HG relapse beyond these windows | Rechallenge may work |
| **BCG-intolerant** | Toxicity prevented adequate BCG | Salvage chemotherapy |

- **Why BCG fails:** inadequate dosing; PD-L1 (programmed death-ligand 1) upregulation on tumour cells.

### Evaluation
- Cystoscopy, cytology, **re-TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour)** and biopsies to exclude invasion.
- **Extravesical disease:** prostatic urethral biopsy (24–30%) and CT urography (upper tract 0.8–25%).

### Management
1. **Timely radical cystectomy** (EAU [European Association of Urology], AUA [American Urological Association]): HG T1 at 3 months progresses in 82%; CIS failure in about 50%.
2. **Bladder sparing** (unfit or refusing):

| Option | Key result (CIS complete response, CR) |
|---|---|
| **Pembrolizumab** IV (KEYNOTE-057) | 41% at 3 months, 19% at 12 months; grade 3–4 toxicity 13% |
| **Nadofaragene firadenovec** (adenovirus carrying IFN-α2b [interferon alpha-2b] gene) every 3 months | 53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months |
| **Gemcitabine–docetaxel** sequential | 60% at 1 year; 84% avoid cystectomy (retrospective) |
| **N-803 + BCG** (IL-15 [interleukin-15] superagonist) | 45% at 12 months |

- **Also know:** BCG-refractory (older term); gemcitabine, gemcitabine–mitomycin, valrubicin, chemohyperthermia, EMDA (electromotive drug administration), chemoradiation; TAR-200; cretostimogene.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: HG recurrence after BCG → adequate? → unresponsive → cystectomy or bladder sparing.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Adequate BCG = 5/6 induction + 2/3 maintenance.
> - Unresponsive: HG within 6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS); HG T1 at 3 months.
> - Timely cystectomy is standard; salvage CR rates halve by 12 months.

## Intravesical therapy for TCC of bladder
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 8 Paper 2, Q3f
(also answered in 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- For NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer: Ta, T1, CIS [carcinoma in situ]) after complete TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour). Problems: recurrence and progression.
- **Chemotherapy reduces recurrence; only BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin) also reduces progression.**

### Risk-adapted use
| Risk | Therapy |
|---|---|
| Low (solitary low-grade Ta ≤3 cm) | Single post-op instillation |
| Intermediate | Chemotherapy or BCG induction + 1-year maintenance |
| High (HG [high-grade] T1, CIS, multifocal HG) | BCG induction + 3-year maintenance |

### 1. Single immediate instillation
- Kills floating cells before implantation, so give **within 6 hours** (not at 24 hours). MMC (mitomycin C) 40 mg, epirubicin or **gemcitabine 2 g (SWOG S0337)**.
- Avoid after perforation. **Never BCG** immediately (sepsis).

### 2. Adjuvant chemotherapy
- MMC weekly ×6, optimised (empty bladder, fluid restriction, alkalinised urine). Gemcitabine; **Gem/Doce** (gemcitabine–docetaxel) for BCG shortage or unresponsive disease.

### 3. BCG immunotherapy
- **Mechanism:** BCG binds **fibronectin** → cytokines → **Th1 (T-helper 1)** response kills tumour.
- Start 2–4 weeks after TURBT; retain 1–2 hours.
- **SWOG schedule:** 6 weekly induction, then 3 weekly doses at 3, 6 months and every 6 months for 3 years. **NIMBUS:** reduced frequency inferior.
- Contraindications: gross haematuria, traumatic catheterisation, active UTI (urinary tract infection), previous BCG sepsis.
- **Toxicity:** cystitis (symptomatic); persistent fever: isoniazid + rifampicin; sepsis (0.5%): stop BCG, triple therapy (not pyrazinamide) ± steroids.

### BCG-unresponsive disease
- Recurrent or persistent HG disease despite **adequate BCG** (≥5 of 6 induction + ≥2 of 3 maintenance).
- **Timely radical cystectomy** is standard.
- Unfit or refusing: **pembrolizumab** (KEYNOTE-057; CIS complete response 41%), **nadofaragene firadenovec**, **N-803 + BCG**, Gem/Doce, TAR-200 device.

### Device-assisted
- EMDA (electromotive drug administration) MMC; chemohyperthermia (RITE, HIVEC); UGN-102 gel.

**Diagram to draw:** BCG mechanism and SWOG schedule timeline.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Single instillation within 6 hours; never BCG.
> - Only maintenance BCG reduces progression.
> - SWOG 3-year schedule; NIMBUS reduced frequency fails.
> - BCG-unresponsive: cystectomy; pembrolizumab 41% CR.

## What is optical coherence tomography. What is its potential role in management of CA bladder?
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 9 Paper 2, Q3c
(also answered in Practice Set 5 Paper 4, Q3c and 2017-Summer Paper 4, Q3c)

### What is OCT (optical coherence tomography)?
- An **"optical biopsy"** technique: the **optical analogue of B-mode ultrasound**. It measures the echo delay of back-scattered **near-infrared light**, using low-coherence interferometry.
- Gives real-time cross-sectional images with about 10–20 µm resolution but only **1–2 mm depth**. First described in 1991 (ophthalmology).
- **Technique:** a fibre-optic probe passes through the cystoscope working channel and is placed **in contact** with the lesion found by WLC (white-light cystoscopy) or blue light.

### Image interpretation
| Tissue | OCT appearance |
|---|---|
| Normal | Three layers: thin low-scatter urothelium, bright lamina propria, darker muscularis |
| Inflammation | Layers preserved but thickened |
| Ta papillary tumour | Thick urothelium; lamina propria boundary intact |
| T1 tumour | **Loss of the urothelium–lamina propria boundary** |

### Potential role in carcinoma bladder
1. **Diagnosis:** separates benign from malignant flat lesions in real time; fewer unnecessary biopsies.
2. **Staging aid:** suggests Ta versus T1 during TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour).
3. **Resection margins:** checks completeness of TURBT.
4. **Surveillance** of NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer), including after BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin).
5. **Upper tract:** through the ureteroscope, OCT correlates well with final grade and stage of UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma).

### Evidence and limitations
- Small studies: **high sensitivity, lower specificity**. Inflammation, scar and post-BCG change mimic tumour; adding OCT to blue light improves specificity.
- Small field; too shallow for muscle invasion; cannot grade; cost; learning curve. **Investigational.**

### Related techniques
- **Blue-light (hexaminolevulinate) cystoscopy:** detects more CIS (carcinoma in situ).
- **NBI (narrow-band imaging):** 415/540 nm light absorbed by haemoglobin.
- **CLE (confocal laser endomicroscopy):** cellular images after fluorescein.

**Diagram to draw:** OCT image of three bladder layers beside a tumour with loss of layering.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Near-infrared "ultrasound with light"; depth 1–2 mm.
> - Loss of layering suggests tumour; boundary loss suggests T1.
> - Sensitive but false positives from inflammation; investigational.

## Von Bruns Nest
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 10 Paper 2, Q3e

*Read as: von Brunn nests of the urothelium.*

### Definition
- **Von Brunn nests** are solid nests of benign urothelial cells lying in the **lamina propria**. They form when the basal layer of the surface urothelium buds downward and the buds pinch off.
- A common normal or reactive finding in most adult bladders, especially at the **trigone and bladder neck**; also in the ureter and renal pelvis.

### Pathogenesis: the proliferative cystitis spectrum

```
Chronic irritation (infection, stones, catheter, outlet obstruction)
   → urothelial buds → von Brunn nests (solid)
   → central degeneration and fluid → cystitis cystica (cysts)
   → glandular (columnar / goblet cell) metaplasia → cystitis glandularis
```

| Lesion | Histology | Malignant potential |
|---|---|---|
| **Von Brunn nests** | Solid urothelial nests, lamina propria | None |
| **Cystitis cystica** | Nests with central cyst, urothelial lining | None |
| **Cystitis glandularis** (typical) | Cuboidal or columnar lining | Low |
| Cystitis glandularis, **intestinal type** | Goblet cells, mucin | Possible link to **adenocarcinoma** |

### Clinical features
- Usually incidental. When florid: irritative symptoms, haematuria, infection; cystoscopy shows submucosal nodules or cysts that can mimic tumour.
- **Associations:** chronic inflammation, outlet obstruction, **pelvic lipomatosis** (florid cystitis glandularis), bladder exstrophy.

### Differential diagnosis (pathologist's trap)
- **Nested variant of urothelial carcinoma:** deceptively bland but aggressive (male:female 6:1). Clues: deep infiltrative nests reaching muscle, nuclear atypia.
- **Inverted papilloma:** endophytic nests with trabeculae, usually at the trigone.
- Nephrogenic adenoma; urothelial carcinoma with glandular differentiation.

### Management
- None for isolated nests; treat the cause (infection, stone, catheter, obstruction).
- **TURBT (transurethral resection) or biopsy** if a mass is present, to exclude carcinoma.
- **Cystitis glandularis:** remove the irritant and arrange **endoscopic surveillance**, because rare change to adenocarcinoma is reported (especially the intestinal type and in exstrophy).
- Upper tract: benign, but coexisting upper-tract urothelial cancer cannot be excluded, so follow up.

**Diagram to draw:** urothelium budding into lamina propria, forming a solid nest, then a cyst, then glandular metaplasia.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Benign urothelial nests in the lamina propria; precursor of cystitis cystica and glandularis.
> - Only cystitis glandularis (intestinal type) carries adenocarcinoma risk → surveillance.
> - The nested variant of urothelial carcinoma mimics von Brunn nests.

## Mitomycin
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 2, Q3b

### Pharmacology
- MMC (mitomycin C) is an antitumour antibiotic from *Streptomyces caespitosus*.
- **Mechanism:** after reductive activation in the cell it acts as an **alkylating agent**, cross-linking DNA and stopping DNA synthesis; cell-cycle non-specific.
- Molecular weight 334 Da: **little systemic absorption** from the bladder.

### Uses in NMIBC (non-muscle-invasive bladder cancer)
1. **Single immediate post-TURBT (transurethral resection of bladder tumour) instillation:** 40 mg. Given **within 6 hours** it reduces recurrence; at 24 hours it does not, because it works by preventing implantation of floating cells. Best for a solitary low-grade papillary tumour. Avoid if perforation or extensive resection is suspected.
2. **Adjuvant induction:** 40 mg weekly for 6 weeks (± maintenance) in low- and intermediate-risk disease. Reduces recurrence but **not progression**; BCG (bacille Calmette–Guérin) with maintenance is superior in high risk.
3. **Optimised delivery (Au, 2001):** empty bladder, overnight fluid restriction, sodium bicarbonate (less drug breakdown), 40 mg in 20 mL; retain 1–2 hours.
4. **Device-assisted:** EMDA (electromotive drug administration) increases tissue uptake; chemohyperthermia at 42 °C (RITE [radiofrequency-induced thermochemotherapeutic effect], HIVEC [hyperthermic intravesical chemotherapy]).
5. **Sequential gemcitabine then MMC** for BCG-exposed disease.

### Other urological uses
- **Upper tract:** single bladder dose after nephroureterectomy (ODMIT-C: bladder recurrence 28% to 16% at 1 year). **UGN-101 (mitomycin gel)**, FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approved 2020 for low-grade UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma): complete response 59%, ureteric stricture about 44%. UGN-102 for low-grade intermediate-risk bladder tumours.
- **Bladder-sparing chemoradiation:** 5-FU (5-fluorouracil) + MMC (BC2001: better locoregional control than radiation alone).
- **Antifibrotic:** injected after incision of recurrent bladder neck contracture or urethral stricture.

### Side effects
- Chemical cystitis (30–40%); **skin rash, palmar or genital dermatitis** (signature toxicity; wash after voiding); contracted bladder (about 5%); encrustation cystitis; myelosuppression rare.
- Extravasation through a perforation can cause pelvic necrosis.

**Diagram to draw:** timeline: TURBT, single MMC within 6 hours, then weekly induction ×6.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Alkylating antibiotic; 40 mg; single dose within 6 hours of TURBT.
> - Reduces recurrence, not progression.
> - Signature toxicity: skin rash; contracted bladder about 5%.

## Mullerionosis of UB
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 2, Q3b

*"Mullerionosis of UB" is read as müllerianosis of the urinary bladder.*

### Definition
- Benign lesions of **müllerian-type epithelium** in the bladder wall, in three forms:

| Lesion | Tissue |
|---|---|
| **Endometriosis** (commonest) | Endometrial glands **and stroma** |
| **Endocervicosis** | Endocervical mucinous glands |
| **Endosalpingiosis** | Ciliated tubal epithelium |

- **Müllerianosis** strictly means two or more of these together.
- Women of reproductive age, often after **caesarean section**. Usually **posterior wall or dome** (vesicouterine pouch).
- Bladder is the commonest urinary tract site of endometriosis, ahead of the ureter.

### Pathogenesis
- **Implantation:** retrograde menstruation (Sampson) into the vesicouterine pouch.
- **Iatrogenic implantation** at caesarean or pelvic surgery (primary vs iatrogenic forms).
- **Metaplasia** of coelomic or subperitoneal müllerian remnants (the "secondary müllerian system").
- Lymphatic or vascular spread; direct extension of anterior uterine adenomyosis.

### Clinical features
- **Cyclical haematuria** or irritative LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) with menses; suprapubic pain, dysmenorrhoea, dyspareunia.
- Endocervicosis: non-cyclical mass or LUTS.
- **Also know:** menouria differential includes vesicouterine fistula (Youssef syndrome).

### Evaluation
- **Ultrasound** (transabdominal plus transvaginal): size and depth of a hypoechoic nodule.
- **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging):** extent; haemorrhagic foci **bright on T1**, fibrotic thickening dark on T2.
- **Cystoscopy:** bluish submucosal nodule with neovascularity, best seen during menses. Of **limited value**, because disease is mainly **extravesical** (from serosa inwards).
- **Biopsy:** excludes adenocarcinoma, especially with endocervicosis. Immunohistochemistry: ER/PR (oestrogen/progesterone receptor) positive; CD10 marks endometrial stroma.
- Ultrasound of upper tracts for ureteric involvement.
- **Differential:** adenocarcinoma, cystitis glandularis, nephrogenic adenoma, urachal remnant.

### Management
- **Hormonal suppression** (combined pill, progestins, GnRH [gonadotropin-releasing hormone] agonists): symptom relief; recurrence after stopping.
- **TUR (transurethral resection):** incomplete for transmural disease; perforation risk; recurs.
- **Partial cystectomy** (laparoscopic or robotic) with a margin: **primary treatment**. Ureteric stent or reimplantation if the orifice is involved.
- Excise other pelvic endometriosis with the gynaecologist.
- Malignant change (endometrioid or clear cell adenocarcinoma) is rare.

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal pelvis with a dome nodule growing from the vesicouterine pouch into the detrusor.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Endometriosis, endocervicosis, endosalpingiosis; ≥2 = müllerianosis.
> - Cyclical haematuria; MRI bright on T1.
> - Disease is extravesical: cystoscopy limited, partial cystectomy preferred.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 12 · Bladder Cancer & Diversion

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### CA bladder, etiology, newer investigations and treatment. (LAQ)

- Smoking causes 30–40%; aromatic amines; NAT2 slow acetylators.
- Blue light finds more CIS; VI-RADS ≥3 suggests invasion; no marker replaces cystoscopy.
- High-risk NMIBC: BCG with 3-year maintenance.
- MIBC: cisplatin NAC + cystectomy; trimodality in selected.

#### Management of T1G3 (high-grade T1) TCC of urinary bladder, including T1 with CIS in a young woman (LAQ)

- Re-TURBT in all T1 at 2–6 weeks (within 6 weeks); residual T1 means progression up to 80%.
- BCG induction + 3-year maintenance (SWOG); only agent that reduces progression.
- T1 + CIS = high risk; very high risk if multiple or ≥3 cm (EAU 2021).
- Very high risk (CIS, LVI, variants, prostatic urethra): offer early cystectomy (92% 10-year disease-free survival).
- BCG-unresponsive: cystectomy; pembrolizumab or nadofaragene if unfit.
- Young woman: organ-sparing cystectomy, neobladder, fertility preservation.

#### Use of intestinal segments in urology, and orthotopic neobladder (LAQ)

- Ileum and colon: hyperchloraemic acidosis (NH₄Cl absorption).
- Jejunum: hyperkalaemia; stomach: hypochloraemic alkalosis.
- Continent diversion: GFR >35, pH ≤5.8, osmolality ≥600.
- Wallace has the lowest stricture rate; detubularisation gives low pressure.
- Sigmoid is the default neovagina; Yang–Monti when the appendix is absent.
- Detubularise and make a sphere; ≥500 mL; 500–600 mL by 1 year.
- Neobladder absolute bar: positive urethral margin, stricture, poor renal function.
- Studer and Hautmann (refluxing) are commonest; USC RCT: Studer = T pouch.
- Day continence 80–90%, night 20–50%; women retain (20–30%).

#### Classification of bladder cancer and bladder-preserving treatment of TCC (LAQ)

- WHO 2004/2016: PUNLMP, low grade, high grade; T3b = macroscopic perivesical fat.
- Re-TURBT within 6 weeks for T1, no muscle or incomplete resection.
- Partial cystectomy: solitary <3 cm (dome tumour), no CIS, frozen-section margins, always PLND.
- TMT = complete TURBT + RT + radiosensitiser; T2, no hydronephrosis, no CIS.
- TMT: BC2001 MMC/5-FU (mitomycin C/5-FU) + RT, DFS 67% vs 48%; salvage cystectomy 10–30%.

#### Discuss principles of continence in continent urinary diversion. Application of Mitrofanoff principle in continent urinary and GI reconstruction. (LAQ)

- Continence = low reservoir pressure (detubularise, sphere, ≥500 mL) against a reliable outlet.
- Mitrofanoff 1980: submucosal flap valve; appendix needs only a 2 cm tunnel.
- Yang–Monti when appendix absent or used for MACE (Malone 1990).
- Continence >95%; stomal stenosis is the commonest complication.

#### Muscle-invasive bladder cancer (TCC): management, neoadjuvant and adjuvant chemotherapy, neobladder types (LAQ)

- NAC: cisplatin combinations only; ABC 5% absolute survival gain; SWOG 8710 pT0 38% vs 15%.
- VESPER: ddMVAC beat GC; NIAGARA: perioperative durvalumab (adds durvalumab) improved event-free and overall survival.
- CheckMate 274: adjuvant nivolumab disease-free survival 20.8 vs 10.8 months.
- TMT: T2, no CIS, no hydronephrosis; salvage cystectomy 10–30%.
- Studer and Hautmann (refluxing) dominate: obstruction, not reflux, destroys kidneys.
- USC RCT: Studer = T pouch; T pouch more reoperations.
- Day continence 80–90%, night 20–50%; women retain 20–30%.

#### Bladder diverticulum. (SAQ)

- No muscle → stasis, UTI, stones, cancer.
- Acquired type: treat the BOO first.
- Tumour invading beyond lamina propria is T3a.

#### Intravesical gene therapy. (SAQ)

- Nadofaragene: non-replicating adenovirus + interferon-α2b + Syn3, every 3 months.
- CIS CR 53% at 3 months, 24% at 12 months.
- Cretostimogene: replication-competent oncolytic adenovirus + GM-CSF.

#### BCG failure (SAQ)

- Adequate BCG = 5/6 induction + 2/3 maintenance.
- Unresponsive: HG within 6 months (Ta/T1) or 12 months (CIS); HG T1 at 3 months.
- Timely cystectomy is standard; salvage CR rates halve by 12 months.

#### Intravesical therapy for TCC of bladder (SAQ)

- Single instillation within 6 hours; never BCG.
- Only maintenance BCG reduces progression.
- SWOG 3-year schedule; NIMBUS reduced frequency fails.
- BCG-unresponsive: cystectomy; pembrolizumab 41% CR.

#### What is optical coherence tomography. What is its potential role in management of CA bladder? (SAQ)

- Near-infrared "ultrasound with light"; depth 1–2 mm.
- Loss of layering suggests tumour; boundary loss suggests T1.
- Sensitive but false positives from inflammation; investigational.

#### Von Bruns Nest (SAQ)

- Benign urothelial nests in the lamina propria; precursor of cystitis cystica and glandularis.
- Only cystitis glandularis (intestinal type) carries adenocarcinoma risk → surveillance.
- The nested variant of urothelial carcinoma mimics von Brunn nests.

#### Mitomycin (SAQ)

- Alkylating antibiotic; 40 mg; single dose within 6 hours of TURBT.
- Reduces recurrence, not progression.
- Signature toxicity: skin rash; contracted bladder about 5%.

#### Mullerionosis of UB (SAQ)

- Endometriosis, endocervicosis, endosalpingiosis; ≥2 = müllerianosis.
- Cyclical haematuria; MRI bright on T1.
- Disease is extravesical: cystoscopy limited, partial cystectomy preferred.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Metastatic RCC (mRCC): assessment and management, including recent concepts
**Asked as:**
- Describe assessment and recent concept in management of metastatic RCC. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Management of metastatic RCC — *Practice Set 11 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- mRCC. — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q1; 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q3b; immunotherapy in detail in 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3e)

*Assumed meaning (mRCC.): management of metastatic renal cell carcinoma (mRCC).*

### Introduction
- About one-third of RCC (renal cell carcinoma) patients have metastases at diagnosis; 20–40% of localised tumours metastasise later.
- Five-year survival is about 12%, but median survival now approaches 3–5 years.
- Therapy (treatment) moved from cytokines to VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor)-targeted TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors), and now to ICI (immune checkpoint inhibitor) combinations. Surgery has become selective.

### Assessment (before treatment)
- **Clinical:** symptoms of metastases (cough, bone pain, neurological deficit), weight loss; paraneoplastic syndromes (hypercalcaemia, polycythaemia, hypertension, Stauffer syndrome); performance status (Karnofsky or ECOG [Eastern Cooperative Oncology Group]); comorbidity.
- **Laboratory:** haemoglobin, corrected calcium, neutrophils, platelets, LDH (lactate dehydrogenase), ALP (alkaline phosphatase), creatinine, liver tests.
- **Imaging:** contrast CT (computed tomography) of chest, abdomen and pelvis (or MRI, also for IVC [inferior vena cava] thrombus). Bone scan only with bone pain, raised ALP or poor performance status; brain imaging (brain MRI [magnetic resonance imaging]) with neurological symptoms; bone scan or brain MRI only when symptoms or tests suggest spread (if symptomatic); PET not routine.
- **Histology:** nephrectomy specimen or **percutaneous biopsy** before systemic therapy. It separates clear cell from non-clear cell RCC and shows sarcomatoid change, which alters drug choice.
- **Prognostic risk group** (decides first-line therapy):

| Adverse factor | MSKCC (Memorial Sloan-Kettering), cytokine era | IMDC (International mRCC Database Consortium [International Metastatic RCC Database Consortium]), TKI era |
|---|---|---|
| Karnofsky <80% | Yes | Yes |
| Haemoglobin < lower limit of normal (below normal) | Yes | Yes |
| Corrected calcium (raised) | >10 mg/dL | > upper limit of normal (ULN) |
| LDH >1.5 × ULN (upper limit of normal) | Yes | No |
| Time factor | No prior nephrectomy | <1 year from diagnosis to systemic therapy |
| Neutrophils, platelets > ULN (raised) | No | Yes (two factors) |

- IMDC 6 factors; MSKCC (Memorial Sloan Kettering) model: 5 factors including LDH (lactate dehydrogenase).
- Favourable 0 factors, intermediate 1–2, poor ≥3. IMDC median survival: 43.2, 22.5 and 7.8 months.

### Management: recent concepts
- Multidisciplinary care: systemic therapy is the backbone; surgery and radiotherapy are added selectively.

**1. First-line (systemic) therapy, clear cell RCC (NCCN [National Comprehensive Cancer Network] and EAU [European Association of Urology])**

| IMDC risk | Preferred | Alternatives (if IO not possible) |
|---|---|---|
| Favourable | Pembrolizumab + axitinib; nivolumab + cabozantinib; pembrolizumab + lenvatinib | Sunitinib or pazopanib if IO (immuno-oncology) not possible; active surveillance for slow, low-volume (indolent low-volume) disease |
| Intermediate/poor | The same three, **or nivolumab + ipilimumab** | Cabozantinib; sunitinib; pazopanib |

- **Rationale:** VHL (von Hippel–Lindau) loss lets HIF (hypoxia-inducible factor) accumulate and drive VEGF (the TKI target). ICIs (checkpoint inhibitors) block PD-1 (programmed death-1) or CTLA-4 (cytotoxic T-lymphocyte antigen-4), releasing the brake on T cells (release T-cell brakes) and giving durable responses.
- **Trials (all versus sunitinib):** CheckMate 214 (nivolumab + ipilimumab: OS [overall survival] HR [hazard ratio] 0.63; overall survival benefit in intermediate/poor risk); KEYNOTE-426 (pembrolizumab + axitinib); CheckMate 9ER (nivolumab + cabozantinib); CLEAR (pembrolizumab + lenvatinib: PFS [progression-free survival] 23.9 vs 9.2 months). PD-L1 (programmed death ligand-1) status does not select patients; benefit is independent of PD-L1 status.

**2. Later lines: switch (the) mechanism**
- After IO (an IO combination): a VEGF TKI not used before (cabozantinib, axitinib, tivozanib) or **belzutifan** (HIF-2α inhibitor). IO rechallenge is not advised (CONTACT-03 negative).
- After both IO and TKI: **belzutifan** (HIF-2α inhibitor); LITESPARK-005 showed better PFS (progression-free survival) than everolimus.
- After TKI alone: nivolumab (CheckMate 025) or cabozantinib (METEOR), both with OS (survival) gain.

**3. Cytoreductive nephrectomy (CN): (from) routine to selective**
- Cytokine era: SWOG 8949 and EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) trials showed longer survival with CN + interferon-α.
- **CARMENA (2018):** sunitinib alone was non-inferior to CN + sunitinib in MSKCC intermediate/poor risk (OS / median survival 18.4 vs 13.9 months).
- **SURTIME:** deferred CN after sunitinib was safe; OS (survival) favoured deferral.
- **Now:** immediate (upfront) CN for fit patients with low metastatic burden (low-burden) not needing (who do not need) immediate systemic therapy; **deferred CN** in responders. Avoid in poor risk, poor performance status or brain metastases.
- Palliative nephrectomy or angioembolisation for haematuria or pain.

**4. Metastasectomy and local therapy (radiotherapy)**
- Complete resection of solitary or few (limited) metastases gives 5-year survival of 35–50%. Better with complete resection, solitary lung lesion, disease-free interval >1 year, metachronous disease.
- **SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy)** (for oligometastases), radiosurgery for brain metastases, or ablation for oligometastatic disease; palliative radiotherapy or surgery for cord compression and weight-bearing bones; palliative radiotherapy for bone pain.

**5. Adjuvant immunotherapy (new) / adjuvant therapy after complete resection**
- **KEYNOTE-564:** pembrolizumab for 1 year after surgery in high-risk clear cell RCC, including **M1 NED** (metastases completely resected; no evidence of disease after metastasectomy; adjuvant pembrolizumab after complete metastasectomy): disease-free survival HR 0.63 (disease-free survival improved); FDA (Food and Drug Administration) approval 2021; a later report showed OS (overall survival) benefit.

**6. Supportive and special groups (non-clear cell RCC and supportive care)**
- Zoledronic acid or denosumab for bone metastases. Immune-related adverse events (colitis, hepatitis, hypophysitis, thyroiditis) need steroids and drug interruption.
- Non-clear cell RCC: clinical trial (trials) preferred; cabozantinib or sunitinib; gemcitabine + carboplatin (platinum) for collecting duct carcinoma.
- **Also know:** high-dose IL-2 (interleukin-2) gives durable complete response 7–9% (another source: in a few percent); temsirolimus for poor risk; chemotherapy and hormones are ineffective.

### Follow-up
- CT every 8–12 weeks on therapy (response by RECIST [Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumours]); monitor thyroid, liver, blood pressure and cortisol.

### Recent advances
- Belzutifan, also approved for VHL-associated RCC (tumours); neoadjuvant ICI trials; biomarkers to choose IO/IO or IO/TKI.

```
Metastatic RCC -> biopsy + IMDC risk
   -> IO/TKI (all risks) or nivo + ipi (intermediate/poor)
   -> deferred CN / metastasectomy / SBRT in responders
   -> progression: switch class (TKI, belzutifan)
```

**Diagram to draw:** the flowchart above (metastatic RCC → biopsy and IMDC risk → IO combination ± deferred CN in responders → metastasectomy or SBRT → switch drug class on progression).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - IMDC (6 factors): Karnofsky, haemoglobin, calcium, neutrophils, platelets, <1 year to therapy; poor = ≥3.
> - IO/TKI (IO combinations) for all risk groups; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor only.
> - CARMENA and SURTIME: no routine upfront CN.
> - Adjuvant pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-564), including M1 NED.
> - Belzutifan after IO and TKI.

## Etiopathogenesis and management of Upper tract TCC
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q1
(management also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

*TCC (transitional cell carcinoma) is taken as UTUC (upper tract urothelial carcinoma).*

### Introduction
- UTUC is 5–10% of urothelial cancers. Pelvicalyceal tumours are about twice as common as ureteric ones. Peak age 70–90; male:female 2:1.
- About 60% are invasive at diagnosis, because the upper tract has a thin muscle wall. Bladder recurrence follows in 22–47%.

### Etiopathogenesis
| Factor | Key facts and mechanism |
|---|---|
| **Tobacco** | Most important modifiable factor; risk 2.5–7-fold. Aromatic amines are activated to mutagenic N-hydroxylamines |
| **Aristolochic acid** | Balkan endemic nephropathy (UTUC 40% of renal tumours there) and Chinese herb nephropathy. Aristolactam–DNA adducts cause A:T → T:A transversions in TP53. Familial but **not inherited**; often bilateral |
| **Lynch syndrome** (HNPCC, hereditary nonpolyposis colorectal cancer) | Germline MMR (mismatch repair) mutation: MLH1, MSH2, MSH6, PMS2. Risk ~14-fold, highest with MSH2; found in 5–9% of screened UTUC |
| **Occupation** | "Amino tumours": benzidine, β-naphthylamine (dyes, rubber, petrochemicals); latency up to 20 years |
| **Analgesic abuse** | Phenacetin: papillary necrosis; capillarosclerosis is pathognomonic |
| **Arsenic** | Well water in south-west Taiwan (blackfoot disease) |

- **Also know:** cyclophosphamide (acrolein); chronic infection and stones (squamous carcinoma); alcohol.
- **Lynch screening:** suspect if age <60, or a personal or family history of Lynch-spectrum cancer (colon, endometrium). Test the tumour for MSI (microsatellite instability) or MMR immunohistochemistry, then germline testing.
- **Pathogenesis:** two theories both operate. **Intraluminal seeding** (one clone spreading downstream) explains frequent bladder recurrence. **Field effect** (carcinogen in urine, independent clones) explains multifocality.
- Sequence: hyperplasia → dysplasia → CIS (carcinoma in situ). Variant histology in ~25%. Molecular subtypes include FGFR3-mutant (low grade), TP53/MDM2-mutant and hypermutated (Lynch).

### Diagnosis
- Haematuria (56–98%), flank pain (20–32%).
- **CTU (computed tomography urography):** best test (sensitivity ~92%). **Cystoscopy** always (concurrent bladder cancer 17%). Selective cytology.
- **Flexible URS (ureteroscopy) with biopsy:** grade is accurate in ~90%, but **stage cannot be assessed**. Staging is TNM (renal parenchymal invasion by a pelvic tumour = T3).

### Risk stratification (EAU, European Association of Urology)
| Low risk: all present | High risk: any one |
|---|---|
| Unifocal | Multifocal |
| Tumour <2 cm | Tumour >2 cm |
| Low-grade cytology and biopsy | High-grade cytology or biopsy |
| No invasion on CTU | Hydronephrosis |
| — | Previous radical cystectomy; variant histology |

- AUA/SUO (American Urological Association/Society of Urologic Oncology) 2023 splits each group into favourable and unfavourable.

### Management
**A. Kidney-sparing surgery**
- **Elective:** low-risk disease, whatever the other kidney. **Imperative:** solitary kidney, bilateral tumours, CKD (chronic kidney disease), unfit patient.
- **Ureteroscopic ablation:** biopsy, then holmium or thulium laser. Closed system; avoid circumferential ablation (stricture).
- **Percutaneous resection:** large pelvic or lower-pole tumours; risk of tract seeding.
- **Distal ureterectomy** with reimplantation (psoas hitch, Boari flap): gives a staging specimen.
- Adjuvant upper-tract BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) or MMC (mitomycin C); **UGN-101** mitomycin gel for low grade.
- Lifelong endoscopic surveillance: upper-tract recurrence in about half.

**B. RNU (radical nephroureterectomy) with bladder cuff: standard for high risk**
- Clip the ureter early; kidney outside Gerota's fascia; never open the urinary tract; remove the **whole intramural ureter and cuff** (stump recurrence 30–75% if left); bag extraction.
- Open, laparoscopic and robotic routes are oncologically equivalent. Abandoned: endoscopic "pluck", laparoscopic stapling.
- **Template lymphadenectomy** for high risk: hilar plus paracaval (right) or para-aortic (left); pelvic nodes for lower ureter.
- **Single postoperative intravesical instillation:** ODMIT-C (MMC 40 mg) cut bladder recurrence 28% → 16% at 1 year.

**C. Perioperative chemotherapy**
- **POUT (2020):** adjuvant platinum after RNU for pT2–T4 or N+: 3-year DFS (disease-free survival) **71% vs 46%**. Gemcitabine–cisplatin; carboplatin if creatinine clearance 30–49.
- **Neoadjuvant** platinum when post-RNU renal function would rule out cisplatin.
- **Also know:** adjuvant nivolumab (CheckMate 274) if platinum-ineligible, but no clear benefit in the UTUC subgroup.

**D. Metastatic disease (brief)**
- First line: **enfortumab vedotin + pembrolizumab** (EV-302, 2023): overall survival ~31 vs ~16 months versus platinum chemotherapy. Alternative: gemcitabine–platinum, then avelumab maintenance.
- Later: pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-045), erdafitinib for FGFR3 alterations. Node-positive: chemotherapy first, then consolidative surgery.

### Follow-up
- After RNU: cystoscopy and cytology from 3 months; CTU yearly. After kidney-sparing: URS and CTU at closer intervals for life.

**Diagram to draw:** RNU specimen with bladder cuff, beside lymph node templates.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Tobacco, aristolochic acid (A:T → T:A in TP53) and Lynch (MSH2) are the key causes.
> - EAU low risk needs every favourable feature; one adverse feature means high risk.
> - RNU with complete bladder cuff; single postoperative MMC (ODMIT-C 28% → 16%).
> - POUT: adjuvant platinum, 3-year DFS 71% vs 46%; EV-302 for metastatic disease.

## Nephron-sparing surgery / partial nephrectomy in RCC: role, indications and approaches
**Asked as:**
- Describe role of nephron sparing surgery in management of RCC — *Practice Set 13 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Partial nephrectomy – indications and approaches. — *Practice Set 6 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q1 and 2025-Winter Paper 1, Q2; nephron-sparing methods also in 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q1)

### Definition and rationale
- NSS (nephron-sparing surgery) / PN (partial nephrectomy) is complete removal of the tumour with **maximal preservation of functioning parenchyma**. PN is the standard form; thermal ablation is the non-excisional form.
- Equal cancer control to RN (radical nephrectomy) with less CKD (chronic kidney disease) and cardiovascular risk.
- **Why it matters:** RN can cause CKD, linked to cardiovascular events and death. PN keeps about **90%** of global function (RN 65–70%) with equal cancer control for small masses.
- **CKD from surgery alone** is stable, with near-normal survival if the new GFR (glomerular filtration rate) stays >45; **CKD from medical disease** (diabetes, hypertension) progresses. So NSS matters most when medical renal disease coexists.

### Role by clinical setting

| Setting | Role of NSS |
|---|---|
| **cT1a (≤4 cm)** | PN is the **standard of care** when intervention is needed; RN is overtreatment |
| cT1b–T2 | PN when feasible and function matters; otherwise debatable |
| Hereditary RCC (renal cell carcinoma) | Observe until the largest tumour is **3 cm**, then enucleate (except HLRCC [hereditary leiomyomatosis and RCC]: wide excision) |
| Bilateral tumours, solitary kidney | Imperative PN, staged if bilateral; avoids dialysis |
| Frail, comorbid, cT1a <3 cm | Thermal ablation or active surveillance |
| Locally advanced, thrombus, cN1, metastatic | Generally RN or cytoreductive surgery |

### Indications

| Category | Situations |
|---|---|
| **Imperative** | Bilateral RCC (tumours); solitary (functioning) kidney; RN would mean (cause) dialysis |
| **Relative** | Contralateral kidney threatened: stones, renal artery stenosis, diabetes, hypertension, proteinuria; hereditary RCC |
| **Elective** | Localised tumour with a normal contralateral kidney; **cT1a with normal contralateral kidney: standard of care**; cT1b–T2 when feasible |
| **Benign** | Symptomatic AML (angiomyolipoma); focal atrophy in duplex kidney; infected calyceal diverticulum |

- **AUA (American Urological Association) 2021:** RN is preferred only if **all three** apply: high complexity; no CKD or proteinuria; normal contralateral kidney with new GFR likely **>45** after RN. Otherwise PN.
- **Relative contraindications:** **<20% of nephron mass retained (left)**; pedicle encasement; venous tumour thrombus; cT4; nodes.

### Preoperative evaluation (planning)
- Multiphase CT (computed tomography) or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging); chest imaging; eGFR (estimated GFR) and urine protein.
- **R.E.N.A.L. nephrometry score** (Radius, Exophytic/endophytic, Nearness to collecting system, Anterior/posterior, Location to polar lines) predicts complexity: **4–6 low, 7–9 moderate, 10–12 high** complexity. PADUA is an alternative. 3D models help.
- **Biopsy** when it changes management (about **20%** of enhancing cT1 masses are benign). Genetic counselling if age ≤46, multifocal or bilateral.

### Techniques of PN
**Approaches:** open (flank extraperitoneal), laparoscopic, or **RAPN (robot-assisted PN)**: transperitoneal for anterior and retroperitoneal for posterior tumours.

| Approach | Features |
|---|---|
| **Open, extraperitoneal flank** (supracostal 11th/12th rib, 8 cm mini-flank) | Posterior exposure; solitary kidney, complex or central tumours, cold ischaemia |
| **Open transperitoneal** (subcostal, chevron, midline) | Bilateral or large tumours, vascular reconstruction |
| **Laparoscopic transperitoneal** | Large space, easier suturing; anterior tumours |
| **Laparoscopic retroperitoneal** | No bowel mobilisation, direct hilum; posterior tumours |
| **Robot-assisted (RAPN)** | Easier suturing, shorter warm ischaemia, fewer conversions |

**Steps (key technical steps)**
1. **Early hilar control** of artery, vein and accessory vessels, even if off-clamp is planned.
2. Defat the kidney; **intraoperative ultrasound** maps borders and depth.
3. Vascular control: clamp the artery (artery ± vein clamp); off-clamp or selective clamping for small tumours. Use **ice slush** if cold ischaemia needed / if ischaemia is expected to exceed **25–30 minutes** (solitary kidney, CKD).
4. Excise with a thin rim (rim of normal tissue), or **enucleation** (hereditary, multifocal). **A negative margin matters; its width does not** (margin width is unimportant if negative).
5. **Two-layer renorrhaphy:** inner layer closes vessels and collecting system; outer capsular layer (sliding-clip) over a haemostatic bolster.
6. Unclamp; check haemostasis; close Gerota fascia; drain.

| | Standard (wedge) resection | Enucleation |
|---|---|---|
| Plane | Through normal parenchyma | Along the pseudocapsule |
| Advantage | Fewer positive margins | Less bleeding, maximal parenchyma |
| Use | Default | Hereditary, multifocal, severe CKD |

- **Ischaemia:** warm ischaemia **<25–30 minutes**. Function depends mainly on the **parenchyma preserved**, less on clamp time; off-clamp, selective clamping and mannitol show no long-term eGFR benefit.

### Thermal ablation
- **Cryoablation** (double freeze–thaw) or **RFA (radiofrequency ablation)**; percutaneous preferred.
- AUA: alternative for **cT1a <3 cm**; biopsy before (preferred) or at ablation. Local recurrence is higher than PN but treatable by repeat ablation.
- **Also know:** MWA (microwave ablation), SBRT (stereotactic body radiotherapy) for non-surgical candidates.

### Outcomes and evidence
- **Trifecta:** negative margin + no major complication + warm ischaemia ≤25 minutes. RAPN: shorter ischaemia than laparoscopy, less blood loss than open.
- Elective PN: local recurrence **1–2%**; imperative 3–5%.
- **EORTC (European Organisation for Research and Treatment of Cancer) 30904 (2011):** RN vs PN for ≤5 cm tumours: PN had better function and similar oncology; 10-year overall survival 81% RN vs 76% PN. The trial is flawed.

### Complications
- **Urine leak** (drain → stent); **haemorrhage (bleeding), pseudoaneurysm or AV (arteriovenous) fistula** (delayed haematuria: angioembolisation); AKI (acute kidney injury); temporary dialysis in a solitary kidney.
- **Positive margin 0–7%:** surveillance, not reoperation.
- **Hyperfiltration injury** when >50% of a solitary kidney is removed: proteinuria, FSGS (focal segmental glomerulosclerosis); give an ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor.
- **Follow-up:** risk-based imaging; eGFR and urine protein.

### Recent advances
- 3D (three-dimensional) models; ICG (indocyanine green) for selective clamping; neoadjuvant TKI (tyrosine kinase inhibitor) downsizing in imperative cases.

**Diagram to draw:** clamped renal artery, tumour excised with a rim, two-layer renorrhaphy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Imperative, relative, elective; PN is the standard for cT1a; ablation or surveillance for frail patients with tumours <3 cm.
> - AUA: RN only if complex + no CKD + new GFR likely >45.
> - Retroperitoneal for posterior, transperitoneal for anterior tumours.
> - Preserved parenchyma, not clamp time, governs function.
> - Trifecta: negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
> - EORTC 30904: better function, similar cancer control.

# Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Acquired renal cystic disease
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q3b

### Definition
- **ARCD (acquired renal cystic disease)**: bilateral cysts in kidneys of patients with **ESRD (end-stage renal disease)** not due to inherited cystic disease. Diagnosis: **≥3 cysts in each kidney**.
- Described in 1977; occurs with haemodialysis and peritoneal dialysis.

### Epidemiology
| Duration of renal failure | Prevalence |
|---|---|
| Before dialysis | About 10% |
| 3 years of dialysis | 44% |
| 5 years | 60% |
| ≥10 years | >90% |
- Male:female 3:1; independent of the cause of renal failure.

### Aetiology
- **Uraemia, not dialysis**, is the cause; dialysis only gives cysts time to form.
- Tubular obstruction (fibrosis, oxalate crystals); uraemic toxins; growth factors (EGF (epidermal growth factor)); compensatory hyperplasia.
- Cysts may regress after transplant (data conflict); cancer risk persists.

### Clinical features
- Mostly asymptomatic.
- **Bleeding into cysts (up to 50%)**: loin pain, haematuria, retroperitoneal haematoma; worsened by uraemic coagulopathy and heparin.
- Cyst infection, stones, polycythaemia (erythropoietin).
- **Neoplasia**: adenomas in 20–25%. **RCC (renal cell carcinoma)** risk about 3 times that without ARCD. **Acquired cystic disease-associated RCC** is a distinct subtype (oxalate crystals, good prognosis); papillary RCC is also common.

### Evaluation
- **Ultrasound**: small echogenic kidneys, many small cysts.
- **CT (computed tomography) with and without contrast**: best for tumours. MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) for small lesions; gadolinium risks NSF (nephrogenic systemic fibrosis).

| Feature | ARCD | ADPKD (autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease) |
|---|---|---|
| Kidney size | Small (<300 g) | Large (>800 g) |
| Cysts | Small | Large |
| Extrarenal | None | Liver cysts, aneurysms |

### Management
- **Bleeding**: conservative; persistent → **embolisation or nephrectomy**; peritoneal dialysis avoids heparin.
- Painful cyst: aspiration; infected cyst: drainage, nephrectomy if it fails.
- **Tumour >3 cm: nephrectomy**. <3 cm: nephrectomy in fit patients, or yearly CT and excise if growing.
- **Screening** (debated): younger fit patients after 3 years of dialysis, and before transplant.
- After transplant, native-kidney RCC is more aggressive; >70% of transplant RCCs arise there.

**Diagram to draw:** small kidney with multiple cysts and a small solid tumour.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ≥3 cysts per kidney in ESRD; >90% after 10 years.
> - Uraemia causes it; transplant may regress cysts, not risk.
> - RCC risk 3 times; image dialysis patients with haematuria.

## ADPKD (Adult polycystic kidney disease)
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3d

### Genetics
- ADPKD (autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease): commonest inherited cystic kidney disease (about 1 in 400–1000); 7–15% of patients on haemodialysis.

| Gene | Chromosome | Share | ESRD (end-stage renal disease) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **PKD1** (polycystin-1) | 16 | ~85% | 50s |
| **PKD2** (polycystin-2) | 4 | ~15% | 70s |

- Polycystins (primary cilium) restrain proliferation.

### Clinical features (30–50 years)
- **Pain** (commonest): mass, cyst haemorrhage, infection, stones. **Haematuria** about 50%; **stones** 20–30%.
- **Hypertension:** about 50% at 20–35 years with normal function; stretched vessels release renin.
- **Renal failure:** tracks kidney volume; worse with PKD1, male sex.
- **Extrarenal:** liver cysts (commonest; up to 90% by 50); **intracranial aneurysm 10–30%**; seminal vesicle (40%), pancreatic, arachnoid cysts; mitral valve prolapse; diverticulosis.
- RCC (renal cell carcinoma) is not commoner, but is more often bilateral and sarcomatoid.

### Diagnosis (ultrasound in at-risk relatives)
| Age | Ravine (PKD1 families) | Pei unified (2009) |
|---|---|---|
| 15–29 | ≥2 cysts, one or both kidneys | ≥3 cysts in total (15–39) |
| 30–59 | ≥2 cysts in each kidney | ≥2 in each kidney (40–59) |
| ≥60 | ≥4 cysts in each kidney | ≥4 in each kidney |

- CT or MRI shows TKV (total kidney volume), which predicts progression. Genetic testing if equivocal or for a living donor.

### Management
- **BP <130/80 mmHg** with ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor or ARB (angiotensin receptor blocker); HALT-PKD: tighter control slowed TKV growth.
- **Tolvaptan** (V2-receptor antagonist; lowers cyclic adenosine monophosphate): **TEMPO 3:4 (2012)** slowed TKV growth and function loss; **REPRISE (2017)** slowed eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) decline in later disease. Causes polyuria and liver toxicity.
- **Pain:** exclude infection, stone, tumour; avoid NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs); aspiration, then laparoscopic unroofing.
- **Infected cyst:** lipid-soluble antibiotics (trimethoprim–sulfamethoxazole, fluoroquinolones); drain if no response.
- **Haemorrhage:** usually settles with rest. **Aneurysm screening** (MR angiography) if family history.
- **ESRD:** dialysis or transplant (no recurrence). **Native nephrectomy** before or at transplant for recurrent infection, bleeding, pain, suspected tumour or no room for the graft.

**Diagram to draw:** large cystic kidneys, liver cysts and berry aneurysm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PKD1: chromosome 16, 85%, earlier ESRD.
> - Ravine: <30 years ≥2 cysts; 30–59 ≥2 each kidney; ≥60 ≥4 each.
> - Tolvaptan (TEMPO 3:4, REPRISE); ACE inhibitor or ARB.

## Biomarkers for patients with advanced RCC
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3d

### Introduction
- A **prognostic** biomarker predicts outcome regardless of treatment; a **predictive** biomarker predicts benefit from a specific drug.
- In advanced RCC (renal cell carcinoma), only clinical risk models are used routinely. No molecular biomarker is yet validated to select treatment (EAU, European Association of Urology; NCCN, National Comprehensive Cancer Network).

### Clinical and laboratory: risk models
| Factor | MSKCC (Memorial Sloan-Kettering Cancer Center) | IMDC (International Metastatic RCC Database Consortium) |
|---|---|---|
| Karnofsky performance status <80% | Yes | Yes |
| Haemoglobin < lower limit of normal | Yes | Yes |
| Corrected calcium high | >10 mg/dL | > upper limit of normal |
| LDH (lactate dehydrogenase) >1.5 × normal | Yes | No |
| Time factor | No prior nephrectomy | <1 year from diagnosis to treatment |
| Neutrophils, platelets > normal | No | Yes |

- IMDC: favourable 0 factors (median survival 43 months), intermediate 1–2 (22.5 months), poor ≥3 (7.8 months).
- IMDC group guides first-line choice (nivolumab–ipilimumab not for favourable risk).
- **Also know:** neutrophil-to-lymphocyte ratio, CRP (C-reactive protein), liver or bone metastases (poor prognosis).

### Tissue biomarkers
- **PD-L1 (programmed death-ligand 1):** benefit of checkpoint combinations is **independent of PD-L1**, so it is not used to select patients.
- **Sarcomatoid features:** respond well to checkpoint inhibitors.
- **Gene expression signatures:** IMmotion150 showed high T-effector signature tumours do better with atezolizumab–bevacizumab; high angiogenic signature tumours do well with sunitinib. Investigational.
- **Genomic:** BAP1 loss and CDKN2A loss indicate poor prognosis; PBRM1 data conflicting. MET alterations in papillary RCC (cabozantinib); FH (fumarate hydratase) deficiency. TMB (tumour mutational burden) is low and not predictive.

### Circulating and other biomarkers
- **KIM-1 (kidney injury molecule-1):** high serum levels predict recurrence and benefit from adjuvant immunotherapy (investigational).
- **ctDNA (circulating tumour DNA):** RCC sheds little DNA; methylation assays are promising.
- **Also know:** antibiotics during checkpoint therapy reduced survival (microbiome); sunitinib-induced hypertension is linked to better outcome.

**Diagram to draw:** IMDC risk groups with survival curves.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - IMDC: 6 factors; poor risk ≥3; LDH only in MSKCC.
> - PD-L1 does not select patients in RCC.
> - Sarcomatoid tumours respond well to immunotherapy.

## Tivozanib
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3b

### Introduction
- Oral **VEGFR TKI (vascular endothelial growth factor receptor tyrosine kinase inhibitor)** used in advanced RCC (renal cell carcinoma).
- Targets **VEGFR-1, -2 and -3** (also c-KIT and PDGFR-β, platelet-derived growth factor receptor-β); highly potent and selective.

### Mechanism and rationale
- In clear cell RCC, **VHL (von Hippel–Lindau) loss** lets HIF (hypoxia-inducible factor) accumulate, driving VEGF and angiogenesis.
- Tivozanib blocks VEGFR signalling, starving the tumour of new vessels.
- High selectivity means fewer off-target effects (less hand-foot skin reaction and diarrhoea than sorafenib).

### Dose and pharmacology
- **1.34 mg orally once daily, 21 days of a 28-day cycle.**
- Long half-life (about 4–5 days); dose reduction for toxicity or hepatic impairment.

### Key trials
| Trial | Setting | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **TIVO-1** (phase III) | 0–1 prior therapy, versus sorafenib | PFS (progression-free survival) 11.9 vs 9.1 months; response 33% vs 23%; OS (overall survival) not better (28.8 vs 29.3 months) |
| **TIVO-3** (phase III) | After 2–3 prior systemic therapies including a VEGFR TKI, versus sorafenib | PFS 5.6 vs 3.9 months (HR, hazard ratio, 0.73); response 18% vs 8%; OS not different (HR 0.97); benefit also after prior checkpoint inhibitor |
| **TiNivo-2** (phase III) | After checkpoint inhibitor; tivozanib ± nivolumab | **Negative:** adding nivolumab did not improve PFS (no immunotherapy re-challenge) |

### Approval and place
- FDA (Food and Drug Administration) declined in 2013 because of the OS concern in TIVO-1; EMA (European Medicines Agency) approved first-line use in 2017.
- **FDA March 2021:** relapsed or refractory advanced RCC **after ≥2 prior systemic therapies**.
- NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network): later-line option, mainly after immunotherapy combinations.

### Adverse effects
- **Hypertension** (commonest; control before starting), dysphonia, fatigue, diarrhoea, proteinuria, hypothyroidism.
- Bleeding, arterial thrombosis, cardiac failure, **impaired wound healing** (stop before surgery), RPLS (reversible posterior leukoencephalopathy syndrome).
- **Monitoring:** blood pressure, thyroid tests, urine protein, liver tests.

**Diagram to draw:** VHL loss → HIF → VEGF → VEGFR, blocked by tivozanib.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Selective VEGFR-1–3 TKI; 1.34 mg daily, 3 weeks on, 1 off.
> - TIVO-3: PFS 5.6 vs 3.9 months; OS not different.
> - FDA 2021: after ≥2 prior systemic therapies.
> - TiNivo-2 negative; main toxicity hypertension.

## Immunotherapy in (metastatic) RCC
**Asked as:**
- Immunotherapy therapy for metastatic RCC — *Winter 2024 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)* (overall management of metastatic RCC: see 2024-Winter Paper 3, Q3b)
- Role of immunotherapy in RCC — *Practice Set 4 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3e and 2025-Winter Paper 4, Q3a)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Rationale
- RCC (renal cell carcinoma) is immunogenic (spontaneous regressions / rare spontaneous regression, T-cell infiltration) and chemoresistant.
- **Checkpoints:** PD-1 (programmed death-1) on CD8 T cells binds tumour PD-L1 and switches the T cell off; CTLA-4 (cytotoxic T-lymphocyte antigen-4) on regulatory T cells damps priming. Blocking them (blockade) **releases the brake** on existing anti-tumour immunity.
- VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor) is immunosuppressive (suppresses immunity), so TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors) synergise.

### Cytokine era
- Interferon-α (IFN-α, interferon-alpha): response 10–15%; obsolete.
- **High-dose IL-2 (interleukin-2):** durable complete response **7–9%**, but capillary leak needs intensive care; selected fit patients only.

### Checkpoint inhibitors: trials (all clear cell)
| Trial | Regimen | Result |
|---|---|---|
| CheckMate 025 | Nivolumab vs everolimus, after TKI | OS (overall survival) 25.0 vs 19.6 months |
| **CheckMate 214** | Nivolumab + ipilimumab vs sunitinib | Intermediate/poor risk: OS HR (hazard ratio) 0.63; complete response 9% |
| KEYNOTE-426 | Pembrolizumab + axitinib vs sunitinib | Better PFS (progression-free survival) and OS |
| CheckMate 9ER | Nivolumab + cabozantinib vs sunitinib | PFS 16.6 vs 8.3 months |
| **CLEAR** | Pembrolizumab + lenvatinib vs sunitinib | PFS 23.9 vs 9.2 months; response 71% |

- **First line:** an IO/TKI (immuno-oncology plus TKI) combination for all IMDC (International Metastatic RCC Database Consortium) risk groups; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor risk only.
- **After IO:** switch to a VEGF TKI; IO re-challenge (rechallenge) failed (CONTACT-03, TiNivo-2). Belzutifan after IO and TKI.

### Adjuvant
- **KEYNOTE-564:** pembrolizumab for 1 year after nephrectomy in high-risk clear cell RCC, including resected metastases (M1 NED, no evidence of disease); DFS (disease-free survival) HR 0.68 (another source: HR 0.63), later OS benefit (HR 0.62). Atezolizumab and nivolumab-based adjuvant trials were negative.

### Toxicity: irAEs (immune-related adverse events)
- Colitis, hepatitis, pneumonitis, thyroiditis, hypophysitis, nephritis, myocarditis, rash.

| Grade | Action |
|---|---|
| 1 | Continue, monitor |
| 2 | Hold; prednisolone 0.5–1 mg/kg |
| 3–4 | Stop; methylprednisolone 1–2 mg/kg; infliximab or mycophenolate if refractory |

- Endocrinopathies: hormone replacement.

### Biomarkers
- **PD-L1 does not select patients.** Sarcomatoid tumours respond well. Gene signatures investigational; antibiotics may reduce benefit.
- **Also know:** avelumab + axitinib (JAVELIN, no OS benefit); non-clear cell data scant.

**Diagram to draw:** T cell–tumour synapse with PD-1/PD-L1 and CTLA-4/B7 blocked.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - IO/TKI for all risks; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor only.
> - CheckMate 214: OS HR 0.63, complete response 9%.
> - KEYNOTE-564: adjuvant pembrolizumab, DFS and OS benefit.
> - irAE grade ≥2: hold drug and give steroids.

## VHL gene and von Hippel–Lindau disease
**Asked as:**
- VHL gene. — *Practice Set 2 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- Von Hippel Lindau disease — *Practice Set 8 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(Practice Set 8 Paper II Q3c: VHL gene also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q3f)

### Introduction
- **VHL (von Hippel–Lindau)** is a **tumour suppressor gene on chromosome 3p25**, identified in 1993; three exons, 213-amino-acid protein.
- Germline mutation causes **VHL disease**: an **autosomal dominant** familial cancer syndrome (about 1 in 36,000), penetrance >95% by 65 years.
- It is the familial form of **clear cell RCC** (renal cell carcinoma). Somatic VHL loss occurs in most sporadic clear cell RCC; chromosome 3p is altered in over 90%.

### Function / genetics and pathogenesis: the oxygen-sensing pathway
- VHL protein binds elongin B, elongin C, cullin-2 and RBX1 to form (VHL protein forms) an **E3 ubiquitin ligase** that degrades **HIF (hypoxia-inducible factor)** in normoxia.
- **Normoxia:** hydroxylated **HIF -1α and -2α** are tagged for proteasomal destruction.
- **VHL loss (or hypoxia):** loss of VHL stabilises HIF, which (chiefly HIF-2α) accumulates, joins ARNT (aryl hydrocarbon receptor nuclear translocator) and switches on **VEGF (vascular endothelial growth factor)**, PDGF (platelet-derived growth factor), TGF-α (transforming growth factor-α), GLUT1, erythropoietin and CA-IX (carbonic anhydrase IX).
- Hence the **hypervascularity** (**highly vascular** tumours) of clear cell RCC.
- **Knudson two-hit:** one germline plus one somatic hit, so VHL tumours are **early, bilateral and multifocal**.

### VHL disease manifestations
| Lesion | Frequency |
|---|---|
| Craniospinal haemangioblastoma (cerebellum commonest) | 60–80% |
| Retinal angioma (earliest) | 25–60% |
| **Clear cell RCC** (mean age 39; commonest cause of death) | 25–75% |
| Renal cysts (commonest renal lesion) | Up to 76% |
| **Phaeochromocytoma** | 10–25% |
| Pancreatic cysts / neuroendocrine tumours | 45–75% / 11–17% |
| Epididymal cystadenoma | 25–60% of men |
- Also endolymphatic sac tumour.
- **RCC is now the commonest cause of death.**

### Genotype–phenotype
| Type | Mutation | Phaeochromocytoma | RCC |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Deletions, truncations | Low | High |
| 2A | Missense | High | Low |
| 2B | Missense | High | High |
| 2C | Missense | Phaeochromocytoma only | Absent |

### Diagnosis
- Family history plus one lesion; otherwise ≥2 haemangioblastomas, or 1 plus a visceral lesion. Genetic testing of relatives.

### Surveillance (VHL Alliance 2020)
- Eye check (examination) yearly from infancy; metanephrines yearly from 5 years; CNS (brain and spine) MRI every 2 years from 11; **abdominal MRI every 2 years from 15**.

### Clinical implications / urological management
- **3 cm rule:** observe solid renal tumours <3 cm; intervene when largest reaches 3 cm; then nephron-sparing surgery (enucleation) or ablation, after excluding phaeochromocytoma.
- **Nephron-sparing surgery** (enucleation) is standard; not curative: local recurrence 75–85%, yet 10-year disease-specific survival 81–94%. Ablation for repeated small tumours.
- Exclude phaeochromocytoma before any surgery (normetanephrine raised; α-blockade). **Partial (cortical-sparing) adrenalectomy.**
- **Targeted therapy:** VEGF TKIs (tyrosine kinase inhibitors), bevacizumab, mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin) inhibitors.
- **Belzutifan** (HIF-2α inhibitor): FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) approved 2021 for VHL disease; renal response 30–50% (another source: responses in about half of renal tumours); anaemia.
- **Also know:** PBRM1, BAP1, SETD2 on 3p.

**Diagram to draw:** VHL–HIF pathway in normoxia versus (vs) VHL loss, with drug targets.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AD; 3p25 tumour suppressor; VHL protein degrades HIF.
> - VHL loss: HIF-2α drives (→) VEGF, giving vascular clear cell RCC.
> - Type 2 = phaeochromocytoma; 3 cm rule for nephron sparing (nephron-sparing surgery).
> - Belzutifan (HIF-2α inhibitor) approved 2021.

## Renal aangiomyolipoma
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 2, Q3f
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 3, Q3d)

*Assumed meaning: renal angiomyolipoma (AML).*

### Introduction
- Benign **PEComa (perivascular epithelioid cell tumour)** of dysmorphic **vessels, smooth muscle and fat**. Female predominance; fourth–fifth decades.
- **Sporadic** (solitary, small) or with **TSC (tuberous sclerosis complex**; younger, bilateral, multifocal, larger) or LAM (lymphangioleiomyomatosis).
- **Mechanism:** loss of **TSC2** (tuberin) or TSC1 (hamartin) → unchecked **mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin)** → growth and angiogenesis.
- **Epithelioid AML**: little fat; aggressive (venous extension, metastasis); treat as RCC (renal cell carcinoma).

### Clinical features
- Mostly incidental. Large tumours bleed: **Wunderlich syndrome** (spontaneous retroperitoneal haemorrhage; Lenk triad: flank pain, mass, shock). CKD (chronic kidney disease) in TSC.

### Diagnosis
- **CT (computed tomography): macroscopic fat (below about −15 to −20 HU [Hounsfield units]) on non-contrast images is diagnostic.**
- US (ultrasound) hyperechoic (so are some RCCs); MRI (magnetic resonance imaging): signal loss on fat suppression, India-ink artefact.
- Fat-poor lesion → MRI ± biopsy (HMB-45 [human melanoma black-45], Melan-A, smooth muscle actin positive).
- **Aneurysm ≥5 mm** on CT angiography predicts bleeding.

### Management
| Situation | Management |
|---|---|
| Acute haemorrhage | Resuscitate → **SAE (selective arterial embolisation)** → nephrectomy if SAE fails |
| Symptomatic | SAE, NSS (nephron-sparing surgery), ablation in selected cases |
| Asymptomatic with **>6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth >0.25 cm/year, childbearing age or pregnancy** | Active treatment |
| None of these | **Surveillance**: imaging at 6 and 12 months, then yearly |
| TSC, asymptomatic, **>3 cm** | **Everolimus**; MRI every 1–3 years |
- The old **4 cm** cut-off overtreats; it should not be a general trigger.

### Treatments
- **SAE:** first line; **post-embolisation syndrome** (fever, pain, leucocytosis), abscess, infarction.
- **Partial nephrectomy:** preferred surgery, essential in TSC (multifocal, recurrent).
- Thermal ablation: limited data.
- **Everolimus (EXIST-2):** ≥50% shrinkage in **42% vs 0%**; stomatitis, acne, hyperlipidaemia.

**Diagram to draw:** management algorithm by bleeding, symptoms, size and TSC.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Macroscopic fat on non-contrast CT is diagnostic.
> - Treat if >6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth, pregnancy or symptoms.
> - SAE for bleeding; partial nephrectomy preferred; everolimus for TSC >3 cm.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 13 · Kidney & UTUC

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Metastatic RCC (mRCC): assessment and management, including recent concepts (LAQ)

- IMDC (6 factors): Karnofsky, haemoglobin, calcium, neutrophils, platelets, <1 year to therapy; poor = ≥3.
- IO/TKI (IO combinations) for all risk groups; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor only.
- CARMENA and SURTIME: no routine upfront CN.
- Adjuvant pembrolizumab (KEYNOTE-564), including M1 NED.
- Belzutifan after IO and TKI.

#### Etiopathogenesis and management of Upper tract TCC (LAQ)

- Tobacco, aristolochic acid (A:T → T:A in TP53) and Lynch (MSH2) are the key causes.
- EAU low risk needs every favourable feature; one adverse feature means high risk.
- RNU with complete bladder cuff; single postoperative MMC (ODMIT-C 28% → 16%).
- POUT: adjuvant platinum, 3-year DFS 71% vs 46%; EV-302 for metastatic disease.

#### Nephron-sparing surgery / partial nephrectomy in RCC: role, indications and approaches (LAQ)

- Imperative, relative, elective; PN is the standard for cT1a; ablation or surveillance for frail patients with tumours <3 cm.
- AUA: RN only if complex + no CKD + new GFR likely >45.
- Retroperitoneal for posterior, transperitoneal for anterior tumours.
- Preserved parenchyma, not clamp time, governs function.
- Trifecta: negative margin, no major complication, warm ischaemia ≤25 min.
- EORTC 30904: better function, similar cancer control.

#### Acquired renal cystic disease (SAQ)

- ≥3 cysts per kidney in ESRD; >90% after 10 years.
- Uraemia causes it; transplant may regress cysts, not risk.
- RCC risk 3 times; image dialysis patients with haematuria.

#### ADPKD (Adult polycystic kidney disease) (SAQ)

- PKD1: chromosome 16, 85%, earlier ESRD.
- Ravine: <30 years ≥2 cysts; 30–59 ≥2 each kidney; ≥60 ≥4 each.
- Tolvaptan (TEMPO 3:4, REPRISE); ACE inhibitor or ARB.

#### Biomarkers for patients with advanced RCC (SAQ)

- IMDC: 6 factors; poor risk ≥3; LDH only in MSKCC.
- PD-L1 does not select patients in RCC.
- Sarcomatoid tumours respond well to immunotherapy.

#### Tivozanib (SAQ)

- Selective VEGFR-1–3 TKI; 1.34 mg daily, 3 weeks on, 1 off.
- TIVO-3: PFS 5.6 vs 3.9 months; OS not different.
- FDA 2021: after ≥2 prior systemic therapies.
- TiNivo-2 negative; main toxicity hypertension.

#### Immunotherapy in (metastatic) RCC (SAQ)

- IO/TKI for all risks; nivolumab + ipilimumab for intermediate/poor only.
- CheckMate 214: OS HR 0.63, complete response 9%.
- KEYNOTE-564: adjuvant pembrolizumab, DFS and OS benefit.
- irAE grade ≥2: hold drug and give steroids.

#### VHL gene and von Hippel–Lindau disease (SAQ)

- AD; 3p25 tumour suppressor; VHL protein degrades HIF.
- VHL loss: HIF-2α drives (→) VEGF, giving vascular clear cell RCC.
- Type 2 = phaeochromocytoma; 3 cm rule for nephron sparing (nephron-sparing surgery).
- Belzutifan (HIF-2α inhibitor) approved 2021.

#### Renal aangiomyolipoma (SAQ)

- Macroscopic fat on non-contrast CT is diagnostic.
- Treat if >6 cm, aneurysm >5 mm, growth, pregnancy or symptoms.
- SAE for bleeding; partial nephrectomy preferred; everolimus for TSC >3 cm.

# Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## How will you evaluate and manage 38 yr male with uncontrolled hypertension
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 2, Q1
(renovascular hypertension also answered in Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q1; phaeochromocytoma in Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q1)

*Assumed focus: secondary (urological, renal and adrenal) causes of hypertension in a young adult.*

### Introduction
- **Resistant hypertension:** BP (blood pressure) above goal despite 3 drugs of different classes, including a diuretic, at optimal doses; or needing 4 or more drugs.
- Poor control below 40 years is a classic trigger to search for a **secondary cause**; many are curable.

### Causes
| Group | Causes | Clues |
|---|---|---|
| Renovascular | FMD (fibromuscular dysplasia), atherosclerotic RAS (renal artery stenosis), Takayasu arteritis, reninoma | Bruit; creatinine rise >30% on ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor; flash pulmonary oedema; kidneys differing >1.5 cm |
| Renal parenchymal | CKD (chronic kidney disease), ADPKD (autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease), reflux nephropathy, glomerulonephritis, Page kidney | Raised creatinine, proteinuria, haematuria |
| Obstructive uropathy | Bilateral obstruction (about 77% hypertensive), unilateral (about 20%), chronic retention | LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms), palpable bladder, hydronephrosis |
| Primary aldosteronism (PA) | Adenoma (Conn), bilateral hyperplasia | Hypokalaemia (present in <40% only) |
| Phaeochromocytoma, paraganglioma | Sporadic or hereditary | Paroxysmal headache, sweating, palpitations |
| Cushing syndrome | Exogenous steroid, adrenal adenoma, pituitary | Central obesity, striae, diabetes |
- **Also know:** sleep apnoea; drugs (NSAIDs [non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs], steroids, decongestants, liquorice); alcohol; thyroid and parathyroid disease; coarctation.

### Evaluation
**Step 1: Exclude pseudo-resistance**
- Correct cuff technique; home or ambulatory monitoring to exclude white-coat effect; check adherence, doses, interfering drugs, salt and alcohol.

**Step 2: History and examination**
- Paroxysms, weakness or polyuria (hypokalaemia), stones, UTI (urinary tract infection) or childhood reflux, snoring, family history.
- BP both arms and radio-femoral delay; abdominal bruit; palpable kidneys or bladder; cushingoid habitus; café-au-lait spots (NF1 [neurofibromatosis type 1]); fundus.

**Step 3: Baseline tests and target-organ damage**
- Creatinine, eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate), electrolytes, urinalysis with albumin–creatinine ratio, glucose, lipids, ECG (electrocardiogram), echocardiography.
- **Ultrasound KUB (kidneys, ureters, bladder):** renal size and asymmetry, scars, cysts, masses, hydronephrosis, residual urine.

**Step 4: Targeted screening (method and interpretation)**
| Cause | Screen | Confirm or localise |
|---|---|---|
| PA | Morning **ARR (aldosterone-to-renin ratio)** >20–30 with aldosterone ≥15–20 ng/dL; stop spironolactone first; correct potassium | **Intravenous saline 2 L over 4 h** (aldosterone stays >5 ng/dL) or oral salt loading → adrenal CT (computed tomography) → **AVS (adrenal vein sampling)**; skip AVS if age <40 with a clear unilateral adenoma >1 cm |
| Phaeochromocytoma | **Plasma free or 24-h urinary fractionated metanephrines** | CT or MRI (magnetic resonance imaging); ⁶⁸Ga-DOTATATE PET (positron emission tomography) or MIBG (metaiodobenzylguanidine); genetic testing |
| Cushing | **1-mg overnight dexamethasone suppression** (cortisol ≤1.8 µg/dL excludes); late-night salivary cortisol; 24-h urinary free cortisol | ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone): low = adrenal, high = pituitary or ectopic |
| Renovascular | **Duplex Doppler**: peak systolic velocity >180–200 cm/s, renal/aortic ratio >3.5 | CT or MR angiography; catheter angiography with pressure gradient if intervention planned |
| Parenchymal, obstructive | Ultrasound, urine tests | Renogram (DTPA [diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid] or MAG3 [mercaptoacetyltriglycine]) for split function and drainage; CT urography |

```
Uncontrolled BP, age 38 → exclude pseudo-resistance
```
