```
 → baseline tests + ultrasound KUB
 → ARR, metanephrines, dexamethasone test, renal Doppler
    ├─ positive → confirm → localise → treat cause
    └─ all negative → essential: optimise drugs
```

### Management
**1. General (all patients)**
- Lifestyle: salt restriction, weight loss, exercise, limit alcohol.
- Optimise **ACE inhibitor or ARB (angiotensin receptor blocker) + calcium channel blocker + thiazide-like diuretic**. Fourth drug: **spironolactone** (PATHWAY-2, 2015: best add-on in resistant hypertension).

**2. Cause-specific**
| Cause | Treatment |
|---|---|
| PA, lateralised | **Laparoscopic adrenalectomy**: biochemical cure about 94%, but only 37–52% need no antihypertensives |
| PA, bilateral or unfit | Spironolactone 25–50 mg/day (up to 400 mg) or eplerenone |
| Phaeochromocytoma | **α-blockade 7–14 days**, β-blocker only after α, volume expansion → laparoscopic adrenalectomy; metanephrines yearly for life |
| Adrenal Cushing | Adrenalectomy with steroid cover; pituitary disease → trans-sphenoidal surgery |
| FMD | **Angioplasty alone** (cure common in the young) |
| Atherosclerotic RAS | Medical therapy (**ASTRAL 2009, CORAL 2014**: stenting no benefit); stent for flash pulmonary oedema or falling function |
| Branch disease, aneurysm | Surgical bypass or ex vivo repair with autotransplantation |
| Reninoma | Partial nephrectomy (cures most) |
| Small non-functioning kidney (reflux, segmental hypoplasia) | **Nephrectomy** if renal vein renin lateralises (ratio >1.5) |
| Page kidney | Drain or decapsulate |
| Obstruction | **Relieve it** (catheter, nephrostomy, pyeloplasty, prostate surgery); watch post-obstructive diuresis |
| CKD | ACE inhibitor or ARB for proteinuria; scheduled diuretics |

**3. Follow-up:** home BP, creatinine, potassium; duplex after angioplasty.

### Recent advances
- **Catheter renal denervation** (radiofrequency or ultrasound), approved in the USA in 2023 for selected resistant hypertension; SPARTACUS trial questioned routine AVS.

**Diagram to draw:** stepwise algorithm from pseudo-resistance to cause-specific treatment.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Hypertension below 40 years: screen for secondary causes.
> - ARR → saline suppression → CT → AVS (skip if <40 with adenoma >1 cm).
> - Metanephrines for phaeochromocytoma; α before β.
> - FMD: angioplasty; atherosclerotic RAS: medical (ASTRAL, CORAL).

# Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Phaeochromocytoma: overview, pre-operative and intra-operative management
**Asked as:**
- Pre operative management of pheochromocytoma. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Intra op mgt of pheo. — *Practice Set 7 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Pheochromocytoma — *Practice Set 1 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(Practice Set 7 Paper II Q3c: preoperative management also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3c; perioperative in Practice Set 6 Paper 1, Q1)
(Practice Set 1 Paper II Q3d: pre-operative management in detail: 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

### Definition and epidemiology
- Catecholamine-secreting tumour of adrenal medullary chromaffin cells; **paraganglioma** is the extra-adrenal form (organ of Zuckerkandl, bladder).
- 1–2 per 100,000 a year; about 5% of adrenal incidentalomas.
- **"Rule of 10" is outdated:** extra-adrenal up to 25%, **hereditary about one-third**, bilateral commoner in hereditary disease.

### Genetics
- **MEN2 (multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2)**, RET: about 50% risk, adrenal, low malignancy. **VHL (von Hippel–Lindau)**: normetanephrine only. **NF1 (neurofibromatosis type 1)**. **SDHB (succinate dehydrogenase subunit B):** extra-adrenal, **30–50% metastatic**. (SDHB/SDHD [succinate dehydrogenase subunits].)
- Offer genetic testing to all: about one-third hereditary (MEN2, VHL, NF1, SDHB/SDHD).

### Clinical features
- Triad: **headache, sweating, palpitations**. Sustained hypertension is commoner than paroxysmal; pallor, hyperglycaemia; >20% asymptomatic. Epinephrine-dominant tumours can cause hypotensive episodes.

### Why prepare / why it is difficult
- Handling the tumour releases catecholamines: hypertensive crisis, arrhythmia, MI (myocardial infarction), stroke. After vein ligation, sudden withdrawal causes hypotension and hypoglycaemia.
- Phaeochromocytoma surgery is a **two-phase haemodynamic problem**: catecholamine **surge** while the tumour is handled, then catecholamine **withdrawal** once the vein is tied (hypotension, hypoglycaemia).
- Surges occur at intubation, positioning, pneumoperitoneum and tumour manipulation.
- Mortality fell from up to 50% to <3% with blockade, now advised for **all** proven tumours, even normotensive.

### Step 1: Confirm, localise, test genes (diagnosis)
- **Biochemistry first:** plasma free or 24-hour urinary fractionated **metanephrines**. Tumours convert catecholamines continuously, so metanephrines are more sensitive than episodic catecholamines. Chromogranin A; clonidine suppression for borderline results.
- **Imaging:** CT (computed tomography): >10 HU (Hounsfield units) unenhanced, no rapid washout; MRI (magnetic resonance imaging). **Functional:** ⁶⁸Ga-DOTATATE PET (positron emission tomography) or MIBG for extra-adrenal or hereditary disease, FDG (fluorodeoxyglucose) PET (best for SDHB), ¹²³I-MIBG (metaiodobenzylguanidine) (best in MEN2).
- **Never biopsy** (hypertensive crisis). Iodinated contrast is safe.

### Step 2: Cardiac assessment
- ECG (electrocardiogram) and echocardiography (catecholamine cardiomyopathy); joint plan with the anaesthetist.

### Step 3: Drug blockade (pre-operative preparation, 7–14 days)
| Drug | Action | Points |
|---|---|---|
| **Phenoxybenzamine** (first line) | Irreversible non-selective α-blocker | 10 mg twice daily, increase every 2–3 days (titrated); about 1 mg/kg total |
| Doxazosin, prazosin | Selective α1-blockers | Fewer side effects |
| β1-blocker (atenolol, metoprolol) | Controls tachycardia | **Only after adequate α-blockade** |
| Calcium channel blocker | Smooth muscle relaxation | Alternative or add-on |
| Metyrosine | Blocks tyrosine hydroxylase | Add-on; sedation, extrapyramidal effects |

- **α-blockade for all**, 7–14 days.
- **β-blocker only after α-blockade**, because unopposed α-stimulation causes crisis.
- **Why α before β:** β-blockade removes β2 vasodilatation, leaving α-vasoconstriction unopposed (unopposed α-stimulation): crisis.
- **Target:** seated BP (blood pressure) 120–130/80 mmHg; mild postural drop (systolic >90 mmHg) shows adequate blockade.

### Step 4: Volume expansion
- High-salt diet and fluids once blockade starts; 1–2 L IV fluid the night before. This prevents post-excision hypotension.

### Step 5: Day of surgery
- Last phenoxybenzamine dose the night before; omit the morning dose.
- Arterial and central lines; nitroprusside, nicardipine or phentolamine for surges; esmolol for tachyarrhythmia; early adrenal vein ligation.

### Intra-operative management
#### Prerequisites
- **α-blockade 7–14 days** (phenoxybenzamine or doxazosin), β-blocker only after α, volume expansion; ECG and echocardiography; joint plan with the anaesthetist.

#### Monitoring
- **Arterial line before induction**, central venous line, ECG, urine output, frequent glucose; cardiac output or transoesophageal echo if cardiomyopathy.

#### Anaesthesia
- Deep, smooth induction (propofol, opioid such as fentanyl or remifentanil); attenuate intubation response.
- **Avoid triggers:** histamine-releasing drugs (morphine, atracurium), ketamine, ephedrine, metoclopramide, droperidol, desflurane.
- Sevoflurane or isoflurane; vecuronium or rocuronium; magnesium sulphate as an adjunct (reduces catecholamine release and arrhythmia).

#### Haemodynamic control
| Event | Action |
|---|---|
| **Hypertensive surge** | **Pause manipulation**; nitroprusside, nicardipine or clevidipine, nitroglycerin, phentolamine |
| **Tachyarrhythmia** | **Esmolol**, lidocaine; amiodarone if needed |
| **Hypotension after vein ligation** | **Aggressive volume**, then α-agonist (phenylephrine, noradrenaline); vasopressin if refractory |
| **Hypoglycaemia** (insulin rebound) | Dextrose infusion, glucose checks |

#### Surgical principles
- **Laparoscopic adrenalectomy** (transperitoneal or posterior retroperitoneoscopic) is standard; open for large or invasive tumours.
- **Minimal handling**; "no-touch" dissection away from the tumour.
- **Early adrenal vein ligation** where possible, after warning the anaesthetist.
- Remove intact in a bag (avoid capsule rupture and seeding).
- **Cortical-sparing** (partial) adrenalectomy in MEN2 (multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2), VHL (von Hippel–Lindau) and bilateral tumours.
- Steroid cover if bilateral adrenalectomy.

### Postoperative
- Fluids ± vasopressors for **hypotension**; glucose monitoring (**rebound hypoglycaemia**); HDU (high dependency unit) overnight: blood pressure, heart rate, glucose; steroid cover after bilateral adrenalectomy.
- Metanephrines after surgery, then yearly for life (**lifelong yearly metanephrines**; recurrence up to 16%).

### Metastatic disease and follow-up
- Follow-up: lifelong yearly metanephrines (see Postoperative).
- Malignancy is defined only by **metastases**. Treat with metastasectomy, ¹³¹I-MIBG, CVD (cyclophosphamide, vincristine, dacarbazine).

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: metanephrines → imaging → α-blockade → β-blockade → salt and fluids → surgery. Intraoperative BP curve: surges during handling, fall after vein ligation, with drugs marked. Catecholamine pathway to metanephrines (COMT (catechol-O-methyltransferase)).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Metanephrines first; never biopsy.
> - α-blockade 7–14 days for all.
> - Never β before α (α before β), plus volume expansion.
> - Volume expansion prevents hypotension; watch glucose.
> - Arterial line before induction; avoid histamine releasers and ephedrine.
> - Surge: pause handling, nitroprusside or phentolamine; esmolol for tachycardia.
> - After vein ligation: fluids, vasopressors, glucose.
> - One-third hereditary; SDHB most often metastatic.

## Cushing syndrome and the dexamethasone suppression test
**Asked as:**
- Cushing Syndrome — *Practice Set 2 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Dexamethasone suppression test — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Definitions
- **Cushing syndrome:** symptomatic hypercortisolism. **Cushing disease:** due to a pituitary ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone)-secreting adenoma.
- **MACS (mild autonomous cortisol secretion):** autonomous cortisol without clinical signs (replaces "subclinical Cushing"); raises cardiovascular, bone and diabetes risk.

### Causes
| Group | Cause | Share |
|---|---|---|
| Exogenous | Glucocorticoids (oral, inhaled, topical) | Commonest overall |
| ACTH-dependent (80–85% of endogenous) | Pituitary adenoma (Cushing disease) | About 70% |
| | Ectopic ACTH (bronchial carcinoid, small cell lung cancer, phaeochromocytoma) | 10–15% |
| ACTH-independent | Adrenal adenoma, ACC (adrenocortical carcinoma), AIMAH (macronodular hyperplasia), PPNAD (pigmented nodular disease; Carney complex) | About 10% |

### Clinical features
- Central obesity, moon face, plethora (90–100%); purple striae, hypertension, hirsutism, ED (70–90%).
- **Discriminating:** proximal weakness, bruising.
- **Urological:** nephrolithiasis in up to 50%; hypogonadotropic hypogonadism (low libido, ED).

### Diagnosis
**Step 1: exclude exogenous steroids (including creams and sprays) first. Step 2: confirm cortisol excess (two tests)**
| Test | Method | Abnormal |
|---|---|---|
| **Overnight 1-mg DST (dexamethasone suppression test)** | 1 mg at 11 pm; 8 am cortisol | >1.8 µg/dL (50 nmol/L) |
| Late-night salivary cortisol | 11 pm, twice | Raised |
| 24-h UFC (urinary free cortisol) | Two collections | Raised; used in pregnancy |

**Step 3: locate the source**
- **Low ACTH:** adrenal; adrenal CT or MRI.
- **High ACTH:** pituitary MRI; **IPSS (inferior petrosal sinus sampling) with CRH (corticotropin-releasing hormone)**: central gradient = pituitary; none = ectopic (chest CT).
- Pitfalls: pseudo-Cushing (depression, alcohol), oestrogens, enzyme inducers.

### Dexamethasone suppression test
**Principle**
- **Dexamethasone** is a potent synthetic glucocorticoid that is **not detected by the cortisol assay**. In a normal axis it suppresses pituitary ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone) by negative feedback, so morning cortisol falls.
- **Autonomous** cortisol (adrenal adenoma) or excess ACTH drive (pituitary, ectopic) fails to suppress.
- "Low-dose" 1 mg is still 3–4 times physiological glucocorticoid.

**Indications**
- Suspected Cushing syndrome; **every adrenal incidentaloma** (5–8% secrete cortisol); suspected ACC (adrenocortical carcinoma).

**Types**
| Test | Method | Abnormal |
|---|---|---|
| **Overnight 1-mg LD-DST** (low-dose DST; preferred screen) | Dexamethasone 1 mg at 11 pm–midnight; serum cortisol 8–9 am | See below |
| 48-hour 2-mg LD-DST | 0.5 mg 6-hourly for 48 hours; cortisol at 48 hours | >1.8 µg/dL; improves accuracy |
| High-dose DST | 8 mg overnight or 2 mg 6-hourly for 48 hours | Over 50% suppression suggests pituitary source; **no longer routine** (some ectopic tumours also suppress) |

**Interpreting the 1-mg test**
| Morning cortisol | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **≤1.8 µg/dL (50 nmol/L)** | Normal; >90% sensitive, rules out |
| 1.8–5.0 µg/dL | Possible autonomous cortisol secretion |
| **>5.0 µg/dL (140 nmol/L)** | Autonomous secretion; >95% specific |
- ESE (European Society of Endocrinology) 2023 incidentaloma guideline: cortisol >1.8 µg/dL without overt signs = **MACS (mild autonomous cortisol secretion)**.
- If abnormal: repeat testing and measure **morning ACTH** (low = adrenal; high = pituitary or ectopic, IPSS [inferior petrosal sinus sampling]).

**Pitfalls**
- **False positive:** oral contraceptives (raise cortisol-binding globulin; up to 50%); CYP3A4 inducers (phenytoin, carbamazepine, rifampicin); missed dose; pseudo-Cushing states (depression, alcohol, obesity, stress).
- **False negative:** CYP3A4 inhibitors (itraconazole, ritonavir, diltiazem).
- **Choice of test:** pregnancy → UFC (urinary free cortisol); epilepsy → LNSC (late-night salivary cortisol); renal failure → DST (not affected by GFR [glomerular filtration rate]).

### Management
| Cause | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Cushing disease | Trans-sphenoidal surgery (cure 60–80%); radiotherapy |
| Adrenal adenoma | **Laparoscopic adrenalectomy** |
| ACC | Open radical adrenalectomy, mitotane |
| Ectopic | Resect source; otherwise bilateral adrenalectomy |
| AIMAH, PPNAD | Bilateral adrenalectomy |

- **Bilateral adrenalectomy:** for failed pituitary surgery or life-threatening hypercortisolism. Cost: lifelong replacement, **Nelson syndrome** (8–29%).
- **Medical (bridge):** metyrapone, ketoconazole, etomidate, mitotane, mifepristone.

### Perioperative care (urological relevance)
- **Contralateral adrenal is suppressed:** autonomous cortisol suppresses the opposite adrenal; adrenal insufficiency in about 99.7% after surgery for overt Cushing.
- Give **perioperative steroid cover** and taper after adrenalectomy: **Hydrocortisone 100 mg IV** on the day of surgery, then slow oral taper; recovery takes a median 11 months.

**Diagram to draw:** HPA (hypothalamic–pituitary–adrenal) axis with dexamethasone feedback and with ACTH-dependent and ACTH-independent sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Exclude exogenous steroids; 1-mg DST cortisol >1.8 µg/dL abnormal.
> - 1 mg at 11 pm; 8 am cortisol ≤1.8 µg/dL excludes; >5.0 µg/dL confirms autonomy.
> - Oral contraceptives and enzyme inducers cause false positives.
> - ACTH low = adrenal; high = pituitary/ectopic (IPSS); ACTH separates adrenal from pituitary/ectopic.
> - High-dose DST no longer routine.
> - Steroid cover after adrenalectomy; Nelson syndrome after bilateral.

## Algorithm for management of an incidentaloma
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q3d
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3f)

*Assumed meaning: adrenal incidentaloma.*

### Definition
- Adrenal mass **≥1 cm** found on imaging done for another reason. Two questions: **Is it malignant (imaging)? Is it functioning (biochemistry)?**
- Causes: non-functioning adenoma (commonest); cortisol-secreting adenoma 5.3%; phaeochromocytoma 5.1%; aldosteronoma 1%; ACC (adrenocortical carcinoma) 4.7%; metastasis 2.5%; myelolipoma, cyst.

### Algorithm
```
Adrenal mass ≥1 cm
 ├─ IMAGING: unenhanced CT (computed tomography)
 │    ≤10 HU (Hounsfield units), homogeneous → benign adenoma
 │    >10 HU → washout CT (15 min) or chemical-shift MRI
 │         APW >60% or RPW >40% → adenoma
 │         indeterminate → MDT: repeat CT 6-12 months, FDG-PET or surgery
 │    Macroscopic fat → myelolipoma
 ├─ HORMONES (all patients)
 │    1-mg overnight DST (dexamethasone suppression test)
 │    plasma or urine metanephrines
 │    ARR (aldosterone-renin ratio) if hypertension or low potassium
 │    sex steroids only if ACC suspected
 └─ DECISION
      functioning, suspicious, >4 cm, or growing → adrenalectomy
      benign, non-functioning → discharge
```
- **APW** (absolute percentage washout) = (enhanced − delayed) ÷ (enhanced − unenhanced) × 100; **RPW** (relative) = (enhanced − delayed) ÷ enhanced × 100.

### Interpreting the tests
- **DST:** cortisol ≤1.8 µg/dL (50 nmol/L) excludes; 1.8–5.0 = possible autonomous secretion (**MACS**, mild autonomous cortisol secretion); >5.0 µg/dL autonomous.
- **Size and malignancy:** <4 cm 0.1%; 4–6 cm 2.4%; >6 cm 19.5%. Benign imaging overrides size.
- **Never biopsy or operate before excluding phaeochromocytoma.** Biopsy only for suspected metastasis in known cancer.

### Management
- **Surgery:** cortisol excess, phaeochromocytoma (after alpha-blockade), aldosteronoma (after AVS, adrenal vein sampling), malignant features, >4 cm in fit patients, significant growth. Laparoscopic for benign; open for suspected ACC.
- **MACS:** screen for hypertension, diabetes, osteoporosis; surgery individualised.
- **Follow-up (ESE–ENSAT 2023, European Society of Endocrinology–European Network for the Study of Adrenal Tumors):** homogeneous ≤10 HU, non-functioning → no further imaging. Indeterminate non-resected → re-image at 6–12 months; growth >20% and ≥5 mm is concerning.
- **Bilateral:** metastases, lymphoma, tuberculosis, haemorrhage, CAH (congenital adrenal hyperplasia; 17-hydroxyprogesterone); exclude adrenal insufficiency.

**Diagram to draw:** the algorithm.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ≤10 HU = lipid-rich adenoma; APW >60%, RPW >40%.
> - 1-mg DST >1.8 µg/dL = MACS.
> - Exclude phaeochromocytoma before biopsy or surgery.
> - Surgery: functioning, suspicious, >4 cm or growing.

## MIBG scan
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3c

### Principle
- MIBG (meta-iodobenzylguanidine) is a guanethidine analogue that resembles **noradrenaline**.
- It enters chromaffin and sympathetic cells through the **noradrenaline transporter** and is stored in **adrenergic secretory vesicles**. So it maps catecholamine-handling tissue: function, not just anatomy.

### Isotopes
| | ¹²³I-MIBG | ¹³¹I-MIBG |
|---|---|---|
| Use | **Diagnostic** (preferred) | Older diagnostic; mainly **therapeutic** |
| Image quality | Better; allows SPECT-CT (single-photon emission computed tomography) | Poorer |
| Emission | Gamma | Beta (therapy) plus gamma |

### Technique
- **Thyroid blockade** with potassium iodide before and after injection (free iodine).
- **Stop interfering drugs:** labetalol, tricyclic antidepressants, sympathomimetics, reserpine, some calcium channel blockers.
- Intravenous injection; planar and SPECT-CT images at about 24 hours.
- **Normal uptake:** salivary glands, liver, heart, bladder, thyroid if unblocked; normal adrenals faint.

### Uses
**1. Phaeochromocytoma and paraganglioma**
- **Extra-adrenal, multiple, metastatic or recurrent disease**; biochemistry positive but imaging negative.
- **Isolated normetanephrine excess** may indicate an extra-adrenal tumour; MIBG finds it.
- Tumour >5 cm: MIBG or FDG (fluorodeoxyglucose) PET (positron emission tomography) to exclude metastases before surgery.
- **Best in MEN-2 (multiple endocrine neoplasia type 2).** Raised metanephrines after surgery: MIBG can unmask metastases.

**2. Neuroblastoma**
- ¹²³I-MIBG is **required by COG (Children's Oncology Group) for staging**, replacing skeletal survey and bone scan. More sensitive for relapse. Non-avid tumours: PET.

**3. Therapy (¹³¹I-MIBG)**
- Confirm uptake first. Metastatic phaeochromocytoma: symptom response in up to two-thirds; complete response <5%. High-specific-activity form FDA (Food and Drug Administration)-approved 2018.
- Relapsed or refractory neuroblastoma: response about 28–39%.

### Limitations
- **Cold scans in SDHB (succinate dehydrogenase subunit B) and VHL (von Hippel–Lindau)**; aggressive metastases lose uptake.
- **FDG PET is now gold standard** for staging, and **⁶⁸Ga-DOTATATE PET/CT** is replacing it at some centres.
- **Also know:** carcinoid and medullary thyroid cancer imaging; cardiac sympathetic imaging.

**Diagram to draw:** whole-body MIBG showing an adrenal hot spot with normal liver, heart and bladder uptake.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Noradrenaline analogue; block thyroid; stop labetalol.
> - ¹²³I diagnostic, ¹³¹I therapeutic.
> - Best for extra-adrenal, metastatic and MEN-2 tumours; cold in SDHB and VHL.

## Adrenal Sx.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 2, Q3d
(preoperative phaeochromocytoma management also in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)

*Assumed meaning: surgery of the adrenal gland (adrenalectomy).*

### Surgical anatomy
- Right: pyramidal; **short vein into posterior IVC (inferior vena cava)**, so it is the riskier side. Left: crescentic; long vein into left renal vein. Three arteries (inferior phrenic, aorta, renal), one vein.

### Indications (AAES [American Association of Endocrine Surgeons] 2022)
- **Functional, any size:** aldosteronoma (Conn; after AVS [adrenal venous sampling]), cortisol-secreting adenoma, phaeochromocytoma, sex-hormone tumour, failed pituitary surgery for Cushing disease.
- **Malignant potential:** ≥4 cm; growth; >10 HU (Hounsfield units) unenhanced; washout <50%; suspected ACC (adrenocortical carcinoma); solitary metastasis.
- Myelolipoma >7 cm or symptomatic; cysts.
- **Contraindications (correct first):** uncontrolled hypertension, coagulopathy, peritonitis (transperitoneal); pregnancy relative.

### Preoperative preparation
- Biochemistry: metanephrines in **every** case before surgery.
- **Phaeochromocytoma:** α-blockade 2 weeks (phenoxybenzamine or doxazosin), β-blocker only after α, salt and fluids.
- Cushing: hydrocortisone 100 mg IV on the day of surgery. Conn: correct potassium.

### Approaches
| Approach | Use |
|---|---|
| **Laparoscopic transperitoneal** (gold standard since 1992) | Familiar view, larger tumours |
| **Retroperitoneoscopic** (lateral or prone) | Prior abdominal surgery, small tumours |
| Robotic | Either route |
| **Open** (subcostal, chevron, thoracoabdominal, flank) | **ACC**, large or invasive tumours |

### Steps (laparoscopic transperitoneal)
- **Right:** elevate liver → Kocher manoeuvre → follow IVC → **control adrenal vein early** (warn anaesthetist) → free gland to psoas → bag intact.
- **Left:** reflect colon and spleen → follow left renal vein to adrenal vein → clip → free from pancreas and splenic vessels.
- Kidney attachment last (handle). ACC: open en bloc, no rupture.
- **Partial adrenalectomy:** bilateral or hereditary phaeochromocytoma, solitary gland.

### Complications
- Bleeding (adrenal vein avulsion, IVC); spleen, pancreatic tail, liver, bowel injury; pneumothorax.
- **Phaeochromocytoma:** surges during handling, **hypotension after vein ligation**, rebound hypoglycaemia.
- **Adrenal insufficiency** after Cushing surgery (~99.7%): steroid taper.
- Conn: **hyperkalaemia** for weeks (high-salt diet).

**Diagram to draw:** right and left adrenal veins and relations.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Function (any size) or malignancy risk (≥4 cm, >10 HU) indicates surgery.
> - Control the vein early; right vein short into IVC.
> - Open en bloc surgery for ACC.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 14 · Adrenal & Retroperitoneum

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### How will you evaluate and manage 38 yr male with uncontrolled hypertension (LAQ)

- Hypertension below 40 years: screen for secondary causes.
- ARR → saline suppression → CT → AVS (skip if <40 with adenoma >1 cm).
- Metanephrines for phaeochromocytoma; α before β.
- FMD: angioplasty; atherosclerotic RAS: medical (ASTRAL, CORAL).

#### Phaeochromocytoma: overview, pre-operative and intra-operative management (SAQ)

- Metanephrines first; never biopsy.
- α-blockade 7–14 days for all.
- Never β before α (α before β), plus volume expansion.
- Volume expansion prevents hypotension; watch glucose.
- Arterial line before induction; avoid histamine releasers and ephedrine.
- Surge: pause handling, nitroprusside or phentolamine; esmolol for tachycardia.
- After vein ligation: fluids, vasopressors, glucose.
- One-third hereditary; SDHB most often metastatic.

#### Cushing syndrome and the dexamethasone suppression test (SAQ)

- Exclude exogenous steroids; 1-mg DST cortisol >1.8 µg/dL abnormal.
- 1 mg at 11 pm; 8 am cortisol ≤1.8 µg/dL excludes; >5.0 µg/dL confirms autonomy.
- Oral contraceptives and enzyme inducers cause false positives.
- ACTH low = adrenal; high = pituitary/ectopic (IPSS); ACTH separates adrenal from pituitary/ectopic.
- High-dose DST no longer routine.
- Steroid cover after adrenalectomy; Nelson syndrome after bilateral.

#### Algorithm for management of an incidentaloma (SAQ)

- ≤10 HU = lipid-rich adenoma; APW >60%, RPW >40%.
- 1-mg DST >1.8 µg/dL = MACS.
- Exclude phaeochromocytoma before biopsy or surgery.
- Surgery: functioning, suspicious, >4 cm or growing.

#### MIBG scan (SAQ)

- Noradrenaline analogue; block thyroid; stop labetalol.
- ¹²³I diagnostic, ¹³¹I therapeutic.
- Best for extra-adrenal, metastatic and MEN-2 tumours; cold in SDHB and VHL.

#### Adrenal Sx. (SAQ)

- Function (any size) or malignancy risk (≥4 cm, >10 HU) indicates surgery.
- Control the vein early; right vein short into IVC.
- Open en bloc surgery for ACC.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Haemodialysis principles, dialysis techniques and dialysis access (vascular and PD)
**Asked as:**
- Discuss principles of hemodialysis and vasular access in details. — *Winter 2017 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Vascular access for hemodialysis — *Winter 2025 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Hemodialysis access. — *Practice Set 10 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Access techniques in dialysis — *Practice Set 12 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- Discuss techniques of dialysis — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

(Winter 2025 Paper II Q3c: also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3b)
(Practice Set 10 Paper II Q3c: also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3b and 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3c)
(Practice Set 12 Paper II Q3f: CAPD also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3a)
(Practice Set 5 Paper II Q3e: HD principles also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q1; CAPD in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)

*Assumed meaning (Winter 2017): "vasular" is a typo for "vascular" access for haemodialysis.*
*Assumed meaning (Practice Set 12): access for PD (peritoneal dialysis) and HD (haemodialysis); emphasis on PD catheter insertion, since HD vascular access is answered in full in Practice Set 12 Paper 2, Q3a.*
*Assumed meaning (Practice Set 5): principles and techniques of HD (haemodialysis) and PD (peritoneal dialysis), with continuous therapies.*

### Introduction
- Haemodialysis (HD) removes solutes and water from blood across a semipermeable membrane in an extracorporeal circuit. It is the commonest KRT (kidney replacement therapy) for ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) and severe AKI (acute kidney injury). It needs an access giving about **250–350 mL/min** of blood flow.
- Access failure is a major cause of morbidity in ESKD (end-stage kidney disease).

### Principles of haemodialysis (physical principles)
| Process | Driving force | What it removes |
|---|---|---|
| **Diffusion** | Concentration gradient across the membrane | Small solutes (urea, creatinine, potassium); bicarbonate enters blood |
| **Convection** | Transmembrane pressure; solute is dragged with water | Middle molecules (e.g. β2-microglobulin) |
| **Ultrafiltration** | Transmembrane (hydrostatic) pressure (HD) or osmotic (PD) gradient | Excess water, down to "dry weight" |

- **Countercurrent flow:** dialysate runs opposite to blood (**countercurrent**), keeping (to keep) a gradient along the whole dialyser. Clearance rises with flows, membrane area and time.
- **Dialyser:** blood (250–350 mL/min) passes through hollow fibres (hollow-fibre) of **synthetic membrane**; high-flux membranes clear more middle molecules.
- **Dialysate:** ultrapure (reverse-osmosis) water with sodium about 140 mmol/L, potassium about 2 mmol/L, bicarbonate and calcium, adjusted to the patient.
- **Anticoagulation:** heparin; heparin-free or regional citrate if bleeding risk is high (e.g. after surgery).
- **Schedule:** about 4 h (hours) three times (3 times) a week. **Also know:** haemofiltration, haemodiafiltration (HDF), CRRT (continuous renal replacement therapy).
- **Access:** AVF (arteriovenous fistula), AVG (arteriovenous graft) or CVC (central venous catheter).

### Adequacy
- **Kt/V** (dialyser urea clearance × time ÷ urea distribution volume): target 1.4, minimum 1.2 (Kt/V ≥1.2) per thrice-weekly session (KDOQI [Kidney Disease Outcomes Quality Initiative] 2015). **URR (urea reduction ratio) ≥65%.**

### Indications
- **Acute ("AEIOU"), refractory to medical treatment:** Acidosis; Electrolytes (hyperkalaemia); Ingestions (lithium, salicylates, alcohols); Overload with respiratory distress; Uraemia (encephalopathy, pericarditis, bleeding).
- **Chronic:** symptomatic ESKD, usually at eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) 5–10; IDEAL (2010): early start gave no benefit.

### Complications of haemodialysis
- **During dialysis:** hypotension (commonest), cramps, arrhythmia, air embolism, bleeding. **Disequilibrium syndrome:** rapid urea removal in early sessions shifts water into the brain (cerebral oedema); prevent with short, gentle first sessions.
- **Long term:** cardiovascular disease (main cause of death), mineral–bone disorder, calciphylaxis, β2-microglobulin amyloidosis (carpal tunnel), infection, hepatitis B and C; acquired cystic kidney disease (about 80% in ESKD; RCC [renal cell carcinoma] in 1–2% of these).
- **Access-related:** access infection, thrombosis, steal.

### Peritoneal dialysis
- The **peritoneum** is the membrane: diffusion for solutes; **glucose or icodextrin** draws water osmotically.
- **Tenckhoff catheter** (two Dacron cuffs), placed open, laparoscopically (omentopexy) or percutaneously (see PD access below).
- **CAPD (continuous ambulatory PD):** about 4 exchanges of 2 L daily. **APD (automated PD):** night cycler.
- **Adequacy:** weekly Kt/V ≥1.7; PET (peritoneal equilibration test) classifies transport.
- **Complications:** peritonitis, exit-site infection, catheter malfunction, hernia, hyperglycaemia, peritoneal fibrosis.

### Continuous and hybrid therapies
- **CRRT (continuous renal replacement therapy):** CVVH (continuous veno-venous haemofiltration; convection), CVVHD (haemodialysis; diffusion), CVVHDF (both). For haemodynamically unstable AKI (acute kidney injury).
- **Also know:** SLED (sustained low-efficiency dialysis); HDF (haemodiafiltration: CONVINCE 2023 lowered mortality versus high-flux HD); home and nocturnal HD.

### HD versus PD
| | HD | PD |
|---|---|---|
| Setting | Centre or home | Home, continuous |
| Haemodynamics | Rapid shifts | Gentle; preserves residual function |
| Favoured in | Abdominal surgery, failed PD | Children, cardiac instability, no vascular access |

### Types of vascular access
| Access | Construction | Ready | Comment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Radiocephalic AVF** (arteriovenous fistula; Brescia–Cimino, 1966) | Radial artery to cephalic vein at the wrist, end-to-side (now usually vein end to artery side) | ≥6–8 weeks | **First choice**: best patency, fewest infections; highest failure to mature |
| Brachiocephalic AVF | Brachial artery to cephalic vein at the elbow | ≥6–8 weeks | Higher flow; more steal |
| Brachiobasilic (transposition) AVF | Deep basilic vein transposed superficially | Longer | When the cephalic vein is unusable |
| **AVG** (arteriovenous graft) | PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene), forearm loop or upper-arm straight | 2–3 weeks | More stenosis, thrombosis, infection |
| **Tunnelled cuffed CVC** (central venous catheter) | Right internal jugular; cuff blocks bacterial migration | At once | Bridge or last resort; highest infection and central stenosis |

- Non-tunnelled 10–12 Fr jugular or femoral catheters are for acute dialysis only.

### Planning the access
- **Hierarchy:** AVF, then AVG, then CVC; **distal before proximal; non-dominant arm first** (US **Fistula First**, 2003). **KDOQI 2019** asks for (favours) an individual "ESKD Life-Plan": the right access for the right patient at the right time.
- **Timing:** refer at eGFR <30; create an AVF at least (about) **6 months** before dialysis is expected.
- **Vein preservation:** no venepuncture, cannula or PICC (peripherally inserted central catheter) in the non-dominant arm (that arm); avoid subclavian catheters (lines).
- **Assessment:** pulses, **Allen test** (ulnar collateral flow), **Doppler vein mapping** (vessels about ≥2 mm), venography if central stenosis is suspected.
- **Maturity, "rule of 6s":** flow >600 mL/min, diameter >6 mm, depth <6 mm, by (at) 6 weeks.

### Access complications
| Complication | Key point | Treatment |
|---|---|---|
| Failure to mature | Juxta-anastomotic stenosis; side veins | Angioplasty; ligate (side) branches |
| Stenosis | Neointimal hyperplasia; AVG at venous anastomosis | PTA (percutaneous transluminal angioplasty), stent graft, revision |
| **Thrombosis** | Usually on an underlying stenosis | Thrombectomy, then treat (fix) the stenosis |
| **Steal syndrome** | Hand ischaemia; diabetes, brachial-based access | Banding, DRIL (distal revascularisation–interval ligation), ligation |
| Aneurysm | Repeated needling at one site | Repair if skin is thin |
| Infection | CVC > AVG > AVF | Antibiotics; excise graft; remove catheter |

- **Also know:** brachiobasilic transposition AVF; venous hypertension (central stenosis); high-output cardiac failure (banding); ischaemic monomelic neuropathy; graft seroma.

### Monitoring
- Each session: thrill and bruit, arm-elevation test (a healthy fistula collapses), prolonged bleeding after needling, rising venous pressure, falling Kt/V. Duplex scan when abnormal; treat a stenosis causing clinical change. Rotate needle sites ("rope-ladder").

### Peritoneal dialysis access
**Catheter:** **Tenckhoff** silicone catheter with **two Dacron cuffs**. The deep cuff sits in the rectus muscle; the superficial cuff about 2–4 cm from the exit. The tip lies in the **pelvis (rectovesical or rectouterine pouch)**; the exit site points **downwards** to reduce infection.

**Insertion techniques**
| Technique | Steps | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **Open (mini-laparotomy)** | Paramedian incision → split rectus → open posterior sheath and peritoneum → catheter to pelvis on a stylet → purse-string round deep cuff → tunnel → exit site | Simple; general or local anaesthesia |
| **Laparoscopic (advanced)** | Rectus sheath tunnel; **omentopexy**, adhesiolysis, hernia repair as needed | Best function; fixes causes of outflow failure |
| **Percutaneous (Seldinger)** | Needle, guidewire, dilator and peel-away sheath under local anaesthesia, ± ultrasound or fluoroscopy | Bedside; avoid after major abdominal surgery |
| Peritoneoscopic | Small-scope guided placement | Variant of percutaneous |
- **Break-in:** delay full-volume exchanges about 2 weeks to prevent leak; urgent start uses small supine volumes.
- **Complications:** outflow failure (constipation, omental wrap, migration), leak, hernia, exit-site or tunnel infection, peritonitis, bowel perforation.
- **Contraindications:** peritoneal scarring, uncorrectable hernia, inability to perform exchanges.

### Haemodialysis catheter access (brief, insertion)
- **Non-tunnelled CVC (central venous catheter):** dual-lumen 10–12 Fr, internal jugular or femoral vein by **ultrasound-guided Seldinger**; for AKI (acute kidney injury); avoid subclavian (central stenosis).
- **Tunnelled cuffed CVC:** right internal jugular, tip at the cavoatrial junction under fluoroscopy; bridge to AVF.
- **AVF (arteriovenous fistula)** first choice, then **AVG (arteriovenous graft)**; HeRO and endovascular AVF in selected patients (see Practice Set 12 Paper 2, Q3a).

### Recent advances
- **Endovascular AVF** (percutaneous creation: WavelinQ, Ellipsys); drug-coated balloons; bioengineered vessels; home and nocturnal HD.
- **CONVINCE (2023):** high-dose HDF lowered all-cause mortality compared with high-flux HD.

**Diagram to draw:** dialyser with countercurrent blood and dialysate (beside a PD catheter in the pelvis), and a radiocephalic end-to-side fistula at the wrist; Tenckhoff catheter with two cuffs, tunnel, downward exit and pelvic tip.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Diffusion clears small solutes, convection middle molecules, ultrafiltration water.
> - HD Kt/V minimum 1.2 (target 1.4); URR ≥65%; PD weekly Kt/V ≥1.7.
> - AVF first, distal, non-dominant arm, ≥6 months ahead; rule of 6s: >600 mL/min, >6 mm, <6 mm deep, 6 weeks.
> - Thrombosis usually sits on a stenosis; steal is treated by (→) banding or DRIL.
> - Tenckhoff: two cuffs, pelvic tip, downward exit.
> - Laparoscopic placement with omentopexy gives best function.
> - Avoid subclavian lines; AVF first for long-term HD.
> - CRRT for unstable AKI; PD preferred in children.

## Deceased (cadaver) kidney donor: selection, identification, maintenance, distribution and retrieval
**Asked as:**
- Describe selection criteria for cadaver renal donor. Describe in details kidney retrieval surgery. — *Summer 2021 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe procedure of identification, maintenance, distribution and retrieval of kidney in a 25 yr old brain dead pt — *Practice Set 3 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(Marginal donor in 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3f; brain death certification in 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3c.)
(also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 2, Q2 and 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3b)

*Assumed meaning: the Indian deceased-donor pathway: identification and brain-stem death certification, donor maintenance, allocation and retrieval.*

### Introduction
- A cadaver (deceased) donor is **DBD** (donation after brain death: circulation maintained until cold perfusion) or **DCD** (donation after circulatory death: warm ischaemia).
- A 25-year-old brain-dead patient is an ideal **standard-criteria DBD (donation after brain death)** donor: circulation continues until cold perfusion, so warm ischaemia is minimal.
- India: **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994**, amended 2011, **Rules 2014**, recognises brain-stem death (makes brain-stem death legal death); **NOTTO** (National Organ and Tissue Transplant Organisation) allocates organs.

### 1. Identification and selection criteria
**Potential donor:** ventilated patient with catastrophic brain injury (head injury, intracranial bleed, hypoxia). ICU (intensive care unit) informs the **transplant coordinator**; **mandatory request** to the family.

**Donor evaluation**
- Cause of death, medical and social (high-risk behaviour) history; kidney: terminal creatinine, urine output, hypertension, diabetes, ultrasound.
- Laboratory: ABO and HLA (human leucocyte antigen) typing; serology and NAT (nucleic acid testing) for HIV (human immunodeficiency virus), hepatitis B and C, CMV (cytomegalovirus), EBV (Epstein–Barr virus), syphilis, toxoplasma.

**Absolute contraindications**
- Active or metastatic cancer, haematological malignancy, history of (past) melanoma, untreatable viral infection.
- Treated bacterial infection (even bacterial meningitis) is acceptable; viral encephalitis needs care.

**Standard versus marginal donor**

| Category | Definition |
|---|---|
| **ECD (expanded criteria donor), UNOS (United Network for Organ Sharing)** | Age ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of: hypertension, creatinine >1.5 mg/dL, death from CVA (cerebrovascular accident) |
| **KDPI (Kidney Donor Profile Index)** | Replaced ECD in the US (2014); >85% = marginal; longevity matching with EPTS (Estimated Post-Transplant Survival) |
| Paediatric <15 kg | En bloc transplant |

- **Remuzzi biopsy score** for marginal kidneys: 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard.

### 2. Legal death and consent: certification of brain-stem death (THOA)
- **DBD preconditions:** apnoeic coma with a known irreversible cause; exclude drugs (sedatives, relaxants), hypothermia, metabolic causes and shock.
- **Board of four (4-member Board):** doctor in charge of the hospital (hospital in-charge); independent specialist from an approved panel; neurologist or neurosurgeon (else a nominated surgeon, physician, anaesthetist or intensivist); treating doctor. **None from the transplant team.**

All brainstem reflexes absent:

| Brainstem reflex (all absent) | Method | Cranial nerves |
|---|---|---|
| Pupillary | Bright light: pupils fixed, no reaction | II, III |
| Corneal | Touch cornea: no blink | V, VII |
| Oculocephalic (doll's eye) | Turn head: eyes move with the head | VIII, III, VI |
| Oculovestibular (caloric) | Ice-cold water into each ear (drum seen): no eye movement | VIII, III, VI |
| Motor response in cranial nerve area (cranial motor response) | Supraorbital pressure: no grimace | V, VII |
| Gag and cough | Touch pharynx; suction catheter in trachea: no response | IX, X |

- **Apnoea test (last):** pre-oxygenate with 100% oxygen; disconnect the ventilator with oxygen insufflation; **no breathing effort at PaCO₂ (arterial carbon dioxide tension) ≥60 mmHg**. Abort for hypotension, desaturation or arrhythmia.
- **Two sets of tests at least 6 hours apart**; **Form 10**. Ancillary tests (angiography, perfusion scan) only if examination is impossible.
- **DCD (Maastricht):** I dead on arrival; II failed resuscitation (both uncontrolled); **III arrest after withdrawal of support (controlled, commonest)**; IV arrest in a brain-dead donor; V euthanasia.
- **Consent:** next-of-kin (near relative) consent after a **mandatory request** by ICU staff; in medico-legal cases inform police.

### 3. Maintenance (donor management)
- **Why:** herniation causes a **catecholamine storm**, then loss of sympathetic tone (hypotension), **diabetes insipidus (DI)**, hypothermia, DIC (disseminated intravascular coagulation), low thyroid hormone and cortisol, and pulmonary oedema.
- **"Rule of 100s":** systolic pressure >100 mmHg, urine >100 mL/h, PaO₂ (arterial oxygen tension) >100 mmHg, haemoglobin >100 g/L.
- **Measures:** fluids guided by CVP (central venous pressure), no starch colloids (nephrotoxic); **vasopressin** first (pressor and treats DI), then noradrenaline; methylprednisolone 15 mg/kg, thyroid hormone, insulin; lung-protective ventilation; warming; correct sodium, potassium and clotting.

### 4. Distribution (allocation)
- **NOTTO (National Organ and Tissue Transplant Organisation)**, New Delhi, with **ROTTOs** (regional) and **SOTTOs** (state organisations), keeps registries and waiting lists.
- **Sequence:** hospital and state list → region → national.
- **Kidney criteria:** ABO compatibility, waiting time on dialysis, HLA match, priority for children and previous living donors, **negative crossmatch** (donor spleen or nodes). Many states let the retrieving hospital keep one kidney.
- 2023 policy removed the domicile requirement and upper age limit for registration.

### 5. Kidney retrieval surgery (multi-organ, DBD)
1. **Checks:** check (death) certificate, consent, blood group; coordinate thoracic, liver, pancreas and kidney teams.
2. **Incision:** midline xiphoid-to-pubis with median sternotomy.
3. **Exposure:** mobilise right colon and small-bowel mesentery off the retroperitoneum (Cattell–Braasch manoeuvre); expose aorta and IVC (inferior vena cava).
4. **Prepare cannulation:** encircle the distal aorta above the bifurcation; ligate the inferior mesenteric artery; prepare the supracoeliac aorta for clamping. **Look for aberrant renal arteries**; keep accessory arteries from the iliacs.
5. **Cold perfusion:** systemic heparin; cannulate the distal aorta; clamp the supracoeliac aorta; **vent** the IVC (or right atrium); flush cold UW (University of Wisconsin) or HTK (histidine–tryptophan–ketoglutarate) solution; crushed ice; open Gerota's fascia laterally to cool the kidneys.
6. **Remove heart, lungs, liver and pancreas first.**
7. **Kidneys en bloc:** mobilise (lift) both with perinephric fat off the posterior wall; keep periureteric tissue; divide ureters low. Divide aorta and IVC (upper limit set by the liver team, lower at the bifurcation); lift everything anteriorly and divide prevertebral tissue.
8. **Extras:** iliac vessels for reconstruction; spleen and lymph nodes for crossmatch.

**DCD:** after death and a no-touch period, **super-rapid** laparotomy, direct aortic cannulation and IVC venting limit warm ischaemia; kidneys usually go on machine perfusion.

### 6. Back-bench preparation
- Open the aorta posteriorly to see the renal ostia and divide it between them (**Carrel patches**); keep the IVC with the right kidney to lengthen the short right renal vein (its vein).
- Keep an aortic **Carrel patch**; ligate tributaries and lymphatics (prevents lymphocele); trim fat without decapsulation; spare the lower pole–ureter triangle.
- Document anatomy and injuries; biopsy marginal kidneys; triple-bag in cold solution on ice; label.

### 7. Preservation
- Cooling to 4 °C slows metabolism. **Hypothermic machine perfusion** reduces DGF (delayed graft function) versus static cold storage (Moers, 2009), not long-term survival (graft loss). Keep cold ischaemia short (thrombosis risk >24 hours; cold ischaemia >24 hours raises thrombosis risk).

- **Pitfalls:** polar artery or intimal injury, short right vein, ureteric devascularisation, capsular tears.
- **Also know:** HCV (hepatitis C virus)-viraemic donors with antivirals; normothermic perfusion (experimental).

**Diagram to draw:** aorta and IVC with distal aortic cannula, supracoeliac clamp, IVC vent and en bloc kidneys.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - THOA: 4-member Board (Board of four), none from the transplant team; two tests ≥6 h apart; Form 10.
> - Apnoea test: no breathing at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.
> - ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death; KDPI >85%.
> - Rule of 100s; vasopressin for hypotension and DI.
> - Distal aortic cannula, supracoeliac clamp, IVC vent, cold UW/HTK and ice.
> - Kidneys en bloc last (kidneys out last); Carrel patch, IVC for right vein; machine perfusion reduces DGF.

## Renovascular hypertension and ischaemic nephropathy: renal vascular anatomy, pathophysiology, evaluation and treatment
**Asked as:**
- Pathophysiology of renovascular hypertension (RVH) and iscemic nephropathy. Describe treatment of RVH in detail. — *Summer 2022 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Discuss evaluation of & management of reno-vascular hypertension — *Practice Set 2 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Anat. of Renal Vasculature + Dx modalities & Rx optns for Renovasc. HTN. — *Practice Set 4 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q2 and Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q1)

*"Iscemic" is read as ischaemic nephropathy.*

### Part A: Anatomy of the renal vasculature
**Renal arteries**
- Arise from the aorta at the **L1–L2 disc**. The longer **right artery passes behind the IVC (inferior vena cava)**. Branches supply the adrenal, renal pelvis and proximal ureter.
- **Hilum, anterior to posterior:** renal vein, renal artery, pelvis; the posterior segmental artery lies behind the pelvis.
- **Anterior division** gives four segmental branches (apical, upper, middle, lower). **Posterior division** gives the posterior segmental artery: the first, most constant branch, passing behind the pelvis. If it crosses in front of the ureter, it can cause UPJO (ureteropelvic junction obstruction).
- **Five segmental arteries are end arteries** with no significant anastomosis. Occlusion causes segmental infarction; each segment can be resected separately.
- **Sequence:** segmental → lobar → interlobar (columns of Bertin) → arcuate (corticomedullary junction) → interlobular → afferent arteriole → glomerulus → efferent arteriole → peritubular capillaries or vasa recta.
- **Brödel line:** avascular plane between anterior and posterior territories, just behind the lateral border. Used for anatrophic nephrolithotomy and posterior calyceal access.
- **Variations (25–40%):** accessory arteries in 25–28% (T11–L4, rarely iliac or SMA [superior mesenteric artery]). They are end arteries, usually to the lower pole. Relevant to donor nephrectomy and endopyelotomy.

**Renal veins**
- Veins **anastomose freely** (venous collars, subcapsular plexus), so segmental vein ligation is safe. 3–5 segmental veins form the renal vein, anterior to the artery.

| | Right renal vein | Left renal vein |
|---|---|---|
| Length | 2–4 cm | 6–10 cm |
| Tributaries | None | Left adrenal, left gonadal, lumbar |
| Course | Direct to IVC | Between SMA and aorta (nutcracker syndrome) |
| Anomalies | Accessory veins commoner | Circumaortic (2–16%); retroaortic |

**Relevance to RVH (renovascular hypertension):** atheroma affects the **ostium and proximal third**; **FMD (fibromuscular dysplasia)** affects the **mid-distal artery and branches**; a stenosed accessory artery can cause segmental RVH.

### Definitions
- **RVH (renovascular hypertension) — definition:** hypertension caused by RAS (renal artery stenosis) that improves after (is relieved by) revascularisation or nephrectomy. RAS is common in older adults; RVH is proved only by cure.
- **Ischaemic nephropathy:** loss of GFR (glomerular filtration rate) from haemodynamically significant RAS affecting the **whole renal mass** (bilateral RAS or stenosis to a solitary kidney).

### Causes
| | Atherosclerotic RAS | FMD (fibromuscular dysplasia) |
|---|---|---|
| Share | Most (about 90%) | Most of the rest |
| Patient | Older, smokers (smoker), diabetic, vascular disease | Young women |
| Site | **Ostium and proximal third** (aortic plaque) | **Mid and distal artery, branches**; "string of beads" (medial fibroplasia, commonest type) |
| Course | Progresses to occlusion; renal function lost | Rarely occludes |
| Treatment | Medical first | **Balloon angioplasty without stent**; good BP (blood pressure) response |

**Also know:** Takayasu arteritis, neurofibromatosis, midaortic syndrome (children), renal artery aneurysm, dissection, embolism, Page kidney, transplant RAS.

### Pathophysiology (mechanism) of RVH: Goldblatt models (basis of the tests)
- Goldblatt (1934) clipped the canine renal artery and produced hypertension. Loop: low perfusion pressure releases **juxtaglomerular renin** → angiotensin II (vasoconstriction) → aldosterone (sodium retention).

| | **Two-kidney, one-clip** (unilateral RAS) | **One-kidney, one-clip** (solitary kidney or bilateral RAS) |
|---|---|---|
| Sodium | Normal contralateral kidney excretes the extra sodium (pressure natriuresis) | No normal kidney: sodium and water retained |
| Renin | **High** from stenotic side; contralateral suppressed | Rises, then returns to normal as volume expands |
| Type | **Renin (angiotensin)-dependent** | **Volume-dependent** |
| ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitor | Lowers BP (ACE inhibitors lower BP); captopril test positive | Less BP effect; ACE inhibitors may cause **acute renal failure** |

- **Phases:** renin-dependent → sodium retention → **chronic phase** (later): hypertension damages the contralateral kidney (nephrosclerosis), BP no longer depends on the stenosis, and **revascularisation cures less** (cure after revascularisation falls with duration).
- Also: sympathetic activation, oxidative stress, endothelin, less nitric oxide.

### Pathophysiology of ischaemic nephropathy
- The kidney receives about **25% of cardiac output**, far more than its metabolic need, and **autoregulation** holds GFR until stenosis is severe (about **70% or more**, or a large translesional gradient).
- Beyond this, the stenotic kidney keeps its GFR by angiotensin II **efferent constriction**; blocking it (ACE inhibitor or ARB [angiotensin receptor blocker]) drops GFR.
- Chronic hypoperfusion → hypoxia, inflammation, microvascular rarefaction → **tubulointerstitial fibrosis** and atrophy. Atheroemboli and hypertensive damage add injury. Early disease is reversible ("hibernating" kidney); fibrosis is not.

### Part B: Diagnosis (evaluation; investigations)
**1. Clinical clues (who to test)**
- Onset <30 years (below 30 years) (FMD) or severe hypertension after 55 years; **resistant** (≥3 drugs) or accelerated hypertension; sudden worsening of controlled BP.
- **Creatinine rise >30% after ACE inhibitor/ARB**; **flash pulmonary oedema**; kidney size difference >1.5 cm (kidneys differing by more than 1.5 cm); abdominal bruit; hypokalaemia.

**2. Basic tests:** creatinine and eGFR (estimated GFR), potassium, urinalysis and proteinuria, ECG; exclude primary aldosteronism and phaeochromocytoma.

**3. Imaging and functional tests**
| Test | Method and finding | Comment |
|---|---|---|
| **Duplex Doppler US (ultrasound)** | High PSV (peak systolic velocity), above about 180–200 cm/s; renal/aortic velocity ratio >3.5; **RI (resistive index) = (PSV − EDV)/PSV** | First line, no contrast; operator dependent. **RI ≥0.8 (>0.8) predicts poor response** (parenchymal disease) |
| **CT angiography** | Stenosis, ostial plaque, beads | Best non-invasive anatomy; contrast nephropathy |
| **MR (magnetic resonance) angiography** | Gadolinium-enhanced | Caution if eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) <30 mL/min |
| **Captopril renography (DTPA, diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid)** | Day 1 after oral captopril: slow (delayed) uptake, low peak; repeat off ACE inhibitor and calcium channel blocker. Improvement of 10% or more (≥10%) = high probability RVH | Delayed uptake after captopril indicates renin-dependence; captopril removes efferent constriction; DTPA is cleared purely by filtration; use declining |
| **Catheter angiography** | Gold standard; **translesional (pressure) gradient** (systolic ≥20 or mean ≥10 mmHg = significant) | Done when intervention is planned |
| Renal vein renin | Ratio >1.5 lateralises | Before nephrectomy |

**Algorithm**
```
Clinical clue -> Duplex US (or CTA/MRA if eGFR allows)
  -> no RAS: treat as essential hypertension
  -> RAS: FMD -> angioplasty
          Atherosclerotic -> optimal medical therapy
             -> high-risk features + viable kidney -> angiography + gradient -> stent
```

### Part C: Treatment (management)
**1. Medical (first line for atherosclerotic RAS)**
- **ACE inhibitor or ARB** (controls renin-dependent BP; check creatinine and potassium in 1–2 weeks (checked at 1–2 weeks)), calcium channel blockers, diuretics.
- **Statin, antiplatelet, smoking cessation (stop smoking), diabetes control**: these patients die mainly of cardiovascular disease (most deaths are cardiovascular).

**2. Landmark trials (atherosclerotic RAS)**
- **ASTRAL (2009):** stenting plus medical therapy versus medical therapy alone gave **no benefit** in renal function, BP, events or mortality (death); procedural complications.
- **CORAL (2014):** stenting versus optimal medical therapy did **not reduce** (**no reduction**) in cardiovascular or renal events; only a small BP fall.
- ASTRAL and CORAL: stenting added **no benefit** over medical therapy. Hence **no routine revascularisation** of atherosclerotic RAS (ESC [European Society of Cardiology] 2017).

**3. Indications for revascularisation**
- **FMD with hypertension** (first choice).
- Atherosclerotic RAS with **flash pulmonary oedema** or recurrent unexplained heart failure, **rapidly declining function** with bilateral or solitary-kidney RAS, or truly resistant hypertension.
- Kidney must be viable (not small and atrophic; RI <0.8).

**4. Endovascular**
- **PTRA (percutaneous transluminal renal angioplasty) alone for FMD**; stent only for dissection or failure.
- **Ostial atherosclerotic lesion (ostial atheroma) → angioplasty plus stent**, because a balloon alone fails from elastic recoil of aortic plaque. Restenosis is the main late problem.
- Complications: contrast nephropathy, **atheroembolism**, dissection, rupture, thrombosis, access haematoma.

**5. Surgical revascularisation**
- Failed endovascular therapy, **branch disease or aneurysm**, children, coexisting aortic surgery.

| Technique | Use |
|---|---|
| **Aortorenal bypass** (saphenous vein; internal iliac artery in children; PTFE [polytetrafluoroethylene]) | Standard |
| **Transaortic endarterectomy** | Bilateral ostial plaque |
| **Splenorenal (left) / hepatorenal (right) bypass** | Hostile (diseased) aorta |
| **Ex vivo bench repair + autotransplantation** | Branch FMD, aneurysms |
| Nephrectomy (partial or total) | Small non-functioning kidney with lateralising renin |

**6. Transplant RAS:** raised velocity on Doppler, contrast imaging confirms; angioplasty or stent first, surgery if this fails.

### Follow-up
- BP, creatinine, duplex at intervals for restenosis; cure is commoner in FMD than atherosclerosis.

**Diagram to draw:** two-kidney one-clip versus one-kidney one-clip with the renin–angiotensin loop (Goldblatt two-kidney, one-clip loop: low pressure, renin, angiotensin II, aldosterone, hypertension); renal artery dividing into anterior (four segments) and posterior divisions, with the Brödel line.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Five segmental end arteries; posterior segmental passes behind the pelvis; veins anastomose freely.
> - Left renal vein 6–10 cm, receives adrenal, gonadal and lumbar veins.
> - 2K1C = renin-dependent; 1K1C = volume-dependent.
> - RVH is proved only by cure; duplex first, angiography with gradient before intervention.
> - ASTRAL 2009 and CORAL 2014: stenting adds nothing to medical therapy in atherosclerotic RAS; no routine stenting.
> - FMD gets balloon angioplasty without stent; ostial atheroma: stent.
> - Revascularise for flash pulmonary oedema, declining GFR (function) in a viable kidney (RI <0.8), resistant hypertension.

## Acute renal failure (AKI) and acute tubular necrosis
**Asked as:**
- Etiology, pathogenesis and management of acute renal failure. — *Winter 2024 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)* (acute tubular necrosis also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3c)
- Acute tubular necrosis. — *Summer 2022 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

*Acute renal failure is now called AKI (acute kidney injury).*

### Definition and staging: KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes)
- AKI (per KDIGO) = rise in serum creatinine **or** fall in urine output; either alone is enough.

| Stage | Serum creatinine | Urine output |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Rise ≥0.3 mg/dL in 48 h, or ×1.5 baseline in 7 days | <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 h |
| 2 | ×2 baseline | <0.5 mL/kg/h for 12 h |
| 3 | ×3, or ≥4 mg/dL, or RRT (renal replacement therapy) | <0.3 mL/kg/h for 24 h, or anuria 12 h |

- One-fifth to one-third of inpatients; mortality about 25%, about 50% (about half) if RRT is needed.
- **ATN definition:** ATN is the commonest **intrinsic** cause of AKI (acute kidney injury): tubular injury from **ischaemia or toxins**. A misnomer: cell death is mainly **apoptosis**.

### Etiology
| Type | Causes |
|---|---|
| **Prerenal** | Hypovolaemia (bleeding, vomiting, burns), heart failure, sepsis, hepatorenal syndrome; worsened by NSAIDs (non-steroidal anti-inflammatory drugs) and ACE (angiotensin-converting enzyme) inhibitors |
| **Intrinsic** | **ATN (acute tubular necrosis):** ischaemia, contrast, aminoglycosides, cisplatin, myoglobin. **AIN (acute interstitial nephritis):** β-lactams, NSAIDs, PPIs (proton pump inhibitors), checkpoint inhibitors. Glomerulonephritis; vascular (renal artery or vein thrombosis, atheroemboli) |
| **Postrenal** | BOO (bladder outlet obstruction), bilateral ureteric obstruction or obstructed single kidney: stones, tumours, retroperitoneal fibrosis, ureteric injury at surgery |

- Prerenal AKI and ATN cause about three-quarters of hospital AKI.

**Causes of ATN**
| Ischaemic | Toxic |
|---|---|
| Prolonged prerenal state: shock, sepsis, haemorrhage, burns, cardiac surgery, intraoperative hypotension, renal artery clamping, **transplant (delayed graft function)** | **Exogenous:** aminoglycosides, amphotericin, cisplatin, iodinated contrast. **Endogenous:** myoglobin (rhabdomyolysis, e.g. prolonged flank position), haemoglobin, light chains |

### Pathogenesis (pathophysiology)
- **Autoregulation** holds GFR (glomerular filtration rate) by afferent dilatation (prostaglandins, myogenic reflex) and efferent constriction (angiotensin II, tubuloglomerular feedback).
- **Prerenal:** low perfusion lowers glomerular pressure. Tubules are intact, so they **conserve sodium and water**. NSAIDs block afferent dilatation; ACE inhibitors block efferent constriction. Prolonged prerenal state becomes ATN.
- **Ischaemic ATN:**
  - **Why tubules:** the medulla works near hypoxia (medullary oxygen tension is only 10–20 mmHg), while the **S3 proximal tubule (segment)** and **medullary thick ascending limb** consume much oxygen and are most vulnerable.
  - **Loss of polarity:** Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase loses its basolateral position (moves off the basolateral membrane) → sodium reabsorption fails → high sodium at macula densa → **tubuloglomerular feedback** constricts afferent arteriole (afferent constriction) → GFR falls (protective).
  - Cells die (mostly apoptosis) and slough (dead cells slough) → **muddy-brown casts** block (obstruct) tubules; filtrate leaks back; endothelial injury and inflammation worsen hypoxia; autoregulation is lost.
  - Phases (clinical course): initiation → **maintenance** (oliguria, rising creatinine) → **recovery** (polyuria: tubules regenerate before concentrating ability returns).
- **Toxic ATN:** vasoconstriction plus direct tubular injury. **Contrast:** risk with age, diabetes, CKD (chronic kidney disease). **Rhabdomyolysis** (long lateral positioning): myoglobin causes toxicity, cast obstruction and hypoperfusion.
- **Postrenal:** raised tubular pressure, then afferent vasoconstriction; recovery depends on duration (dog studies of complete obstruction: full at 1 week, 70% at 2 weeks, none at 6 weeks).

### Evaluation (diagnosis: ATN versus prerenal)
- History (fluid loss, hypotension, drugs, contrast), volume status, bladder scan.
- **Ultrasound early** in unexplained AKI to exclude obstruction.

| Index | Prerenal | ATN |
|---|---|---|
| Urine sodium | <20 mmol/L | >20 mmol/L |
| FeNa (fractional excretion of sodium) | <1% | **>1%** |
| FeUrea (on diuretics) | <35% | >35% |
| Specific gravity | >1.015 | <1.010 |
| Sediment | Bland | **Muddy-brown granular casts**, tubular cells |
| Fluid challenge (response to fluids) | Quick (rapid) recovery | Lag of days to weeks |

- Creatine kinase; white cells (AIN); red cell casts (glomerulonephritis); biopsy only if recovery lags (fails).

### Management
**General**
- Treat (remove) the cause; **avoid hypotension** (MAP, mean arterial pressure, >65 mmHg or near baseline) and nephrotoxins; stop NSAIDs and ACE inhibitors or ARBs (angiotensin receptor blockers) (RAAS (renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system) blockers); adjust drug doses.
- Isotonic crystalloid (fluids) for hypovolaemia; aggressive fluids in rhabdomyolysis; intravenous fluid before contrast (pre-contrast hydration).
- **Postrenal:** urethral or suprapubic catheter; **double-J stent or PCN (percutaneous nephrostomy)**; infected obstructed kidney is an emergency.
- AIN: stop drug ± steroids; glomerulonephritis: nephrology and biopsy.

**Complications**
- **Hyperkalaemia:** calcium gluconate 10 mL of 10% (protects heart) → insulin 10 U with 50 mL 50% dextrose, salbutamol nebuliser (shift) → binders, diuretics or dialysis (remove).
- **Volume overload:** loop diuretics in escalating doses. **Diuretics (only for volume overload) do not treat ATN.**
- Acidosis: bicarbonate. Uraemic bleeding: desmopressin 0.3 µg/kg.

**Dialysis indications (AEIOU), refractory to medical therapy**

| | Indication |
|---|---|
| A | Acidosis |
| E | Electrolytes, mainly hyperkalaemia |
| I | Intoxications / ingestions (lithium, salicylate, alcohols) |
| O | Overload with respiratory distress |
| U | Uraemia: encephalopathy, pericarditis, bleeding |

- Intermittent haemodialysis via 10–12 Fr jugular or femoral catheter; **CRRT (continuous RRT)** if haemodynamically unstable. **STARRT-AKI (2020):** early start gave no survival benefit.

**Post-obstructive diuresis**
- Causes: retained urea (osmotic diuresis), volume expansion with ANP (atrial natriuretic peptide), and down-regulated aquaporin-2 and sodium transport.
- Up to 300 mL/h for about 2 days. Free oral fluids; if unable to drink, replace about 75% of output with 0.45% saline; electrolytes every 4–6 hours. Gradual bladder decompression has no role.

### Outcome
- Full or partial recovery, or dialysis dependence; persistence ≥90 days = CKD. ATN recovery takes days to weeks.
- **Also know:** (early) biomarkers NGAL (neutrophil gelatinase-associated lipocalin), KIM-1 (kidney injury molecule-1); AKD (acute kidney disease, under 3 months); gadolinium (groups I and III) avoided in AKI.

**Diagram to draw:** tubular cell losing polarity → sloughing → casts and back-leak.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - KDIGO: creatinine ≥0.3 mg/dL or ×1.5; urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 h.
> - FeNa <1% prerenal, >1% ATN; specific gravity <1.010, muddy-brown casts in ATN; ultrasound to exclude obstruction.
> - S3 segment and thick ascending limb are most vulnerable.
> - Diuretics only for overload (do not treat ATN); dialysis for refractory AEIOU.
> - Post-obstructive diuresis: oral fluids; replace 75% with 0.45% saline if needed.

## Principles of vascular anastomosis and vascular access surgery for HD
**Asked as:**
- Describe principles of vascular anastomosis. Enumerate and describe options for vascular access surgery for HD — *Practice Set 5 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe the principles of vascular anastomosis. Enumerate and describe options for vascular access surgery for HD — *Practice Set 12 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(vascular access also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q3b and 2025-Winter Paper 2, Q3c)
(Practice Set 12 Paper II Q3a: also answered in Practice Set 5 Paper 2, Q2)

*Assumed meaning: HD = haemodialysis.*

### Introduction
- A vascular anastomosis joins two vessels so that flow is smooth, leak-free and lasting (transplantation, autotransplantation, caval repair, dialysis access).
- **Types:** end-to-end, **end-to-side** (commonest: transplant, fistula), side-to-side, and patch (Carrel aortic patch).

### Principles of vascular anastomosis
| Principle | How and why |
|---|---|
| Exposure and control | Proximal and distal control (vessel loops, Rummel tourniquets, bulldog or Satinsky clamps) |
| Anticoagulation | Systemic heparin before clamping; heparinised saline flush; prevents thrombosis in the static column |
| Atraumatic handling | Hold the adventitia, never the intima (adventitia only); clamp clear of atheroma, because clamp injury causes intimal dissection and stenosis; avoids intimal dissection |
| Healthy ends | Trim diseased ends; resect venous valve cusps at the suture line; with no tension, twist or kink, prevents stenosis and leak |
| No tension, twist or kink | Adequate length; trial the final position before suturing |
| **Intima-to-intima eversion** | Smooth endothelium faces the lumen; adventitia and rough surfaces stay outside, as they are thrombogenic |
| Wide opening | Spatulate or bevel small vessels, or use end-to-side with an elliptical arteriotomy (aortic punch), to avoid stenosis (avoids stricture) |
| Suture | **Non-absorbable monofilament polypropylene**, double-armed: smooth, low drag, low thrombogenicity. IVC (inferior vena cava) or aorta 3-0/4-0; transplant vessels 5-0/6-0; wrist fistula about 6-0/7-0 |
| Needle direction | Equal, small, close bites; pass **inside-out on the artery** so plaque and intima are tacked down (tacks down plaque and intima), not lifted into a flap |
| Continuous or interrupted | Continuous is quick and haemostatic; **interrupted** for small vessels and children, allowing growth (allows growth) |
| Technique | **Carrel triangulation** (three stay sutures keep the back wall clear; clear back wall); **parachute** (sew loosely, then draw up; one knot) or tack both apices and run to the midpoint |
| Flush and release | Before the last knot, briefly release clamps to flush air, clot and debris (removes air and clot); leave slack ("growth factor") to avoid purse-stringing (avoids purse-string); release venous/distal clamp first, then arterial |
| Confirm | Haemostasis, pulse, thrill, Doppler |
- **Failure:** early from technical error (thrombosis, leak, stenosis); late from **intimal hyperplasia**.
- **Also know:** vascular staplers; grafts (autologous vein for small or contaminated fields; PTFE [polytetrafluoroethylene] for large vessels).

### Options for vascular access surgery for HD
- Haemodialysis needs a blood flow of about **250–350 mL/min**.

| Option | Construction / description | Ready | Comment |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1. Non-tunnelled CVC** (central venous catheter) | Dual-lumen 10–12 Fr in internal jugular or femoral vein | At once | AKI (acute kidney injury) and emergencies; days only |
| **2. Tunnelled cuffed CVC** | Right internal jugular; cuff blocks bacterial migration | At once | Bridge or last resort; infection, central stenosis |
| **3. Native AVF** (arteriovenous fistula) | Artery joined to a superficial vein, usually vein end to artery side | 6–8 weeks (matures) | **First choice:** best patency, fewest infections |
| a. Snuffbox or **radiocephalic (Brescia–Cimino)** | Radial artery to cephalic vein at wrist | | Most distal; preserves proximal sites; higher failure to mature |
| b. Brachiocephalic | Brachial artery to cephalic vein at elbow | | Higher flow; more steal |
| c. Brachiobasilic transposition | Deep basilic vein mobilised and moved superficially | Longer, often two stages | When the cephalic vein is unusable |
| **4. AVG** (arteriovenous graft) | PTFE forearm loop, upper-arm or thigh | 2–3 weeks (usable) | Poor veins; more stenosis (venous anastomosis), thrombosis, infection |
| **5. HeRO (Haemodialysis Reliable Outflow) graft** | Graft outflow bypasses occluded central veins (central vein occlusion) into the right atrium | | Central vein occlusion |
| **6. Endovascular AVF** | Percutaneous radiofrequency or thermal anastomosis between adjacent forearm vessels | | No incision; selected anatomy |
- **Also know:** lower-limb femoral vein transposition when arms are exhausted; peritoneal dialysis needs no vascular access.

### Planning
- **Hierarchy:** AVF, then AVG, then CVC; **distal before proximal; non-dominant arm first**; individualised (KDOQI [Kidney Disease Outcomes Quality Initiative] 2019 "Life-Plan").
- **Timing:** refer at eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) <30; create the AVF about **6 months** before dialysis.
- **Vein preservation:** preserve arm veins; no venepuncture or PICC (peripherally inserted central catheter) in that arm; avoid subclavian lines.
- **Assessment:** pulses, **Allen test** (ulnar collateral flow), **Doppler vein mapping** (vessels about ≥2 mm).
- **Maturity "rule of 6s":** flow >600 mL/min, diameter >6 mm, depth <6 mm, at 6 weeks.

### Complications of access
| Complication | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Failure to mature (non-maturation); stenosis (neointimal hyperplasia) | PTA (percutaneous transluminal angioplasty), ligate side branches, stent graft |
| **Thrombosis** (usually on a stenosis) | Thrombectomy, then treat the stenosis |
| **Steal syndrome** (hand ischaemia) | Banding, DRIL (distal revascularisation–interval ligation) |
| Aneurysm; infection (CVC > AVG > AVF) | Repair; antibiotics, excise graft, remove catheter |
- **Also know:** venous hypertension; high-output failure; monitor thrill and bruit.

**Diagram to draw:** radiocephalic end-to-side fistula with Carrel triangulation stay sutures.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Intima-to-intima eversion, monofilament polypropylene, inside-out on the artery, flush before tying.
> - AVF first, distal, non-dominant arm, about 6 months ahead.
> - Rule of 6s: flow >600 mL/min, >6 mm wide, <6 mm deep, 6 weeks.
> - Thrombosis sits on a stenosis; steal → banding or DRIL.

# Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Role of vascular grafts in urology
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3e

### Introduction
- Grafts and patches replace, bypass, extend or widen vessels when primary repair fails or narrows them.

### Types
| Type | Examples | Pros | Cons |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Autologous vein** | Saphenous, gonadal | Resists infection; small vessels | Limited length |
| **Autologous artery** | Internal iliac, Carrel aortic patch | Renal arteries | Limited supply |
| **Biological** | Bovine pericardium | Pliable patch | Cost |
| **Synthetic** | **PTFE (polytetrafluoroethylene)**, Dacron | Off-the-shelf, large calibre | Infection; venous grafts thrombose |

### Applications
1. **Renal transplantation**: multiple arteries (18–43%) joined by Carrel patch or bench reconstruction, preserving the lower pole artery (supplies the ureter). Short right renal vein extended with donor IVC (inferior vena cava), external iliac or spiral gonadal vein. Transplant artery stenosis: angioplasty first, graft if it fails.
2. **RCC (renal cell carcinoma) with IVC thrombus**: patch cavoplasty if residual lumen would be <50%; **PTFE replacement (16–20 mm)** if the wall is invaded circumferentially.
3. **Post-chemotherapy RPLND (retroperitoneal lymph node dissection)**: IVC: repair if narrowing <25%, patch or graft if more; ligation tolerated with collaterals. Aorta: about 1% need PTFE or Dacron replacement; never ligated.
4. **Renovascular surgery and autotransplantation**: aortorenal bypass (saphenous vein, internal iliac artery, PTFE), splenorenal or hepatorenal bypass, ex vivo bench repair of branch disease.
5. **Dialysis access**: PTFE AV (arteriovenous) graft.
- **Also know:** iatrogenic or traumatic vascular injury (interposition graft); retroperitoneal sarcoma caval resection; superior mesenteric vein injury (vein graft); bland infrarenal thrombus (filter or caval interruption).

### Principles
- Autologous graft in contaminated fields and small vessels; synthetic for large high-flow vessels.
- Tension-free, spatulated anastomosis with fine monofilament (polypropylene); heparin before clamping; antiplatelet or anticoagulant afterwards as indicated; omental wrap separates synthetic grafts from bowel and urine.

### Complications
- Thrombosis (caval grafts 10–40%), stenosis, infection (often needing removal), enteric or urinary fistula.

**Diagram to draw:** Bench arterial reconstruction.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Transplant: Carrel patch, bench reconstruction; preserve the lower pole artery.
> - IVC: patch if lumen <50%; PTFE replacement if wall invaded.
> - Post-chemotherapy RPLND: IVC can be ligated, aorta must be grafted.

## Pre-operative evaluation of the living (related and unrelated) renal donor
**Asked as:**
- Pre operative investigations for renal donor. — *Summer 2017 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*
- Pre-op assessment of unrelated donor — *Practice Set 8 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(related: 2023-Winter Paper 4, Q3e; 2022-Summer Paper 3, Q3a)

*Assumed meaning: evaluation of a living kidney donor; for the unrelated question, evaluation of a living unrelated kidney donor (Indian context).*

### Principles
- **Donor safety first**. **KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017** is **risk-based**: the donor's lifetime ESKD (end-stage kidney disease) risk is compared with the centre's threshold. Team includes an **independent donor advocate**.
- For an unrelated donor the medical work-up equals that of a related donor; the extra step is **legal and ethical**: proof of genuine, unpaid motive.
- India: **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994, amended 2011** (Rules 2014): near relatives, or authorisation committee approval for others; swap donation allowed.

### Legal assessment of the unrelated donor (THOA)
- Donor "other than near relative" donates for **affection or attachment**: **Authorisation Committee approval is mandatory**.
- Committee: hospital-based if >25 transplants a year, otherwise district or state; members are not from the transplant team.
- Process: joint application; **video-recorded interview** of donor and recipient; verify identity, documents, financial status and long association; exclude middlemen and payment; confirm free, informed consent.
- Foreign recipient with unrelated Indian donor not approved; appeal within 30 days.

### Sequence of evaluation (medical assessment)
**1. Compatibility (immunological)**
- ABO group, HLA (human leukocyte antigen) typing, **crossmatch** (CDC (complement-dependent cytotoxicity) and flow), DSA (donor-specific antibody); incompatible pairs → paired exchange.
- Unrelated donor: more HLA mismatch is expected, yet living unrelated grafts outperform deceased-donor grafts.

**2. Kidney function and urine**

| Test | Accept | Individualise | Decline |
|---|---|---|---|
| GFR (glomerular filtration rate), mL/min/1.73 m² | ≥90 | 60–89 | <60 |
| Albuminuria (ACR (albumin–creatinine ratio), mg/g) | <30 | 30–100 | >100 |
- GFR by two methods, one measured (e.g. DTPA (diethylenetriamine pentaacetic acid) GFR or creatinine clearance).
- Urinalysis, culture; persistent microscopic haematuria → cystoscopy, imaging, ± kidney biopsy.

**3. Medical and metabolic**
- Blood pressure (ambulatory if borderline), fasting glucose, **HbA1c (glycated haemoglobin)**, OGTT (oral glucose tolerance test) if at risk; lipids, liver tests, coagulation, BMI (body mass index).
- **Type 1 diabetes excludes**; prediabetes, controlled hypertension and obesity: individual risk. (Unrelated-donor source: diabetes, significant obesity and uncontrolled hypertension exclude.)
- **Stones**: metabolic work-up; bilateral or recurrent usually exclude.

**4. Infection screen**
- HIV (human immunodeficiency virus), HBsAg (hepatitis B surface antigen), anti-HBc, HCV (hepatitis C virus), CMV (cytomegalovirus), syphilis; **tuberculosis** screen.

**5. Cardiopulmonary**: ECG (electrocardiogram), chest X-ray, echo; stress test if indicated.

**6. Cancer screening** by age (age-appropriate): Pap smear, mammogram, PSA (prostate-specific antigen), stool test or colonoscopy.

**7. Urological:** stones (bilateral exclude), haematuria, malignancy, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection).

**8. Anatomy**
- **CT (computed tomography) angiography**: number of vessels, early branching, stones, masses.
- **Split function** (DTPA or DMSA (dimercaptosuccinic acid)) if asymmetric: the **better kidney stays with the donor**; left preferred (longer vein).
