**9. Psychosocial** evaluation: voluntary, no coercion or payment; independent donor advocate.

### Counselling figures (Muzaale 2014)
- Function settles at 60–80% of baseline.
- Lifetime ESKD (ESRD, end-stage renal disease) 90 per 10,000 donors vs 14 in matched non-donors; mortality 3 per 10,000.

**Also know:** APOL1 (apolipoprotein L1) testing in African ancestry; healthy older (marginal) donors acceptable.

**Diagram to draw:** donor work-up flowchart: legal approval → compatibility → medical → anatomy → surgery.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - KDIGO 2017: risk-based; GFR ≥90 accept; decline GFR <60 or ACR >100 (albuminuria >100 mg/g declines).
> - ABO and crossmatch first; CT angiography for anatomy.
> - Leave the better kidney with the donor (better kidney stays with donor).
> - THOA: near relatives or authorisation committee; unrelated donor = Authorisation Committee approval.
> - Interview video-recorded; no commerce.

## UW solution and organ preservation solutions
**Asked as:**
- UW solution. — *Summer 2017 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*
- Organ preservation solution — *Practice Set 7 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Assumed context: preservation of the kidney for transplantation.*

### Introduction
- **University of Wisconsin solution** (Belzer, late 1980s): the **gold standard** cold-storage solution for kidney, liver and pancreas.

### Principles of cold preservation
- Cooling / **Hypothermia** to **4 °C** cuts (slows) metabolism to roughly a tenth, but does not stop it, so injury accumulates with time.
- Ischaemia causes ATP (adenosine triphosphate) loss (depletion) → **Na⁺/K⁺-ATPase pump failure** → **cell swelling**; anaerobic glycolysis → **acidosis**; calcium influx. On reperfusion, hypoxanthine is converted by xanthine oxidase to **oxygen free radicals** → ischaemia–reperfusion injury, seen clinically as (clinically this appears as) **DGF (delayed graft function)**.
- A solution must therefore prevent swelling and oedema, buffer acid, scavenge radicals and supply ATP precursors. It therefore needs:
  - **Impermeants** (lactobionate, raffinose, mannitol, glucose) to prevent cell swelling.
  - **Buffers** (phosphate, histidine) against acidosis.
  - **Colloid** (hydroxyethyl starch, polyethylene glycol) against interstitial oedema.
  - **Antioxidants and energy precursors** (glutathione, allopurinol, adenosine).
  - Electrolytes: "intracellular" (high K⁺, low Na⁺) or "extracellular" (low K⁺, high Na⁺) type.

### UW composition (per litre)
| Component | Amount | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Lactobionate** (K⁺ salt) | 100 mmol | Impermeant anion; prevents cell swelling; chelates calcium |
| **Raffinose** | 30 mmol | Impermeant trisaccharide |
| **Hydroxyethyl starch** | 50 g | Colloid; prevents interstitial oedema |
| KH₂PO₄ | 25 mmol | Buffer |
| MgSO₄ | 5 mmol | Membrane stability |
| **Adenosine** | 5 mmol | ATP precursor |
| **Glutathione** | 3 mmol | Antioxidant |
| **Allopurinol** | 1 mmol | Xanthine oxidase inhibitor; fewer free radicals |
| Insulin, dexamethasone, antibiotic | Small | Additives |
- **Intracellular-type**: K⁺ about 125 mmol/L, Na⁺ about 30 mmol/L; osmolality ~320 mOsm/kg; pH 7.4.

### UW drawbacks
- **Viscous** (starch): slow flush. **High K⁺**: flush before reperfusion (hyperkalaemia, vasospasm). May crystallise (filter).

### Comparison of solutions
| Feature | UW | HTK (Custodiol) | Euro-Collins |
|---|---|---|---|
| Type | Intracellular (high K⁺) | Low K⁺, low Na⁺ | Intracellular |
| Impermeant | Lactobionate, raffinose | Mannitol | Glucose (enters cells; weaker) |
| Buffer | Phosphate | **Histidine** (strong) | Phosphate |
| Colloid | HES (hydroxyethyl starch) | None (low viscosity) | None |
| Others | Antioxidants, adenosine | Tryptophan, ketoglutarate | — |

| Solution | Type and key components | Points |
|---|---|---|
| **UW (University of Wisconsin, Belzer)** | Intracellular; lactobionate, raffinose, hydroxyethyl starch, glutathione, adenosine, allopurinol | Standard for static storage; viscous; flush out high K⁺ before reperfusion |
| **HTK (histidine–tryptophan–ketoglutarate, Custodiol)** | Low K⁺; histidine buffer, tryptophan (membrane stabiliser), ketoglutarate (substrate), mannitol | Low viscosity, fast cooling; large volume; outcomes similar to UW |
| Euro-Collins | Intracellular; glucose impermeant, phosphate | First generation; largely abandoned |
| Celsior | Extracellular; lactobionate, mannitol, glutathione, histidine | Designed for heart; also abdominal organs |
| IGL-1 (Institut Georges Lopez) | UW-like, but polyethylene glycol and reversed Na⁺/K⁺ | Extracellular; less viscous |
- Kidney outcomes with UW and HTK (histidine–tryptophan–ketoglutarate) are similar; Euro-Collins largely abandoned. **Also know:** Celsior, IGL-1, Marshall's (hypertonic) citrate.

### Practical use and methods
- Living donor, very short cold ischaemia: cold lactated Ringer's suffices; deceased donor or exchange: UW or HTK.
- **Static cold storage:** in situ aortic flush, IVC (inferior vena cava) venting, ice slush; storage at 4 °C. Keep (aim) cold ischaemia short (ideally <24 h / hours): DGF and thrombosis rise with time (beyond).
- **Hypothermic machine perfusion** (Belzer MPS (machine perfusion solution), gluconate-based): pulsatile cold perfusion; flow and resistance help assess viability. Reduces DGF vs static storage; **Moers (2009)**: DGF 20.8% vs 26.5%, 1-year graft survival 94% vs 90%, without long-term survival gain (the chapter notes no difference in long-term survival). Used especially for DCD (donation after circulatory death) kidneys.
- **Normothermic perfusion:** experimental; allows assessment and repair; limited experience.

**Diagram to draw:** ischaemia(–reperfusion) cascade with the UW / solution component acting at (that blocks) each step.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Hypothermia slows, not stops, metabolism.
> - Lactobionate and raffinose (impermeants), HES / starch (colloid).
> - Allopurinol, glutathione (antioxidants); adenosine (ATP).
> - High K⁺: flush before reperfusion.
> - HTK: histidine buffer, tryptophan, ketoglutarate, low viscosity, low K⁺.
> - Machine perfusion reduces DGF.

## CAPD (Continuous Ambulatory Peritoneal Dialysis)
**Asked as:**
- CAPD (Continuous Ambulatory Peritoneal Dialysis). — *Summer 2018 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*
- CAPD — *Practice Set 1 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3e)

### Definition and principle
- CAPD (continuous ambulatory peritoneal dialysis): home dialysis using the **peritoneum** (about 1–2 m²) as the membrane; dialysate dwells in the abdomen.
- **Diffusion:** urea, creatinine and potassium move into dialysate down a concentration gradient.
- **Ultrafiltration:** an osmotic agent draws water (removes water by osmosis): **glucose** (1.5%, 2.5%, 4.25%) or **icodextrin** (for the long dwell).
- **CAPD:** about **4 exchanges of 2 L daily**, dwell 4–6 hours (4–6-hour dwells), overnight long dwell. **APD** (automated peritoneal dialysis) uses a night cycler.

### Catheter
- **Tenckhoff catheter:** silicone with **two Dacron cuffs** (deep cuff in rectus muscle; superficial cuff 2 cm from (near) the exit), exit site pointing downward, tip in the pelvis.
- **Insertion:** open; **laparoscopic** (rectus sheath tunnelling, omentopexy, adhesiolysis); percutaneous Seldinger. Wait (delay full volumes) about 2 weeks before full volumes to prevent leaks.

### Adequacy
- Weekly **Kt/V urea ≥1.7** (peritoneal plus residual renal urea clearance × time ÷ urea distribution volume), with clinical well-being.
- **PET** (peritoneal equilibration test) identifies high transporters, who suit (do better on) short dwells (APD).

### Advantages versus (over) haemodialysis
- Home-based, no vascular access, **haemodynamically gentle**, **preserves residual renal function**. Preferred (favoured) in children, poor vascular access and unstable heart disease.

### Contraindications
- **Absolute:** peritoneal adhesions or fibrosis (scarring), loss of membrane function (membrane failure), uncorrectable hernia, no one (nobody) able to do exchanges.
- **Relative:** recent abdominal surgery, stoma, abdominal wall infection, massive polycystic kidneys, morbid obesity, severe malnutrition.

### Complications
- **Peritonitis:** diagnosis needs 2 of (3): (1) abdominal pain or **cloudy effluent**; (2) effluent **WCC (white cell count) >100/µL with >50% neutrophils**; (3) positive culture. Commonest: coagulase-negative *Staphylococcus* (touch contamination). Give **intraperitoneal** antibiotics (Gram-positive and Gram-negative cover) covering Gram-positives (vancomycin or cefazolin) and Gram-negatives (ceftazidime or aminoglycoside). Remove the catheter if (for) fungal or refractory peritonitis (no response in 5 days).
- **Exit-site and tunnel infection:** prevent with topical mupirocin or gentamicin.
- **Mechanical:** outflow failure (constipation, omental wrap, migration), pericatheter **leak**, **hernias** (raised abdominal pressure), hydrothorax, scrotal oedema (patent processus vaginalis).
- **Metabolic:** hyperglycaemia, weight gain, hypokalaemia, protein loss.
- **Long term:** ultrafiltration failure; **EPS (encapsulating peritoneal sclerosis)**: rare, presents as bowel obstruction.
- **Also know:** PD (peritoneal dialysis) catheter is removed after a successful transplant; retroperitoneal laparoscopy protects the peritoneum for PD patients.

**Diagram to draw:** Tenckhoff catheter: deep and superficial (two) cuffs, tunnel, downward exit site, tip in the pelvis (pelvic tip).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Peritoneum is the membrane; glucose or icodextrin gives (drives) ultrafiltration.
> - Tenckhoff catheter with two cuffs (two-cuff); Kt/V ≥1.7 per week (weekly).
> - Peritonitis: cloudy fluid, WCC >100/µL, >50% neutrophils; give intraperitoneal antibiotics.

## Pre transplant nephrectomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2022-Summer Paper 2, Q3e

*Assumed meaning: removal of the native kidney(s) before or at kidney transplantation.*

### Principle
- **Default: leave native kidneys.** They give residual urine (less fluid restriction), erythropoietin and vitamin D activation; nephrectomy adds morbidity.
- Remove them only when they will threaten the patient or graft under **immunosuppression**.

### Indications
| Group | Indication | Reason |
|---|---|---|
| **Infection** | Chronic pyelonephritis, infected stones, recurrent upper UTI (urinary tract infection) with **high-grade VUR (vesicoureteral reflux)** or obstruction, XGP (xanthogranulomatous pyelonephritis) | Sepsis under immunosuppression |
| **ADPKD (autosomal dominant polycystic kidney disease)** | No room for graft (kidney below iliac crest), recurrent cyst infection or bleeding, pain, early satiety | Space and symptoms |
| **Malignancy** | Suspicious mass or complex cyst (acquired cystic disease), high tumour risk (e.g. Denys–Drash) | Most RCC (renal cell carcinoma) in recipients arises in native kidneys |
| **Hypertension** | Uncontrolled despite multiple drugs | Renin-driven |
| **Heavy proteinuria** | Nephrotic syndrome with malnutrition or thrombosis (e.g. congenital nephrotic syndrome) | Hides graft proteinuria, hypercoagulability |
| Others | Massive polyuria, large stone burden | Graft fluid balance, infection |

### Timing
- **Before transplantation:** infection or tumour; reconstruction 6–12 weeks earlier.
- **Simultaneous:** ADPKD needing space, ipsilateral through the transplant incision; avoided in small children.
- **After transplantation:** symptomatic ADPKD once the graft works.

### Technique
- **Laparoscopic** (transperitoneal or retroperitoneal), open, or **embolisation** in high-risk patients.
- **PD (peritoneal dialysis) patients:** retroperitoneal route keeps PD going; transperitoneal surgery needs temporary haemodialysis.
- Bilateral: prone approach for small kidneys; morcellation or open for large ADPKD kidneys.
- **Ureter:** transect high and leave a normal ureter (for later ureteroureterostomy); **remove a dilated refluxing ureter completely**; keep it as a possible continent stoma if catheterisation may be needed.

### Complications
- Bleeding, transfusion (sensitisation), infection, bowel injury, hypotension after bilateral removal, loss of residual function.

**Also know:** pre-transplant urodynamics; bladder augmentation if capacity <75% expected or pressure >40 cmH₂O.

**Diagram to draw:** enlarged ADPKD kidney reaching the iliac fossa beside the planned graft site.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Default: leave native kidneys.
> - Indications: infection/VUR, ADPKD space, tumour, hypertension, proteinuria.
> - Retroperitoneal route for PD; leave a normal ureter.

## GFR estimation: clinical assessment and in the prospective transplant donor
**Asked as:**
- Methods for GFR estimation in prospective tx donor. — *Practice Set 9 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*
- GFR clinical assessment — *Winter 2023 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3a; donor setting answered in 2019-Summer Paper 1, Q3a)

*Assumed meaning: tx = transplant (prospective living kidney donor).*

### Definition
- GFR (glomerular filtration rate) is the plasma volume filtered by both kidneys per minute; normal about 120 mL/min/1.73 m². It is the standard index of kidney function. The ideal marker is freely filtered and neither secreted nor reabsorbed.

### Why it matters (donor)
- The donor loses half the nephron mass; function settles at 60–80% of the pre-donation level. GFR (glomerular filtration rate) is assessed by at least two methods, **including a measured one**.

### Methods
**1. Serum creatinine and eGFR (estimated GFR)**
- Creatinine comes from muscle; freely filtered but also secreted, so it **overestimates GFR** as function falls.
- Creatinine is freely filtered but depends on **muscle mass, diet and age**, and is also secreted, so it overestimates GFR. Varies with **muscle mass, diet and age**; trimethoprim blocks secretion.
- **CKD-EPI (Chronic Kidney Disease Epidemiology Collaboration) 2021** equation: age and sex, **no race term**; currently recommended. MDRD (Modification of Diet in Renal Disease) and Cockcroft–Gault are older.
- Less accurate above 60 mL/min, where donors lie: a screening test only.

**2. Cystatin C**
- 13-kDa protein from all nucleated cells; freely filtered, then (fully) reabsorbed and broken down. Independent of muscle mass; affected by thyroid state, steroids, inflammation.
- The **combined creatinine–cystatin C equation** is more accurate than either alone; it is the most accurate estimate.

**3. 24-hour creatinine clearance**
- (Urine creatinine × urine volume) ÷ serum creatinine; collection errors common; secretion causes overestimation.

**4. Measured GFR (mGFR): reference**
| Marker | Method |
|---|---|
| **Inulin** | Urinary clearance; gold standard, research only |
| **Iohexol** | Plasma clearance; safe and accurate (another source: plasma or urinary clearance) |
| **Iothalamate** | Urinary or plasma clearance |
| **⁵¹Cr-EDTA (ethylenediaminetetraacetic acid)** | Plasma clearance |
| **⁹⁹ᵐTc-DTPA (diethylenetriaminepentaacetic acid)** | Plasma sampling, or gamma camera (Gates method); purely filtered; also gives **split function** |

**5. Split function**
- DTPA renogram (or DMSA, dimercaptosuccinic acid) gives each kidney's share. If the difference exceeds 10%, **leave the better kidney with the donor**.

### Clinical use
- **CKD (chronic kidney disease) staging (KDIGO, Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes):** G1 ≥90, G2 60–89, G3a 45–59, G3b 30–44, G4 15–29, G5 <15 mL/min/1.73 m², always with albuminuria (ACR, albumin-to-creatinine ratio).
- **Living donor (KDIGO 2017):** see table below; confirm with mGFR.
- **Urology:** drug and contrast dosing, cisplatin eligibility, continent diversion (>35 mL/min), partial versus radical nephrectomy.
- **Acute change:** eGFR is invalid when function is unstable; AKI (acute kidney injury) uses creatinine change and urine output. NGAL (neutrophil gelatinase-associated lipocalin) is emerging.

### KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2017 donor decision
| GFR (mL/min/1.73 m²) | Decision |
|---|---|
| **≥90** | Accept |
| **60–89** | Individualise (age, lifetime risk) |
| **<60** | Decline |
- Initial eGFR is confirmed by mGFR, measured creatinine clearance or creatinine–cystatin C eGFR.
- Indian practice: DTPA GFR with split function, plus CT (computed tomography) angiography.

**Diagram to draw:** plasma tracer clearance curve after a single injection.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Creatinine overestimates GFR (secretion); CKD-EPI 2021 is race-free.
> - Cystatin C adds accuracy; creatinine–cystatin C combined equation is most accurate.
> - Inulin is the gold standard; iohexol or DTPA in practice; DTPA gives GFR and split function.
> - KDIGO 2017: ≥90 accept, 60–89 individualise, <60 decline.

## Post transplant malignancy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3c

### Introduction
- Cancer risk after kidney transplantation is about 2–3 times the general population; a leading cause of late death with a functioning graft.

### Causes
- **Lost immune surveillance** from immunosuppression (dose and duration matter; depleting antibodies add risk).
- **Oncogenic viruses:** EBV (Epstein–Barr virus) → PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder); HHV-8 (human herpesvirus-8) → Kaposi sarcoma; HPV (human papillomavirus) → skin and anogenital cancer; hepatitis B/C → liver cancer.
- **Direct drug effects:** azathioprine sensitises skin to UV (ultraviolet) light; calcineurin inhibitors promote tumour growth. mTOR (mammalian target of rapamycin) inhibitors are **antitumour**.
- **Pre-existing risk:** ESRD (end-stage renal disease), acquired cystic disease, analgesic nephropathy; rarely donor-transmitted.

### Common cancers
| Cancer | Key facts |
|---|---|
| **Non-melanoma skin** | Commonest; squamous exceeds basal cell (reverse of general population); multiple, aggressive |
| **PTLD** | Mostly B-cell, EBV-driven; highest in first year, children and **EBV-negative recipient of EBV-positive donor**; may present as graft mass |
| **Kaposi sarcoma** | HHV-8; regresses with less immunosuppression and switch to sirolimus |
| **RCC (renal cell carcinoma)** | 4.6% of post-transplant cancers; **90% in native kidneys**; graft tumours mostly papillary, low grade |
| **Bladder cancer** | About **3-fold** risk; more aggressive |
| Prostate | Same incidence as general population |

### Management
- **Reduce immunosuppression** and switch to an mTOR inhibitor; sirolimus conversion reduced new skin squamous cancers (Euvrard, 2012).
- **PTLD:** reduce immunosuppression, rituximab, then chemotherapy (R-CHOP).
- RCC, prostate and bladder: treat as in the general population; protect graft vessels and ureter at surgery. BCG (bacillus Calmette–Guérin) is usable in selected bladder cancer.

### Prevention and screening
- Pre-transplant cancer screening and waiting periods (none after curative treatment of small RCC <3 cm, Gleason 6 prostate cancer, superficial bladder cancer).
- Sun protection, yearly skin examination, age-appropriate screening.
- KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) does **not** endorse routine native-kidney screening; screen only high-risk recipients.

**Diagram to draw:** pie chart of post-transplant cancers (skin largest).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Skin cancer commonest; squamous more than basal cell.
> - PTLD: EBV-negative recipient of EBV-positive donor, first year.
> - RCC mostly in native kidneys; bladder cancer 3-fold and aggressive.
> - Reduce immunosuppression and switch to mTOR inhibitor.

## Pre transplant Immunosupression treatment options
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2023-Winter Paper 2, Q3e
(maintenance drugs answered in 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

*Taken as induction immunosuppression and desensitisation given before or at transplantation.*

### Principle
- Rejection risk is highest in the first weeks. **Induction** gives intense immunosuppression around transplant; **desensitisation** removes preformed antibody in HLA (human leucocyte antigen)- or ABO-incompatible recipients.
- Immunological risk is assessed first: cPRA (calculated panel-reactive antibody), DSA (donor-specific antibody) on Luminex beads, crossmatch (CDC, complement-dependent cytotoxicity, and flow).

### Induction agents
| Agent | Mechanism | Use and drawbacks |
|---|---|---|
| **Basiliximab** | Anti-CD25 (IL-2, interleukin-2, receptor); non-depleting | Low-risk recipients; 20 mg on days 0 and 4; few side effects |
| **rATG (rabbit antithymocyte globulin)** | Polyclonal T-cell depletion | High-risk (sensitised, retransplant, delayed graft function); less rejection than basiliximab; cytokine release, leucopenia, infection, PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder) |
| **Alemtuzumab** | Anti-CD52; profound depletion | Allows steroid avoidance; off-label |
| IV methylprednisolone | Cytokine suppression | Given intraoperatively with all regimens |

- Over 90% receive induction, then **tacrolimus + mycophenolate** ± steroids. In living-donor transplants, tacrolimus and mycophenolate often start days before surgery.

### Desensitisation: HLA-incompatible
- **Plasmapheresis** (removes antibody) plus **low-dose IVIG (intravenous immunoglobulin)** after each session; or **high-dose IVIG** (immunomodulation).
- **Rituximab** (anti-CD20) reduces B cells that renew antibody. Bortezomib (plasma cells) and eculizumab (anti-C5) in selected cases.
- **Imlifidase:** bacterial enzyme that **cleaves all IgG** within hours, turning a positive crossmatch negative; EMA (European Medicines Agency) conditional approval 2020 for highly sensitised patients.
- Desensitised live-donor transplant gives better survival than staying on dialysis (Montgomery, 2011).

### Desensitisation: ABO-incompatible
- **Rituximab** 2–4 weeks before; **plasmapheresis or immunoadsorption** to lower anti-A/B titres to a centre target (commonly ≤1:8); IVIG; tacrolimus–mycophenolate started before surgery. Splenectomy is now obsolete.
- Post-transplant titre monitoring; A2 donors carry lower risk.

### Alternatives
- **Kidney paired exchange** avoids desensitisation; acceptable-mismatch programmes.

**Diagram to draw:** antibody titre falling with plasmapheresis sessions to target before transplant.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Basiliximab for low risk; rATG for high immunological risk.
> - HLA-incompatible: plasmapheresis + IVIG ± rituximab; imlifidase cleaves IgG.
> - ABO-incompatible: rituximab + plasmapheresis to target titre.

## Brainstem death: biology and clinical implications
**Asked as:**
- Brain stem Death: Biology and clinical implications. — *Winter 2024 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Brainstem death — *Practice Set 13 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(Winter 2024 Paper II Q3d: also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3b and 2015-Summer Paper 1, Q3d)
(Practice Set 13 Paper II Q3c: also answered in 2022-Summer Paper 4, Q3b and 2024-Winter Paper 2, Q3d)

### Definition and law
- **Irreversible loss of the capacity for consciousness and breathing**; legal death (legally death) in India: THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994, amended 2011, Rules 2014.

### Biology (pathophysiology)
```
Brain injury -> oedema -> raised ICP (intracranial pressure)
 -> falling cerebral perfusion -> herniation ("coning")
 -> Cushing reflex (hypertension, bradycardia)
 -> catecholamine storm (hypertension, tachyarrhythmia)
 -> loss of sympathetic tone -> vasodilatation, hypotension
```

| System | Consequence |
|---|---|
| Heart and vessels | Storm causes myocardial injury and arrhythmia, then **hypotension** |
| Lungs | Neurogenic pulmonary oedema |
| Pituitary | **Diabetes insipidus (DI)** (no ADH, antidiuretic hormone): polyuria, hypernatraemia; low thyroid hormone and cortisol |
| Hypothalamus | **Hypothermia** (poikilothermia) |
| Blood | DIC (disseminated intravascular coagulation) from brain thromboplastin |
| Whole body | Cytokine injury to organs; hyperglycaemia |
- Consequences (summary): **diabetes insipidus** (polyuria, hypernatraemia), hypothermia, DIC, neurogenic pulmonary oedema.

### Diagnosis
#### Preconditions
- Apnoeic coma on a ventilator with a **known irreversible cause**.
- Exclude drugs (sedatives, relaxants), hypothermia, metabolic or endocrine causes, shock.

#### Tests: all brainstem reflexes absent

| Brainstem reflex (all absent) | Method | Cranial nerves |
|---|---|---|
| Pupillary | Bright light: pupils fixed | II, III |
| Corneal | Touch cornea: no blink | V, VII |
| Oculocephalic | Turn head: eyes move with head | VIII, III, VI |
| Oculovestibular | 50 mL ice-cold water in each ear: no eye movement | VIII, III, VI |
| Motor in cranial nerve area | Supraorbital pressure: no grimace | V, VII |
| Gag and cough | Pharynx touch, tracheal suction: no response | IX, X |

- **Apnoea test (last):** pre-oxygenate with 100% oxygen; disconnect ventilator with oxygen insufflation; **no breathing at PaCO₂ (arterial carbon dioxide tension) ≥60 mmHg**. Abort for hypotension, desaturation or arrhythmia.
- **Ancillary tests** if examination is impossible: angiography, perfusion scan, EEG (electroencephalogram).

#### Indian Board of Medical Experts
- **India:** 4-member Board (four members): hospital in-charge; independent specialist nominated from an approved panel; neurologist or neurosurgeon; treating doctor. **No transplant-team member.**
- **Two sets of tests at least 6 hours apart (two tests ≥6 hours apart)**; recorded on **Form 10**.

### Clinical implications: donor management
- **Mandatory request** for donation; next-of-kin consent; NOTTO (National Organ and Tissue Transplant Organisation) allocates.
- **Rule of 100s:** systolic pressure >100 mmHg, urine >100 mL/h, PaO₂ (arterial oxygen tension) >100 mmHg, haemoglobin >100 g/L.
- **Vasopressin** (pressor, treats DI); thyroid hormone, methylprednisolone (steroids), insulin; warming; protective ventilation.
- **DBD (donation after brain death)** keeps circulation, so warm ischaemia is minimal.

**Diagram to draw:** herniation sequence leading to storm, DI, hypothermia, DIC. Flowchart: coma → preconditions → test 1 → 6 h → test 2 → Form 10 → consent → NOTTO.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Raised ICP → Cushing reflex → storm → hypotension, DI, hypothermia, DIC.
> - Six (brainstem) reflexes absent; apnoea at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.
> - Four-member Board; no transplant-team member.
> - Two tests ≥6 h apart; Form 10; Rule of 100s.

## Anomalies of renal artery.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3c

### Normal pattern
- One renal artery from the aorta at L1–L2; anterior and posterior divisions; **five segmental end arteries** (apical, upper, middle, lower, posterior). The right artery passes behind the IVC (inferior vena cava).

### Terminology
| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Multiple renal arteries** (preferred) | More than one vessel from the aorta to one kidney |
| **Aberrant** | Arises from a vessel other than aorta or main renal artery (iliac, SMA (superior mesenteric artery), gonadal) |
| **Accessory** | Two or more branches supplying the same segment |

### Incidence
- Single artery in **71–85%**; variants in 25–40%. Accessory arteries 25–28% (aorta T11–L4), usually to the **lower pole**. True aberrant vessels are rare except in **ectopic and horseshoe kidneys**.
- **Also know:** early prehilar branching; right artery anterior to IVC in about 5%.

### Embryology
- The ascending kidney takes successive aortic branches; the lower ones normally degenerate. **Failure of degeneration** gives polar or multiple arteries.

### Clinical significance
- **PUJ (pelviureteric junction) obstruction** from a lower-pole crossing vessel; but crossing vessels occur in a fifth to a quarter of normal PUJs.
- Compression of an infundibulum: hydrocalyx, pain, stones, UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **Surgery:** bleeding at endopyelotomy (incise posterolaterally); polar arteries are end arteries, so ligation infarcts that segment; **donor nephrectomy** needs CT (computed tomography) angiography; aortic surgery.
- Not linked to hypertension.

### Renal artery aneurysm
- About **0.1%**. **Abeshouse types:** saccular (about 93%), fusiform, dissecting, arteriovenous.
- Mostly asymptomatic; hypertension, haematuria, pain; wreath-like calcification.
- **Treat** (endovascular or open) if rupture, **>2 cm**, expanding, **pregnancy or planned pregnancy**, symptoms or emboli.

### Renal arteriovenous fistula
| | Congenital (cirsoid AVM (arteriovenous malformation)) | Acquired |
|---|---|---|
| Share | <25% | Majority (biopsy, PCNL (percutaneous nephrolithotomy), trauma) |
| Features | Bruit, haematuria, high-output failure | Often resolves |
| Treatment | Selective embolisation | Observe; embolise if bleeding |

**Diagram to draw:** kidney with main artery, lower-pole accessory artery crossing the PUJ.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Single artery in 71–85%; accessory arteries usually lower pole.
> - Crossing vessel can cause PUJ obstruction.
> - Treat aneurysm >2 cm or in pregnancy.

## Mycophenolate Mofetil.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 2 Paper 2, Q3b
(immunosuppressants also answered in 2021-Summer Paper 1, Q3f and 2020-Summer Paper 4, Q1)

### Introduction
- **MMF (mycophenolate mofetil):** an antimetabolite immunosuppressant; morpholinoethyl ester **prodrug** of MPA (mycophenolic acid). With tacrolimus it forms maintenance therapy in **over 90%** of kidney recipients.

### Mechanism
- MPA is a reversible, non-competitive inhibitor of **IMPDH (inosine monophosphate dehydrogenase)**, the key enzyme of **de novo guanosine nucleotide synthesis**.
- **Why lymphocyte-selective:** T and B lymphocytes depend on the de novo pathway; other cells use the salvage pathway. MPA also inhibits the type II isoform found in activated lymphocytes.
- Result: arrest of T- and B-cell proliferation and reduced antibody formation.

### Pharmacology
| Feature | Detail |
|---|---|
| Dose (kidney) | **1 g twice daily** oral or IV; EC-MPS (enteric-coated mycophenolate sodium) 720 mg twice daily is equivalent |
| Metabolism | Hydrolysed to MPA; liver glucuronidation to inactive MPAG (MPA glucuronide); **enterohepatic recirculation** |
| Excretion | MPAG in urine; accumulates in renal failure |
| Interactions | **Ciclosporin lowers MPA** (blocks enterohepatic recirculation), tacrolimus does not; antacids, cholestyramine reduce absorption; never with azathioprine |

### Uses
- Kidney transplant maintenance (tacrolimus + MMF ± steroids); other solid organs.
- Lupus nephritis, vasculitis; steroid-refractory immune-related adverse events of checkpoint inhibitors.

### Adverse effects
- **GI (gastrointestinal):** diarrhoea, nausea, colitis (commonest; dose split or EC-MPS).
- **Marrow:** leucopenia, anaemia, thrombocytopenia: blood count weekly in month 1.
- **Infection:** CMV, BK virus (ureteric stricture), rarely PML (progressive multifocal leucoencephalopathy).
- **Malignancy:** skin cancer, PTLD (post-transplant lymphoproliferative disorder).
- **Teratogenic:** miscarriage, ear and facial malformations; contraception mandatory; switch to azathioprine before conception.
- Advantage: **not nephrotoxic**; no hypertension, diabetes or dyslipidaemia.

### Evidence
- Three mid-1990s randomised trials (US, European, Tricontinental) showed fewer early acute rejections than azathioprine or placebo, so MMF replaced azathioprine.
- **ELITE-Symphony (2007):** low-dose tacrolimus + MMF + daclizumab + steroids gave the lowest rejection and best GFR.

**Diagram to draw:** purine synthesis pathway showing IMPDH block (de novo) and the salvage pathway spared.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Prodrug of MPA; IMPDH inhibitor; lymphocytes need de novo purine synthesis.
> - 1 g twice daily; ciclosporin lowers MPA levels.
> - Toxicity: diarrhoea, leucopenia, CMV/BK, teratogenic; not nephrotoxic.

## Metabolic abnormalities in CKD
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q3e

### Definition
- **CKD (chronic kidney disease), KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes):** abnormal kidney structure or function for **≥3 months**. Staged by GFR (glomerular filtration rate) G1–G5 (G3a 45–59, G3b 30–44, G4 15–29, G5 <15 mL/min/1.73 m²) and albuminuria A1–A3.
- Each abnormality is a lost kidney function: excretion, acid–base balance, hormone production.

### Abnormalities

| Abnormality | Mechanism | Management |
|---|---|---|
| **Sodium and water** | Fixed urine concentration; sodium retention → overload, hypertension | Salt restriction; diuretics (chlorthalidone works even in advanced CKD) |
| **Hyperkalaemia** | Less distal sodium delivery; RAAS (renin–angiotensin–aldosterone system) blockers lower aldosterone | Potassium binders and diuretics, so RAAS blockers can continue |
| **Metabolic acidosis** | Less ammonium excretion; retained sulphate and phosphate (anion gap rises late); bicarbonate loss | Sodium bicarbonate or citrate to keep bicarbonate **≥22 mEq/L**; slows progression, protects bone and muscle |
| **CKD-MBD** (mineral and bone disorder) | Phosphate retention → FGF-23 (fibroblast growth factor 23) rises → less **calcitriol** → hypocalcaemia → **secondary hyperparathyroidism** | Phosphate restriction, binders with meals, active vitamin D; calcimimetic; parathyroidectomy if tertiary |
| **Anaemia** | Erythropoietin falls, mainly below GFR 30 | Iron first, then erythropoietin; Hb (haemoglobin) target 10–11.5 g/dL (harm above 13) |
| **Hyperuricaemia** | Reduced excretion | Treat gout; asymptomatic lowering unproven (CKD-FIX) |
| **Lipids** | High triglycerides, low HDL (high-density lipoprotein) | Statin ± ezetimibe if ≥50 years, not on dialysis (SHARP) |
| **Glucose** | Insulin resistance, but slower insulin clearance → hypoglycaemia | Lower insulin doses |

### Bone disease (renal osteodystrophy)
- **High turnover:** osteitis fibrosa (PTH [parathyroid hormone] excess).
- **Low turnover:** adynamic bone (oversuppressed PTH), osteomalacia.
- **Tertiary hyperparathyroidism:** autonomous gland, hypercalcaemia; persists after transplant.
- **Vascular calcification** adds cardiovascular death.

### Other endocrine and uraemic effects
- **Also know:** hypogonadism, hyperprolactinaemia, erectile dysfunction (better after transplant); growth failure in children; protein-energy wasting; dialysis amyloid; uraemic platelet dysfunction (desmopressin).
- **Urological relevance:** dose drugs by GFR; avoid gadolinium if eGFR (estimated GFR) <30; each AKI (acute kidney injury) speeds progression.

**Diagram to draw:** CKD-MBD cascade: phosphate ↑ → FGF-23 ↑ → calcitriol ↓ → calcium ↓ → PTH ↑ → bone disease.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - FGF-23 rises first; secondary then tertiary hyperparathyroidism.
> - Bicarbonate ≥22 mEq/L; Hb 10–11.5 g/dL.
> - Hyperkalaemia: binders rather than stopping RAAS blockade.

## Renal transplant in neurogenic bladder
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 4 Paper 2, Q3a

### Why the bladder matters
- A neurogenic bladder with **poor compliance, low capacity or detrusor overactivity** transmits high pressure to the graft even without reflux. Poor emptying adds infection. Recurrent pyelonephritis, not augmentation, causes graft loss.
- **Five goals:** unimpeded drainage, low-pressure capacious storage, effective emptying, no infection, fewest procedures.

### Pretransplant evaluation
- Renal ultrasound, VCUG (voiding cystourethrogram) and **urodynamics** (capacity, compliance, overactivity, emptying, sphincter). Repeat them; the bladder changes over time.
- **Target:** storage to expected capacity at detrusor pressure **<40 cm H₂O**.
- **Defunctionalised bladder** (anuria or diversion): **bladder cycling** (saline fills of increasing volume via catheter), then urodynamics.

### Pretransplant optimisation (stepwise)
```
Antimuscarinic (oxybutynin), mirabegron, botulinum toxin
  → CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation) ± overnight drainage
  → bladder cycling if defunctionalised
  → still >40 cm H₂O, <75% expected capacity or CIC ≤3-hourly → augmentation
```
- **Augmentation:** ileum or sigmoid most used; dilated ureter (ureterocystoplasty) preferred when available (no mucus or metabolic effects). **Stomach avoided** (haematuria–dysuria syndrome in anuric patients). Augmentation does not clearly raise transplant complications.
- **Mitrofanoff** channel if urethral CIC is difficult; site it **medial** to the planned transplant incision.
- Do reconstruction **before** transplant; allow **6–12 weeks** to heal. Neuropathic bladders may deteriorate under high graft output, so earlier reconstruction is justified.
- **Native nephrectomy** only for named reasons: recurrent upper-tract infection, massive reflux, stones, uncontrolled hypertension.

### At transplant
- Extravesical non-refluxing ureteroneocystostomy into bladder or augment (know the augment pedicle). Ureteroureterostomy to a native ureter is an option.
- Ileal conduit drainage if the bladder is unusable.

### Post-transplant care
- Continue CIC; colonisation is expected, so treat only symptomatic UTI (urinary tract infection).
- **Creatinine rise with hydronephrosis:** catheter drainage for 1–2 weeks first. If it improves, the bladder is the cause; otherwise stent or nephrostomy and consider rejection.
- Febrile UTI with graft reflux: reimplant after optimising the bladder.
- Lifelong surveillance of augments (malignancy, stones).

**Diagram to draw:** stepwise bladder optimisation ladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Target <40 cm H₂O storage before transplant.
> - Drugs → CIC → cycling → augmentation; reconstruct 6–12 weeks before.
> - Pyelonephritis, not augmentation, loses grafts.

## Pre-emptive renal transplant
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 6 Paper 2, Q3a

### Definition
- Kidney transplantation **before starting maintenance dialysis** in a patient with advanced CKD (chronic kidney disease).

### Rationale
- Every year on dialysis adds cardiovascular disease, vascular calcification and bone–mineral damage that the graft later inherits. Pre-emptive transplantation **avoids this exposure**.
- Transplantation beats dialysis: Wolfe (1999) showed early risk is higher, equal at 106 days, then 68% lower long term.

### Advantages
- Better patient and graft survival than transplantation after dialysis; longer dialysis time worsens outcome (registry data).
- No dialysis access surgery, access infection or catheter complications.
- Fewer transfusions, so less HLA (human leucocyte antigen) sensitisation.
- Better quality of life, employment; lower cost.
- Children: better growth and development.

### Frequency
- 28.7% of US transplants in 2021 (living donor 51%, deceased donor 21%, children 56%). Commonest with a **living donor**.

### Timing and evaluation
- Evaluate early, ideally before dialysis. KDIGO (Kidney Disease: Improving Global Outcomes) 2020: refer when eGFR (estimated glomerular filtration rate) is <30 mL/min/1.73 m², 6–12 months before expected dialysis. Living-donor transplant is usually done at eGFR <20 with progressive decline.
- Deceased-donor listing (US) at eGFR ≤20.
- Full recipient work-up: cardiac, vascular (iliac calcification), infection (TB [tuberculosis], hepatitis B and C, HIV [human immunodeficiency virus]), malignancy, psychosocial; recurrence risk (primary FSGS [focal segmental glomerulosclerosis]).
- **Urological:** bladder function and lower tract (valves, neurogenic bladder); native nephrectomy only for specific indications.
- India: mainly living related donors under THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act).

### Limitations
- Needs early referral and a ready donor.
- Transplant may come earlier than needed, using up dialysis-free native function; adherence untested.
- In advanced CKD, bowel augmentation may speed decline.

**Also know:** ABO-incompatible or paired exchange programmes widen pre-emptive options.

**Diagram to draw:** Wolfe curve: relative risk of death after transplant vs dialysis over time.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Transplant before dialysis = best outcome.
> - Refer at eGFR <30; transplant usually at eGFR <20.
> - Commonest with living donors and children.

## THOA 1994 and its 2011 amendment (THOTA) and 2014 Rules
**Asked as:**
- Amendments to THOA: 2011. — *Practice Set 9 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- HOTA and its modification — *Practice Set 11 Paper II Q3c (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

*Indian law; not covered in the chapter notes.*
*Assumed meaning (Practice Set 11): India's Transplantation of Human Organs Act (THOA) 1994, its 2011 amendment and 2014 Rules.*

### Background: THOA 1994 aims
- **THOA (Transplantation of Human Organs Act) 1994** regulated (regulates) removal, storage and transplantation of organs, banned commercial dealing (**prevents commercial dealing**) and legalised **brain-stem death** (**recognised brain-stem death as legal death**, allowing deceased donation).
- Organ trade continued and deceased donation stayed low. The **Transplantation of Human Organs (Amendment) Act 2011** responded; **Rules followed in 2014**.

### Main provisions (1994)
- **Living donors:** **near relatives** (spouse, parents, children, siblings) after verification. Others, donating "by reason of affection or attachment", need **Authorisation Committee** approval.
- **Appropriate Authority** registers and inspects hospitals; only registered centres may transplant.
- **Brain-stem death certification by a Board of four:** doctor in charge of the hospital; independent specialist from an approved panel; neurologist or neurosurgeon; treating doctor. **None from the transplant team.** Two sets of tests ≥6 hours apart.
- **Organ sale is a punishable offence.**

### Main amendments: 2011 amendment (THOTA, Transplantation of Human Organs and Tissues Act)
| Area | Change | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Scope** | **Tissues** added (included); renamed the **Transplantation of Human Organs and Tissues Act (THOTA)**; **tissue banks** registered | Regulates tissue banks |
| **Near relatives** | **Grandparents and grandchildren** added to spouse, children, parents and siblings (become near relatives) | Wider related donor pool |
| **Swap donation** | **Paired exchange** (swap (paired) donation) between near-relative pairs allowed, with Authorisation Committee approval | Overcomes ABO or crossmatch incompatibility |
| **Retrieval centres** | Hospitals with ICU (intensive care unit) facilities can register as **non-transplant organ retrieval centres** | |
| **Transplant coordinator** | Mandatory in every registered transplant hospital | Organises donation |
| **Mandatory ("required") request** | ICU doctor (staff) must ask relatives (families) of a potential brain-dead donor (brain-dead patients) about donation and inform the retrieval centre | More deceased donors |
| **Brain-death board** | If a neurologist or neurosurgeon is unavailable (if no neurologist), a surgeon, physician, anaesthetist or intensivist nominated by the hospital can serve | Smaller hospitals can certify |
| **Eye donation** | Trained technicians may remove corneas | |
| **Registry and network** | National human organ and tissue removal and storage network and **national registry** of donors and recipients (now run by NOTTO, the National Organ and Tissue Transplant Organisation) | Fair allocation |
| **Penalties** | Raised: **higher penalties** for organ trade (up to 10 years' imprisonment; fine up to ₹1 crore) (see below) | Deterrence |

### Penalties under the amendment
- **Removal of organs without authority:** imprisonment up to **10 years** and fine up to ₹20 lakh.
- **Commercial dealing in organs:** imprisonment **5–10 years** and fine **₹20 lakh to ₹1 crore**.
- Registered medical practitioner: name removed from the register for **3 years** for a first offence and **permanently** for a later offence.

### Safeguards retained
- Unrelated donors and foreigners need **Authorisation Committee** approval.
- Minors and mentally ill persons cannot be living donors except in exceptional, approved cases.

### Rules 2014 (THOT Rules)
- Standard forms for consent and certification (**Form 10** for brain-stem death); brainstem reflexes and apnoea test set out.
- Authorisation Committee interviews donor and recipient to exclude coercion or payment.
- **Also know:** regional and state networks (ROTTO, SOTTO); 2023 guidelines removed the domicile rule and age limit for deceased-donor listing.

**Diagram to draw:** flowchart: potential donor (brain-dead patient) in ICU → required request → transplant coordinator → brain-stem death board (Board of four) → family consent → retrieval → national registry and allocation (NOTTO allocation).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - THOA 1994: brain-stem death legal; anti-commercial; Authorisation Committee for unrelated donors.
> - 2011: tissues added; grandparents and grandchildren became near relatives.
> - Swap donation, retrieval centres, transplant coordinators (coordinator), required request, higher penalties.
> - Commercial dealing: 5–10 years and ₹20 lakh to ₹1 crore.
> - Rules 2014: Form 10; Board of four; tests ≥6 hours apart.

## Marginal kidney donor
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 2, Q3d
(also answered in 2024-Winter Paper 4, Q3e and 2016-Summer Paper 3, Q3f)

### Definition
- A **marginal (extended) donor** is one whose kidney carries **more DGF (delayed graft function) and shorter graft survival** than a standard donor. Such kidneys are used because of organ shortage.
- They still give better survival than staying on dialysis (Ojo, 2001).

### Deceased marginal donors
- **ECD (expanded criteria donor), UNOS (United Network for Organ Sharing):** age **60 or over**, or age **50–59 with 2 or more of:** hypertension, **creatinine >1.5 mg/dL**, death from **CVA (cerebrovascular accident)**. Graft-loss risk about 1.7 times a standard donor.
- **KDPI (Kidney Donor Profile Index) >85%** replaced ECD in the US (2014); it uses 10 donor factors. Longevity matching pairs low-KDPI kidneys with recipients with the best EPTS (Estimated Post-Transplant Survival).
- **DCD (donation after circulatory death):** warm ischaemia raises DGF; long-term survival similar to DBD (donation after brain death).
- **Others:** donor AKI (acute kidney injury); HCV (hepatitis C virus)-positive donors with antiviral treatment; paediatric donors under 15 kg (en bloc).

### Living marginal donors
- Older donors, controlled hypertension, obesity, small stones, multiple renal arteries. Acceptable after careful evaluation and consent.

### Evaluation: Remuzzi biopsy score
- Pre-implantation biopsy; four compartments, each 0–3 (total 0–12):

| Component | 0 | 1 | 2 | 3 |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Glomerular sclerosis | None | <20% | 20–50% | >50% |
| Tubular atrophy | None | <20% | 20–50% | >50% |
| Interstitial fibrosis | None | <20% | 20–50% | >50% |
| Arterial narrowing | None | Wall < lumen | Wall = or slightly > lumen | Severe narrowing |

- **0–3: single** transplant; **4–6: dual** kidney transplant; **7–12: discard**.

### Optimising use
- Short cold ischaemia; **HMP (hypothermic machine perfusion)** reduces DGF without changing long-term survival.
- **Old-for-old** allocation (Eurotransplant Senior Program, 65 or over).
- Informed consent; induction; delayed calcineurin inhibitor if DGF.
- **Urological point:** older kidneys have more urine leak and ureteric stricture.

**Diagram to draw:** Remuzzi grid with four compartments scored 0–3.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death.
> - KDPI >85% replaced ECD (2014).
> - Remuzzi 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 15 · Renal, Nephrology & Transplant

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Haemodialysis principles, dialysis techniques and dialysis access (vascular and PD) (LAQ)

- Diffusion clears small solutes, convection middle molecules, ultrafiltration water.
- HD Kt/V minimum 1.2 (target 1.4); URR ≥65%; PD weekly Kt/V ≥1.7.
- AVF first, distal, non-dominant arm, ≥6 months ahead; rule of 6s: >600 mL/min, >6 mm, <6 mm deep, 6 weeks.
- Thrombosis usually sits on a stenosis; steal is treated by (→) banding or DRIL.
- Tenckhoff: two cuffs, pelvic tip, downward exit.
- Laparoscopic placement with omentopexy gives best function.
- Avoid subclavian lines; AVF first for long-term HD.
- CRRT for unstable AKI; PD preferred in children.

#### Deceased (cadaver) kidney donor: selection, identification, maintenance, distribution and retrieval (LAQ)

- THOA: 4-member Board (Board of four), none from the transplant team; two tests ≥6 h apart; Form 10.
- Apnoea test: no breathing at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.
- ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death; KDPI >85%.
- Rule of 100s; vasopressin for hypotension and DI.
- Distal aortic cannula, supracoeliac clamp, IVC vent, cold UW/HTK and ice.
- Kidneys en bloc last (kidneys out last); Carrel patch, IVC for right vein; machine perfusion reduces DGF.

#### Renovascular hypertension and ischaemic nephropathy: renal vascular anatomy, pathophysiology, evaluation and treatment (LAQ)

- Five segmental end arteries; posterior segmental passes behind the pelvis; veins anastomose freely.
- Left renal vein 6–10 cm, receives adrenal, gonadal and lumbar veins.
- 2K1C = renin-dependent; 1K1C = volume-dependent.
- RVH is proved only by cure; duplex first, angiography with gradient before intervention.
- ASTRAL 2009 and CORAL 2014: stenting adds nothing to medical therapy in atherosclerotic RAS; no routine stenting.
- FMD gets balloon angioplasty without stent; ostial atheroma: stent.
- Revascularise for flash pulmonary oedema, declining GFR (function) in a viable kidney (RI <0.8), resistant hypertension.

#### Acute renal failure (AKI) and acute tubular necrosis (LAQ)

- KDIGO: creatinine ≥0.3 mg/dL or ×1.5; urine <0.5 mL/kg/h for 6 h.
- FeNa <1% prerenal, >1% ATN; specific gravity <1.010, muddy-brown casts in ATN; ultrasound to exclude obstruction.
- S3 segment and thick ascending limb are most vulnerable.
- Diuretics only for overload (do not treat ATN); dialysis for refractory AEIOU.
- Post-obstructive diuresis: oral fluids; replace 75% with 0.45% saline if needed.

#### Principles of vascular anastomosis and vascular access surgery for HD (LAQ)

- Intima-to-intima eversion, monofilament polypropylene, inside-out on the artery, flush before tying.
- AVF first, distal, non-dominant arm, about 6 months ahead.
- Rule of 6s: flow >600 mL/min, >6 mm wide, <6 mm deep, 6 weeks.
- Thrombosis sits on a stenosis; steal → banding or DRIL.

#### Role of vascular grafts in urology (SAQ)

- Transplant: Carrel patch, bench reconstruction; preserve the lower pole artery.
- IVC: patch if lumen <50%; PTFE replacement if wall invaded.
- Post-chemotherapy RPLND: IVC can be ligated, aorta must be grafted.

#### Pre-operative evaluation of the living (related and unrelated) renal donor (SAQ)

- KDIGO 2017: risk-based; GFR ≥90 accept; decline GFR <60 or ACR >100 (albuminuria >100 mg/g declines).
- ABO and crossmatch first; CT angiography for anatomy.
- Leave the better kidney with the donor (better kidney stays with donor).
- THOA: near relatives or authorisation committee; unrelated donor = Authorisation Committee approval.
- Interview video-recorded; no commerce.

#### UW solution and organ preservation solutions (SAQ)

- Hypothermia slows, not stops, metabolism.
- Lactobionate and raffinose (impermeants), HES / starch (colloid).
- Allopurinol, glutathione (antioxidants); adenosine (ATP).
- High K⁺: flush before reperfusion.
- HTK: histidine buffer, tryptophan, ketoglutarate, low viscosity, low K⁺.
- Machine perfusion reduces DGF.

#### CAPD (Continuous Ambulatory Peritoneal Dialysis) (SAQ)

- Peritoneum is the membrane; glucose or icodextrin gives (drives) ultrafiltration.
- Tenckhoff catheter with two cuffs (two-cuff); Kt/V ≥1.7 per week (weekly).
- Peritonitis: cloudy fluid, WCC >100/µL, >50% neutrophils; give intraperitoneal antibiotics.

#### Pre transplant nephrectomy (SAQ)

- Default: leave native kidneys.
- Indications: infection/VUR, ADPKD space, tumour, hypertension, proteinuria.
- Retroperitoneal route for PD; leave a normal ureter.

#### GFR estimation: clinical assessment and in the prospective transplant donor (SAQ)

- Creatinine overestimates GFR (secretion); CKD-EPI 2021 is race-free.
- Cystatin C adds accuracy; creatinine–cystatin C combined equation is most accurate.
- Inulin is the gold standard; iohexol or DTPA in practice; DTPA gives GFR and split function.
- KDIGO 2017: ≥90 accept, 60–89 individualise, <60 decline.

#### Post transplant malignancy (SAQ)

- Skin cancer commonest; squamous more than basal cell.
- PTLD: EBV-negative recipient of EBV-positive donor, first year.
- RCC mostly in native kidneys; bladder cancer 3-fold and aggressive.
- Reduce immunosuppression and switch to mTOR inhibitor.

#### Pre transplant Immunosupression treatment options (SAQ)

- Basiliximab for low risk; rATG for high immunological risk.
- HLA-incompatible: plasmapheresis + IVIG ± rituximab; imlifidase cleaves IgG.
- ABO-incompatible: rituximab + plasmapheresis to target titre.

#### Brainstem death: biology and clinical implications (SAQ)

- Raised ICP → Cushing reflex → storm → hypotension, DI, hypothermia, DIC.
- Six (brainstem) reflexes absent; apnoea at PaCO₂ ≥60 mmHg.
- Four-member Board; no transplant-team member.
- Two tests ≥6 h apart; Form 10; Rule of 100s.

#### Anomalies of renal artery. (SAQ)

- Single artery in 71–85%; accessory arteries usually lower pole.
- Crossing vessel can cause PUJ obstruction.
- Treat aneurysm >2 cm or in pregnancy.

#### Mycophenolate Mofetil. (SAQ)

- Prodrug of MPA; IMPDH inhibitor; lymphocytes need de novo purine synthesis.
- 1 g twice daily; ciclosporin lowers MPA levels.
- Toxicity: diarrhoea, leucopenia, CMV/BK, teratogenic; not nephrotoxic.

#### Metabolic abnormalities in CKD (SAQ)

- FGF-23 rises first; secondary then tertiary hyperparathyroidism.
- Bicarbonate ≥22 mEq/L; Hb 10–11.5 g/dL.
- Hyperkalaemia: binders rather than stopping RAAS blockade.

#### Renal transplant in neurogenic bladder (SAQ)

- Target <40 cm H₂O storage before transplant.
- Drugs → CIC → cycling → augmentation; reconstruct 6–12 weeks before.
- Pyelonephritis, not augmentation, loses grafts.

#### Pre-emptive renal transplant (SAQ)

- Transplant before dialysis = best outcome.
- Refer at eGFR <30; transplant usually at eGFR <20.
- Commonest with living donors and children.

#### THOA 1994 and its 2011 amendment (THOTA) and 2014 Rules (SAQ)

- THOA 1994: brain-stem death legal; anti-commercial; Authorisation Committee for unrelated donors.
- 2011: tissues added; grandparents and grandchildren became near relatives.
- Swap donation, retrieval centres, transplant coordinators (coordinator), required request, higher penalties.
- Commercial dealing: 5–10 years and ₹20 lakh to ₹1 crore.
- Rules 2014: Form 10; Board of four; tests ≥6 hours apart.

#### Marginal kidney donor (SAQ)

- ECD: ≥60, or 50–59 with 2 of hypertension, creatinine >1.5, CVA death.
- KDPI >85% replaced ECD (2014).
- Remuzzi 0–3 single, 4–6 dual, ≥7 discard.

# Section 16 · BPH — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## BPH: medical management and surgical modalities
**Asked as:**
- Describe medical management of BPH and role of various surgical modalities in its management. — *Summer 2015 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Medical treatment of BPH — *Practice Set 6 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)* (also answered in 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q1)

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction, pathophysiology and rationale
- **BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)** is stromal and epithelial hyperplasia of the transition zone; it may cause BPO (benign prostatic obstruction) and **LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms)**.
- Obstruction has a **static** component (gland bulk, DHT (dihydrotestosterone)-dependent / DHT-driven gland bulk) and a **dynamic** component (dynamic part: α1A-mediated smooth-muscle tone; static part: DHT-driven gland bulk). Drugs target each. The bladder responds with detrusor overactivity or later underactivity.

### Evaluation (before treatment)
- **IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score)** 0–35: mild 0–7, moderate 8–19, severe 20–35.
- DRE (digital rectal examination), urinalysis, **PSA (prostate-specific antigen)**, frequency-volume chart, Qmax (maximum flow), PVR (post-void residual); PVR >300 mL is managed as chronic retention (PVR >300 mL = chronic retention).
- Measure prostate volume before surgery (AUA (American Urological Association)); pressure-flow study if unclear.

### Medical management
| Option / class | Doses | Key facts |
|---|---|---|
| **Watchful waiting** | — | Mild or non-bothersome LUTS; lifestyle advice |
| **α1-blockers** (first line) | Tamsulosin 0.4 mg; alfuzosin 10 mg; silodosin 8 mg; doxazosin 4–8 mg (titrate) | First line; act within days; no effect on gland size, AUR (acute urinary retention) or surgery (do not prevent AUR or surgery). Hypotension, ejaculatory dysfunction; silodosin causes most anejaculation. **IFIS (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome)** |
| **5-ARIs (5α-reductase inhibitors)** | Finasteride 5 mg; dutasteride 0.5 mg | For glands (prostate) >30–40 mL or PSA >1.5 ng/mL. Shrink the gland about 18–25% (another source: ~20%) (maximal at 6 months; act after 6 months), halve PSA. PLESS (Proscar Long-Term Efficacy and Safety Study): AUR or surgery down (↓) 51%. Low libido, ED (erectile dysfunction). Also BPH haematuria |
| **Combination** | — | For high-risk men. The α-blocker gives early relief; the 5-ARI prevents hard events |
| **Antimuscarinic or β3-agonist** (mirabegron, vibegron) | Add-on | Add for persisting storage symptoms; trials included only men with low PVR (PVR <150 mL) |
| **Tadalafil 5 mg daily** | PDE5 (phosphodiesterase-5) inhibitor | LUTS with or without (±) ED; not with nitrates |
| **Desmopressin** | — | Nocturnal polyuria (night urine >33% of 24-h output); check (monitor) sodium, especially ≥65 years |

#### Combination therapy
- **MTOPS (Medical Therapy of Prostatic Symptoms)**: progression fell (↓) 39% (doxazosin), 34% (finasteride) and **66% (combination)**; only finasteride or combination (finasteride-containing arms) reduced AUR and surgery.
- **CombAT (Combination of Avodart and Tamsulosin)**: combination cut AUR or surgery by about 66% versus tamsulosin.
- Combine when **PV (prostate volume) >30–40 mL and PSA >1.5 ng/mL**; α-blocker may stop (withdrawable) after 6 months of combination (SMART (Symptoms Management After Reducing Therapy) trial).
- Tadalafil + finasteride fixed dose approved (FDA [Food and Drug Administration] 2021).

#### Side effects and important cautions
- **Side effects**: α-blockers cause dizziness, postural hypotension, ejaculatory dysfunction and **IFIS (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome)**, which persists after stopping, so avoid starting them before planned cataract surgery. 5-ARIs: low libido, ED, gynaecomastia.
- **IFIS:** billowing iris, prolapse, miosis; stopping the drug does not prevent it.
- 5-ARIs: double the PSA; not for cancer prevention (more high-grade tumours in PCPT [Prostate Cancer Prevention Trial] and REDUCE).
- **AUR**: catheterise (catheter), then TWOC (trial without catheter) on an α-blocker (ALFAUR (Alfuzosin in Acute Urinary Retention): success 62% vs 48%).
- **Also know:** phytotherapy (saw palmetto) not recommended by guidelines.

### Surgical management
**Indications**: refractory retention, recurrent UTI (urinary tract infection) or haematuria, bladder stones, renal insufficiency from BPO, failed medical therapy (surgery if refractory).

| Modality | Size (AUA 2023) | Key feature |
|---|---|---|
| **TURP (transurethral resection)**, monopolar or bipolar | 30–80 mL | Reference standard; retrograde ejaculation ~two-thirds |
| **TUIP (transurethral incision)** | ≤30 mL | Less retrograde ejaculation; more reoperation |
| **PVP (photoselective vaporization)** | ≤80 mL | Safe when anticoagulation cannot stop; no histology |
| **HoLEP/ThuLEP (laser enucleation)** | Any size | Lowest retreatment, least bleeding |
| **Simple prostatectomy** (open or robotic) | >80 mL | Complete removal; diverticula or stones |
| **Aquablation** | 30–80 mL (consider 80–150) | Heat-free; preserves ejaculation |
| **PUL (prostatic urethral lift), Rezūm (water vapour)** | 30–80 mL (PUL: no median lobe) | Preserve sexual function; higher retreatment |
| **iTind (temporary implant)** | 25–75 mL, no median lobe | Removed after a few days; modest efficacy |
| **PAE (prostate artery embolisation)** | Larger glands (AUA 2023: may be offered) | Local anaesthesia; less effective than TURP; radiation, non-target embolisation |

- **TUR syndrome** (monopolar TURP): hypo-osmolar glycine or water is absorbed through open sinuses, causing dilutional hyponatraemia, fluid overload and confusion. Risk rises with gland >45 mL and resection >90 minutes. Treat with diuretics and, if severe, 3% saline, correcting slowly. **Bipolar TURP** uses saline and eliminates it.
- **Complications**: bleeding, stricture, bladder neck contracture, retreatment.
- **GOLIATH**: 180 W PVP non-inferior to TURP. **WATER (Waterjet Ablation Therapy for Endoscopic Resection)**: Aquablation non-inferior, with less retrograde ejaculation (10% vs 36%).
- **Also know:** TUVP (transurethral vaporization); TUMT (transurethral microwave therapy) dropped as legacy; Optilume paclitaxel-coated balloon (2023).

### Follow-up
- Reassess at 4–12 weeks (α-blocker), 3–6 months (5-ARI) (another source: 6 months for 5-ARIs), then yearly; surgery if refractory.

**Diagram to draw:** Size-based surgical algorithm (≤30, 30–80, >80 mL); algorithm: LUTS → size, PSA, storage symptoms → drug choice.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - α-blockers: rapid relief, no prevention.
> - 5-ARIs: prevent AUR and surgery in glands >30–40 mL.
> - MTOPS: combination cut progression 66% (↓66%); only 5-ARI-containing arms cut AUR and surgery.
> - AUA sizes: TUIP ≤30; TURP/PVP/MISTs (minimally invasive surgical therapies) 30–80; HoLEP any size; simple prostatectomy >80 mL.
> - TUR syndrome: gland >45 mL, resection >90 min; bipolar TURP avoids it.

# Section 16 · BPH — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Millin's prostatectomy
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3c

### Definition
- **Retropubic extravesical simple prostatectomy** (Millin, 1945): enucleation through a transverse anterior capsulotomy without opening the bladder. The plane is the surgical capsule (compressed peripheral zone).

### Indications and work-up
- LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) or obstructive complications with gland **>80–100 g**, or when lithotomy is impossible. Prefer suprapubic (Freyer) for a large median lobe, diverticulum or stones.
- Work-up: exclude cancer (PSA (prostate-specific antigen), MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)), TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) volume, cystoscopy, ± urodynamics.

### Steps
1. Extraperitoneal approach to the **space of Retzius**; control the dorsal venous complex.
2. **Transverse capsulotomy ~2 cm below the bladder neck** (a longitudinal cut may extend into the sphincter).
3. **Finger enucleation** in the adenoma–capsule plane.
4. **Divide the apical urethra under vision**, sparing the sphincter.
5. Trim the bladder neck lip; advance mucosa into the fossa.
6. **Figure-of-eight sutures at 5 and 7 o'clock**, where prostatic arteries enter.
7. 22 Fr three-way catheter, **watertight capsule closure**, drain.

### Millin versus Freyer
| Aspect | **Millin (retropubic)** | **Freyer (suprapubic)** |
|---|---|---|
| Bladder | Not opened | Opened |
| Apex | Divided under vision | Pinched by finger |
| Fossa haemostasis | Directly visible | Less visible |
| Stones, diverticulum, median lobe | Difficult | Ideal |
| Specific risk | Dorsal venous bleeding | Cystotomy leak |

### Complications
- Bleeding (transfusion <5%), urine leak, infection, ureteric injury, bladder neck contracture (2–6%), stricture, thromboembolism.
- Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%; ED (erectile dysfunction) 3–5%; incontinence rare.
- **Also know:** bleeding control by catheter traction, Malament purse-string or O'Conor capsular plication; vesicocapsular variant; versus TURP (transurethral resection of prostate): lower retreatment, no TUR syndrome, longer stay.

### Modern alternatives
- HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation), robotic (multiport, single-port) prostatectomy: less bleeding.

**Diagram to draw:** Sagittal capsulotomy view.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Millin 1945: transverse capsulotomy ~2 cm below bladder neck; bladder not opened.
> - Apex divided under vision; 5 and 7 o'clock sutures.
> - Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%; transfusion <5%; bladder neck contracture 2–6%.

## Catheter free trial
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2016-Summer Paper 2, Q3e

*Assumed meaning: trial without catheter (TWOC) after acute urinary retention.*

### Definition
- **TWOC (trial without catheter)**: catheter removal after decompression to test whether the patient voids adequately.
- **AUR (acute urinary retention)**: sudden, painful inability to void; commonest in older men with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms).

### Types of AUR
| Spontaneous | Precipitated |
|---|---|
| BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) progression | Anaesthesia, surgery, UTI (urinary tract infection), overdistension, anticholinergic or sympathomimetic drugs |
| TWOC fails more often; about 75% later need surgery | Better TWOC outcome; about a quarter need surgery |

### Initial management
- Catheterise; record drained volume; check creatinine; treat the trigger.
- Start an **α1-blocker** (alfuzosin or tamsulosin).

### Rationale for α-blockers
- Sympathetic bladder-neck tone (dynamic component) adds to retention; α1-blockers relax it.
- **ALFAUR (Alfuzosin in Acute Urinary Retention)**: alfuzosin 10 mg for 3 days → TWOC success **62% vs 48%**.
- Meta-analysis: success relative risk about 1.5; fewer recurrences.

### Procedure
- Remove the catheter after **2–3 days of α-blocker**, usually in the morning (no evidence favours short or long catheterisation).
- Encourage fluids; record voided volumes and **PVR (post-void residual)** by bladder scan.
- **Success**: comfortable voiding, low residual (unit threshold). **Failure**: recatheterise or start CISC (clean intermittent self-catheterisation).

### Predictors of failure
- Advanced age; **drained volume >1 L**; spontaneous AUR; severe LUTS.
- Ultrasound: **IPP (intravesical prostatic protrusion) ≥10 mm**; thick bladder wall.

### Results
- Success about **60%**; a second or third TWOC still succeeds in over a quarter.
- Recurrence after success is common, mostly within **6 months**; then offer surgery.

### After TWOC
- **Success**: continue α-blocker; add a **5-ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor)** for large glands, because 5-ARIs, not α-blockers, prevent further AUR.
- **Failure**: TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) or other surgery; CISC or long-term catheter if unfit.
- **Also know:** immediate surgery has higher morbidity; TWOC also follows prostate surgery.

**Diagram to draw:** algorithm: AUR → catheter + α-blocker → TWOC at 2–3 days → success / failure.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - TWOC is first choice after AUR; success about 60%.
> - ALFAUR: alfuzosin 10 mg raises success to 62% vs 48%.
> - Fails more with age, >1 L drained, spontaneous AUR; 5-ARI prevents recurrence.

## Irrigation fluids for TURP.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Summer Paper 2, Q3a

### Ideal irrigant
- Transparent, **non-conducting** (monopolar), isotonic, non-toxic, non-haemolytic, cheap.
- **Why non-conducting?** In monopolar TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate), current flows loop → patient → pad; ionic saline would disperse it. **Bipolar** TURP completes the circuit in the instrument, so it uses **normal saline**.

### Fluids
| Fluid | Osmolality (approx.) | Points |
|---|---|---|
| Sterile water | 0 | Best vision, cheap; haemolysis, hyponatraemia; now avoided for TURP |
| **Glycine 1.5%** | ~200 mOsm/kg | Commonest (UK, North America); glycine → ammonia, serine; transient blindness (retinal inhibitory neurotransmitter), encephalopathy |
| Sorbitol 3% | ~165 | Metabolised to fructose and glucose → hyperglycaemia; lactic acidosis |
| Mannitol 5% | ~275 | Near iso-osmolar; not metabolised; osmotic diuresis; volume overload in renal failure |
| Sorbitol 2.7% + mannitol 0.54% | ~180 | Continental Europe |
| **Normal saline 0.9%** | ~308 | Bipolar TURP, lasers (HoLEP, PVP); no TUR syndrome |
- Plasma is about 290 mOsm/kg, so monopolar fluids are **hypo-osmolar**.

### TUR (transurethral resection) syndrome
- **Mechanism**: prostatic venous pressure is ~10 mmHg; irrigant at higher pressure enters open sinuses → volume overload, **dilutional hyponatraemia** and hypo-osmolality → cerebral oedema; plus glycine toxicity.
- **Features**: confusion, nausea, vomiting, visual disturbance, hypertension then hypotension, bradycardia, seizures, coma.
- **Risk factors**: gland >45 mL, resection >90 minutes (VA (Veterans Affairs) study), high irrigation pressure, open sinuses or capsular perforation.
- **Prevention**: bag height ~60 cm (each extra 10 cm more than doubles absorption), continuous-flow resectoscope, time limit, spinal anaesthesia (awake patient), bipolar TURP. Ethanol 1% tagging can monitor absorption.
- **Treatment**: stop surgery, check sodium; Na <120 mEq/L serious. Furosemide for overload; **3% saline** 100 mL boluses (max 300 mL) for neurological signs; raise sodium by 4–6 mEq/L initially, not over 12 mEq/L in 24 h (risk of central pontine myelinolysis).

### Bipolar advantage
- Meta-analyses: no TUR syndrome, less transfusion and clot retention; NNT (number needed to treat) about 50 to prevent one TUR syndrome.

**Diagram to draw:** monopolar vs bipolar current pathways.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Monopolar: non-ionic hypo-osmolar fluid; bipolar: saline.
> - Glycine 1.5%: ~200 mOsm/kg; transient blindness.
> - Risk: gland >45 mL, time >90 min; bag height 60 cm.
> - 3% saline; ≤12 mEq/L rise in 24 h.

## LASERS in BPH.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3a

### Principle
- LASER (light amplification by stimulated emission of radiation): the tissue effect depends on wavelength and **chromophore** (water or haemoglobin), which set **penetration depth**.
- About 60 °C coagulates tissue (delayed sloughing); ≥100 °C vaporises it (immediate removal).

### Lasers used in BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)
| Laser | Wavelength | Chromophore | Penetration | Procedure |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Ho:YAG (holmium:yttrium-aluminium-garnet) | ~2120 nm | Water | 0.4 mm | **HoLEP** (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate) |
| Thulium (Tm:YAG, TFL [thulium fibre laser]) | 2013 / 1940 nm | Water (TFL at peak) | 0.2 mm | ThuLEP (thulium laser enucleation), vaporesection, vaporisation |
| KTP (potassium titanyl phosphate) / LBO (lithium triborate) | 532 nm | Haemoglobin | 0.8 mm | **PVP** (photoselective vaporisation), 120–180 W |
| Diode | 940–1470 nm | Water and haemoglobin | 0.5–5 mm | Vaporisation, enucleation |
| Nd:YAG | 1064 nm | Weak | About 1 cm | Coagulation; abandoned (sloughing) |

### Enucleation versus vaporisation
| Feature | Enucleation (HoLEP, ThuLEP) | Vaporisation (PVP) |
|---|---|---|
| Tissue | Whole transition zone to the capsule, then morcellated | Removed layer by layer |
| Histology | Yes | No |
| Gland size | **Any (size-independent)** | Up to 80 cc |
| Retreatment | Rare | Higher, especially >80 cc |

### Evidence
- **HoLEP** (about 2 J × 40–50 Hz): the only endoscopic procedure **superior to TURP** (transurethral resection of prostate) for IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and Qmax. Less bleeding, shorter catheter time and stay.
- **GOLIATH RCT** (randomised controlled trial; 180 W PVP vs TURP, about 270 men, ≤100 cc): PVP non-inferior for IPSS, Qmax and complications at 2 years; shorter catheter time and stay.
- **ThuLEP:** outcomes equal to HoLEP. TFL energy is absorbed about four times more by water than holmium.
- **AUA (American Urological Association):** HoLEP or ThuLEP for any size; PVP for <80 cc; HoLEP, ThuLEP or PVP for men at **higher bleeding risk**.

### Complications
- Storage symptoms and dysuria; transient stress incontinence; retrograde ejaculation (about three-quarters after HoLEP); bladder neck contracture (0–3%); stricture; capsular perforation; morcellator bladder injury.
- **Safety:** wavelength-specific eyewear for all; laser safety officer.

**Diagram to draw:** HoLEP planes: enucleated adenoma pushed into the bladder, then morcellated.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Chromophore sets penetration: thulium 0.2, holmium 0.4, KTP 0.8 mm, Nd:YAG 1 cm.
> - HoLEP is size-independent and superior to TURP.
> - GOLIATH: PVP non-inferior to TURP; best for anticoagulated men.

## Silodosin.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2017-Winter Paper 2, Q3c

### Pharmacology
- The **most α1A-selective** blocker (well over 100-fold vs α1B; about 50-fold vs α1D), used for LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) due to BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).
- Sparing α1B gives **minimal hypotension**. Blocking α1A in the vas and seminal vesicles causes **failure of emission**.
- Onset: flow improves within 2–6 hours, symptoms within 3–4 days. Metabolised by CYP3A4.

### Dose and contraindications
| Item | Detail |
|---|---|
| Dose | **8 mg once daily with a meal** |
| CrCl (creatinine clearance) 30–50 mL/min | **4 mg daily** |
| Contraindicated | CrCl <30; severe liver impairment; strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (e.g. ketoconazole) |
| Caution | Other α-blockers, PDE5 (phosphodiesterase 5) inhibitors, planned cataract surgery |

### Efficacy
- Better than placebo for IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and flow, including severe LUTS. **Non-inferior to tamsulosin 0.4 mg**, not better (European trial, 2011).
- **Greatest fall in BOOI (bladder outlet obstruction index)** of all α1-blockers.
- Does not shrink the prostate; add a 5-ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) for large glands.

### Adverse effects
- **Anejaculation:** over a quarter of men in trials (about 14% vs 2% with tamsulosin head-to-head), yet few stop. Men with anejaculation had better responses.
- No excess hypotension in the elderly or with antihypertensives; no QT prolongation.
- **IFIS (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome):** not specifically studied but reported; warn before cataract surgery.

### Other uses
- **MET (medical expulsive therapy)** for distal ureteric stones 5–10 mm. α-blockers reduce ureteric spasm. Silodosin gave faster expulsion than tamsulosin in meta-analyses; **SUSPEND (2015)** found no overall tamsulosin benefit.
- **Also know:** before ureteroscopy (easier access); recent-onset CP/CPPS (chronic prostatitis/chronic pelvic pain syndrome).

| Drug | Selectivity | Dose | Hypotension | Ejaculatory dysfunction |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Silodosin** | α1A (highest) | 8 mg | Lowest | **Highest** |
| Tamsulosin | α1A, α1D > α1B | 0.4 mg | Low | Moderate |
| Alfuzosin | Clinically uroselective | 10 mg | Low | Lowest |
| Doxazosin, terazosin | Non-selective; titrate | 1–8 mg, 1–10 mg | Highest | Low |

**Diagram to draw:** α1A in prostate and vas, α1B in vessels, α1D in bladder.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 8 mg with a meal; 4 mg if CrCl 30–50; avoid if <30.
> - Equal to tamsulosin 0.4 mg; greatest BOOI fall.
> - Anejaculation is the signature effect; least hypotension.

## Dutasteride.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** 2018-Summer Paper 2, Q3c

### Pharmacology
- A **dual 5ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor)**: it blocks **type 1** (liver, skin) and **type 2** (prostate stroma, genital tissue) isoenzymes. Finasteride blocks type 2 only.
- **Mechanism:** blocks conversion of testosterone to DHT (dihydrotestosterone), the main prostatic androgen; serum DHT falls about **90%**. Epithelial atrophy shrinks the gland and relieves the **static** component of obstruction.
- **Dose:** **0.5 mg once daily**; hepatic metabolism; **half-life about 5 weeks**.

### Effects
- **Prostate volume falls about 25%** (maximum by 6 months).
- Symptoms and Qmax (maximum flow rate) improve modestly and slowly (**at least 6 months**).
- **Changes natural history:** pivotal trials: AUR (acute urinary retention) down 57%, BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) surgery down 48%.
- **PSA (prostate-specific antigen) halves** by 6–12 months: **double the PSA** to interpret it. A rise from the nadir on treatment needs evaluation.

### Indications
- Moderate-to-severe LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) with an enlarged prostate: **>40 mL (EAU [European Association of Urology])** or PSA about >1.5 ng/mL; AUA (American Urological Association): >30 mL.
- **Combination with tamsulosin** for men at risk of progression (fixed-dose capsule available).
- Off-label: BPH haematuria (lowers prostatic microvessel density); before TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) to reduce bleeding (inconsistent evidence).

### Landmark trials
| Trial | Result |
|---|---|
| **CombAT** (Combination of Avodart and Tamsulosin, 4 years; PV [prostate volume] ≥30 mL, PSA ≥1.5) | Combination best for symptoms and progression; dutasteride (and combination) beat tamsulosin for AUR and surgery |
| **REDUCE** (Reduction by Dutasteride of Prostate Cancer Events, 4 years) | Prostate cancer down about 23%, but slightly more Gleason 8–10; **not licensed for prevention** |

### Side effects
- **Sexual:** ED (erectile dysfunction), low libido, ejaculatory disorders; lessen with time.
- Gynaecomastia and breast tenderness; depression (FDA [Food and Drug Administration] warning).
- **No IFIS** (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome) and no hypotension, unlike α-blockers.

### Precautions
- **Teratogenic:** pregnant women must not handle leaking capsules. **No blood donation for 6 months** after the last dose.

**Diagram to draw:** testosterone to DHT pathway with 5α-reductase types 1 and 2 blocked by dutasteride.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Dual type 1 + 2 inhibitor; 0.5 mg; DHT down 90%; half-life about 5 weeks.
> - Volume down 25%; AUR and surgery down about half; needs 6 months.
> - Double the PSA; CombAT and REDUCE.

## Measures to decrease complications of TURP
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 9 Paper 2, Q3b
(TUR syndrome also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 3, Q3f)

### Introduction
- TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) is the benchmark; overall morbidity approaches one-fifth, mostly preventable.

### Preoperative measures
- **Selection:** AUA (American Urological Association) 2023: TURP for glands up to 80 mL; larger glands need HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation) or simple prostatectomy.
- **Sterile urine:** treat UTI (urinary tract infection) first; single-dose prophylaxis covering gram-negatives and enterococci.
- **Anticoagulants:** stop after advice from the prescriber; if they cannot be stopped, choose HoLEP, ThuLEP or PVP (photoselective vaporisation).
- **Finasteride** for recurrent BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) bleeding.
- Spinal anaesthesia shows early TUR syndrome; pad the peroneal nerve.

### Intraoperative measures
| Complication | Prevention |
|---|---|
| **TUR syndrome** (hypo-osmolar fluid absorbed, dilutional hyponatraemia) | **Bipolar TURP with saline**; else irrigant ≤60 cm high, resection <90 min, avoid capsular perforation, check sodium in long resections |
| **Bleeding** | Systematic resection to capsule; control arterial bleeders as you go; bipolar "cut and seal"; catheter traction for venous ooze |
