| **Ureteric orifice injury** | Identify orifices first; take a large median lobe down first |
| **Perforation** | Respect the capsule; visible fat means perforation |
| **Incontinence** | **Stop at the verumontanum**; resect apex last in a bloodless field |
| **Stricture** | Calibrate or dilate meatus, lubricate generously, correct sheath size |
| **Bladder neck contracture** | Avoid over-resection and excess fulguration; TUIP (transurethral incision of the prostate) for small glands |

### Postoperative measures
- Large-bore three-way catheter; careful **continuous bladder irrigation** (blocked outflow distends the bladder and worsens bleeding).
- Even routine cases absorb 800–1000 mL of irrigant; check sodium if confused.
- TUR syndrome treatment: **3% saline 100 mL** boluses; raise sodium by no more than 12 mEq/L in 24 h (pontine myelinolysis).
- Stool softeners; no cycling for 4–6 weeks; counsel about **retrograde ejaculation** (about two-thirds).

**Diagram to draw:** resection field from bladder neck to verumontanum with the external sphincter marked.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bipolar saline TURP removes TUR syndrome risk; fewer transfusions.
> - Monopolar: irrigant ≤60 cm, resection <90 min, gland <45 mL safer.
> - Verumontanum is the distal limit.

## Tamsulosin
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 2, Q3d

### Introduction
- A **uroselective α1-adrenergic antagonist**, first-line drug for bothersome male LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) due to BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) (AUA [American Urological Association], EAU [European Association of Urology]).

### Receptor basis
| Subtype | Site | Effect of blockade |
|---|---|---|
| **α1a** (about 70% in prostate) | Prostate, bladder neck, prostatic urethra, iris dilator | Relieves dynamic obstruction; ejaculatory dysfunction; IFIS (intraoperative floppy iris syndrome) |
| α1b | Blood vessels | Hypotension (tamsulosin has 10-fold less affinity) |
| α1d | Detrusor, spinal afferents | Storage symptom relief |

### Mechanism
- Blocks noradrenaline at α1a receptors → relaxes prostatic and bladder-neck smooth muscle → lowers the **dynamic component** of BOO (bladder outlet obstruction). It does not shrink the prostate.

### Pharmacology and dose
- **0.4 mg once daily** after the same meal (up to 0.8 mg); no titration.
- Modified release; hepatic metabolism (CYP3A4, CYP2D6); avoid strong CYP3A4 inhibitors (ketoconazole).
- Flow rises within 4–8 hours, symptoms by 1 week; 0.2 mg is not better than placebo.

### Indications
1. **BPH/LUTS:** IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) falls about 30–40%; Qmax rises; storage and nocturia improve.
2. **Combination with 5ARI** (5α-reductase inhibitor) for prostate >30–40 mL: **CombAT** (dutasteride + tamsulosin) and MTOPS (combination cut progression by 66%).
3. **MET (medical expulsive therapy):** distal ureteric stones 5–10 mm; SUSPEND (2015) negative overall but included all sizes.
4. **AUR (acute urinary retention):** before trial without catheter.
5. **Also know:** ureteric stent symptoms; female functional outlet obstruction.

### Adverse effects
- **Ejaculatory dysfunction** (dose-dependent; less than silodosin): retrograde ejaculation from bladder-neck relaxation, or anejaculation (emission failure).
- **IFIS:** floppy, billowing iris, iris prolapse, progressive miosis during cataract surgery; **highest risk with tamsulosin**. Stopping the drug does not remove the risk, so warn the ophthalmologist.
- Dizziness, rhinitis, headache; less orthostatic hypotension than non-selective agents. Caution: sulfonamide allergy.

### Comparison
| | Tamsulosin | Silodosin | Alfuzosin |
|---|---|---|---|
| Selectivity | α1a/α1d | Most α1a | Clinical only |
| Ejaculatory effect | Moderate | Highest | Lowest |

**Diagram to draw:** prostate cross-section showing α1a receptors in stroma and bladder neck.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - 0.4 mg once daily; α1a/α1d selective; no titration.
> - IFIS highest with tamsulosin; persists after stopping.
> - MET benefit in distal stones 5–10 mm.

## Prostatic stents
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 2, Q3f
(urethral stents also answered in Practice Set 4 Paper 4, Q3f and 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3d)

### Definition
- A device placed in the **prostatic urethra**, between bladder neck and verumontanum, to relieve BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) without resection. Mainly for men **unfit for surgery**.

### Classification

| Type | Examples | Features |
|---|---|---|
| **Temporary, first generation** | Urospiral, Prostakath (spirals) | Steel or gold-plated coil; not epithelialised |
| **Temporary, second generation** | **Memokath** (thermo-expandable nitinol); Allium (covered nitinol) | Expands with warm water, softens with cold water for removal |
| Temporary polyurethane | Intraurethral catheter, Spanner | Bulbar anchor; sphincter left free |
| Biodegradable | Polylactic acid stents | Dissolve; trial use |
| **Permanent, epithelialising** | **UroLume** (woven mesh) | Urothelium covers the mesh; withdrawn |

### Indications
- Retention or severe BOO from BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) in men unfit for surgery: avoids a long-term catheter.
- Drainage after thermal treatments; trial of de-obstruction.
- **Also know:** DSD (detrusor–sphincter dyssynergia), now abandoned; bulbar stricture.

### Contraindications
- Poor detrusor contractility (do urodynamics if in doubt); untreated UTI (urinary tract infection); large median lobe; bladder stones; stricture.

### Technique
- Measure prostatic urethral length cystoscopically. Place under local anaesthesia with endoscopic or fluoroscopic guidance. The stent must **end above the external sphincter**, or incontinence follows.

### Complications
- **Migration** (commonest with temporary stents); **encrustation**; UTI; irritative symptoms, perineal pain, haematuria.
- **Hyperplastic ingrowth** and restenosis with permanent stents (difficult removal); incontinence if the sphincter is crossed.

### Current status
- Most unfit men void after placement, but many stents are later removed.
- **EAU (European Association of Urology):** alternative to catheterisation in men unfit for surgery (weak). Not in the **AUA (American Urological Association) 2023** BPH list.
- **Recent:** **iTind**: temporary nitinol device left 5–7 days; it remodels the urethra by ischaemic pressure necrosis. AUA 2023 option for 25–75 mL without median lobe. **Optilume BPH** (paclitaxel balloon).

**Diagram to draw:** sagittal prostate with a Memokath from bladder neck to verumontanum, above the external sphincter.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Temporary (Memokath) vs permanent (UroLume, withdrawn).
> - Need detrusor contractility; never cross the sphincter.
> - Migration, encrustation, ingrowth; iTind is the modern temporary device.

## Role of UDS before prostate surgery
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 2, Q3a

### Why consider UDS (urodynamic study)
- Symptoms, flow rate, PVR (post-void residual), prostate volume and cystoscopy **cannot separate BOO (bladder outlet obstruction) from DUA (detrusor underactivity)**: low Qmax (maximum flow rate) occurs in both. DUA is found in 9–48% of men with LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms) studied.
- Surgery helps obstruction, not a weak bladder. **PFS (pressure–flow study) is the gold standard** for BOO.

### Components
- **Filling cystometry:** sensation, compliance, **DO (detrusor overactivity)**.
- **PFS:** detrusor pressure at Qmax plotted against flow (ICS (International Continence Society) nomogram).

| Index | Formula | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| BOOI (bladder outlet obstruction index) | PdetQmax − 2Qmax | >40 obstructed; 20–40 equivocal; <20 unobstructed |
| BCI (bladder contractility index) | PdetQmax + 5Qmax | >150 strong; 100–150 normal; <100 weak |

- High pressure, low flow = BOO; low pressure, low flow = DUA.
- **Videourodynamics** adds anatomy (diverticula, reflux).

### Indications before BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) surgery (EAU (European Association of Urology))
- Previous failed invasive treatment.
- Cannot void >150 mL (unreliable flow).
- PVR >300 mL.
- Age >80 years with mainly voiding LUTS.
- Age <50 years with mainly voiding LUTS.
- **Also:** suspected neurogenic bladder, prior pelvic surgery or radiation, Qmax >10 mL/s (AUA (American Urological Association): diagnostic uncertainty).

### How results change management
- **Unobstructed or DUA:** poorer surgical results; counsel, consider CIC (clean intermittent catheterisation).
- **DO with BOO:** storage symptoms persist in about one-third after TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate); warn the patient.
- **Low compliance:** the only "absolute" urodynamic indication to relieve BOO (protects upper tracts).

### Evidence and limitations
- Not routine. **UPSTREAM RCT (randomised controlled trial):** routine UDS did not change IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) or surgery rates.
- Invasive: UTI (urinary tract infection), discomfort, cost. Before **radical prostatectomy**, not routine.

**Diagram to draw:** ICS nomogram with obstructed, equivocal and unobstructed zones.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PFS is the only test that separates BOO from DUA.
> - BOOI >40 obstructed; BCI <100 weak.
> - EAU indications: failed surgery, <150 mL voided, PVR >300 mL, age <50 or >80.
> - UPSTREAM: routine UDS did not change outcomes.

## Monopolar v/s Bipolar TURP.
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 15 Paper 2, Q3a

### Introduction
- TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) removes transition-zone tissue from bladder neck to verumontanum, down to the surgical capsule; the benchmark BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) operation.
- **AUA (American Urological Association):** no preference; choose by expertise.

### Principles
- **Monopolar (m-TURP):** current runs from the loop through the patient to a grounding pad. Saline would disperse the current, so a **non-conducting, hypo-osmolar irrigant** (glycine 1.5%, sorbitol–mannitol, water) is needed.
- **Bipolar (b-TURP):** active and return poles are on the same instrument, so current stays local and **normal saline** can be used. The Plasma system turns saline into a plasma layer (orange glow) that cuts at lower temperature and seals vessels.

### Comparison

| Feature | Monopolar | Bipolar |
|---|---|---|
| Current path | Loop → patient → pad | Within the loop |
| Irrigant | Glycine, sorbitol, water | Normal saline |
| TUR syndrome | Yes | Practically eliminated |
| Resection time | Limited (speed over haemostasis) | No time pressure; large glands |
| Loop | Tungsten | Platinum–iridium (Plasma) |
| Bleeding, transfusion, clot retention | Higher | Lower |
| Catheter time, hospital stay | Longer | Shorter |
| Obturator reflex | Risk | Reduced |
| IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score), QoL, PVR | Equivalent (Qmax marginally better with bipolar) | Equivalent |
| Stricture, BNC (bladder neck contracture), sexual function, retreatment | Similar | Similar |

### TUR syndrome (monopolar)
- Irrigant absorbed through open sinuses (venous pressure about 10 mmHg) → **dilutional hyponatraemia**: confusion, visual disturbance, nausea, bradycardia, hypertension, seizures; glycine also yields ammonia.
- **Risk factors:** gland >45 mL, resection >90 minutes, high irrigation bag.
- **Treatment:** stop surgery, check sodium; 3% saline 100 mL boluses for neurological symptoms; avoid over-rapid correction (central pontine myelinolysis).

### Evidence
- Meta-analyses: equal efficacy up to 5 years; b-TURP safer (NNT about 50 to prevent one TUR syndrome, about 20 to prevent one clot retention).
- **Also know:** TURis (sheath as return) caused strictures; bipolar enucleation and vaporisation (PlasmaButton).

**Diagram to draw:** monopolar circuit (loop → patient → pad) versus bipolar (loop to return electrode on the same resectoscope).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Bipolar allows saline; TUR syndrome practically eliminated.
> - Equal efficacy; bipolar has less bleeding and shorter catheter time.
> - TUR syndrome risk: >45 mL gland, >90 minutes.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 16 · BPH

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### BPH: medical management and surgical modalities (LAQ)

- α-blockers: rapid relief, no prevention.
- 5-ARIs: prevent AUR and surgery in glands >30–40 mL.
- MTOPS: combination cut progression 66% (↓66%); only 5-ARI-containing arms cut AUR and surgery.
- AUA sizes: TUIP ≤30; TURP/PVP/MISTs (minimally invasive surgical therapies) 30–80; HoLEP any size; simple prostatectomy >80 mL.
- TUR syndrome: gland >45 mL, resection >90 min; bipolar TURP avoids it.

#### Millin's prostatectomy (SAQ)

- Millin 1945: transverse capsulotomy ~2 cm below bladder neck; bladder not opened.
- Apex divided under vision; 5 and 7 o'clock sutures.
- Retrograde ejaculation 80–90%; transfusion <5%; bladder neck contracture 2–6%.

#### Catheter free trial (SAQ)

- TWOC is first choice after AUR; success about 60%.
- ALFAUR: alfuzosin 10 mg raises success to 62% vs 48%.
- Fails more with age, >1 L drained, spontaneous AUR; 5-ARI prevents recurrence.

#### Irrigation fluids for TURP. (SAQ)

- Monopolar: non-ionic hypo-osmolar fluid; bipolar: saline.
- Glycine 1.5%: ~200 mOsm/kg; transient blindness.
- Risk: gland >45 mL, time >90 min; bag height 60 cm.
- 3% saline; ≤12 mEq/L rise in 24 h.

#### LASERS in BPH. (SAQ)

- Chromophore sets penetration: thulium 0.2, holmium 0.4, KTP 0.8 mm, Nd:YAG 1 cm.
- HoLEP is size-independent and superior to TURP.
- GOLIATH: PVP non-inferior to TURP; best for anticoagulated men.

#### Silodosin. (SAQ)

- 8 mg with a meal; 4 mg if CrCl 30–50; avoid if <30.
- Equal to tamsulosin 0.4 mg; greatest BOOI fall.
- Anejaculation is the signature effect; least hypotension.

#### Dutasteride. (SAQ)

- Dual type 1 + 2 inhibitor; 0.5 mg; DHT down 90%; half-life about 5 weeks.
- Volume down 25%; AUR and surgery down about half; needs 6 months.
- Double the PSA; CombAT and REDUCE.

#### Measures to decrease complications of TURP (SAQ)

- Bipolar saline TURP removes TUR syndrome risk; fewer transfusions.
- Monopolar: irrigant ≤60 cm, resection <90 min, gland <45 mL safer.
- Verumontanum is the distal limit.

#### Tamsulosin (SAQ)

- 0.4 mg once daily; α1a/α1d selective; no titration.
- IFIS highest with tamsulosin; persists after stopping.
- MET benefit in distal stones 5–10 mm.

#### Prostatic stents (SAQ)

- Temporary (Memokath) vs permanent (UroLume, withdrawn).
- Need detrusor contractility; never cross the sphincter.
- Migration, encrustation, ingrowth; iTind is the modern temporary device.

#### Role of UDS before prostate surgery (SAQ)

- PFS is the only test that separates BOO from DUA.
- BOOI >40 obstructed; BCI <100 weak.
- EAU indications: failed surgery, <150 mL voided, PVR >300 mL, age <50 or >80.
- UPSTREAM: routine UDS did not change outcomes.

#### Monopolar v/s Bipolar TURP. (SAQ)

- Bipolar allows saline; TUR syndrome practically eliminated.
- Equal efficacy; bipolar has less bleeding and shorter catheter time.
- TUR syndrome risk: >45 mL gland, >90 minutes.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Long answer questions (LAQ)

## Castration-resistant prostate cancer (CRPC): definition and treatment
**Asked as:**
- CRPC in Ca prostate. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*
- Discuss treatment of CRPC — *Practice Set 6 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3a)

### Definition (EAU [European Association of Urology])
- CRPC (castration-resistant prostate cancer): castrate testosterone **<50 ng/dL (1.7 nmol/L)** plus either:
  - **Biochemical progression:** three consecutive PSA (prostate-specific antigen) rises 1 week apart, two of them 50% above nadir, with PSA >2 ng/mL; or
  - **Radiological progression:** ≥2 new bone lesions, or soft-tissue progression by RECIST (Response Evaluation Criteria in Solid Tumours).

### Why it happens: the AR still drives growth
- Castration-resistant is **not** androgen-independent, so treatment first targets the AR (androgen receptor) more deeply.
- Mechanisms: AR overexpression and amplification; AR mutation (activated by other steroids); **splice variants lacking the ligand-binding domain**; adrenal and intratumoural androgen synthesis (CYP17 overexpressed); bypass pathways (PI3K/AKT, PTEN loss in ~40%).
- **Lineage plasticity:** neuroendocrine change with low PSA, visceral and lytic disease (visceral disease).

### Evaluation
- Testosterone (exclude non-compliance), PSA, **PSADT (PSA doubling time)**; CT and bone scan; PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET increasingly.
- Prognosis: performance status, pain, haemoglobin, ALP (alkaline phosphatase), LDH (lactate dehydrogenase), visceral disease.
- **Germline testing** in all metastatic disease; somatic sequencing of tumour tissue (germline and tumour testing) for HRR (homologous recombination repair) genes such as BRCA2, and MSI (microsatellite instability).
- Biopsy a metastasis if disease progresses fast with a low PSA (neuroendocrine change).
- PCWG3 (Prostate Cancer Clinical Trials Working Group 3): early bone-scan lesions may be **flare**; re-baseline at 12–16 weeks, then progression = ≥2 new lesions.

**Clinical states model (PCWG3):**
```
Localised → rising PSA → mCSPC (castration-sensitive)
                  ↓ (on ADT)
              nmCRPC → mCRPC 1st line → 2nd line → 3rd line
```

### General principles of management
1. **Continue ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) lifelong**, including during chemotherapy; try antiandrogen withdrawal.
2. **Antiandrogen withdrawal** (e.g. bicalutamide): watch PSA 4–8 weeks; brief responses in a minority.
3. Choose by prior therapy, symptoms, visceral disease, genomics and comorbidity.
4. **Sequencing: switch mechanism**; a second ARPI rarely works (cross-resistance). Cross-resistance means a second ARPI (AR-pathway inhibitor) helps only 15–30%.

### nmCRPC (non-metastatic)
- **PSADT ≤10 months:** add an ARPI. Each improved MFS (metastasis-free survival) and overall survival (improve metastasis-free and overall survival):
  - Apalutamide 240 mg (**SPARTAN**: MFS HR 0.28); enzalutamide 160 mg (**PROSPER**); darolutamide 300 mg twice daily (**ARAMIS**: MFS 40.4 vs 18.4 months).
- Slower PSADT: continue ADT and monitor.

### mCRPC: life-prolonging options
| Class | Agent and dose | Key trial (overall survival) |
|---|---|---|
| ARPI (AR-pathway inhibitor) | **Abiraterone** 1000 mg fasting + prednisone | COU-AA-301: 14.8 vs 10.9 months; COU-AA-302 (pre-chemotherapy) |
| | **Enzalutamide** 160 mg | AFFIRM: 18.4 vs 13.6 months; PREVAIL |
| Taxane | **Docetaxel** 75 mg/m² 3-weekly + prednisone | TAX 327: 18.9 vs 16.4 months (mitoxantrone) |
| | **Cabazitaxel** 20–25 mg/m² after docetaxel | TROPIC: 15.1 vs 12.7; CARD: beats a second ARPI |
| Radioligand (mCRPC) | **Radium-223** 50 kBq/kg 4-weekly ×6 (symptomatic bone-only, no visceral) | ALSYMPCA: 14.9 vs 11.3 months |
| | **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617** (PSMA-positive, after ARPI + taxane) | VISION: 15.3 vs 11.3 months |
| PARP (poly-ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitor | Olaparib (HRR genes); rucaparib (BRCA) | PROfound; TRITON3 |
| | Olaparib + abiraterone; niraparib + abiraterone; talazoparib + enzalutamide (first line) | PROpel; MAGNITUDE; TALAPRO-2 |
| Immunotherapy | **Sipuleucel-T** (asymptomatic, no visceral disease) | IMPACT: 25.8 vs 21.7 months |
| | Pembrolizumab for MSI-high/dMMR (mismatch repair-deficient), 2–5% | Tumour-agnostic approval |

- **Abiraterone** blocks CYP17, which also lowers cortisol. ACTH (adrenocorticotropic hormone) then rises and causes mineralocorticoid excess (hypokalaemia, hypertension, oedema), so prednisone is co-prescribed. Never combine with radium-223 (more deaths and fractures).
- **Enzalutamide:** seizures (~1%), fatigue, falls. Apalutamide: rash, hypothyroidism.
- **PARP inhibitors** cause synthetic lethality in HRR-deficient cells; benefit is greatest with BRCA2 and weak with ATM.

### Sequencing by prior mCSPC therapy
| Prior therapy | Next option |
|---|---|
| Docetaxel only | ARPI |
| ARPI only | Docetaxel, radium-223, sipuleucel-T |
| Both | Cabazitaxel, ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA, PARP inhibitor if HRR mutation |

### Neuroendocrine/aggressive variant
- Neuroendocrine variant: low PSA, liver or brain metastases, lytic bone lesions with hypercalcaemia. Treat with **platinum + etoposide** (or carboplatin + taxane) ± radiotherapy; survival <12 months.

### Supportive and palliative care
- **Bone health:** denosumab 120 mg SC or zoledronic acid 4 mg IV (renal dose adjustment) every 4 weeks against skeletal events, with calcium and vitamin D. Denosumab delayed the first SRE (skeletal-related event) longer (20.7 vs 17.1 months); neither prolongs life. Risk: osteonecrosis of the jaw.
- Focal bone pain: external-beam radiotherapy (radiotherapy for bone pain).
- **Cord compression** needs high-dose dexamethasone → urgent MRI → radiotherapy ± decompression (Patchell trial: surgery + radiotherapy better).
- Ureteric obstruction: stent or nephrostomy. Anaemia; hospice care.

### Recent advances
- PSMA-ligand therapy earlier (PSMAfore); AR degraders (ARV-110); AKT inhibition in PTEN loss; bipolar androgen therapy; antibody–drug conjugates.

**Diagram to draw:** clinical states: rising PSA → nmCRPC → mCRPC first, second, third line.

**Diagram to draw:** clinical states model with drugs placed at each state.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Testosterone <50 ng/dL with PSA or radiological progression; keep ADT going.
> - AR still drives CRPC.
> - nmCRPC with PSADT ≤10 months: apalutamide, enzalutamide or darolutamide.
> - mCRPC: ARPI, taxanes (docetaxel, cabazitaxel), radium-223, ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA, PARP inhibitors, sipuleucel-T.
> - Switch mechanism; never radium-223 with abiraterone.

## Metastatic prostate cancer with bone metastases (retention and bone pain; bone mets; zoledronic acid)
**Asked as:**
- Treatment of metastatic CA prostate in 60 yr male with retention of urine and bone pain — *Practice Set 1 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Management of bone mets in prostatic CA — *Practice Set 3 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Zolendronic acid — *Practice Set 14 Paper II Q3b (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)  
(mHSPC systemic therapy also answered in 2025-Winter Paper 3, Q3b)
(mHSPC with retention and bone pain also answered in Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q1; related: bone metastases in Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q2)

*Zolendronic acid: read as zoledronic acid.*

### Introduction
- Bone is the commonest metastatic site; visceral metastasis occurs in <20%.
- Aims: control the cancer, relieve pain, **prevent SREs (skeletal-related events)** and treat emergencies (cord compression, fracture).
- **Retention + bone pain case:** this is newly diagnosed (synchronous) **mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer)** with two urgent problems: an obstructed outlet and painful bone metastases. Treat the emergencies first, start castration safely (no flare), then intensify systemic therapy.

### Pathophysiology
- Tumour cells lodge in axial marrow, adhering through integrins and collagen receptors.
- Metastases are mainly **osteoblastic (sclerotic)**, but resorption also rises. Tumour cytokines induce **RANKL (receptor activator of nuclear factor-κB ligand)**, which activates osteoclasts; released growth factors feed the tumour (**vicious cycle**: tumour cytokines activate osteoclasts, and resorbed bone releases growth factors that feed the tumour). ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) adds osteoporosis through loss of oestrogen.
- **SREs:** pathological fracture, spinal cord compression, need for radiotherapy or surgery to bone. Marrow replacement causes anaemia. Hypercalcaemia is rare and suggests neuroendocrine disease.

### Evaluation (immediate assessment)
- **Clinical:** pain, neurological signs, performance status. **Ask and examine for cord compression:** back pain, leg weakness, sensory level, bowel symptoms. DRE (digital rectal examination).
- **Bloods:** PSA (prostate-specific antigen), ALP (alkaline phosphatase), calcium, creatinine, haemoglobin, testosterone.
- **Imaging:** ultrasound for hydronephrosis and residual; **bone scan and CT (computed tomography)** define volume; PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET is more sensitive, but trials used conventional imaging. **Plain X-ray or CT of painful weight-bearing sites** (lytic lesion, impending fracture). **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) of the whole spine (whole-spine MRI)** if any back pain or neurological sign (for back pain with neurological signs).
- **Histology:** prostate biopsy (or TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) chips). **Germline genetic testing** for all men with metastatic disease.
- **Volume (CHAARTED):** high volume = ≥4 bone metastases with ≥1 outside the axial skeleton, or visceral metastasis.

### Step 1: Relieve the retention
- **Urethral catheter** (suprapubic if urethral fails). Watch for post-obstructive diuresis.
- Persisting renal impairment with hydronephrosis means ureteric obstruction by tumour at the trigone: **nephrostomy or stent** (stent or nephrostomy for obstruction).
- ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) shrinks the prostate, so try **TWOC (trial without catheter)** after a few months of ADT.
- If TWOC fails or symptoms persist: **channel (palliative) TURP**. ADT alone does not prevent local progression (older series: about a third needed TURP within 60 days of orchidectomy).

### Step 2 / 1. Systemic treatment of the cancer
**Hormone-sensitive (mHSPC): start ADT at once, without flare**
- **ADT** (orchidectomy, LHRH [luteinising hormone-releasing hormone] agonist or antagonist):

| Option | Point |
|---|---|
| **Bilateral orchidectomy** | Castrate within hours; best for impending cord compression |
| **GnRH (gonadotropin-releasing hormone) antagonist**: degarelix 240 mg subcutaneously, then 80 mg monthly; oral relugolix | **No flare**; castrate by day 3 in >95% (degarelix) |
| **LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) agonist + antiandrogen** | Agonist raises LH (luteinising hormone) up to 10-fold (raises testosterone) for 10–20 days (**flare**). Give bicalutamide 50 mg daily with or before the agonist, for 21–28 days |

- **Why avoid flare:** the testosterone surge can worsen bone pain, retention and cord compression in spinal metastases. Cover it with an antiandrogen for 21–28 days, or use an antagonist (degarelix) or orchidectomy.
- Target castrate testosterone **<50 ng/dL**. Antiandrogen alone is not recommended.

**Intensify systemic therapy (mHSPC)**
- ADT alone is no longer standard. Classify volume (CHAARTED, above).

| Add to ADT | Trial (overall survival HR (hazard ratio)) |
|---|---|
| Abiraterone 1000 mg + prednisone 5 mg | LATITUDE (0.62); STAMPEDE arm G |
| Enzalutamide or apalutamide (ARPIs (androgen receptor pathway inhibitors)) | ENZAMET (0.67), ARCHES (enzalutamide); TITAN (0.67) |
| Docetaxel 75 mg/m² × 6 + abiraterone or darolutamide (**triplet**) | PEACE-1; ARASENS (0.68) |

- Hormonal agents work regardless of volume; docetaxel helps only high volume, so it is now given within a triplet.
- **Low-volume synchronous disease:** add **RT to the prostate** (**prostate radiotherapy**; STAMPEDE arm H, 3-year survival 81% vs 73%); it also helps local control.

**Castration-resistant (mCRPC):**
- ARPIs (androgen receptor pathway inhibitors), docetaxel then cabazitaxel, PARP (poly-ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors for HRR (homologous recombination repair) mutations.
- **Radium-223** (alpha emitter, path <100 µm, spares marrow): for **symptomatic bone-only** disease (licensed only in castration-resistant disease); 50 kBq/kg every 4 weeks × 6. **ALSYMPCA:** overall survival 14.9 vs 11.3 months; delayed SREs. **Never with abiraterone** (more fractures and deaths).
- **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617** (beta emitter, PSMA-avid disease at any site): after ARPI and taxane; **VISION:** survival 15.3 vs 11.3 months.

### Step 3 / 3. Bone pain
- **Analgesia** by the WHO (World Health Organization) ladder (analgesic ladder); neuropathic pain needs gabapentinoids or tricyclics; steroids for diffuse pain.
- **Focal pain: EBRT (external beam radiotherapy) single 8 Gy fraction.** Pain relief equals multiple fractions (30 Gy in 10 fractions, RTOG 9714); retreatment is commoner.
- X-ray or CT of painful weight-bearing sites to exclude lytic lesions and impending fracture; **orthopaedic fixation** then RT (radiotherapy).
- **Diffuse pain:** steroids, wide-field RT; radiopharmaceuticals (strontium-89, samarium-153 relieve pain only; radium-223 adds survival). Radium-223 is licensed only in castration-resistant disease.
- **SBRT (stereotactic body RT)** for oligometastases (STOMP, ORIOLE) and spinal re-irradiation.

### 2. Bone-targeted agents

| Feature | Zoledronic acid | Denosumab |
|---|---|---|
| Mechanism / target | Bisphosphonate: inhibits osteoclasts (osteoclast enzyme) | Antibody against RANKL (receptor activator of nuclear factor-κB ligand) |
| SRE dose (mCRPC) / route | 4 mg IV every 4 weeks (IV 4-weekly), adjust for creatinine clearance | 120 mg SC (subcutaneous) every 4 weeks (4-weekly), no renal adjustment |
| Renal adjustment | Yes | No |
| Evidence | Fewer SREs than placebo | Time to first SRE 20.7 vs 17.1 months vs zoledronate |
| Toxicity | Fever, myalgia, renal impairment, hypocalcaemia, ONJ | Hypocalcaemia, hypophosphataemia, ONJ 2–4% |

- **No survival benefit** (**neither improves survival**); they protect the skeleton only.
- **In mHSPC, SRE doses are not indicated** (no benefit, e.g. CALGB 90202, STAMPEDE). SRE doses (zoledronic acid 4 mg or denosumab 120 mg 4-weekly) are for CRPC (castration-resistant prostate cancer). Use osteoporosis doses for ADT bone loss: denosumab 60 mg every 6 months (6-monthly) or zoledronic acid yearly, by DEXA (dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry) and FRAX; these protect against ADT osteoporosis.
- **ONJ (osteonecrosis of the jaw) prevention:** dental check before starting; calcium 1000 mg and vitamin D 800 IU daily.
- Give a bone agent with radium-223 or any ARPI.

#### Zoledronic acid (in detail)
- **Introduction:** a potent IV (intravenous) **nitrogen-containing bisphosphonate**.
- **Mechanism:**
  - Binds hydroxyapatite at sites of active bone turnover and is taken up by **osteoclasts**.
  - Inhibits **farnesyl pyrophosphate synthase** (mevalonate pathway), causing osteoclast dysfunction and apoptosis, so resorption falls.
  - Breaks the "vicious cycle".
  - Excreted unchanged by the kidneys.

| Indication (indications and doses) | Dose |
|---|---|
| **SRE (skeletal-related event) prevention, mCRPC (metastatic castration-resistant prostate cancer)** and other bone metastases (RCC (renal cell carcinoma), bladder) | **4 mg IV over ≥15 min every 4 weeks**, adjusted for creatinine clearance |
| ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) bone loss (by DEXA (dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry) and FRAX) | 4–5 mg IV yearly |
| Hypercalcaemia of malignancy | 4 mg IV with saline hydration |

- 12-weekly dosing after the first doses is non-inferior to 4-weekly (CALGB 70604).
- **Not indicated at SRE doses in mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer):** no benefit (CALGB 90202, STAMPEDE).
- **Evidence:** **Zometa 039 trial (mCRPC):** fewer SREs than placebo (about 33% vs 44%). **Denosumab vs zoledronate (1904 men, mCRPC):** time to first SRE 20.7 vs 17.1 months. **Neither improves survival.**
- **Precautions and monitoring:** creatinine before every dose; avoid if creatinine clearance <30 mL/min. **Dental review before starting**; avoid invasive dental work during therapy. Calcium and vitamin D supplements.
- **Adverse effects:** **acute-phase reaction** (fever, myalgia) after the first infusion; nephrotoxicity, hypocalcaemia, hypophosphataemia; **ONJ (osteonecrosis of the jaw)**, atypical femoral fracture; ocular inflammation (rare).

### 4. Emergencies
**Spinal cord compression: watch constantly** (every focal back pain is cord compression until proven otherwise):
```
Back pain ± weakness, sensory level, sphincter change
 → dexamethasone 10 mg IV load, then 4–10 mg 6–12-hourly
 → urgent whole-spine MRI
 → decompressive surgery + RT (unstable spine, progression on RT, prior RT)
   or RT alone
 → orchidectomy or antagonist if hormone-naive (never agonist alone)
```
- **Dexamethasone** at once (load 10 mg intravenously), urgent whole-spine MRI, then **RT ± decompressive surgery**. Surgery plus RT beats (beat) RT alone (for walking ability) (**Patchell, 2005**).
- Castrate with orchidectomy or antagonist, never an agonist alone.

**Pathological or impending fracture:** orthopaedic fixation then RT. **Mirels score** ≥9 (site, pain, lytic type, size) favours prophylactic fixation.

### Follow-up
- PSA, ALP, liver tests, calcium, renal function and blood count every 3–6 months; testosterone if PSA rises; new scan if symptoms or PSA rise. Rising PSA with castrate testosterone = CRPC.
- PSA nadir ≤0.2 ng/mL at 7 months predicts long survival (SWOG 9346).
- PCWG3 (Prostate Cancer Working Group 3): bone scan progression needs ≥2 new lesions, confirmed later (early "flare" misleads).
- Manage ADT effects: bone loss (DEXA (dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry)), diabetes, cardiovascular risk, hot flushes.
- **Also know:** stent or nephrostomy for obstruction; marrow-failure anaemia; hospice care.

```
Retention + bone pain + metastases
 ├─ Catheter ± nephrostomy;   cord signs? → dexamethasone → MRI → RT ± surgery
 ├─ Orchidectomy / antagonist / agonist + antiandrogen
 ├─ Analgesia + 8 Gy single-fraction RT; bone protection
 ├─ Add ARPI or triplet; prostate RT if low volume
 └─ TWOC after ADT → channel TURP if it fails
```

**Diagram to draw:** the flowchart above; vicious cycle: tumour → RANKL → osteoclast → growth factors → tumour, with the sites blocked by denosumab and zoledronate (tumour–osteoclast vicious cycle with zoledronate's site of action).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Never start an LHRH agonist alone with spinal metastases: cover the flare or use an antagonist (avoid flare: antiandrogen cover, antagonist or orchidectomy).
> - Single 8 Gy fraction gives the same pain relief as longer courses (for focal pain); dexamethasone and MRI for cord compression.
> - Focal back pain = cord compression until MRI proves otherwise.
> - ADT plus abiraterone or an ARPI, or a triplet, for every fit man.
> - Denosumab 120 mg or zoledronate 4 mg (IV) 4-weekly prevent SREs in CRPC (mCRPC), not in HSPC; adjust zoledronate for creatinine clearance.
> - No survival benefit; not for SREs in mHSPC.
> - Dental check first (ONJ); calcium and vitamin D.
> - Denosumab delays SREs longer (20.7 vs 17.1 months).
> - Radium-223 (ALSYMPCA) for symptomatic bone-only mCRPC; never with abiraterone.

## PSA screening for prostate cancer: evidence, problems and solutions
**Asked as:**
- What are the various problems in PSA screening — *Practice Set 8 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Screening of CA prostate. — *Practice Set 13 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks); also asked as SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction (concept)
- Screening tests healthy, symptom-free (asymptomatic) men; its aim is lower prostate cancer mortality through earlier treatment (cure). The price is **overdiagnosis** (cancers that would never cause harm) and **overtreatment**.
- PSA (prostate-specific antigen) is a kallikrein (hK3) made by prostatic epithelium. It reaches blood when glandular architecture is disrupted. It is **organ-specific, not cancer-specific**.
- Since PSA testing began, (US) mortality has fallen by >40% (over 40%; SEER data), but at the price of overdiagnosis.

### Tools
- **PSA:** biopsy threshold 4 ng/mL (many use 2.5–3; ERSPC used 3). Repeat a raised PSA before biopsy.
- **DRE (digital rectal examination):** little value alone; abnormal DRE raises risk.
- **Refinements:** PSA density ≥0.15; free PSA ≤25%; PHI (Prostate Health Index), 4Kscore, Stockholm3; urine tests (PCA3 [prostate cancer antigen 3], ExoDx, SelectMDx).
- **MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) before biopsy:** fewer biopsies and less low-grade overdiagnosis.

### Evidence from screening trials
| Trial | Design | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **ERSPC** (European Randomised Study of Screening for Prostate Cancer) | >182,000 men; PSA every 2–4 years vs none | **20% relative fall** in (prostate) cancer death at 16 years; 570 invited and 18 extra diagnoses per death prevented |
| **PLCO** (Prostate, Lung, Colorectal and Ovarian), USA | Annual PSA vs usual care | **No difference** at 17 years: control arm heavily PSA-tested (contamination), lower power |
| **Göteborg-1** (Sweden) | Biennial PSA, men 50–64 | 44% fall in cancer death at 14 years (larger mortality reduction with biennial screening) |
| **CAP** (Cluster randomised trial of PSA testing for prostate cancer), UK | Single PSA invitation | No mortality difference at 10 years; only a small fall at 15 years |
| **Göteborg-2 (2022)** | MRI-targeted biopsy only | Halved overdiagnosis |
- Corrected for non-attendance and contamination, ERSPC benefit is 27–31%; it appears only after about 10 years.

### Problems in PSA screening

**1. Poor specificity (false positives)**
- BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia), prostatitis, retention, catheterisation, biopsy and TURP (transurethral resection of prostate) all raise PSA.
- At PSA 4–10 ng/mL with a normal DRE (digital rectal examination), only 17–32% of biopsies find cancer. Most men biopsied therefore have no cancer.

**2. Poor sensitivity: no safe cut-off**
- PCPT (Prostate Cancer Prevention Trial): risk is continuous; about 1 in 11 men biopsied at PSA 0–1 ng/mL has cancer.
- A high threshold misses curable cancer; a low threshold adds biopsies and overdiagnosis. Thresholds used: 4, 3 or 2.5 ng/mL.

**3. Biological variability of PSA**
- Rises with age, prostate volume (about 4% per mL) and Black race; physiological fluctuation; assay differences.
- Lowered by 5α-reductase inhibitors (about 50% at 12 months, so double the value), obesity, hypogonadism and statins.

**4. Overdiagnosis** (the central problem)
- Detection of cancers that would never have caused symptoms or death. Autopsy prevalence is high: 29% at 60–69 years and 59% after 79.
- Mostly Grade Group 1 cancers.

**5. Overtreatment**
- Overdiagnosed men treated radically suffer ED (erectile dysfunction), incontinence and bowel toxicity for no gain, especially above 65 years.

**6. Harms of biopsy**
- Pain, haematuria, haematospermia, retention and sepsis (transrectal route); anxiety from repeat biopsies.

**7. Modest and delayed benefit**
- Screening prevents only 20–30% of cancer deaths; no overall survival benefit has been shown. Men with life expectancy <10 years cannot benefit.

**8. Biases in interpreting screening data**
- **Lead-time bias**: earlier diagnosis lengthens apparent survival without delaying death.
- **Length-time bias**: screening preferentially finds slow-growing tumours.
- **Healthy-user bias**: health-conscious men seek testing, confounding risk-factor studies.

**9. Unsettled policy**
- Start age, stop age, interval and threshold are all debated; further large randomised trials are unlikely.
- Opportunistic screening is common and inequitable.

### Guideline positions (guidelines)
| Body | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| **USPSTF (US Preventive Services Task Force) 2012** | Grade D: against screening |
| **USPSTF 2018 (current)** | **Grade C, 55–69 years: individual shared decision; Grade D ≥70** (not ≥70; was Grade D in 2012) |
| AUA (American Urological Association) | Shared decision 55–69; every 2 years (2-yearly interval) |
| NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network) | Baseline PSA and discussion at 45 |
| EAU (European Association of Urology) | Risk-adapted early detection: informed men from 50; earlier start (45) with family history or African descent, 40 for BRCA2 carriers |
- **No body recommends (advises) unselected population screening.**
- **High risk:** Black men, father or brother diagnosed <65, BRCA2 and other DNA-repair mutations: start earlier.
- Stop when life expectancy is under 10–15 years.

### Solutions: risk-adapted, MRI-first pathway (reducing harm)
```
Informed man, life expectancy >10–15 y
   → PSA (repeat if raised)
   → risk stratify: PSA density, free/total PSA, risk calculator,
     PHI / 4Kscore / Stockholm3
   → MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)
        PI-RADS 1–2, low density → no biopsy, keep in follow-up
        PI-RADS ≥3 → targeted ± systematic biopsy (transperineal)
   → Grade Group 1 → active surveillance
```
- **Baseline PSA** predicts future risk (Malmö): low PSA at 40–60 allows longer intervals (intervals by baseline PSA).
- **PROMIS (2017):** MRI sensitivity 93% vs 48% for TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) biopsy; 27% could avoid biopsy.
- **PRECISION (2018):** MRI-targeted pathway found more significant cancer (38% vs 26%) and less insignificant cancer; 28% avoided biopsy.
- **Göteborg-2 (2022):** MRI with targeted biopsy in screening roughly halved overdiagnosis.
- **Active surveillance** of low-risk disease (Grade Group 1) uncouples overdiagnosis from overtreatment; MRI and biomarkers before biopsy.
- **Also know:** germline testing (BRCA2, ATM, HOXB13); polygenic risk scores; transperineal biopsy lowers sepsis.

**Diagram to draw:** iceberg of latent vs clinically significant cancer, plus the MRI-first flowchart (pathway: PSA → repeat → risk calculator or MRI → targeted biopsy → active surveillance or treatment).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - ERSPC: (about) 20% fall in prostate cancer death; PLCO null from contamination.
> - Core problems: low specificity, no safe cut-off, overdiagnosis, overtreatment.
> - USPSTF 2018: Grade C at 55–69, Grade D ≥70.
> - Fix: risk-adapted PSA, MRI before biopsy, active surveillance for GG1; MRI-first and active surveillance reduce overdiagnosis and overtreatment.

## Staging of prostate cancer and management of locally advanced (non-organ-confined) prostate cancer; complications of bilateral orchidectomy
**Asked as:**
- Describe staging of CA prostate and management of non-organ confirmed CA prostate — *Practice Set 10 Paper II Q1 (LAQ)*
- Desc. mgt optns for locally adv. CaP. Discuss complic. of b/l orchidectomy. — *Practice Set 16 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*
- Describe management of LAPC — *Practice Set 17 Paper II Q2 (LAQ)*

**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
(related: Practice Set 10 Paper 2, Q1)

*Read as: staging of prostate cancer, and management of non-organ-confined (locally advanced and node-positive, non-metastatic) disease.*
*Assumed meaning: describe the management options for locally advanced carcinoma prostate, and discuss the complications of bilateral orchidectomy.*
*LAPC is read as locally advanced prostate cancer.*

### Introduction and definition
- **Locally advanced CaP (carcinoma prostate) / LAPC:** **cT3–4, N0 or N1 (N+), M0:** extracapsular extension (spread) (cT3a), seminal vesicle invasion (cT3b), fixed or invading adjacent organs (cT4), ± pelvic nodes.
- At least 10% of new prostate cancers (new diagnoses, new cases) are locally advanced (T3–4, N0 or N+, M0); these men contribute a disproportionate share of cancer deaths. Any single treatment carries a high recurrence risk, so **multimodal therapy** (**combined therapy**) is the rule; no single modality suffices.
- In the PSA (prostate-specific antigen) era, high risk is defined more by grade and PSA than by palpation (today most "high-risk" disease is defined by grade and PSA rather than palpation).
- Untreated, 15-year disease-specific survival is 57% for T3–T4 vs 81% for T1–T2 (Swedish cohort). Untreated, Gleason 8–10 disease carries high cancer mortality, so fit men need treatment; no single modality suffices, hence **combined therapy**.

### Staging: TNM (tumour, node, metastasis) 2017 (AJCC 8th edition)

| Stage | Definition |
|---|---|
| T1a / T1b | Incidental at TURP (transurethral resection of prostate): ≤5% / >5% of tissue |
| T1c | Not palpable; found on needle biopsy (for raised PSA) |
| T2a / T2b / T2c | Palpable, confined: ≤ half of one lobe / > half of one lobe / both lobes |
| **T3a** | Extraprostatic extension (unilateral or bilateral) |
| **T3b** | **Seminal vesicle invasion** (not T4) |
| **T4** | Fixed or invading sphincter, rectum, bladder, levators, pelvic wall |
| N1 | Regional (pelvic) nodes |
| M1a / M1b / M1c | Non-regional nodes / bone / other sites ± bone |

- **Prognostic stage groups** add PSA and grade group: stage III = T3–4, PSA ≥20 ng/mL or grade group 5; IVA = N1; IVB = M1. pT2 is not subdivided.
- **Grade group (ISUP, International Society of Urological Pathology):** GG1 ≤6; GG2 3+4; GG3 4+3; GG4 8; GG5 9–10.

### Risk groups

| System | High risk | Very high / locally advanced |
|---|---|---|
| D'Amico | T2c, PSA >20 or Gleason 8–10 (any one) | – |
| NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network) | cT3a, GG4–5 or PSA >20 | cT3b–T4, primary Gleason pattern 5, ≥2 high-risk features, or >4 cores GG4–5 (grade group 4–5) |
| EAU (European Association of Urology) | PSA >20, ISUP 4–5 or cT2c | **Locally advanced: cT3–4 or cN+, any PSA, any grade** |

- **Also know:** CAPRA (Cancer of the Prostate Risk Assessment) score; Partin tables; Kattan nomograms; Decipher.

### Staging work-up (evaluation)
- **DRE (digital rectal examination)** sets clinical T stage; PSA, biopsy ISUP (International Society of Urological Pathology) grade group. PSA (prostate-specific antigen) >20 ng/mL: nodes in about 20%.
- **mpMRI (multiparametric MRI, magnetic resonance imaging):** for local extent; separates T2 from T3 (PI-RADS v2 reporting); best in higher-risk men.
- **Bone scan + CT/MRI** (CT, computed tomography) for high risk, PSA >20 or GG4–5.
- **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET-CT (positron emission tomography),** which is more accurate; proPSMA trial: accuracy 92% vs 65% for conventional imaging.
- Assess life expectancy: a fit man in his 70s with high-grade disease will live long enough to die of it.

### Management of locally advanced N0 (cT3–4 N0 M0)
**A (1). Radiotherapy (RT) + long-term ADT (androgen deprivation therapy): standard (the commonest standard)**
- Dose-escalated IMRT (intensity-modulated RT, ≥78–81 Gy), or EBRT (external-beam RT, ≥78 Gy) with or without pelvic node RT, with brachytherapy boost (**ASCENDE-RT:** LDR (low-dose-rate) boost halved biochemical failure (**brachytherapy boost** improves biochemical control); overall survival (OS) equal).
- **Why add ADT:** it shrinks the target and acts synergistically (is synergistic) with radiation; ADT works synergistically with RT: neoadjuvant, concurrent and adjuvant, **18–36 months** for high risk.

| Trial | Finding |
|---|---|
| **EORTC 22863** | 3 years of goserelin with RT improved 5-year OS (overall survival), 78% vs 62% (other sources: 79% vs 62%; 78–79% vs 62%) |
| **RTOG 85-31 / 92-02** | Long adjuvant ADT better; RTOG 92-02: 28 vs 4 months ADT better on all endpoints except overall OS; OS gain in Gleason 8–10 |
| **TROG 96.01** | 6 months better than 3 months |
| **SPCG-7; MRC PR07** | **RT is still needed with ADT:** ADT + RT beats ADT alone (overall survival gain): SPCG-7 (cancer death RR (relative risk) 0.44) and MRC PR07 (OS HR (hazard ratio) 0.77) showed ADT alone is inferior |
| **STAMPEDE** (high-risk M0) | Adding 2 years of abiraterone: metastasis-free survival HR (hazard ratio) 0.53 |
| **RTOG 0521; GETUG 12** | Docetaxel added modest benefit |

- **Duration:** NCCN: ADT 18–36 months with EBRT (12–36 with brachytherapy boost); 18 vs 36 months gave similar OS.
- Pelvic nodal RT: RTOG 94-13 inconclusive; POP-RT (Tata Memorial) improved biochemical failure-free survival in (favoured it in) high-risk N0.

**B. Systemic intensification**
- **STAMPEDE (abiraterone, M0):** for N1, or two of T3–4, Gleason ≥8 or PSA ≥40, **abiraterone + prednisolone for 2 years** with ADT + RT gave metastasis-free survival HR 0.53 and OS HR 0.60. NCCN now lists it for very high risk.
- Docetaxel (RTOG 0521 docetaxel + RT, OS HR 0.69): not routine.

**C. Radical prostatectomy (RP) + extended PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection, ePLND), within multimodal therapy**
- For fit men with a resectable (**completely resectable**) tumour (mainly cT3a; cT3a, selected cT3b, limited nodes). As part of multimodal therapy. cT3 is overstaged in 7–27% (clinical overstaging occurs in 7–27%), so cT3 alone is no bar.
- **Why surgery / advantages:** exact (accurate) pathological staging, removal of the primary (removes the source of local morbidity: an untreated prostate causes retention, haematuria and ureteric obstruction that ADT does not prevent), and RT kept for salvage.
- RP alone gives disease-free survival in at least half at 8–10 years, and 10-year disease-specific survival ~80–90%. Disease-specific survival 85–92% at 5 years and 79–82% at 10 years, but biochemical recurrence is common, so multimodal care is planned.
- **Neoadjuvant ADT before RP:** clinically downstages (clinical downstaging) and fewer positive margins, but **no survival gain** (no survival or PSA benefit).
- **Adjuvant vs early salvage RT (post-RP RT):** EORTC 22911, SWOG 8794 (metastasis-free survival and OS improved) and ARO 96-02 showed benefit / favour adjuvant RT over observation. RADICALS-RT, RAVES and GETUG-AFU 17 favour **early salvage RT** (PSA rise) for most men. Adjuvant RT is still considered for very high risk (pT3b, GG4–5 / Gleason 8–10, positive margins / positive margin). Dose 64–66 Gy; adding short-term ADT to salvage RT improved progression-free survival (GETUG-AFU 16).

**D. ADT alone**
- Only for men unfit for or refusing (declining) local treatment (therapy). Consider immediate ADT if PSA doubling time is short (<12 months), PSA is very high (>50 ng/mL) or symptoms; otherwise deferred ADT is acceptable. Early ADT delays progression; survival gain mainly in aggressive disease. In fit men it is inferior to ADT + RT.
- Options: bilateral orchidectomy, LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone) agonist or antagonist; bicalutamide 150 mg monotherapy preserves libido (spares sexual interest; harmful in localised disease); intermittent ADT.

**E. Others**
- Watchful waiting with deferred ADT in elderly comorbid men.
- **Also know:** cryotherapy and HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound) not standard (remain investigational); TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) or stents for local obstruction; trial enrolment.

### Node-positive disease

| Setting | Management |
|---|---|
| **cN1 M0** | RT to prostate and pelvis + ADT 2–3 years + abiraterone 2 years (STAMPEDE). RP + extended PLND (ePLND) in selected men |
| **pN1 (pN+) after RP** | **Immediate ADT** (ECOG 7887: OS benefit; better survival) **or ADT + adjuvant RT** (better survival than ADT alone); observation with undetectable PSA and ≤2 nodes. pN+: adjuvant ADT (ECOG 7887: better survival) ± RT |

### Algorithm

```
cT3–4 / cN1, M0 (PSMA PET or CT + bone scan)
   ├─ Fit, resectable  → RP + ePLND → early salvage RT ± ADT
   ├─ Most             → RT + ADT 18–36 months (+ abiraterone if N1 / very high risk)
   └─ Unfit / refuses  → ADT alone (immediate or deferred)
```

### Complications and local sequelae
- ADT: hot flushes, osteoporosis, metabolic syndrome, cardiovascular risk, loss of libido; give bone protection.
- RT: proctitis, cystitis. RP: incontinence, erectile dysfunction.
- A prostate left in place can cause outflow obstruction, haematuria and ureteric obstruction.

### Bilateral orchidectomy
- Removes about 95% of testosterone: **castrate level (<20 ng/dL) within 12 hours**. Cheap, no compliance issues, no flare, so useful in impending cord compression. Simple or subcapsular (all Leydig tissue must be removed).
- Versus LHRH agonists: fewer cardiac events, fractures and peripheral arterial disease reported.

**A. Surgical complications**
- Scrotal haematoma (commonest), wound infection, chronic scrotal or groin pain.
- Psychological: empty scrotum and altered self-image. Prevent with subcapsular or epididymis-sparing technique, or a testicular prosthesis.
- Irreversible: intermittent ADT is impossible.

**B. Systemic effects of castration**
| Effect | Details and management |
|---|---|
| Hot flushes | Commonest (50–80%); loss of hypothalamic oestrogen signalling; venlafaxine, gabapentin, megestrol or cyproterone if bothersome |
| Sexual dysfunction | Loss of libido, ED (erectile dysfunction), penile shortening; ED aids help selected men |
| Osteoporosis, fractures | Fracture risk up 20–45%; baseline DEXA (dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry), FRAX; calcium, vitamin D, exercise; **denosumab 60 mg 6-monthly** or zoledronic acid |
| Metabolic | Weight gain, fat gain, **sarcopenia**, insulin resistance, diabetes (HR about 1.34), metabolic syndrome |
| Cardiovascular | Coronary events, sudden cardiac death, thromboembolism |
| Anaemia | Normocytic; reversible after stopping |
| Others | Gynaecomastia (≤5%), fatigue, depression, cognitive change |

- **Also know:** risk of castration resistance over time; check testosterone with rising PSA.

### Follow-up and recent advances
- PSA every 3–6 months; bone and metabolic health.
- PSMA PET staging; trials adding apalutamide or enzalutamide (ATLAS, ENZARAD); SPCG-15 (RP vs RT + ADT).

**Diagram to draw:** TNM T-stage diagram (T2 confined, T3a extracapsular, T3b seminal vesicle, T4 adjacent organs) (extension of a prostate tumour), and subcapsular orchidectomy.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Seminal vesicle invasion is T3b; non-regional nodes are M1a.
> - Locally advanced = T3–4, N0/N+, M0; treat with combined therapy.
> - RT + long-term ADT (18–36 months) is the standard; ADT alone is inferior (SPCG-7).
> - RT + 18–36 months ADT: EORTC 22863 overall survival 79% vs 62%; STAMPEDE abiraterone metastasis-free survival HR 0.53.
> - STAMPEDE: abiraterone 2 years for N1 or very high-risk M0 (metastasis-free survival HR 0.53).
> - Neoadjuvant ADT before RP: no survival benefit (gain); early salvage RT favoured.
> - Early salvage RT preferred over adjuvant RT for most men after RP.
> - Orchidectomy: castrate in 12 hours; flushes, osteoporosis (denosumab), metabolic, sexual effects.

## 65 yr male underwent RP for stage T1c Gleason's score (4+3). At 6 months post surgery, his S. PSA has risen to 2 ng/ml above his preop PSA value. Discuss treatment and prognosis
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 12 Paper 2, Q1
(biochemical recurrence also discussed in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q2)

*Assumed meaning: 6 months after RP (radical prostatectomy) the PSA (prostate-specific antigen) never became undetectable and is now ≥2 ng/mL, i.e. PSA persistence with early rise, suggesting residual or systemic disease.*

### Introduction
- After RP, PSA (half-life about 3 days) should become undetectable (<0.1 ng/mL) within about 6 weeks.
- **PSA persistence:** failure to become undetectable after RP. **BCR (biochemical recurrence):** a rise after an undetectable nadir.
- **Gleason 4+3 = grade group 3** (unfavourable intermediate risk).

### Definitions of BCR after RP
| Body | Definition |
|---|---|
| AUA (American Urological Association) | PSA ≥0.2 ng/mL |
| NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network) | Failure to reach undetectable, two consecutive rises, or PSA ≥0.1 ng/mL |
| **EAU (European Association of Urology) risk groups** | **Low risk:** PSADT (PSA doubling time) >1 year **and** grade group <4. **High risk:** PSADT ≤1 year **or** grade group 4–5 |

### Why is the PSA high? (sources)
1. **Residual local disease:** positive margin (the only modifiable factor), residual benign tissue (low, stable PSA).
2. **Occult nodal metastases** (limited or no lymph node dissection).
3. **Distant micrometastases:** likeliest with persistent PSA and a fast rise.

### Evaluation
- **Repeat PSA** to confirm; calculate PSADT.
- **Review pathology:** pT stage, margin (length >1 mm, Gleason 4 at margin), seminal vesicles, nodes. **Also know:** CAPRA-S (Cancer of the Prostate Risk Assessment–Surgery) nomogram; Decipher genomic classifier.
- **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET/CT (positron emission tomography/computed tomography):** best test; detection about half below 0.5 ng/mL and nearly 100% above 2 ng/mL. Choline or fluciclovine if PSMA is unavailable.
- **mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) pelvis:** best for local recurrence in the prostatic fossa.
- CT and bone scan are rarely positive below PSA 10 ng/mL.

### Treatment
```
Confirm PSA, PSADT, pathology → PSMA PET ± mpMRI
 ├ Negative or fossa only → salvage RT to bed + ADT
 ├ Pelvic nodes only      → bed + pelvic node RT + ADT
 ├ Oligometastatic (≤3)   → ADT ± metastasis-directed therapy
 └ Polymetastatic         → treat as mHSPC: ADT + ARPI ± docetaxel
```
**1. Salvage RT (radiotherapy): the only curative option**
- Dose **≥64–66 Gy** to the bed (SAKK 09/10: 70 Gy gave no gain over 64 Gy); 40–45 Gy to pelvic nodes.
- Best at the lowest PSA: each 0.1 ng/mL costs about 2.6% recurrence-free survival.
- **RADICALS-RT, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17 (ARTISTIC meta-analysis):** early salvage at PSA 0.1–0.2 equals adjuvant RT and spares ≥60% from RT. These apply to men who become undetectable, not to this patient.

**2. Add ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) to salvage RT: essential here**
| Trial | Arms | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **RTOG 9601** (Radiation Therapy Oncology Group) | RT ± 2 years bicalutamide 150 mg; PSA 0.2–4.0 | 12-year OS (overall survival) 76.3% vs 71.3% |
| **GETUG-AFU 16** | RT ± 6 months goserelin; PSA 0.2–2.0 | 10-year BCR-free 64% vs 49%; MFS (metastasis-free survival) better; OS unchanged |
| **SPPORT (RTOG 0534)** | Bed / bed + 4–6 months ADT / pelvis + ADT | 5-year freedom from progression 70.9% / 81.3% / 87.4% |
| **RADICALS-HD** | None vs 6 vs 24 months ADT | 24 months better MFS than 6 months |
- At PSA ≥2 ng/mL with grade group 3, give whole-pelvis RT with **long-course (about 2 years) ADT**.

**3. Systemic therapy**
- **PSMA-positive metastases:** ADT + ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor: abiraterone, enzalutamide, apalutamide) ± docetaxel (LATITUDE, ARCHES, PEACE-1, ARASENS).
- **Oligometastases (≤3):** MDT (metastasis-directed therapy) by SBRT (stereotactic body RT) delays ADT (**STOMP:** ADT-free survival 21 vs 13 months; **ORIOLE:** progression 19% vs 61%).
- **High-risk BCR not fit for salvage:** **EMBARK** (PSADT ≤9 months, PSA ≥1 ng/mL): enzalutamide + leuprolide, MFS HR (hazard ratio) 0.42.
- **Also know:** castrate testosterone <50 ng/dL; intermittent ADT acceptable (PR.7); observation only for EAU low-risk BCR or short life expectancy; salvage lymph node dissection is investigational.

### Prognosis
- **Untreated BCR (Pound):** metastasis in about one-third, median 8 years; death a median 5 years later. Predictors: **BCR <2 years, PSADT <10 months, Gleason 8–10.**
- **This patient is poor-risk:** persistent PSA, high level within 6 months, likely short PSADT. Persistent PSA carries a higher risk of metastasis than late BCR.
- **Salvage RT:** one-third to one-half BCR-free at 4–5 years; poorer above PSA 0.5–1 ng/mL. Cancer-specific survival gain mainly with PSADT <6 months treated within 2 years.
- Lifelong PSA follow-up.

**Diagram to draw:** the treatment flowchart above, with PSA kinetics curve after RP.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - EAU high-risk BCR: PSADT ≤1 year or grade group 4–5.
> - PSMA PET first: almost 100% detection above PSA 2 ng/mL.
> - Salvage RT ≥64 Gy + ADT: RTOG 9601 (OS), GETUG-AFU 16, SPPORT (add pelvis).
> - Pound: BCR <2 years, PSADT <10 months, Gleason 8–10 predict death.

## 48 yr male presented with mixed voiding symptoms with PSA 18. Discuss management option in brief.
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 2, Q1
(related: prognosis in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3e; staging of non-organ-confined disease in Practice Set 10 Paper 2, Q1)

*"Mixed voiding symptoms" is read as mixed storage and voiding LUTS (lower urinary tract symptoms).*

### Introduction
- In a 48-year-old, a PSA (prostate-specific antigen) of 18 ng/mL is far above what BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) usually produces. **Prostate cancer must be excluded first**; the LUTS are then treated on their own merits.
- PSA is organ-specific, not cancer-specific: BPH, prostatitis, UTI (urinary tract infection), retention and instrumentation also raise it. At PSA >10 ng/mL, biopsy yield is 43–65% with a normal DRE (digital rectal examination) and 70–90% with a suspicious one.

### History
- **LUTS:** IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) with bother score; storage versus voiding; retention, haematuria, recurrent UTI.
- **Red flags:** bone pain, weight loss, leg oedema, cord compression.
- Family history (prostate cancer <65 years; BRCA (breast cancer gene)-related cancers), recent catheter, fever or perineal pain (prostatitis), 5ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) use, neurological disease, erectile function.

### Examination
- **DRE:** size, nodule, induration, extension, fixity (clinical T stage). Palpable bladder, neurological and spinal examination.

### Investigations
| Test | Purpose |
|---|---|
| Urinalysis, culture | Exclude UTI or prostatitis; treat and repeat PSA later (no empirical antibiotics without symptoms) |
| Repeat PSA, free PSA | Confirm the rise; low free PSA favours cancer |
| Creatinine, ultrasound KUB (kidney, ureter, bladder) | Upper tracts, prostate volume (PSA density) |
| Uroflowmetry, PVR (post-void residual) | Baseline LUTS assessment |
| **mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) before biopsy** | PI-RADS (Prostate Imaging Reporting and Data System) score; guides targeted cores; local staging |
| **Biopsy** | Targeted (PI-RADS ≥3) **plus systematic** 12 cores; **transperineal** preferred (lower sepsis) |

- At PSA 18, biopsy is needed **even if MRI is negative**. Blood or urine biomarkers (PHI (Prostate Health Index), 4Kscore, PCA3) matter mainly in the 2–10 ng/mL grey zone.

### Staging if cancer is found
- Grade group, cores involved, PSA, T stage → risk group. PSA 10–20 makes him at least **intermediate risk** (D'Amico, NCCN (National Comprehensive Cancer Network)).
- **High risk:** bone scan with CT (computed tomography), or **PSMA PET (prostate-specific membrane antigen positron emission tomography)**. Organ-confined disease is <50% at PSA >10; nodes about 20% at PSA >20.
- **Germline testing** (BRCA2, mismatch repair genes) for high-risk or metastatic disease or strong family history.

### Management of the cancer (life expectancy >25 years)
| Situation | Management |
|---|---|
| Low or favourable intermediate risk | AS (active surveillance) is possible, but uncommon at PSA 18 |
| Localised intermediate or high risk | **RP (radical prostatectomy)**, usually robotic, with extended PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection) when node risk exceeds the nomogram threshold; or **EBRT (external beam radiotherapy) + ADT (androgen deprivation therapy)**: 4–6 months intermediate, 18–36 months high risk |
| Locally advanced (T3–T4, N1) | Multimodal: RP ± adjuvant or early salvage RT, or EBRT + long-term ADT; abiraterone added for high-risk node-positive or high-risk N0 disease (STAMPEDE) |
| Metastatic | ADT + ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor) ± docetaxel; RT to the prostate for low volume |

- **Why surgery suits him:** young, gives pathological staging, and **removes the obstructing gland**, curing the LUTS. SPCG-4: surgical benefit is concentrated in men <65.
- Brachytherapy is relatively contraindicated with severe LUTS, a large gland or prior TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) (retention, incontinence).

### If biopsy is negative
- PSA 18 still needs explaining (prostatitis, large gland, missed anterior tumour): repeat MRI-targeted transperineal biopsy; %free PSA, PCA3, ConfirmMDx; close PSA follow-up.

### Management of LUTS
- **Behavioural measures** (fluid timing, less caffeine) and an **α-blocker** (e.g. tamsulosin) at once; it does not alter PSA.
- **Avoid starting a 5ARI until cancer is excluded:** it halves PSA and masks cancer; if used later, record a baseline PSA and double readings after 12 months.
- Persistent storage symptoms with low PVR: add an antimuscarinic or β3-agonist (mirabegron).
- **Surgery** (TURP, HoLEP (holmium laser enucleation of the prostate)) for retention, refractory symptoms or complications, only once cancer is excluded. In locally advanced cancer with retention, a channel TURP relieves obstruction.
- **Also know:** pressure-flow study before surgery at age <50 with mainly voiding LUTS (EAU (European Association of Urology)).

```
PSA 18 + LUTS → history, DRE, urine culture → repeat PSA
      → mpMRI → targeted + systematic biopsy (transperineal)
   Cancer → risk group + staging → RP / RT + ADT / systemic
   Benign → explain PSA, re-biopsy if needed → BPH pathway
LUTS throughout: α-blocker; no 5ARI until cancer excluded
```

**Diagram to draw:** the flowchart above.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - PSA 18 at 48: exclude cancer first; MRI before biopsy, transperineal targeted + systematic cores.
> - PSA >10: organ-confined <50%; PSA >20: nodes about 20%.
> - Young fit man: RP treats both cancer and obstruction.
> - α-blocker for LUTS; no 5ARI until cancer excluded (halves PSA).

## Discuss management of organ confined CA prostate
**Type:** LAQ (25 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 15 Paper 2, Q2

### Introduction
- Organ-confined prostate cancer is **cT1–T2 N0 M0**. Treatment must balance cancer mortality against **competing mortality**: life expectancy, comorbidity, baseline urinary, sexual and bowel function, and patient preference (shared decision-making).

### Risk stratification (NCCN, National Comprehensive Cancer Network)

| Risk group | Criteria |
|---|---|
| Very low | All of: T1c, GG (grade group) 1, PSA (prostate-specific antigen) <10 ng/mL, <3 positive cores with ≤50% cancer in each, PSA density <0.15 |
| Low | All of: T1–T2a, GG1, PSA <10 |
| Favourable intermediate | 1 intermediate factor (T2b–c, GG2–3, PSA 10–20), GG1–2, <50% cores positive |
| Unfavourable intermediate | 2–3 intermediate factors, GG3, or ≥50% cores positive |
| High | Any of: T3a, GG4–5, PSA >20 |

- **Grade groups:** GG1 = 3+3; GG2 = 3+4; GG3 = 4+3; GG4 = 8; GG5 = 9–10.

### Evaluation
- PSA, DRE (digital rectal examination), mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) with targeted and systematic biopsy.
- **Staging imaging** from unfavourable intermediate risk: bone scan + CT/MRI, or PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography). **proPSMA:** accuracy 92% vs 65% for conventional imaging.
- Life expectancy (life tables, Charlson index); IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) and erectile function.
- **Also know:** Partin tables, Kattan nomogram, CAPRA (Cancer of the Prostate Risk Assessment) score; genomic tests (Decipher, Prolaris, Oncotype DX) for borderline surveillance decisions.

### Options by risk

| Risk | Preferred options |
|---|---|
| Very low / low | **Active surveillance (AS)**; watchful waiting if life expectancy short |
| Favourable intermediate | AS in selected men; RP (radical prostatectomy); EBRT (external-beam radiotherapy) or brachytherapy alone |
| Unfavourable intermediate | RP + PLND (pelvic lymph node dissection); EBRT + **4–6 months ADT** (androgen deprivation therapy) |
| High (organ-confined, e.g. GG4–5 or PSA >20) | RP + extended PLND (multimodal); EBRT + **18–36 months ADT** |

### 1. Active surveillance vs watchful waiting
- **AS:** deferred treatment with **curative intent**. Monitor by PSA every 3–6 months, DRE, **confirmatory biopsy or MRI within 6–12 months**, then periodic MRI and biopsy. Trigger for treatment: grade progression (GG ≥2–3), increasing volume or patient choice.
- Entry criteria: Johns Hopkins (strict, Epstein), PRIAS (Prostate Cancer Research International Active Surveillance), Toronto (inclusive). About half are treated by 10–15 years.
- **Watchful waiting:** no curative intent; ADT for symptomatic progression. For limited life expectancy.

### 2. Radical prostatectomy
- Open retropubic or **robot-assisted** (similar outcomes in the randomised trial). Nerve sparing when there is no same-side extracapsular risk.
- **PLND** when nodal risk is significant on nomograms (extended template).
- **Complications:** incontinence, ED (erectile dysfunction), anastomotic stricture, rectal injury.
- **Advantages:** pathological staging; relieves obstruction; RT kept for salvage.

### 3. Radiotherapy
- **EBRT:** IMRT (intensity-modulated RT) with image guidance. Fractionation: conventional 78 Gy in 39; **moderate hypofractionation 60 Gy in 20** (CHHiP: non-inferior); **SBRT (stereotactic body RT) 36.25 Gy in 5** (PACE-B: similar toxicity).
- **Why add ADT:** synergistic cell kill and target shrinkage. **RTOG 94-08:** 4 months ADT improved OS in intermediate risk; **DFCI 95-096:** 6 months ADT improved 8-year OS (61% → 74%).
- **Brachytherapy:** LDR (low-dose-rate) seeds for low and favourable intermediate risk. Relative contraindications: gland >60 mL, high IPSS, recent TURP. HDR (high-dose-rate) or LDR **boost** with EBRT for higher risk (ASCENDE-RT: half the biochemical failure).
- **Toxicity:** proctitis, irritative urinary symptoms, late ED, second cancers (controversial).

### 4. Focal and whole-gland ablation
- HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound), cryotherapy, focal laser, IRE (irreversible electroporation), VTP (vascular-targeted photodynamic therapy).
- Based on the **index lesion hypothesis**. Best for MRI-visible, biopsy-confirmed, unifocal **intermediate-risk (GG2)** disease. Investigational; ideally in trials. VTP is the only modality with a randomised trial (versus AS in low risk).

### Landmark trials

| Trial | Comparison | Result |
|---|---|---|
| **SPCG-4** (pre-PSA era) | RP vs watchful waiting | Cancer death RR (relative risk) 0.56 at 23 years; NNT 8; benefit <65 years |
| **PIVOT** | RP vs observation (PSA era) | No mortality difference; benefit only in intermediate risk |
| **ProtecT** | Monitoring vs RP vs RT + ADT | 15-year cancer mortality 2.7%, 2.2%, 2.9% (no difference); more metastases with monitoring |

- **ProtecT function:** RP worst for continence and sexual function; RT transient bowel effects.

### Algorithm
```
Organ-confined CaP → risk group + life expectancy
 ├─ short life expectancy → watchful waiting
 ├─ very low / low → active surveillance
 ├─ favourable intermediate → AS (selected), RP, EBRT, brachytherapy
 └─ unfavourable intermediate / high → RP + PLND
                                     or EBRT + ADT (4–6 or 18–36 months)
```

### Follow-up
- PSA every 6–12 months. **Biochemical recurrence:** after RP, PSA ≥0.2 ng/mL confirmed; after RT, **nadir + 2 ng/mL (Phoenix)**.

### Recent advances
- PSMA PET staging; MRI-led surveillance; SBRT; genomic classifiers.

**Diagram to draw:** stepwise decision tree (treat or not → partial or whole gland → surgery or radiation).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Organ-confined = cT1–T2 N0 M0; NCCN risk groups drive choice.
> - AS is preferred for low risk; ProtecT: no mortality difference at 15 years.
> - EBRT + ADT: 4–6 months (intermediate), 18–36 months (high).
> - Focal therapy rests on the index lesion hypothesis; still investigational.

# Section 17 · Prostate Cancer — Short answer questions (SAQ)

## Transrectal ultrasonography (TRUS)
**Asked as:**
- Transrectal ultrasonography — *Summer 2015 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- TRUS — *Practice Set 10 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

(also answered in 2017-Winter Paper 1, Q3e and 2015-Summer Paper 2, Q3f)

*Read as: transrectal ultrasonography (TRUS) of the prostate.*

### Physics, principle and technique
- Piezoelectric crystals; higher frequency: better resolution, less penetration (higher frequency gives better resolution but less depth).
- The prostate lies directly in front of the rectum. Probes: **6–10 MHz** end-fire, side-fire or biplane probes; left lateral position after DRE (digital rectal examination); transverse and sagittal planes, base to apex.

### Sonographic anatomy (normal)
| Zone (McNeal) | Glandular volume (share) | TRUS appearance | Cancers (share) |
|---|---|---|---|
| Peripheral | ~70% | Homogeneous, echogenic (posterior) | ~70% |
| Transition | 5–10% (BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia)) | Heterogeneous, anterior | ~20–25% |
| Central | ~25% | Homogeneous, echogenic, at base | ~5–10% |
| Anterior fibromuscular stroma | Non-glandular | Hypoechoic | – |

- TRUS separates echogenic posterior PZ (peripheral zone) from heterogeneous TZ (transition zone).
- **Corpora amylacea** mark the surgical capsule; seminal vesicles about 4.5–5.5 × 2 cm; absent seminal vesicle → ipsilateral renal agenesis in 79%.

### Measurements
- **Volume (ellipsoid) = 0.52 × transverse × AP (anteroposterior) × length**; 1 mL ≈ 1 g; normal 20–25 g. **Planimetry** for brachytherapy.
- **PSAD (PSA (prostate-specific antigen) density)** = PSA/volume; **>0.15** is suspicious (suggests cancer).

### Findings
- **Cancer**: classically a hypoechoic peripheral-zone (PZ) lesion, but **39% are isoechoic**; false positives are BPH nodules, prostatitis and infarcts. Capsular bulge suggests extracapsular extension.
- **Cysts**: midline Müllerian; off-midline ejaculatory duct cyst (near midline); seminal vesicle cyst (lateral; renal agenesis).
- Abscess, calculi.

### Indications
- **Guided biopsy:** 12-core systematic ± MRI (magnetic resonance imaging)-targeted fusion.
- Volume before BPH surgery, brachytherapy or cryotherapy; fiducial markers.
- **Azoospermia:** ejaculatory duct obstruction (seminal vesicles >1.5 cm wide, dilated ducts, midline cyst).
- Abscess drainage (drainage of abscess or cysts); haematospermia.

### TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)-guided biopsy (biopsy notes)
- **12-core** systematic biopsy: medial and lateral cores at base, mid-gland and apex (sextant alone is inadequate). Periprostatic lidocaine block at the junction of prostate base and seminal vesicle.
- Contraindications: coagulopathy, acute prostatitis, severe immunosuppression. Continue low-dose aspirin; stop warfarin or clopidogrel 5–7 days.
- Complications: haematuria, haematospermia, rectal bleeding, **sepsis** (0.3–3%) from FQ (fluoroquinolone)-resistant *E. coli*; povidone-iodine rectal cleansing plus targeted or augmented prophylaxis.

### Current shift and advances
- **Transperineal biopsy** preferred by EAU (European Association of Urology) for lower infection risk (the transperineal route lowers infection).
- **MRI-first pathway**: PROMIS (2017): mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) sensitivity 93% versus 48% for TRUS biopsy. PRECISION (2018): MRI-targeted biopsy found more significant cancer (38% vs 26%). Targeting: cognitive, fusion or in-bore.
- **Also know:** colour Doppler, contrast-enhanced TRUS, **elastography**; **micro-ultrasound (29 MHz)** with PRI-MUS scoring, detection comparable to mpMRI; saturation biopsy after negative biopsies; treated glands become diffusely hypoechoic.

**Diagram to draw:** 12-core biopsy template.

**Diagram to draw:** transverse and sagittal TRUS views labelling PZ, TZ, seminal vesicles and urethra.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Probe 6–10 MHz; volume = 0.52 × T × AP × L; PSAD >0.15 suspicious.
> - 39% of cancers isoechoic; hypoechoic PZ lesion is classic; 12-core biopsy; sepsis 0.3–3%.
> - Seminal vesicle >1.5 cm wide: ejaculatory duct obstruction.
> - PROMIS and PRECISION established the MRI-first pathway.

## Radiotherapy and brachytherapy for CA prostate
**Asked as:**
- Radiotherapy for CA prostate. — *Summer 2017 Paper II Q3f (SAQ)*
- Brachytherapy for CA prostate — *Practice Set 8 Paper II Q3e (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Radiobiology
- Prostate cancer has a **low α/β ratio (~1.5 Gy)**, lower than the rectum, so larger fractions favour tumour kill over late toxicity: the basis of **hypofractionation**. Prostate cancer's low α/β ratio also favours high dose per fraction (HDR).

### EBRT (external beam radiotherapy)
- **IMRT (intensity-modulated radiotherapy)**/VMAT (volumetric modulated arc therapy) with **IGRT (image-guided radiotherapy)** (fiducials, cone-beam CT (computed tomography)) is standard, allowing dose escalation with less toxicity.

| Schedule | Dose per fraction | Example | Trial |
|---|---|---|---|
| Conventional | 1.8–2 Gy | 78 Gy in 39 | — |
| **Moderate hypofractionation** (standard) | 2.25–4 Gy | **60 Gy in 20** | **CHHiP**: non-inferior to 74 Gy in 37 |
| **SBRT (stereotactic body RT)** | ≥5 Gy | 36.25 Gy in 5 | **PACE-B**, HYPO-RT-PC |
- **Also know:** focal MRI boost (FLAME); protons; **hydrogel rectal spacer** reduces rectal toxicity.

### Brachytherapy
#### Definition and principle
- Brachytherapy (BT; Greek *brakhus*, short): radioactive sources placed **within the prostate**.
- **Steep dose fall-off** gives an intraprostatic dose far above what EBRT (external beam radiotherapy) can safely give.

#### Types
| Feature | **LDR (low-dose-rate)**, permanent seeds | **HDR (high-dose-rate)**, temporary |
|---|---|---|
| Source | ¹²⁵I (half-life 59.6 days), ¹⁰³Pd (17 days), ¹³¹Cs (9.7 days) | **¹⁹²Ir** in remote afterloader (temporary afterloading) |
| Monotherapy dose | ¹²⁵I 140–160 Gy; ¹⁰³Pd 110–125 Gy | 27 Gy/2 (e.g. 27 Gy in 2), 31.5 Gy/3, 34–38 Gy/4 fractions |
| Dosimetry | Post-implant CT at 14–30 days | Real time; none afterwards |
- **LDR (low dose rate)**: permanent ¹²⁵I (140–160 Gy) or ¹⁰³Pd seeds via TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)-guided perineal template; monotherapy for low and favourable intermediate risk.

#### Technique
- **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)-guided transperineal** implant under anaesthesia: high lithotomy, stepper, template grid, needles, seeds deployed on withdrawal.
- **Peripheral loading** spares the urethra. Intraoperative planning gives best coverage.
- Quality: **D90 >90%** of prescription and **V100 >85%** of gland; urethral and rectal (V100 <1 cm³) limits. Low D90 predicts failure.

#### Indications
- **Monotherapy:** low and favourable intermediate risk (**RTOG 0232**: adding EBRT gave no benefit, more toxicity).
- **Boost with EBRT 45 Gy (+ implant) ± ADT (androgen deprivation therapy):** unfavourable intermediate and high risk. **ASCENDE-RT:** LDR boost halved biochemical failure vs EBRT boost, but (with more urinary toxicity) grade 3 urinary toxicity 18.4% vs 5.2%.
- Also salvage and focal BT.

#### Contraindications (relative)
- **IPSS (International Prostate Symptom Score) >20, Qmax <10 mL/s**, large median lobe; recent or large TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) defect; gland >60 cm³ (cytoreduce with ADT); pubic arch interference; prior pelvic radiation (RT [radiotherapy]); inflammatory bowel disease; cannot lie in lithotomy.

#### Complications of brachytherapy
- Acute obstructive and irritative symptoms; retention (29% if IPSS >20, 2% if <10); α-blockers help.
- Chronic grade ≥2 urinary toxicity about 20%; urethral stricture; rectal complications 1–2%; rare rectourethral fistula; ED (erectile dysfunction).

#### Follow-up after brachytherapy
- Nadir is deeper and later (about 2.5 years). **PSA bounce** in about one-third: observe for 30 months before biopsy. Failure: Phoenix (nadir + 2 ng/mL).

### ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) with RT, by risk
| Risk | ADT |
|---|---|
| Low, favourable intermediate | None |
| Unfavourable intermediate | **4–6 months** |
| High / locally advanced | **24–36 months** (EORTC 22863); abiraterone if very high risk (STAMPEDE) |
- RT added to ADT improves survival in locally advanced disease (**SPCG-7, NCIC PR.3/MRC PR07**).

### Post-prostatectomy RT
- **Adjuvant RT** (pT3, positive margin) improves control (EORTC 22911, ARO 96-02) and survival (SWOG 8794).
- **RADICALS-RT, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17** (ARTISTIC meta-analysis): **early salvage at PSA (prostate-specific antigen) 0.1–0.2 ng/mL** equals adjuvant RT and spares most men RT.
