- Salvage 64–66 Gy + ADT (**RTOG 9601**: 2 years bicalutamide improved survival) ± pelvic nodes (SPPORT).

### Outcomes
- **ProtecT** (2016; 15-year 2023): equally low prostate-cancer mortality with RT, surgery or active monitoring; RT more bowel effects, surgery more incontinence and ED (erectile dysfunction).
- Failure after RT: **Phoenix definition** = nadir + 2 ng/mL.

### Toxicity
- Acute: frequency, dysuria, diarrhoea. Late: **proctitis** with bleeding (6–18 months; sucralfate, argon plasma coagulation), cystitis, stricture, ED, second cancers.

**Diagram to draw:** TRUS-guided perineal template brachytherapy (TRUS-template transperineal implant set-up).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - α/β ~1.5 Gy: basis of hypofractionation (CHHiP 60 Gy in 20).
> - ADT: 4–6 months unfavourable intermediate, 24–36 months high risk.
> - Early salvage (PSA 0.1–0.2) = adjuvant (RADICALS, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17).
> - Phoenix: nadir + 2 ng/mL.
> - LDR ¹²⁵I 145 Gy; HDR ¹⁹²Ir.
> - D90 >90%, V100 >85%.
> - IPSS >20 or Qmax <10 = relative contraindication.
> - ASCENDE-RT: boost halves biochemical failure.

## TRUS / prostate biopsy
**Asked as:**
- TRUS biopsy. — *Summer 2020 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Prostate biopsy — *Practice Set 4 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)* (TRUS biopsy also answered in 2020-Summer Paper 2, Q3d)

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)

### Introduction
- TRUS (transrectal ultrasound)-guided systematic 12-core biopsy has been the standard for diagnosing prostate cancer; about 30% are positive at first biopsy.

### Indications and contraindications
- Raised PSA (prostate-specific antigen) with risk factors; suspicious DRE (digital rectal examination); MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) PIRADS (Prostate Imaging Reporting and Data System) ≥3; suspected metastasis.
- **Repeat:** rising PSA; ASAP (atypical small acinar proliferation; 50% cancer risk); positive biomarkers (PHI [Prostate Health Index], 4Kscore, PCA3); active surveillance.
- **Contraindications:** coagulopathy, acute UTI (urinary tract infection) or prostatitis, severe immunosuppression; no anus (use transperineal route).

### MRI first
- **PROMIS (2017):** MRI sensitivity 93% vs 48% for TRUS (transrectal ultrasound) biopsy. **PRECISION (2018):** targeted biopsy found more significant cancer (38% vs 26%) and avoided biopsy in 28% (avoided 28% of biopsies).
- Targeting: cognitive, MRI/US fusion or in-bore; 2–3 cores per target ± systematic cores.

### Preparation
- Continue low-dose aspirin; stop warfarin and clopidogrel 5–7 days, DOACs (direct oral anticoagulants) 2–5 days before.
- **Transrectal: antibiotics for all** (contaminated route), ≤24 hours: fluoroquinolone, or cephalosporin ± aminoglycoside. Augmented (augment) or swab-directed if risk factors (recent antibiotics / antibiotics in last 6 months, diabetes, previous infection).
- Enema optional. **Periprostatic block:** 1–2% lidocaine, 5 mL each side at the seminal vesicle–prostate junction.

### Technique
| | Transrectal | Transperineal |
|---|---|---|
| Position | Left lateral | Lithotomy |
| Needle route | Rectal wall (contaminated) | Sterile perineal skin |
| Antibiotics | Mandatory | Skin-flora only or none |
| Anterior/apex | Poor | Good |
| Anaesthesia | Periprostatic block | Perineal and periapical triangle block |
- Left lateral position; DRE; 6–10 MHz probe; measure volume (PSA density >0.15 ng/mL/cc suggests cancer).
- 18-gauge spring-loaded needle; touch (indent) the capsule, withdraw slightly, then fire, so the core samples peripheral zone.
- **12 cores**: base, mid and apex on each side, medial and lateral (sextant missed lateral cancers). Add MRI-targeted cores (cognitive or fusion). Saturation only after negative biopsies.
- **EAU (European Association of Urology) 2023 prefers transperineal biopsy.**

### Complications (transrectal)
| Complication | Rate |
|---|---|
| Haematuria | 23–63% |
| Haematospermia | 10–50% |
| Rectal bleeding | 2–22% |
| Infection | 0.1–7%; sepsis 0.3–3% |
| Retention | 0.2–0.4% |
| Vasovagal reaction | 1–5% |
- **Sepsis** from fluoroquinolone-resistant *E. coli*: carbapenem until cultures return. Rectal bleeding: probe pressure, balloon tamponade, endoscopy, embolisation.

### Recent advances
- **MRI first:** PROMIS, PRECISION (above).
- **Transperineal biopsy** under local anaesthesia: fewer infections, better apex and anterior sampling; preferred by EAU (European Association of Urology) 2023.

**Also know:** PSA density; TZ (transition zone) cores if gland >50 mL; PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET.

**Diagram to draw:** 12-core template on a transverse prostate outline.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - MRI before biopsy (PROMIS, PRECISION).
> - 12 cores; antibiotics ≤24 hours for all (transrectal).
> - Bleeding commonest; sepsis most serious.
> - Transperineal now preferred.

## PIN
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 1 Paper 2, Q3f

*Assumed meaning: prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia.*

### Definition
- PIN (prostatic intraepithelial neoplasia): **architecturally benign** acini or ducts lined by **cytologically atypical** cells, with an intact or patchy **basal cell layer**.
- Graded **LGPIN (low-grade PIN)** and **HGPIN (high-grade PIN)**. LGPIN is not reported: it is irreproducible and carries no excess risk.

### Histology
- HGPIN: nuclear enlargement, hyperchromasia, **prominent nucleoli**.
- Patterns: tufting, micropapillary, flat. **Cribriform lesions are now classed as IDC-P (intraductal carcinoma of the prostate) or AIP (atypical intraductal proliferation).**
- Immunohistochemistry: basal cells stain with **HMWCK (high-molecular-weight cytokeratin) and p63**; AMACR (α-methylacyl-CoA racemase) positive. Mainly in the peripheral zone.

### Evidence that HGPIN is a precursor
- More and larger HGPIN foci in cancerous prostates; carcinoma seen budding from HGPIN.
- About 20% carry **TMPRSS2:ERG fusion** (about 50% of cancers). Extra genomic changes are needed for invasion.

### Incidence
- About **4–5%** of biopsies (range 0–25%, due to observer variation).

### Related lesions
| Lesion | Meaning | Action on isolated finding |
|---|---|---|
| HGPIN | Weak risk marker | **No repeat biopsy** |
| PINATYP (HGPIN with adjacent atypical glands) | As "atypical" | Manage as ASAP (atypical small acinar proliferation) |
| ASAP | Cancer on re-biopsy about 40% | Repeat biopsy ± MRI (magnetic resonance imaging) |
| AIP | May herald IDC-P | Consider re-biopsy |
| IDC-P | Usually high-grade cancer present | Definitive treatment; excludes active surveillance |

### Management
- Isolated HGPIN, unifocal or multifocal: **no immediate repeat biopsy**. Decide further testing on PSA (prostate-specific antigen), DRE (digital rectal examination) and **mpMRI (multiparametric MRI)**.
- HGPIN on TURP (transurethral resection of the prostate) chips: no further sampling on current data.
- **Chemoprevention:** none proven; green tea showed reduced progression in small trials.
- *Guideline note:* EAU (European Association of Urology) guidelines still list extensive HGPIN (≥3 biopsy sites) as a reason for repeat biopsy; the chapter does not.

**Diagram to draw:** benign gland, HGPIN gland (atypical cells, intact basal layer) and cancer (no basal layer).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Atypical cells, benign architecture, basal layer retained.
> - Isolated HGPIN needs no repeat biopsy; ASAP does.
> - Cribriform "PIN" is now IDC-P or AIP.

## Maximum (combined) androgen blockade and bicalutamide
**Asked as:**
- Maximum androgen blockade — *Practice Set 2 Paper II Q3d (SAQ)*
- Bicalutamide — *Practice Set 13 Paper II Q3a (SAQ)*

**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
(Practice Set 2 Paper II Q3d: also answered as Combined Androgen Blockade in 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3f)
(Practice Set 13 Paper II Q3a: combined androgen blockade is answered in 2018-Summer Paper 4, Q3f)

### Definition and rationale of MAB
- **MAB (maximum androgen blockade)**, also CAB (combined) or total androgen blockade: castration (orchidectomy or LHRH [luteinising hormone-releasing hormone] agonist) **plus a non-steroidal antiandrogen**.
- **Rationale (Labrie):** castration removes about 90–95% of testosterone, but **adrenal androgens** (DHEA, dehydroepiandrosterone; androstenedione) are converted to DHT (dihydrotestosterone) in the prostate. The antiandrogen blocks these at the AR (androgen receptor).

### Antiandrogens
| Drug | Dose | Signature toxicity |
|---|---|---|
| Flutamide | 250 mg three times daily | Diarrhoea, hepatotoxicity |
| Nilutamide | 300 mg, then 150 mg daily | Delayed dark adaptation, pneumonitis |
| **Bicalutamide** | **50 mg daily** (with LHRH agent) | Best tolerated; gynaecomastia |
| Cyproterone (steroidal) | 100 mg two to three times daily | Cardiovascular, hepatic; no CAB benefit |

- Class effects: gynaecomastia, hepatotoxicity, pneumonitis.

### Bicalutamide: introduction and mechanism
- Non-steroidal **AR (androgen receptor) antagonist**, called first- or second-generation. It competes with testosterone and DHT (dihydrotestosterone) at the ligand-binding domain.
- Given alone, it blocks central feedback, so **LH (luteinising hormone) and testosterone rise** (about 1.5 times); aromatisation to oestradiol causes gynaecomastia; libido is partly preserved.
- **Half-life about 6 days** (once daily); unaffected by age or renal impairment. Most potent and best tolerated of its class.

### Evidence for MAB
- **Crawford (1989):** leuprolide + flutamide improved survival (partly flare control); **Eisenberger (1998):** orchidectomy + flutamide, **no** benefit.
- **PCTCG (Prostate Cancer Trialists' Collaborative Group) meta-analysis, 2000** (27 trials, about 8,000 men): with non-steroidal antiandrogens, 5-year survival improved by only **about 2–3%**; cyproterone slightly worse.
- Small gain, more cost and toxicity: **AUA (American Urological Association) advises against long-term CAB**.

### Uses of bicalutamide

| Use | Dose | Evidence |
|---|---|---|
| **CAB (combined androgen blockade)** with LHRH (LH-releasing hormone) agonist, metastatic | 50 mg daily | Small survival gain (2–3%, PCTCG [Prostate Cancer Trialists' Collaborative Group]) |
| **Flare prevention** | 50 mg, with or before the agonist, 2–4 weeks | Main use today |
| Monotherapy, locally advanced | 150 mg daily | **EPC (Early Prostate Cancer) programme:** better progression-free survival; **harm in localised disease** (death HR [hazard ratio] 1.23) |
| With salvage radiotherapy | 150 mg for 2 years | **RTOG 9601:** 12-year overall survival 76.3% vs 71.3% |
| CRPC (castration-resistant prostate cancer) | 50 mg | Second-line step; largely replaced by newer agents |

- 150 mg monotherapy roughly equals castration in locally advanced disease, with better sexual interest; not FDA (US Food and Drug Administration) approved alone, but used in some countries.
- **Also know:** stuttering priapism; feminising hormone therapy; studied in BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia).

### Current uses of MAB
1. **Flare prevention:** an LHRH agonist raises LH (luteinising hormone) up to 10-fold for 10–20 days, risking cord compression, pain or obstruction with bone metastases. Start antiandrogen **with or before** the agonist for **21–28 days**. Antagonists (degarelix, relugolix) or orchidectomy cause no flare.
2. **Antiandrogen withdrawal:** on progression, stopping bicalutamide gives a PSA (prostate-specific antigen) fall >50% in **15–30%** for 3.5–5 months (mutant AR turns the drug into an agonist).

### Adverse effects of bicalutamide
- **Gynaecomastia and breast pain** (two-thirds at 150 mg): prevent with breast radiotherapy (10–15 Gy single fraction) or tamoxifen 10 mg daily.
- **Hepatotoxicity** (monitor liver tests); rare **interstitial pneumonitis**; erectile dysfunction; fewer hot flushes than castration.

### Modern replacement in mHSPC (metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer)
- ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) plus abiraterone, enzalutamide, apalutamide, docetaxel, or triplets (PEACE-1, ARASENS).
- **ENZAMET:** enzalutamide beat a CAB control arm for overall survival.
- **Comparison:** enzalutamide beat bicalutamide in CRPC (TERRAIN, STRIVE) and in **ENZAMET**.

**Diagram to draw:** testicular and adrenal androgen sources with LHRH agonist and antiandrogen sites of action. Hypothalamus–pituitary–testis axis with bicalutamide blocking the AR.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - MAB = castration + non-steroidal antiandrogen (blocks adrenal androgens).
> - PCTCG 2000: only 2–3% 5-year survival gain.
> - Main use now: flare cover for 21–28 days.
> - Newer AR pathway inhibitors beat CAB (ENZAMET).
> - Bicalutamide half-life about 6 days; 50 mg with LHRH agonist, 150 mg monotherapy.
> - Monotherapy raises testosterone; gynaecomastia two-thirds.
> - EPC: harm in localised disease; RTOG 9601: survival gain with salvage radiotherapy.
> - Withdrawal response 15–30%.

## Gleason's score and its significance
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 3 Paper 2, Q3f

### Introduction
- **Gleason grading** (Donald Gleason, late 1960s) grades prostate adenocarcinoma by **glandular architecture at low magnification**, not cytology. Patterns run from 1 (most differentiated) to 5. Modified by ISUP (International Society of Urological Pathology) in **2005** and **2014**.

### Patterns (modern)

| Pattern | Architecture |
|---|---|
| 1–2 | Not used on biopsy now |
| **3** | Discrete, well-formed glands of any size |
| **4** | **Cribriform** (including glomeruloid), poorly formed or fused glands |
| **5** | Sheets, cords, single cells, solid nests, **comedonecrosis** |

### Scoring rules
- **Radical prostatectomy:** primary (commonest) + secondary pattern.
- **Biopsy (2005):** commonest + **highest** pattern, however small. Single pattern is doubled (3+3).
- A tertiary pattern 5 under 5% at prostatectomy is reported as a comment; it adds risk but does not change the grade group.
- Report **% pattern 4** in score 7; report cribriform and intraductal carcinoma (both adverse).

### ISUP Grade Groups (2014; WHO [World Health Organization] 2016)

| Grade group | Gleason score | 5-year BCR (biochemical recurrence)-free after prostatectomy |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | 3+3=6 | 96% |
| 2 | 3+4=7 | 88% |
| 3 | 4+3=7 | 63% |
| 4 | 8 (4+4, 3+5, 5+3) | 48% |
| 5 | 9–10 | 26% |

- Grade groups separate 3+4 from 4+3 and 8 from 9–10. Starting at "1" avoids patients misreading the lowest score, 6, as moderate cancer.

### Significance
- **Most powerful biopsy predictor** of pathological stage, EPE (extraprostatic extension), nodal spread (0% in GG1, 4.3% in GG3), recurrence and death.
- **Risk groups** (D'Amico, NCCN [National Comprehensive Cancer Network]) combine grade, PSA (prostate-specific antigen) and stage.
- **Treatment choice:** GG1 → active surveillance; selected GG2 with little pattern 4 → surveillance; GG ≥4 → high risk, longer ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) with radiotherapy; also selects for brachytherapy and focal therapy.
- **Limitations:** biopsy undergrades about one-third of GG1 (sampling error); inter-observer variation; after ADT or radiotherapy, grading is unreliable.
- **Also know:** pure GG1 does not metastasise, so "is it cancer?" is debated.

**Diagram to draw:** Gleason's pattern diagram 3 → 4 → 5.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Biopsy score = commonest + highest pattern.
> - 3+4 (GG2) and 4+3 (GG3) differ: BCR-free 88% vs 63%.
> - GG4 = score 8; GG5 = 9–10.

## HIFU
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 5 Paper 2, Q3d

### Definition and principle
- HIFU (high-intensity focused ultrasound) focuses ultrasound from a bowl-shaped transducer or lens onto a point. Energy is absorbed only where the beams converge, so tissue there is heated while the path is spared.
- **Intensity 1500–10,000 W/cm²** (diagnostic ultrasound causes negligible heating).
- **Mechanisms:** **thermal coagulative necrosis** (≥55 °C; prostate exceeds 100 °C within seconds) plus **cavitation** (bubbles collapse and damage cells).
- Each lesion is a **rice grain**; the target is "painted" with overlapping lesions. Ablated and viable tissue look alike on ultrasound, so gaps occur.

### Devices and routes
- **Transrectal** probe that both monitors and treats (posterior zone).
- **TULSA (MRI [magnetic resonance imaging]-guided transurethral ultrasound ablation):** **MR thermometry** confirms lethal temperature; reaches the anterior gland.

### Indications
| Setting | Use |
|---|---|
| **Focal therapy, prostate cancer** | MRI-visible, unilateral, grade group 2–3 index lesion; hemiablation; based on the index-lesion hypothesis |
| Whole-gland ablation | Localised cancer; retreatment 29–38% |
| **Salvage after EBRT** (external beam radiotherapy) | Biopsy-proven local recurrence, **≥2 years after EBRT** |
| BPH (benign prostatic hyperplasia) | Approved |
| Kidney | Investigational (incomplete ablation) |

### Limitations
- **Anterior lesions** (energy lost, oedema displaces target), large gland, gland height and **calcification** (some advise TURP [transurethral resection of the prostate] first).

### Outcomes
- **Focal HIFU:** continence 90–95%, potency 50–78%; infield positive biopsy 16.7–54% (csPCa [clinically significant prostate cancer] 0–21.8%).
- **HEAT registry** (1379 men): 7-year failure-free survival 69%.
- **Salvage HIFU:** biochemical progression-free survival about 48% at 3 years; continence 64–87.5%.

### Complications
- Retention (about 8%), UTI (urinary tract infection) 6–18%, stricture (4%), ED (erectile dysfunction); rectourethral fistula rare (about 2% after salvage); osteitis pubis.

### Follow-up
- PSA (prostate-specific antigen) falls only 50–80%; **mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) + targeted biopsy at 3–6 months**, then at 1–2 and 5 years. EAU (European Association of Urology): focal therapy within trials or registries.

**Also know:** **histotripsy** (>10,000 W/cm², mechanical liquefaction, visible bubble cloud).

**Diagram to draw:** transrectal transducer focusing beams on a rice-grain lesion in the posterior prostate.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Focused ultrasound → ≥55 °C coagulative necrosis plus cavitation.
> - Transrectal HIFU suits posterior lesions; TULSA for anterior.
> - Focal HIFU: continence 90–95%; HEAT 7-year failure-free 69%.

## Saturation biopsy in CA prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 7 Paper 2, Q3d
(also answered in 2016-Summer Paper 4, Q3a)

### Definition
- Systematic prostate biopsy with **more cores than the extended 12-core scheme**, usually **18–21 or more** (often 20–30), sampling the peripheral zone, transition zone, anterior gland and apex.
- Route: transrectal, or **transperineal template mapping biopsy** through a brachytherapy grid with a core every **5 mm**.

### Rationale
- Sextant biopsy (Hodge, 1989) under-sampled the lateral peripheral zone; the 12-core scheme is today's standard.
- Saturation reduces sampling error, mainly for **anterior and apical cancers** missed transrectally.

### Indications
- **Repeat biopsy** after a negative biopsy with persistent suspicion: rising PSA (prostate-specific antigen), ASAP (atypical small acinar proliferation), negative or unavailable MRI (magnetic resonance imaging).
- **Active surveillance** confirmation; **focal therapy** mapping (shows multifocality; TRUS [transrectal ultrasound] biopsy misses almost half of disease relevant to hemiablation).

### Templates
| Template | Division | Cores |
|---|---|---|
| Original Barzell | Octants (anterior/posterior × apex/base, each side) | 4–8 per octant |
| Modified Barzell | Apex and base of each side × 5 sectors | 20 |
| Ginsburg | Anterior, mid, posterior sectors per side | 4 per sector |
- Lithotomy, stepper and grid; general or spinal anaesthesia, or periprostatic block (up to 22 mL of 1% lidocaine).

### Results
- **Initial biopsy**: 12 to 18–21 cores did not raise detection.
- **Repeat setting**: early series gained; a later large prospective study did not. Extra cancers are often insignificant (overdiagnosis).
- **PROMIS (2017)** used 5-mm template mapping as the reference: mpMRI (multiparametric MRI) sensitivity 93% vs TRUS biopsy 48%.

### Complications
- More haematuria, haematospermia, pain and **urinary retention**; anaesthesia and theatre time. Transperineal route has less infection.

### Current position
- Largely replaced by **mpMRI with targeted biopsy**: **PRECISION (2018)** detected more significant cancer (38% vs 26%) and 28% avoided biopsy.
- Still used for MRI-negative high suspicion and focal therapy planning.

**Diagram to draw:** transperineal grid over the prostate with sampling sectors.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Saturation = >12 cores (18–21 or more); 5-mm template mapping.
> - No gain at initial biopsy; used after negatives and for focal therapy.
> - PROMIS: template mapping as reference; mpMRI sensitivity 93%.

## Biological effects of cryosurgery
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 11 Paper 2, Q3a
(related: cryotherapy for carcinoma prostate, 2015-Summer Paper 4, Q3f)

### Principle
- Tissue destruction by extreme cold. **Argon** expanding in the probe tip cools by the **Joule–Thomson effect**; **helium** expanding warms (active thaw).
- Used for small renal masses and prostate cancer.

### Mechanisms of cell death
Tissue dies during **both freezing and thawing**, by four mechanisms:

| Mechanism | What happens |
|---|---|
| **1. Direct cell injury** | *Freeze:* extracellular water freezes first and becomes hyperosmolar; cells dehydrate and membranes distort. Rapid cooling forms **intracellular ice**, which ruptures organelles. *Thaw:* water rushes back, cells swell and burst; ice recrystallises into larger crystals |
| **2. Vascular injury** | Endothelial damage, platelet activation and **thrombosis** cause ischaemic necrosis; the main delayed killing effect |
| **3. Apoptosis** | Sublethal cold at the periphery triggers programmed cell death |
| **4. Immunomodulation** | Necrotic cells release antigens and DAMPs (damage-associated molecular patterns); dendritic cells activate T cells against tumour |

- **End result:** coagulative necrosis, then fibrous scar.

### Factors deciding lethality
- **Temperature:** normal renal tissue dies at about −19.4 °C; tumour may need −40 to −50 °C. **Target ≤−40 °C.**
- **Fast freeze, slow thaw** maximise injury.
- **Double freeze–thaw cycle** enlarges the necrotic zone (standard of care); each freeze about 8–10 minutes.
- **Heat sink:** nearby large vessels warm tissue; more probes or longer freezing needed.

### Zones of the ice ball
- Centre: complete necrosis. Edge: 0 °C; below −20 °C only about 3 mm inside the edge. The lethal −40 °C isotherm lies well inside the visible ice ball.
- So the ice ball must extend **5–10 mm beyond the tumour**.

### Clinical relevance
- Real-time imaging shows the ice ball; thermocouples and a **urethral warmer** protect sphincter and urethra in prostate cryotherapy.
- **Also know:** passive versus active thaw (both used); insulated probe shafts reduce skin injury.

**Diagram to draw:** ice ball with probe, −40 °C isotherm inside the 0 °C edge and tumour margin.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Argon freezes, helium thaws (Joule–Thomson).
> - Four mechanisms: direct injury, vascular thrombosis, apoptosis, immune effect.
> - ≤−40 °C, two freeze–thaw cycles, margin 5–10 mm.

## Imaging measures in CA prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 13 Paper 2, Q3e
(mpMRI detail in 2024-Winter Paper 1, Q3d; PSMA PET in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3a)

### 1. Ultrasound
- **TRUS (transrectal ultrasound):** guides biopsy, measures volume (PSA [prostate-specific antigen] density). Cancer is a hypoechoic PZ (peripheral zone) lesion, but **39% are isoechoic**.
- **Also know:** Doppler, contrast and elastography (adjuncts); **micro-ultrasound** (29 MHz; PRI-MUS [Prostate Risk Identification using Micro-Ultrasound] score; close to MRI accuracy).

### 2. MRI: detection and local staging
- **mpMRI (multiparametric magnetic resonance imaging):**

| Sequence | Shows | Cancer |
|---|---|---|
| T2-weighted | Anatomy; EPE (extraprostatic extension), SVI (seminal vesicle invasion) | Low signal |
| DWI (diffusion-weighted imaging) with ADC (apparent diffusion coefficient) | Cellularity | Bright on DWI, dark on ADC |
| DCE (dynamic contrast-enhanced) | Vascularity | Early enhancement, washout |

- **PI-RADS v2.1 (Prostate Imaging Reporting and Data System)** 1–5: DWI dominant in PZ, T2 in TZ (transition zone); DCE only upgrades PZ 3 to 4.
- **PROMIS** (sensitivity 93% vs 48% for TRUS biopsy) and **PRECISION** (more significant, less insignificant cancer): **MRI before biopsy**. Cochrane: sensitivity 0.91, specificity 0.37.
- **Also know:** biparametric MRI; **PI-QUAL** (Prostate Imaging Quality); **PRECISE** (scores change on surveillance); fusion biopsy.

### 3. Staging by risk group
- **Low or intermediate risk:** no routine CT (computed tomography) or bone scan.
- **High risk:** CT plus **⁹⁹ᵐTc-MDP (methylene diphosphonate) bone scan**, or **PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) PET (positron emission tomography)**.
- **proPSMA:** accuracy 92% vs 65% for conventional imaging. Nodal sensitivity only 30–40%, specificity >90%: a negative scan does not exclude micrometastases.

### 4. Biochemical recurrence
- **PSMA PET** works at low PSA. **Calais:** 56% vs 26% for fluciclovine. **CONDOR:** management changed in 63.9%.
- Choline and fluciclovine PET are older; FDG (fluorodeoxyglucose) PET is poor except in neuroendocrine change.

### 5. Advanced disease
- PSMA PET selects men for **¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA-617** (VISION); whole-body MRI. No proof yet that PSMA-guided treatment improves survival.

**Diagram to draw:** prostate zones with a PZ lesion dark on ADC; PSMA PET nodal uptake.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - MRI before biopsy (PROMIS, PRECISION); PI-RADS v2.1.
> - High risk: bone scan + CT or PSMA PET (proPSMA 92% vs 65%).
> - PSMA nodal sensitivity 30–40%.

## LHRH analogue
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 2, Q3c
(also answered in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3b and 2017-Winter Paper 4, Q3c)

### Physiology
- **LHRH (luteinising hormone-releasing hormone), also GnRH (gonadotrophin-releasing hormone)**, is a hypothalamic decapeptide released in **pulses**. It drives pituitary LH (luteinising hormone) and FSH (follicle-stimulating hormone); LH stimulates Leydig cells (about 95% of testosterone).
- **Continuous** exposure desensitises and down-regulates the pituitary receptor: the basis of agonist therapy.

### Agonists
| Drug | Formulation |
|---|---|
| Leuprolide | Depot 1, 3, 6 monthly |
| Goserelin | SC (subcutaneous) implant 1 or 3 monthly |
| Triptorelin | IM (intramuscular) depot |
| Histrelin | Implant, 1 year |

- Substitution at **position 6** raises potency and half-life.

```
Agonist → LH surge (flare) → receptor down-regulation
        → LH falls → castrate testosterone in 2–4 weeks
```
- Survival **equals orchidectomy**. Castrate level **<50 ng/dL**; aim **<20 ng/dL** (orchidectomy median about 15).

### Flare phenomenon
- LH rises up to **10-fold for 10–20 days**: bone pain, **spinal cord compression**, retention.
- **Prevent:** antiandrogen (bicalutamide) started with or before the agonist, for **21–28 days**.
- Impending cord compression → antagonist or orchidectomy.

### Antagonists
- Immediate receptor block, **no flare**, FSH falls.
- **Degarelix** (SC monthly): castrate by day 3 in >95%.
- **Relugolix** (oral). **HERO** vs leuprolide: sustained castration 97% vs 89%, faster recovery, fewer MACE (major adverse cardiovascular events).

### Indications
- Metastatic hormone-sensitive prostate cancer (with ARPI (androgen receptor pathway inhibitor) or docetaxel).
- With radiotherapy for intermediate/high-risk and locally advanced disease.
- BCR (biochemical recurrence) with short doubling time; continued in CRPC (castration-resistant prostate cancer).
- **Also know:** intermittent ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) acceptable; precocious puberty, endometriosis.

### Side effects
- Hot flushes, loss of libido, ED (erectile dysfunction).
- **Osteoporosis:** DEXA (dual-energy X-ray absorptiometry), calcium, vitamin D, denosumab or zoledronic acid.
- Diabetes, cardiovascular events, sarcopenia, anaemia, gynaecomastia (≤5%); slow testosterone recovery.

**Diagram to draw:** hypothalamic–pituitary–testis axis showing agonist and antagonist sites.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Agonist: surge, then down-regulation; castrate in 2–4 weeks.
> - Flare: LH 10-fold for 10–20 days; antiandrogen 21–28 days.
> - Castrate <50 ng/dL; aim <20 ng/dL.
> - Antagonist: no flare; preferred in cord compression.

## Expectant treatment of CA prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 2, Q3d

### Rationale
- Overdiagnosis: autopsy prevalence of microfocal cancer roughly equals age as a percentage; lifetime risk of death is about 3%.
- **Grade group 1 (Gleason 6) has virtually no metastatic potential.** Deaths on observation come from higher grade missed at biopsy (about 30% upgraded at prostatectomy).

### Two forms of expectant treatment
| | **AS (active surveillance)** | **WW (watchful waiting)** |
|---|---|---|
| Patient | Fit for radical therapy, life expectancy ≥10 years | Limited life expectancy (age, comorbidity) |
| Intent | **Curative, deferred** | **Palliative** |
| Monitoring | PSA (prostate-specific antigen), DRE (digital rectal examination), MRI, biopsy | Infrequent PSA and review; no biopsy |
| Action | Radical treatment on reclassification | ADT (androgen deprivation therapy) or palliation for symptoms |

### Evidence
| Trial | Result |
|---|---|
| **SPCG-4** (pre-PSA) | Prostatectomy cut cancer death (19.6% vs 31.3%), mainly <65 years and higher grade |
| **PIVOT** | No significant mortality benefit; low-risk cancer death 2.7% |
| **ProtecT** (15 years) | Cancer death <3% in all arms; metastasis doubled on active monitoring (9.4% vs about 5%) |

### Selection for AS
- **Very low risk (Epstein):** grade group 1, ≤2 positive cores, no core >50%, PSA density <0.15.
- **Low risk:** grade group 1, PSA <10 ng/mL; all guidelines prefer AS.
- **Favourable intermediate risk** (small-volume grade group 2): selected cases.
- Caution: **BRCA2** carriers, high-volume disease.

### AS protocol
- MRI and **confirmatory biopsy** within 1–3 years; PSA 6-monthly; DRE every 1–2 years; MRI every 18–24 months; biopsy every 3–5 years.
- **Triggers:** grade group ≥3 → treat; volume or PSA rise → MRI or biopsy first.

### Outcomes
- Cancer mortality **0.5–5% at 15 years** by entry criteria (Johns Hopkins vs Toronto); about **25% treated by 5 years**.

### Watchful waiting
- Switch AS to WW when life expectancy falls below about 5–10 years.
- **Also know:** genomic tests refine selection; 5ARI (5α-reductase inhibitor) has no proven role.

**Diagram to draw:** AS flowchart: diagnosis → MRI + confirmatory biopsy → surveillance → trigger → treatment.

> **Key points to remember:**
> - AS is deferred cure; WW is palliation.
> - Grade group 1 hardly ever metastasises; risk lies in missed higher grade.
> - ProtecT: no mortality difference at 15 years; more metastases on monitoring.
> - About 25% leave AS by 5 years.

## Molecular staging of CA prostate
**Type:** SAQ (10 marks)  
**Source:** Practice Set 14 Paper 2, Q3e
(related: prognosis in 2019-Summer Paper 4, Q3e)

### Definition and rationale
- Clinical staging (DRE (digital rectal examination), PSA (prostate-specific antigen), grade, imaging) misses occult spread and cannot fully predict behaviour.
- **Molecular staging** uses DNA, RNA or protein markers in tissue, blood or nodes to find hidden disease and predict outcome.

### 1. Detection of occult spread
- **RT-PCR (reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction)** for PSA or PSMA (prostate-specific membrane antigen) mRNA in blood, marrow or "negative" nodes: detects micrometastases, but inconsistent, so not used clinically.
- **CTCs (circulating tumour cells):** counts are prognostic in metastatic disease; **AR-V7** (splice variant lacking the ligand-binding domain) predicts resistance to ARPIs (androgen receptor pathway inhibitors).
- **ctDNA (circulating tumour DNA)** liquid biopsy: DNA-repair mutations; clonal haematopoiesis gives false calls.

### 2. Tissue genomic classifiers
| Test | Basis | Predicts |
|---|---|---|
| **Decipher** | 22-RNA classifier | Metastasis, cancer death; post-prostatectomy radiotherapy decision |
| **Oncotype DX GPS (Genomic Prostate Score)** | 17 genes (12 cancer, 5 reference) by RT-PCR | Adverse pathology; AS (active surveillance) choice |
| **Prolaris** | 31 cell-cycle progression genes | Progression, cancer death |

- **Also know:** PORTOS (24-gene radiotherapy response score); ConfirmMDx (methylation, after negative biopsy).

### 3. Genetic markers
- **Germline:** BRCA2 (aggressive disease), BRCA1, ATM, CHEK2, Lynch genes, HOXB13 (risk, not aggressiveness); test in metastatic or high-risk disease.
- **Somatic:** PTEN loss, TMPRSS2:ERG fusion, homologous recombination repair defects (guide PARP (poly ADP-ribose polymerase) inhibitors).

### 4. Molecular imaging
- **PSMA PET (positron emission tomography):** more sensitive and specific than CT and bone scan for nodes and metastases (proPSMA), but cannot exclude micrometastases.

### Clinical use
- Refines **AS vs treatment** and post-operative radiotherapy decisions; guides targeted therapy.

### Limitations
- Mostly retrospective; **multifocality and heterogeneity** (the sampled focus may not be the worst); cost; added value still being tested (G-MAJOR trial).

**Diagram to draw:** clinical stage vs molecular stage (tissue, blood, nodes, imaging).

> **Key points to remember:**
> - Molecular staging finds occult disease and predicts behaviour beyond TNM.
> - Decipher 22 genes; Oncotype 17 genes; Prolaris cell-cycle genes.
> - AR-V7 on CTCs predicts ARPI resistance; BRCA2 means aggressive disease.
> - Limitation: heterogeneity and mostly retrospective data.

## Rapid Revision Sheet – Section 17 · Prostate Cancer

*All the key points from this section in one place. Read this in the last 30 minutes before the exam.*

#### Castration-resistant prostate cancer (CRPC): definition and treatment (LAQ)

- Testosterone <50 ng/dL with PSA or radiological progression; keep ADT going.
- AR still drives CRPC.
- nmCRPC with PSADT ≤10 months: apalutamide, enzalutamide or darolutamide.
- mCRPC: ARPI, taxanes (docetaxel, cabazitaxel), radium-223, ¹⁷⁷Lu-PSMA, PARP inhibitors, sipuleucel-T.
- Switch mechanism; never radium-223 with abiraterone.

#### Metastatic prostate cancer with bone metastases (retention and bone pain; bone mets; zoledronic acid) (LAQ)

- Never start an LHRH agonist alone with spinal metastases: cover the flare or use an antagonist (avoid flare: antiandrogen cover, antagonist or orchidectomy).
- Single 8 Gy fraction gives the same pain relief as longer courses (for focal pain); dexamethasone and MRI for cord compression.
- Focal back pain = cord compression until MRI proves otherwise.
- ADT plus abiraterone or an ARPI, or a triplet, for every fit man.
- Denosumab 120 mg or zoledronate 4 mg (IV) 4-weekly prevent SREs in CRPC (mCRPC), not in HSPC; adjust zoledronate for creatinine clearance.
- No survival benefit; not for SREs in mHSPC.
- Dental check first (ONJ); calcium and vitamin D.
- Denosumab delays SREs longer (20.7 vs 17.1 months).
- Radium-223 (ALSYMPCA) for symptomatic bone-only mCRPC; never with abiraterone.

#### PSA screening for prostate cancer: evidence, problems and solutions (LAQ)

- ERSPC: (about) 20% fall in prostate cancer death; PLCO null from contamination.
- Core problems: low specificity, no safe cut-off, overdiagnosis, overtreatment.
- USPSTF 2018: Grade C at 55–69, Grade D ≥70.
- Fix: risk-adapted PSA, MRI before biopsy, active surveillance for GG1; MRI-first and active surveillance reduce overdiagnosis and overtreatment.

#### Staging of prostate cancer and management of locally advanced (non-organ-confined) prostate cancer; complications of bilateral orchidectomy (LAQ)

- Seminal vesicle invasion is T3b; non-regional nodes are M1a.
- Locally advanced = T3–4, N0/N+, M0; treat with combined therapy.
- RT + long-term ADT (18–36 months) is the standard; ADT alone is inferior (SPCG-7).
- RT + 18–36 months ADT: EORTC 22863 overall survival 79% vs 62%; STAMPEDE abiraterone metastasis-free survival HR 0.53.
- STAMPEDE: abiraterone 2 years for N1 or very high-risk M0 (metastasis-free survival HR 0.53).
- Neoadjuvant ADT before RP: no survival benefit (gain); early salvage RT favoured.
- Early salvage RT preferred over adjuvant RT for most men after RP.
- Orchidectomy: castrate in 12 hours; flushes, osteoporosis (denosumab), metabolic, sexual effects.

#### 65 yr male underwent RP for stage T1c Gleason's score (4+3). At 6 months post surgery, his S. PSA has risen to 2 ng/ml above his preop PSA value. Discuss treatment and prognosis (LAQ)

- EAU high-risk BCR: PSADT ≤1 year or grade group 4–5.
- PSMA PET first: almost 100% detection above PSA 2 ng/mL.
- Salvage RT ≥64 Gy + ADT: RTOG 9601 (OS), GETUG-AFU 16, SPPORT (add pelvis).
- Pound: BCR <2 years, PSADT <10 months, Gleason 8–10 predict death.

#### 48 yr male presented with mixed voiding symptoms with PSA 18. Discuss management option in brief. (LAQ)

- PSA 18 at 48: exclude cancer first; MRI before biopsy, transperineal targeted + systematic cores.
- PSA >10: organ-confined <50%; PSA >20: nodes about 20%.
- Young fit man: RP treats both cancer and obstruction.
- α-blocker for LUTS; no 5ARI until cancer excluded (halves PSA).

#### Discuss management of organ confined CA prostate (LAQ)

- Organ-confined = cT1–T2 N0 M0; NCCN risk groups drive choice.
- AS is preferred for low risk; ProtecT: no mortality difference at 15 years.
- EBRT + ADT: 4–6 months (intermediate), 18–36 months (high).
- Focal therapy rests on the index lesion hypothesis; still investigational.

#### Transrectal ultrasonography (TRUS) (SAQ)

- Probe 6–10 MHz; volume = 0.52 × T × AP × L; PSAD >0.15 suspicious.
- 39% of cancers isoechoic; hypoechoic PZ lesion is classic; 12-core biopsy; sepsis 0.3–3%.
- Seminal vesicle >1.5 cm wide: ejaculatory duct obstruction.
- PROMIS and PRECISION established the MRI-first pathway.

#### Radiotherapy and brachytherapy for CA prostate (SAQ)

- α/β ~1.5 Gy: basis of hypofractionation (CHHiP 60 Gy in 20).
- ADT: 4–6 months unfavourable intermediate, 24–36 months high risk.
- Early salvage (PSA 0.1–0.2) = adjuvant (RADICALS, RAVES, GETUG-AFU 17).
- Phoenix: nadir + 2 ng/mL.
- LDR ¹²⁵I 145 Gy; HDR ¹⁹²Ir.
- D90 >90%, V100 >85%.
- IPSS >20 or Qmax <10 = relative contraindication.
- ASCENDE-RT: boost halves biochemical failure.

#### TRUS / prostate biopsy (SAQ)

- MRI before biopsy (PROMIS, PRECISION).
- 12 cores; antibiotics ≤24 hours for all (transrectal).
- Bleeding commonest; sepsis most serious.
- Transperineal now preferred.

#### PIN (SAQ)

- Atypical cells, benign architecture, basal layer retained.
- Isolated HGPIN needs no repeat biopsy; ASAP does.
- Cribriform "PIN" is now IDC-P or AIP.

#### Maximum (combined) androgen blockade and bicalutamide (SAQ)

- MAB = castration + non-steroidal antiandrogen (blocks adrenal androgens).
- PCTCG 2000: only 2–3% 5-year survival gain.
- Main use now: flare cover for 21–28 days.
- Newer AR pathway inhibitors beat CAB (ENZAMET).
- Bicalutamide half-life about 6 days; 50 mg with LHRH agonist, 150 mg monotherapy.
- Monotherapy raises testosterone; gynaecomastia two-thirds.
- EPC: harm in localised disease; RTOG 9601: survival gain with salvage radiotherapy.
- Withdrawal response 15–30%.

#### Gleason's score and its significance (SAQ)

- Biopsy score = commonest + highest pattern.
- 3+4 (GG2) and 4+3 (GG3) differ: BCR-free 88% vs 63%.
- GG4 = score 8; GG5 = 9–10.

#### HIFU (SAQ)

- Focused ultrasound → ≥55 °C coagulative necrosis plus cavitation.
- Transrectal HIFU suits posterior lesions; TULSA for anterior.
- Focal HIFU: continence 90–95%; HEAT 7-year failure-free 69%.

#### Saturation biopsy in CA prostate (SAQ)

- Saturation = >12 cores (18–21 or more); 5-mm template mapping.
- No gain at initial biopsy; used after negatives and for focal therapy.
- PROMIS: template mapping as reference; mpMRI sensitivity 93%.

#### Biological effects of cryosurgery (SAQ)

- Argon freezes, helium thaws (Joule–Thomson).
- Four mechanisms: direct injury, vascular thrombosis, apoptosis, immune effect.
- ≤−40 °C, two freeze–thaw cycles, margin 5–10 mm.

#### Imaging measures in CA prostate (SAQ)

- MRI before biopsy (PROMIS, PRECISION); PI-RADS v2.1.
- High risk: bone scan + CT or PSMA PET (proPSMA 92% vs 65%).
- PSMA nodal sensitivity 30–40%.

#### LHRH analogue (SAQ)

- Agonist: surge, then down-regulation; castrate in 2–4 weeks.
- Flare: LH 10-fold for 10–20 days; antiandrogen 21–28 days.
- Castrate <50 ng/dL; aim <20 ng/dL.
- Antagonist: no flare; preferred in cord compression.

#### Expectant treatment of CA prostate (SAQ)

- AS is deferred cure; WW is palliation.
- Grade group 1 hardly ever metastasises; risk lies in missed higher grade.
- ProtecT: no mortality difference at 15 years; more metastases on monitoring.
- About 25% leave AS by 5 years.

#### Molecular staging of CA prostate (SAQ)

- Molecular staging finds occult disease and predicts behaviour beyond TNM.
- Decipher 22 genes; Oncotype 17 genes; Prolaris cell-cycle genes.
- AR-V7 on CTCs predicts ARPI resistance; BRCA2 means aggressive disease.
- Limitation: heterogeneity and mostly retrospective data.
